# Criminal Appeals and Revision - India Legal Protocol

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-CRIM-APPEALS
- **Version**: 1.0.0
- **Effective Date**: January 2025
- **Last Updated**: January 2025
- **Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)
- **Domain**: Criminal Law
- **Subdomain**: Criminal Procedure
- **IPC Provisions**: N/A
- **BNS 2023 Provisions**: N/A
- **CrPC Provisions**: Sections 372-405, 397-406, 432-435, 482
- **BNSS 2023 Provisions**: Sections 431-455, 456-461, 475-478, 528
- **Related Protocols**: IL-CRIM-TRIAL, IL-CRIM-EVIDENCE, IL-CRIM-BAIL

---

## Overview and Purpose

### What is a Criminal Appeal?

A **criminal appeal** is a **legal proceeding** where a higher court **reviews the decision** of a lower court in a criminal case, to determine if errors of law or fact occurred that warrant reversal or modification of the judgment.

### Purpose of Criminal Appeals

1. **Correct Errors**: Rectify mistakes of law or fact made by trial court
2. **Ensure Justice**: Second opinion on conviction/acquittal/sentence
3. **Protect Rights**: Safeguard constitutional and legal rights
4. **Consistency**: Ensure uniform application of law
5. **Public Confidence**: Demonstrate fairness through multi-tier review

### Types of Appellate Remedies

| Remedy | Nature | Forum | Against |
|--------|--------|-------|---------|
| **Appeal** | As of right OR with leave | Sessions Court, High Court, Supreme Court | Conviction, acquittal, sentence |
| **Revision** | Discretionary | High Court, Sessions Court | Any order (not just conviction/acquittal) |
| **Reference** | By lower court seeking guidance | High Court, Supreme Court | Difficult questions of law |
| **Review** | Reconsideration by same court | High Court, Supreme Court | Own judgment (limited grounds) |
| **Writ** | Constitutional remedy | High Court (226), Supreme Court (32) | Illegal orders, violations of rights |

---

## Legal Framework and Statutory Basis

### Constitutional Foundation

#### Article 134: Appellate Jurisdiction of Supreme Court (Criminal Matters)
- Appeal to Supreme Court if High Court:
  - Reverses acquittal and sentences to death, OR
  - Withdraws case for trial before itself and convicts with death/life/≥10 years, OR
  - Certifies case fit for appeal to Supreme Court

#### Article 136: Special Leave Petition (SLP)
- Supreme Court may grant **special leave to appeal** from any judgment/order of any court/tribunal
- **Discretionary** power

### Primary Legislation

#### Code of Criminal Procedure (CrPC) 1973
- **Chapter XXIX**: Appeals (Sections 372-394)
- **Chapter XXX**: Reference and Revision (Sections 395-405)
- **Chapter XXXI**: Transfer of Criminal Cases (Sections 406-412)
- **Chapter XXXII**: Execution, Suspension, Remission, Commutation (Sections 413-435)
- **Section 482**: Inherent Powers of High Court

#### Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita (BNSS) 2023
- **Chapters corresponding to above** (Sections 431-528)
- Effective from **July 1, 2024**
- Substantively similar to CrPC with procedural enhancements

---

## Types of Appeals

### 1. Appeal to Sessions Court

#### A. Appeal by Convicted Person (Section 374 CrPC / 432 BNSS)

**Who Can Appeal**: Any person convicted by Magistrate

**Against**:
- Conviction
- Sentence
- Both

**Appealable Orders**:
- Conviction by Magistrate
- Sentence imposed by Magistrate

**Limitation**: **30 days** from date of judgment (Section 389 / 445 BNSS)
- Court can condone delay (up to 90 days with sufficient cause)

**Contents of Appeal**:
- Grounds of appeal
- Copy of judgment appealed against
- Memorandum of appeal

**Powers of Sessions Court** (Section 386 CrPC / 442 BNSS):
1. **Reverse** finding and acquit
2. **Alter** finding (convict of lesser offence)
3. **Reduce** sentence
4. **Enhance** sentence (with notice to accused)
5. **Remit** case to Magistrate for retrial
6. **Dismiss** appeal

**Important**: Sessions Court can **enhance sentence** (Section 386(b)(ii))
- But must give notice to accused (opportunity to be heard)

#### B. Appeal by Acquitted Person: NOT PERMITTED
- Acquitted person cannot appeal against acquittal (no relief needed)
- But prosecution can appeal against acquittal (to High Court)

### 2. Appeal to High Court

#### A. Appeal by Convicted Person (Section 374 CrPC / 432 BNSS)

**Who Can Appeal**: Person convicted on trial by:
- **Sessions Court**, OR
- **High Court** (in exercise of original jurisdiction)

**Against**:
- Conviction
- Sentence
- Both

**Appealable Convictions**:
- Any conviction by Sessions Court
- Conviction by High Court in original jurisdiction

**Limitation**: **30 days** from date of judgment (Section 389 / 445 BNSS)
- Condonation possible (90 days with sufficient cause)

#### B. Appeal by Prosecution Against Acquittal (Section 378 CrPC / 436 BNSS)

**Who Can Appeal**: **State Government** (through Public Prosecutor)

**Against**: Order of acquittal by:
- **Magistrate** (if Sessions Court confirms or High Court certifies), OR
- **Sessions Court**

**Procedure**:
- State Government **directs** Public Prosecutor to appeal
- High Court hears appeal

**Important**: Not automatic; State Government's sanction required

**Landmark Case**: **State of U.P. v. Shambhu Nath Singh (2001)**
- Prosecution's right to appeal against acquittal is statutory
- High Court can reverse acquittal if finding perverse or illegal
- But acquittal orders require **greater scrutiny** (presumption of double innocence)

#### C. Appeal Against Sentence (Section 377 CrPC / 435 BNSS)

**Who Can Appeal**:
- **Convicted person**: Against sentence (if no appeal against conviction)
- **State Government**: Against **inadequate sentence** (seeking enhancement)

**When State Appeals for Enhancement**:
- If sentence is **inadequate**
- Public Prosecutor argues for higher sentence
- High Court can enhance sentence
- But must give notice to accused and opportunity to be heard

**Landmark Case**: **Deo Narain v. State of U.P. (1973)**
- Prosecution can appeal for enhancement of sentence
- High Court has power to enhance under Section 377
- Notice to accused mandatory before enhancement

#### D. Appeal by Complainant/Victim (Section 301, 372 CrPC / 355, 431 BNSS)

**Limitation**: Complainant/victim **cannot directly appeal** against acquittal

**Procedure**:
- Can **file application** to State Government requesting appeal
- State Government decides whether to appeal
- If State Government refuses, victim can file **protest petition** before High Court (Section 301)
- High Court can **direct** State to file appeal OR **entertain** victim's plea

**Landmark Case**: **Santosh Kumar v. State of Haryana (2012)**
- Victim has right to file protest petition against acquittal
- High Court can entertain victim's complaint even without State's appeal
- But victim has no **independent right** to appeal

### 3. Appeal to Supreme Court

#### A. As of Right (Article 134)

**When Available** (Section 379 CrPC / 437 BNSS + Article 134):

1. **High Court Reverses Acquittal** and sentences to **death**
2. **High Court Withdraws Case** to itself and convicts with **death, life, or ≥10 years**
3. **High Court Certifies** case fit for appeal to Supreme Court (on **substantial question of law** of general importance)

**Automatic Right**: If High Court reverses acquittal and gives death sentence
- No leave required
- Accused has **absolute right** to appeal

**Limitation**: **60 days** from date of High Court judgment (can be condoned)

#### B. Special Leave Petition (SLP) - Article 136

**Nature**: **Discretionary** remedy

**When**: When no appeal as of right exists

**Examples**:
- High Court confirms conviction by Sessions Court
- High Court confirms acquittal
- High Court reduces sentence (no acquittal)
- Any other case where Supreme Court deems fit

**Procedure**:
- File **Special Leave Petition** under Article 136
- Supreme Court may grant leave or dismiss
- If leave granted, becomes regular appeal

**Landmark Case**: **Pritam Singh v. State (1950)**
- Article 136 is **discretionary** power
- Supreme Court not bound to entertain every SLP
- Exercises discretion where:
  - Substantial question of law
  - Grave miscarriage of justice
  - Violation of fundamental rights
  - High Court judgment perverse

**Statistics**: Supreme Court admits only ~1-2% of SLPs (very selective)

---

## Revision (Sections 397-401 CrPC / 456-460 BNSS)

### What is Revision?

**Revision** is a **supervisory jurisdiction** where higher court can call for records of subordinate court and examine legality/propriety of any order (not limited to conviction/acquittal).

### Difference Between Appeal and Revision

| Aspect | Appeal | Revision |
|--------|--------|----------|
| **Nature** | Right (in most cases) | Discretionary |
| **Scope** | Re-hearing on merits | Supervisory (legality/propriety) |
| **Orders** | Conviction, acquittal, sentence | Any order (including interlocutory) |
| **Forum** | Sessions Court, High Court, Supreme Court | Sessions Court (limited), High Court |
| **Fresh Evidence** | Can be taken | Generally not taken |
| **Limitation** | 30/60 days | **No limitation** prescribed (but should be within reasonable time) |

### Revision to High Court (Section 397 CrPC / 456 BNSS)

**Who Can File**: Any person aggrieved by any order passed by Magistrate or Sessions Court

**Against What Orders**:
- Any order passed in course of proceeding
- Includes:
  - Bail orders
  - Discharge/acquittal orders
  - Conviction/sentence orders
  - Interlocutory orders (e.g., framing of charges, summoning witnesses)

**Grounds**:
- **Illegality** in order
- **Impropriety** in order
- **Material irregularity**

**Exclusions** (Section 397(2)):

High Court **cannot** revise:
1. Order of acquittal if **appeal lies** and not filed
2. Conviction if appeal lies and not filed
3. Order where **alternative remedy** (appeal) exists and not availed

**Principle**: Revision is **not substitute for appeal**
- If appeal available and not filed (or filed but dismissed), revision barred
- Revision only for orders where no appeal lies OR appeal filed but on limited grounds

**Powers of High Court** (Section 401 CrPC / 460 BNSS):
1. **Set aside** order
2. **Modify** order
3. **Remit** case for retrial
4. **Dismiss** revision

**Landmark Case**: **Akilu v. State of Rajasthan (1997)**
- Revision is supervisory jurisdiction
- Not re-hearing on merits
- High Court interferes only if illegality or material irregularity

### Revision to Sessions Court (Section 397(3) CrPC / 456(3) BNSS)

**Limited Power**: Sessions Judge can revise orders of **Magistrates subordinate to him**

**Same Restrictions**: Cannot revise if appeal lies and not filed

**Less Common**: Revision to Sessions Court rarely used (most go to High Court)

---

## Reference (Sections 395-396 CrPC / 454-455 BNSS)

### Reference to High Court (Section 395 CrPC / 454 BNSS)

**What is Reference?**
When **Sessions Court** has doubt on a question of law, it can **refer** the question to High Court for opinion

**Procedure**:
1. Sessions Court frames **question of law**
2. Submits reference to High Court
3. Parties heard in High Court
4. High Court gives **opinion**
5. Sessions Court decides case based on High Court's opinion

**Not Binding**: High Court's opinion is persuasive, but Sessions Court not absolutely bound (rare to deviate)

### Reference to Supreme Court (Article 143)

**Presidential Reference**: President can refer questions of law of public importance to Supreme Court

**Advisory Opinion**: Supreme Court gives advisory opinion

**Rare**: Used very rarely (e.g., Ayodhya reference, constitutional interpretation)

---

## Review (Order XLVII Rule 1 CPC, applied to criminal cases)

### What is Review?

**Review** is **reconsideration** by the **same court** that passed the judgment/order

### Grounds for Review (Very Limited)

**Only Three Grounds**:
1. **Discovery of new and important matter or evidence** which, after exercise of due diligence, was not within knowledge or could not be produced at time of judgment
2. **Mistake or error apparent on face of record**
3. **Any other sufficient reason** (interpreted very restrictively)

**Error Apparent**: Obvious, self-evident error; not requiring long-drawn reasoning to establish

**NOT Grounds for Review**:
- Re-appreciation of evidence
- Different interpretation of law
- Change of opinion

**Limitation**: **30 days** from date of judgment (can be extended)

**Landmark Case**: **Thungabhadra Industries v. Govt. of A.P. (1964)**
- Review is **not re-hearing**
- Only on limited grounds
- No re-appreciation of evidence
- Error must be **glaring and obvious**

**Criminal Cases**: Review rarely granted in criminal cases (finality important)

---

## Inherent Powers of High Court (Section 482 CrPC / 528 BNSS)

### What are Inherent Powers?

**Section 482 CrPC / 528 BNSS**: High Court has **inherent powers** to make such orders as necessary to:
1. Give effect to any order under CrPC/BNSS
2. Prevent **abuse of process** of court
3. Secure **ends of justice**

**Nature**: **Residuary and plenary** powers

**Not Codified**: Cannot be exhaustively listed; used in exceptional circumstances

### When Invoked?

**Common Situations**:
1. **Quashing of FIR**: If no cognizable offence disclosed or frivolous/vexatious complaint
2. **Quashing of charge sheet**: If no prima facie case
3. **Quashing of proceedings**: If continuation would be abuse of process
4. **Compounding of offences**: In certain cases
5. **Stay of proceedings**: Pending investigation/inquiry

**Landmark Case**: **State of Haryana v. Bhajan Lal (1992)**

**Bhajan Lal Guidelines** (When High Court can quash proceedings):
1. FIR/complaint does not disclose cognizable offence
2. Allegations, even if true, do not make out offence
3. Criminal proceedings are abuse of process (e.g., to harass, settle civil dispute)
4. Proceedings initiated with mala fide intent
5. Allegations are absurd and inherently improbable
6. No legal evidence to support allegations
7. Complaint not made in good faith
8. Proceedings would be futile (e.g., statutory bar, limitation)

**Important**: Inherent powers used **sparingly**
- Not to be used as substitute for appellate/revisional powers
- Interference only in **exceptional cases**

---

## Suspension of Sentence Pending Appeal (Section 389 CrPC / 445 BNSS)

### Meaning

**Suspension of Sentence**: Temporary stay of execution of sentence while appeal is pending

**Effect**: Convicted person released from jail during pendency of appeal

### Who Can Grant?

**Appellate Court** (Sessions Court, High Court, Supreme Court) hearing the appeal

### When Granted?

**Discretionary**: Court considers:
1. **Prima facie merit** in appeal
2. **Nature of offence** (serious or not)
3. **Severity of sentence**
4. **Period already undergone**
5. **Chances of accused absconding**
6. **Possibility of tampering** with evidence/witnesses

**General Principle**: Suspension granted liberally if:
- Short sentence
- Non-serious offence
- Prima facie strong grounds of appeal
- Substantial period already served

**Restrictive**: If serious offence (murder, rape, terrorism), suspension rarely granted

### Conditions

**Section 389(1)**: Appellate court may:
- **Suspend execution** of sentence
- **Release accused on bail**
- Impose **conditions** (surety, reporting, surrender passport)

**Duration**: Until appeal disposed of (or until court revokes)

**Landmark Case**: **Gurbaksh Singh Sibbia v. State of Punjab (1980)**
- Suspension of sentence is different from bail
- Once convicted, accused loses presumption of innocence
- Suspension granted only if **prima facie case** in appeal

---

## Powers of Appellate Court (Section 386 CrPC / 442 BNSS)

### Disposal of Appeal

**Appellate Court may**:

#### 1. Reverse Finding and Acquit (Section 386(a))
- If prosecution failed to prove guilt beyond reasonable doubt
- Trial court erred in convicting
- Accused acquitted

#### 2. Alter Finding (Section 386(b))

**Three Sub-Options**:

##### (i) Convict of Lesser Offence
- Trial court convicted of major offence, but evidence proves only minor offence
- Example: Conviction under Section 302 (murder) altered to Section 304 Part II (culpable homicide not amounting to murder)

##### (ii) Reduce Sentence
- Finding of conviction upheld
- But sentence reduced
- Example: Death sentence reduced to life imprisonment

##### (iii) Enhance Sentence (With Notice)
- If prosecution appeals
- Sentence increased
- **Mandatory**: Notice to accused and opportunity to be heard before enhancement

#### 3. Remit Case for Retrial (Section 386(c))
- If trial vitiated due to irregularity/illegality
- Fresh trial ordered
- Example: Denial of cross-examination, violation of Section 313

#### 4. Dismiss Appeal (Section 386(d))
- If no merit
- Conviction and sentence confirmed

**Landmark Case**: **Jameel v. State of U.P. (2010)**
- Appellate court has wide powers under Section 386
- Can alter finding and convict of lesser offence if evidence supports
- But cannot convict of different offence not charged at trial

---

## Appreciation of Evidence in Appeal

### Principles for Appellate Court

**General Rule**: Appellate court can **re-appreciate evidence** and come to its own conclusion

**Unlike Trial Court**: Appellate court does not have advantage of seeing witnesses (demeanor, credibility)

### Guidelines for Interfering with Acquittal

**Stricter Standard**: Acquittal orders have **presumption of double innocence**

**Landmark Case**: **Chandrappa v. State of Karnataka (2007)** (Leading case on appeals against acquittal)

**Principles**:
1. **Double Presumption**: Accused has (i) presumption of innocence, (ii) presumption that trial court acquitted him correctly
2. **Not Re-Trial**: Appellate court should not re-try case
3. **Perversity Test**: Interfere only if findings are **perverse, illegal, or suffer from material irregularity**
4. **Two Views**: If two views possible (one leading to conviction, other to acquittal), view favorable to accused should prevail
5. **Reasons Required**: If reversing acquittal, detailed reasons mandatory
6. **Benefit of Doubt**: Continued application of "benefit of doubt" principle

**Effect**: Reversing acquittal more difficult than confirming conviction

### Guidelines for Confirming Conviction

**Less Restrictive**: Appellate court can more easily uphold conviction if satisfied of guilt beyond reasonable doubt

**Fresh Evidence**: Can take fresh evidence (Section 391 CrPC / 447 BNSS) if necessary

---

## Compounding of Offences (Section 320 CrPC / 380 BNSS)

### What is Compounding?

**Compounding** means **settlement/compromise** of criminal case between accused and victim, leading to **termination of prosecution**

### Types of Compoundable Offences

**Section 320 CrPC / 380 BNSS** lists compoundable offences in **two categories**:

#### Category 1: Compoundable by Victim Without Court Permission
**Examples**:
- Assault (Section 323 IPC / 115 BNS)
- Defamation (Section 500 IPC / 356 BNS)
- Theft (certain circumstances)

**Procedure**: Parties settle; inform court; case closed

#### Category 2: Compoundable by Victim With Court Permission
**Examples**:
- Voluntarily causing hurt (Section 325 IPC / 117 BNS)
- Wrongful restraint/confinement
- House-trespass

**Procedure**: Parties settle; apply to court; court grants permission; case closed

### Non-Compoundable Offences

**Serious Offences NOT Compoundable**:
- Murder (Section 302 IPC / 103 BNS)
- Rape (Section 376 IPC / 64 BNS)
- Dacoity
- Terrorism
- Corruption

**Rationale**: These offences affect society at large, not just victim

### Inherent Powers for Compounding

**Section 482 CrPC / 528 BNSS**: High Court can quash proceedings to give effect to compromise **even in non-compoundable offences** (in exceptional circumstances)

**Landmark Case**: **Gian Singh v. State of Punjab (2012)**
- Supreme Court held High Court can quash even non-compoundable offences under Section 482 in exceptional cases
- Factors: Nature of offence, whether private/public injury, settlement genuine
- But offences affecting society (murder, rape, corruption) generally NOT quashed even if compromise

---

## Sentencing Appeals and Modification

### Appeal on Sentence Alone (Section 377 CrPC / 435 BNSS)

**Convicted Person**:
- Can appeal against **sentence** even if not appealing conviction
- Example: Accepts conviction but argues sentence too harsh

**Prosecution**:
- Can appeal for **enhancement** of sentence if inadequate
- High Court can enhance (with notice to accused)

### Principles of Sentencing on Appeal

**Appellate Court Considers**:
1. **Mitigating factors**:
   - First-time offender
   - Young age
   - Provocation
   - Socio-economic background
   - Period already undergone
   - Remorse
2. **Aggravating factors**:
   - Heinous manner of commission
   - No remorse
   - Danger to society
   - Previous convictions

**Death Sentence Appeals**:
- **Automatic Reference** to High Court for confirmation (Section 366 CrPC / 420 BNSS)
- High Court must confirm or commute
- Supreme Court reviews under **"Rarest of Rare"** doctrine (Bachan Singh)

**Landmark Case**: **Swamy Shraddananda v. State of Karnataka (2008)**
- Supreme Court introduced **"Special Category"** sentence
- Life imprisonment for **minimum 30 years** without remission (for offences falling short of "rarest of rare" but still very serious)
- Now applied in several cases

---

## Limitation Periods for Appeals and Revisions

### Appeals

| Appeal Type | Limitation Period | Condonation Possible? |
|-------------|-------------------|----------------------|
| **To Sessions Court** | 30 days | Yes (up to 90 days total) |
| **To High Court** | 30 days | Yes (up to 90 days total) |
| **To Supreme Court** (as of right) | 60 days | Yes (Supreme Court has liberal power) |
| **SLP to Supreme Court** | Reasonable time | Supreme Court discretion |

**Section 389 CrPC / 445 BNSS**: Limitation periods

**Condonation**: If sufficient cause shown, delay can be condoned

**Landmark Case**: **N. Balakrishnan v. M. Krishnamurthy (1998)**
- Sufficient cause for condonation to be decided on facts
- Bona fide reasons, not deliberate delay
- Each day of delay must be explained

### Revision

**No Specific Limitation**: Section 397 CrPC / 456 BNSS does not prescribe limitation

**But**: Should be filed within **reasonable time**
- Inordinate delay can be ground for dismissal
- Laches (delay and acquiescence) is relevant

---

## Bail Pending Appeal

### After Conviction

**Section 389 CrPC / 445 BNSS**: Appellate court can suspend sentence and release on bail

**Factors**:
- Prima facie merit in appeal
- Nature of offence
- Severity of sentence
- Period already undergone

**Restrictive for Serious Offences**: Murder, rape, terrorism - bail rarely granted

### After Acquittal (Prosecution Appeal)

**If Prosecution Appeals Against Acquittal**:
- Accused already acquitted at trial
- Can be arrested pending appeal **only if High Court directs**
- Generally, accused not arrested pending prosecution's appeal
- Arrested only if strong case and flight risk

**Landmark Case**: **Ranjit Singh v. State of Punjab (1994)**
- Acquitted person has additional protection
- Arrest pending prosecution's appeal only in exceptional cases

---

## CrPC to BNSS Transition (Appellate Provisions)

### Appeals and Revision: CrPC 1973 vs BNSS 2023

| Aspect | CrPC 1973 | BNSS 2023 | Change |
|--------|-----------|-----------|---------|
| **Appeals to Sessions Court** | Section 374 | Section 432 | Section number changed; **substance same** |
| **Appeals to High Court** | Sections 374, 377, 378 | Sections 432, 435, 436 | Section numbers changed; **substance same** |
| **Appeals to Supreme Court** | Section 379 | Section 437 | Section number changed |
| **Powers of appellate court** | Section 386 | Section 442 | Same powers |
| **Revision** | Sections 397-401 | Sections 456-460 | Section numbers changed; **procedure same** |
| **Reference** | Sections 395-396 | Sections 454-455 | Same |
| **Suspension of sentence** | Section 389 | Section 445 | Same |
| **Inherent powers** | Section 482 | Section 528 | **Section number changed; scope same** |
| **Compounding** | Section 320 | Section 380 | Same offences compoundable |
| **Limitation** | 30/60 days | 30/60 days | **Same** |

### Key Observations

**No Material Change**: BNSS 2023 **retains** CrPC appellate provisions with minimal changes
- Section numbers updated
- Substance remains same
- Judicial precedents continue to apply

**Victim Rights**: BNSS enhances victim participation, but appeal rights remain same (victim cannot independently appeal; must through State)

---

## Practical Compliance Checklist

### For Convicted Accused (Filing Appeal)

#### Pre-Filing
- [ ] Obtain **certified copy** of judgment (apply immediately after conviction)
- [ ] Consult **appellate lawyer** (trial lawyer may not be expert in appeals)
- [ ] Evaluate **grounds of appeal**:
  - Errors of law
  - Errors of fact
  - Improper appreciation of evidence
  - Illegal/improper procedure
  - Excessive sentence
- [ ] Check **limitation period** (30 days to Sessions/High Court, 60 days to Supreme Court)

#### Filing Appeal
- [ ] Prepare **memorandum of appeal** with grounds
- [ ] Attach **certified copy** of judgment
- [ ] If delay, file **condonation application** with affidavit explaining delay
- [ ] Pay **court fees**
- [ ] Apply for **suspension of sentence** (Section 389 / 445):
  - Prepare separate application
  - Show prima facie merit
  - Undertake to abide by conditions

#### After Filing
- [ ] Attend **hearings** regularly
- [ ] Comply with **bail conditions** (if sentence suspended)
- [ ] If sentence not suspended, file **bail application**
- [ ] Prepare **written arguments** (helpful for court)
- [ ] If fresh evidence needed, apply under Section 391 / 447

### For Prosecution (Appeal Against Acquittal)

#### Decision to Appeal
- [ ] Evaluate **merits**: Whether trial court judgment perverse/illegal
- [ ] Obtain **State Government sanction** (Section 378 / 436)
- [ ] File within **30 days**

#### Filing Appeal
- [ ] Prepare detailed grounds showing:
  - Trial court's findings are perverse
  - Material evidence ignored
  - Improper appreciation
  - Legal errors
- [ ] Attach certified copy of acquittal order
- [ ] If seeking arrest of acquitted person, file separate application (generally not granted)

#### Hearing
- [ ] Present arguments showing **perversity** in acquittal
- [ ] Respect **double presumption** of innocence (Chandrappa principle)
- [ ] Provide **detailed reasons** why acquittal should be reversed
- [ ] If successful and conviction ordered, argue for appropriate sentence

### For Appellate Courts

#### Hearing Appeals

**Against Conviction**:
- [ ] Re-appreciate evidence
- [ ] Consider whether guilt proved beyond reasonable doubt
- [ ] Check if trial suffered from material irregularity
- [ ] If acquitting, provide detailed reasons
- [ ] If confirming conviction, briefly explain agreement with trial court

**Against Acquittal** (Chandrappa Principles):
- [ ] Apply **double presumption** of innocence
- [ ] Interfere only if findings **perverse, illegal, or materially irregular**
- [ ] If two views possible, prefer view favorable to accused
- [ ] If reversing acquittal, provide **detailed, cogent reasons**
- [ ] Consider whether fresh trial needed (if irregularity vitiated trial)

**Against Sentence**:
- [ ] Consider mitigating and aggravating factors
- [ ] Proportionality of sentence
- [ ] Period already undergone
- [ ] If enhancing sentence, **give notice** to accused and hear them (mandatory)

#### Suspension of Sentence Applications
- [ ] Consider prima facie merit
- [ ] Nature of offence (serious or not)
- [ ] Likelihood of absconding
- [ ] Period already undergone
- [ ] Impose appropriate conditions (surety, reporting, surrender passport)

---

## Common Errors and Pitfalls

### For Accused

1. **Missing Limitation Period**: Not filing within 30/60 days (condonation possible but not guaranteed)
2. **Not Obtaining Certified Copy Promptly**: Delay in obtaining judgment copy wastes time
3. **Filing Appeal Without Merits**: Frivolous appeals waste time and money
4. **Not Applying for Suspension of Sentence**: Serving full sentence before appeal heard
5. **Violating Bail Conditions**: Leads to cancellation and arrest

### For Prosecution

1. **Filing Appeal Against Every Acquittal**: Not evaluating merits; should appeal only if perversity
2. **Not Obtaining State Sanction**: Appeal dismissed for lack of sanction (Section 378 / 436)
3. **Weak Grounds**: Not showing perversity; appellate court dismisses
4. **Not Preparing Detailed Arguments**: Acquittal has double presumption; weak arguments fail

### For Courts

1. **Not Giving Notice Before Enhancing Sentence**: Violates natural justice; enhancement set aside
2. **Reversing Acquittal Without Detailed Reasons**: Supreme Court sets aside (Chandrappa violation)
3. **Re-Trying Case Instead of Reviewing**: Appellate court should not act as trial court
4. **Not Applying Correct Standard**: "Beyond reasonable doubt" for conviction, "Perversity" for acquittal

## Conclusion

Criminal appeals are **essential safeguards** ensuring:
- **Errors corrected**: Trial court mistakes rectified
- **Justice served**: Second opinion on conviction/acquittal/sentence
- **Rights protected**: Fair trial, due process
- **Finality with fairness**: Balance between finality and correctness

**Key Principles**:
1. **Appeal is right** in most convictions (not discretionary)
2. **Appellate court can re-appreciate evidence**
3. **Acquittal has double presumption** (Chandrappa principle)
4. **Reversal of acquittal requires detailed reasons**
5. **Sentence can be enhanced** (with notice to accused)
6. **Suspension of sentence discretionary** (based on merit and nature of offence)
7. **Compounding possible** in specified offences
8. **Inherent powers used sparingly** (Section 482 / 528 - Bhajan Lal guidelines)
9. **Limitation: 30/60 days** (condonable with sufficient cause)
10. **Supreme Court SLP discretionary** (Article 136)

**Special Considerations**:
- **Death Penalty**: Automatic reference to High Court (Section 366 / 420); Supreme Court applies "rarest of rare" (Bachan Singh)
- **Acquittal Appeals**: Stricter standard (perversity, illegality, material irregularity)
- **Victim Rights**: Cannot independently appeal; must through State (Section 378 / 436)

The transition from CrPC to BNSS **retains appellate framework** with section number changes but **same substantive provisions** - ensuring continuity of jurisprudence and procedure.

**Ultimate Goal**: **Correct errors** while ensuring **finality** - balance between justice and closure.

---

## Document Control

**Version History**:
- v1.0.0 (January 2025): Initial protocol creation

**Review Schedule**: Annual review; immediate update for landmark judgments or statutory amendments

**Feedback**: Submit to protocol administrator

**Disclaimer**: Informational purposes only. Consult qualified legal professionals for specific cases.

---

**End of Protocol: IL-CRIM-APPEALS**


---
## IL-CRIM-ARREST-CUSTODY.md
---

# Arrest and Custody - India Legal Protocol

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-CRIM-ARREST-CUSTODY
- **Version**: 1.0.0
- **Effective Date**: January 2025
- **Last Updated**: January 2025
- **Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)
- **Domain**: Criminal Law
- **Subdomain**: Criminal Procedure
- **IPC Provisions**: N/A
- **BNS 2023 Provisions**: N/A
- **CrPC Provisions**: Sections 41-60A, 167, 436-450
- **BNSS 2023 Provisions**: Sections 35(3), 41-60A, 187, 482-489
- **Related Protocols**: IL-CRIM-INVESTIGATION, IL-CRIM-BAIL, IL-CRIM-FIR

---

## Overview and Purpose

### What is Arrest?

**Arrest** is the **act of depriving a person of liberty by legal authority** for the purpose of holding or detaining them to answer a criminal charge or to prevent commission of a criminal offence.

### Definition Under Law

**Section 41 CrPC / 41 BNSS**: Defines when police may arrest without warrant

**Black's Law Dictionary**: "The apprehending or detaining of the person in order to be forthcoming to answer an alleged or suspected crime"

### Purpose of Arrest

1. **Ensure Appearance**: Secure accused's presence during investigation and trial
2. **Prevent Escape**: Stop accused from fleeing jurisdiction
3. **Prevent Evidence Tampering**: Protect witnesses and evidence
4. **Public Safety**: Protect society from further offences
5. **Investigation**: Facilitate questioning and investigation

### Types of Arrest

| Type | Legal Basis | Warrant Required? |
|------|-------------|-------------------|
| **Arrest with warrant** | Section 70-81 CrPC / 69-80 BNSS | Yes |
| **Arrest without warrant** | Section 41 CrPC / 41 BNSS | No (if conditions met) |
| **Anticipatory bail** | Section 438 CrPC / 482 BNSS | Court protection |
| **Citizen's arrest** | Section 43 CrPC / 43 BNSS | No (limited situations) |

---

## Legal Framework and Statutory Basis

### Constitutional Foundation

#### Article 21: Right to Life and Personal Liberty
- Liberty is fundamental right
- Arrest must be in accordance with procedure established by law
- Arbitrary arrest prohibited

#### Article 22: Protection Against Arrest and Detention

**Article 22(1)**: Right to be informed of grounds of arrest
**Article 22(2)**: Right to consult and be defended by lawyer of choice
**Article 22(3)**: No detention beyond 24 hours without Magistrate's order

### Primary Legislation

#### Code of Criminal Procedure (CrPC) 1973
- **Chapter V**: Arrest of Persons (Sections 41-60A)
- **Chapter XII**: Remand (Section 167)

#### Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita (BNSS) 2023
- **Chapter V**: Arrest of Persons (Sections 41-60A)
- **Chapter XIII**: Remand (Section 187)
- Effective from **July 1, 2024**
- Enhanced safeguards for arrest

### Judicial Precedents (Constitutional Validity)

**D.K. Basu v. State of West Bengal (1997)**: Landmark case establishing 11 commandments for arrest and custody

**Arnesh Kumar v. State of Bihar (2014)**: Mandatory notice of appearance for offences punishable with less than 7 years

**Joginder Kumar v. State of U.P. (1994)**: Arrest is not mandatory even if power exists

---

## When Can Police Arrest Without Warrant?

### Section 41 CrPC / 41 BNSS: Conditions for Arrest

Police officer may arrest **without warrant** only if:

#### Category A: Cognizable Offences (Section 41(1))

**ANY police officer** may arrest without warrant:

##### (a) Person Who Has Committed Cognizable Offence
- In officer's presence
- Or against whom reasonable complaint/credible information/reasonable suspicion exists

##### (b) Person in Possession of Stolen Property
- Reasonably suspected to be in possession of stolen property

##### (c) Obstruction of Police Officer
- Obstructs police officer in discharge of duty
- Or escapes or attempts to escape from lawful custody

##### (d) Proclaimed Offender
- Against whom proclamation issued under Section 82 CrPC / 81 BNSS

##### (e) In Possession of Implements of Housebreaking
- Without lawful excuse

##### (f) Proclaimed as Deserter
- From Armed Forces

##### (g) Outside India Offences
- Committed act outside India which if committed in India would be cognizable

##### (h) Released Convict Committing Breach
- Of conditions of release

##### (i) Reasonable Suspicion of Being Habitual Offender

#### Category B: Restricted Arrest (Section 41(1) Proviso & Section 41A)

**MAJOR RESTRICTION** (Added by CrPC Amendment 2008, continued in BNSS):

Police **CANNOT arrest** even for cognizable offence if:
1. Offence punishable with **imprisonment less than 7 years** OR **only fine**
2. AND accused is **not a habitual offender**
3. AND officer is **satisfied** about identity and residence

**Instead of arrest**, officer must issue **Notice of Appearance** asking accused to appear before officer/court

**Conditions for Notice** (Section 41A):
- Person must sign undertaking to appear
- If refuses to sign or fails to appear, then arrest permitted

**Landmark Case**: **Arnesh Kumar v. State of Bihar (2014)**
- Supreme Court mandated compliance with Section 41A
- **No automatic arrest** for offences below 7 years
- Arrest is exception, not rule
- Magistrate must scrutinize grounds of arrest in remand applications

### Section 41(2) CrPC / 41 BNSS: Further Restrictions

Even if power to arrest exists, officer may arrest only if:

1. Officer has **reason to believe** arrest is necessary:
   - To prevent accused from committing further offence; OR
   - For proper investigation; OR
   - To prevent tampering with evidence; OR
   - To prevent influencing witnesses; OR
   - To prevent accused from fleeing

2. **Unless arrest is necessary, arrest should not be made**

**Principle**: **Arrest is the exception, not the rule** (Joginder Kumar principle)

---

## Arrest With Warrant

### When Warrant Required?

**Non-Cognizable Offences**: Arrest only with warrant issued by Magistrate (Section 155)

**Cognizable Offences**: Warrant may be issued if Magistrate considers necessary

### Execution of Warrant (Sections 70-81 CrPC / 69-80 BNSS)

#### How Warrant Executed:
1. **Show warrant** to person to be arrested (Section 75)
2. **Inform** about substance of warrant
3. **Apprehend** the person
4. **Use reasonable force** if person resists (Section 46)

#### Territorial Limits:
- Generally within jurisdiction of issuing court
- Can be endorsed for execution outside jurisdiction (Section 77)

#### Validity: Until executed or cancelled

---

## Procedure for Arrest

### Mandatory Procedures (Sections 46-50, 50A, 54 CrPC / 45-49, 49A, 53 BNSS)

#### 1. Information of Grounds of Arrest (Section 50 CrPC / 49 BNSS)

**Constitutional Mandate** (Article 22(1)):
- Person arrested must be **informed of grounds of arrest** as soon as possible
- Cannot be denied this right

**Procedure**:
- Inform in language person understands
- In writing if requested
- Communicate full particulars of offence

**Exception**: Need not inform if arrested in another's presence

#### 2. Right to Bail (Section 50(2))

If arrested for **bailable offence**, officer must **inform** person of:
- Right to be released on bail
- Right to arrange sureties

#### 3. Search of Arrested Person (Section 51 CrPC / 50 BNSS)

Officer may **search** person arrested and:
- Seize any articles (weapons, stolen property, evidence)
- Make inventory
- Two witnesses required

**Female Search**:
- By another **female** only
- With **strict regard to decency** (Section 51(2))

#### 4. Medical Examination (Section 53, 53A, 54 CrPC / 52, 52A, 53 BNSS)

##### Section 53: Examination of Accused by Medical Practitioner
- **When**: If arrested person requests OR officer in charge directs
- **Purpose**: Document injuries, if any (protect against torture allegations)
- **Registered Medical Practitioner** to conduct
- **Blood, DNA samples**: Can be taken with consent or Magistrate's order

##### Section 54: Examination of Person Arrested by Police
- Medical examination **at time of arrest**
- Medical examination **after release** (to show condition)
- **D.K. Basu mandate**: Compulsory medical examination

#### 5. Production Before Magistrate (Section 56-57 CrPC / 55-56 BNSS)

**Article 22(3) + Section 57**:
- Person arrested **without warrant** must be produced before **Magistrate within 24 hours**
- 24 hours excludes journey time from place of arrest to Magistrate
- **Cannot be detained beyond 24 hours** without Magistrate's remand order

**Purpose**: Judicial oversight over detention

**Landmark Case**: **Khatri v. State of Bihar (1981)** - Bhagalpur blinding case
- 24-hour rule is fundamental right
- Cannot be violated under any circumstances

---

## D.K. Basu Guidelines (Arrest Safeguards)

### D.K. Basu v. State of West Bengal (1997)

**Issue**: Custodial torture and deaths in police custody

**Supreme Court Directions** (11 Commandments):

### Safeguards at Time of Arrest

#### 1. Arrest Memo
- Prepare **Arrest Memo** at time of arrest
- Countersigned by arrested person
- Attested by one witness (preferably family member)
- Counter-signature need not be insisted if arrested person refuses

#### 2. Inform Family/Friend
- **Inform** a friend/relative/known person of arrest
- Inform that person is being taken to particular police station

#### 3. Right to Inform
- Arrested person has **right to inform** one person (family/friend) about arrest and place of custody

#### 4. Entry in Diary
- **Name and address** of person informed about arrest to be entered in **station diary**

### Safeguards During Custody

#### 5. Medical Examination (Timing)
- **Medical examination** of arrested person by competent doctor:
  - **At time of arrest** (to record pre-existing injuries)
  - **After release/bail** (to show condition)
- Copy of medical report to arrested person/relative

#### 6. Inspection Rights
- **Inspection of lock-up** by officer of rank of Deputy Superintendent or above
- Surprise visits to ensure no torture

#### 7. Interrogation Memo
- Interrogation to take place in presence of lawyer (if requested)
- Memo of interrogation prepared, signed by arrested person

#### 8. Copies of Documents
- Arrested person entitled to **copies** of all documents including:
  - Arrest memo
  - Medical examination report
  - Interrogation memo

### Safeguards for Remand

#### 9. Magistrate's Duty
- Magistrate to **satisfy** that arrest is in accordance with law
- Accused must be **produced in person**
- Magistrate to record satisfaction about no torture

#### 10. Legal Aid
- Inform arrested person of **right to legal aid**
- Provide legal aid if indigent

#### 11. Recording Statement
- If arrested person requests, Magistrate to **record statement** (to check torture)

**Status**: These guidelines have **force of law** until legislated (Prakash Singh case 2006)

**Implementation**: Now incorporated in Section 41B CrPC / 41B BNSS (Rights of Arrested Person)

---

## Police Custody and Remand

### Types of Custody

| Type | Granted By | Duration | Purpose |
|------|-----------|----------|---------|
| **Police Custody** | Magistrate (Section 167) | Max 15 days (in stages) | Investigation, interrogation |
| **Judicial Custody** | Magistrate (Section 167) | Max 60/90 days total | Awaiting investigation completion |
| **Judicial Custody (post charge sheet)** | Court | Until trial concludes | Awaiting trial |

### Section 167 CrPC / 187 BNSS: Procedure When Investigation Cannot Be Completed

#### Situation: Accused Arrested and Detained

**24-Hour Rule**:
- Accused must be produced before Magistrate within 24 hours
- Magistrate may authorize **detention** (remand)

#### Police Custody Remand

**Section 167(2)**:
- **Maximum**: 15 days **in total** (not continuous)
- Can be given in **stages** (e.g., 3 days, then 5 days, then 7 days)
- **First 15 days** from date of arrest
- Cannot exceed 15 days even if investigation not complete

**Purpose**: For investigation purposes (interrogation, confrontation, recovery)

**Conditions**:
- Magistrate must record **reasons** for police custody
- **"Adequate grounds"** must exist
- Not automatic; prosecution must justify

**Landmark Case**: **Niranjan Singh v. Prabhakar Rajaram (1980)**
- Police custody is exception
- Should be minimum necessary for investigation

#### Judicial Custody

**After police custody period or if police custody not needed**:
- Accused remanded to **judicial custody**
- Kept in **jail**, not police station
- No interrogation by police (without Court permission)

#### Maximum Detention Without Charge Sheet

**Section 167(2) Proviso**:

| Type of Offence | Maximum Detention |
|----------------|-------------------|
| Punishable with **death, life imprisonment, or ≥10 years** | **90 days** |
| **Other offences** | **60 days** |

**Consequence if charge sheet not filed within period**:
- Accused entitled to **DEFAULT BAIL** (indefeasible right)
- Must be released (even if serious offence)

**Landmark Case**: **Sanjay Dutt v. State (1994)**
- Default bail is **indefeasible statutory right**
- Once period expires, accused must be released
- This is mandatory, not discretionary

### Default Bail (Section 167(2) Proviso)

**Conditions**:
1. Accused arrested and detained
2. 60/90 day period expired
3. Charge sheet not filed

**Effect**: Accused **must** be released on bail

**Nature**: Statutory right (not discretionary)

**Important**: This is **in case custody** (person already arrested)
- Different from regular bail under Section 437

---

## Rights of Arrested Person

### Constitutional Rights

#### 1. Right to Be Informed of Grounds of Arrest (Article 22(1))
- Immediately on arrest
- In language understood
- Full particulars of offence

#### 2. Right to Consult Lawyer (Article 22(1))
- Right to consult legal practitioner of choice
- Right to be defended by lawyer

#### 3. Right Not to Be Detained Beyond 24 Hours (Article 22(3))
- Without Magistrate's order
- Fundamental right, cannot be violated

#### 4. Right Against Self-Incrimination (Article 20(3))
- Cannot be compelled to be witness against self
- Right to remain silent
- Applies during interrogation

#### 5. Right to Life and Liberty (Article 21))
- Arrest must be in accordance with fair procedure
- No torture, no custodial violence
- Humane treatment

### Statutory Rights (Section 41B, 50, 50A, 54, 56 CrPC / BNSS)

#### Section 41B CrPC / 41B BNSS: Rights of Arrested Person

Every arrested person has right to be informed:
1. **Grounds of arrest**
2. **Right to bail** (if bailable offence)
3. **Right to inform** friend/relative/known person about arrest and place of detention
4. **Right to consult lawyer** of choice
5. **Right to medical examination**
6. **Right to be produced before Magistrate** within 24 hours

#### Section 50A CrPC / 49A BNSS: Obligation to Inform About Arrest

**Police must inform** nominated person about arrest:
- **Immediately** after arrest
- By **letter** dispatched within 8-12 hours
- **Free of cost**

**If Nominated Person Outside State**:
- Additional communication to police officer of that area
- That officer to inform nominated person

#### Rights During Custody

1. **Medical examination** (Section 54): At time of arrest and release
2. **No handcuffs** (except in exceptional circumstances - Prem Shankar Shukla case)
3. **Human treatment**: Food, clothing, sanitation
4. **Protection from torture**: D.K. Basu safeguards
5. **Access to lawyer**: For legal advice
6. **Magistrate production**: Regular production if remand extended

---

## Restrictions on Arrest

### Constitutional and Judicial Restrictions

#### 1. No Arbitrary Arrest (Article 21)

**Joginder Kumar v. State of U.P. (1994)**:
- Arrest **only if necessary**
- Not mandatory even if power exists
- Officer must justify necessity

#### 2. Arnesh Kumar Principle (2014)

For offences **punishable with less than 7 years**:
- **No arrest** unless necessary
- Issue **notice of appearance** (Section 41A)
- Arrest only if person absconds or doesn't comply

#### 3. Anticipatory Bail Protection (Section 438 CrPC / 482 BNSS)

Person **apprehending arrest** can approach court for protection
- **See Protocol**: IL-CRIM-BAIL.md

### Statutory Restrictions

#### 1. Female Arrest (Section 46(4) CrPC / 45(4) BNSS)

**Special Protection**:
- Female cannot be arrested **after sunset and before sunrise**
- **Exception**: With permission of Magistrate in exceptional circumstances
- **Search**: Only by female officer with strict decency

#### 2. Arrest of Children (Juvenile Justice Act 2015)

- **Child in Conflict with Law** (below 18 years): Cannot be arrested like adults
- Special procedure under JJ Act
- Brought before **Juvenile Justice Board**, not Magistrate
- Kept in **observation home**, not jail
- **No handcuffs**

#### 3. Arrest of Mentally Ill (Section 330 CrPC / 395 BNSS)

- Special provisions for custody
- Must be kept in suitable place
- Treatment required

#### 4. Arrest of Members of Parliament/Legislative Assemblies

**Article 22(2)**: No special immunity
**BUT**: Information to Speaker/Chairman required (Rules of House)

#### 5. Arrest of Public Servants (Section 41(1)(a) CrPC)

For offences under Prevention of Corruption Act:
- **Sanction required** for prosecution (Section 19 PCA 1988)
- But **no sanction required for arrest** (CBI v. V.C. Shukla)
- However, practically sanction sought before arrest

---

## Use of Force in Arrest

### Section 46 CrPC / 45 BNSS: Arrest How Made

#### General Rule
- Police officer may use **all means necessary** to effect arrest
- Arrested person must **submit** to custody

#### Use of Force
**Section 46(2)**: Force permitted if:
1. Person **resists** or attempts to evade arrest
2. **Only reasonable force** can be used
3. No more force than necessary

#### Restriction on Causing Death
**Section 46(3)**:
- **Cannot cause death** of person accused of offence punishable with:
  - **Fine only**, or
  - Imprisonment **not exceeding 7 years**

- For offences punishable with **death or life imprisonment**:
  - Can cause death **if person cannot be otherwise apprehended**
  - **Last resort only**

**Principle**: Proportionality - force must be proportionate to offence and resistance

**Landmark Case**: **Darshan Singh v. State of Punjab (2010)**
- Use of excessive force in arrest amounts to assault
- Can lead to criminal prosecution of police officer
- Only reasonable force permitted

### Handcuffing

**General Rule**: Handcuffing is **violation of human dignity**

**Landmark Case**: **Prem Shankar Shukla v. Delhi Administration (1980)**
- Supreme Court held handcuffing is prima facie **inhuman and unreasonable**
- Violates Article 21 (right to human dignity)

**When Permitted**:
- **Only in exceptional circumstances**:
  1. Accused is extremely dangerous
  2. History of violence or escape
  3. Accused may cause harm to self or others
  4. Specific reasons must be recorded in writing

**Section 49 CrPC / 48 BNSS**: Person arrested not to be subjected to more restraint than necessary

**Guidelines** (Citizens for Democracy v. State of Assam, 1995):
- Handcuffing prohibited as routine
- Only in exceptional cases with justification
- Alternative restraint methods preferred
- If used, reasons to be recorded

---

## Arrest by Private Person (Citizen's Arrest)

### Section 43 CrPC / 43 BNSS: When Private Person May Arrest

**Any private person** may arrest:

1. Person who commits **non-bailable and cognizable offence** in his presence
2. **Proclaimed offender**

**Condition**: Must **without unnecessary delay** hand over person to police

**Limitation**: Cannot detain; must immediately give to police

**Section 45**: Private person acting under Section 43 is **protected** (same as police officer)

**Practical Examples**:
- Shopkeeper catching shoplifter red-handed
- Citizen apprehending person committing robbery
- Security guard catching trespasser committing theft

**Caution**: Risk of false arrest lawsuit if mistake made

---

## Arrest in Special Circumstances

### 1. Arrest Outside Jurisdiction

**Section 43 CrPC / 43 BNSS**: Police officer can pursue into other jurisdiction
**Section 48**: Transit warrant for taking accused through other states

### 2. Arrest at Night

**Generally Permitted**: No bar on arrest at night for cognizable offences
**Female**: Cannot arrest after sunset and before sunrise (Section 46(4))

### 3. Arrest Without Uniform

**Section 42A CrPC / 42A BNSS**:
- Officer not in uniform must **show identity card**
- Must carry authority letter from senior officer

### 4. Re-arrest After Release

**Permitted**: Person released on bail can be re-arrested
- If bail cancelled
- If commits fresh offence
- If violates bail conditions

### 5. Arrest of Accused Already on Bail

**Section 437(5) CrPC / 483(5) BNSS**: Can be arrested if:
- Court cancels bail
- Fresh offence committed
- Bail conditions violated

---

## Remedies Against Illegal/Wrongful Arrest

### 1. Habeas Corpus (Article 226 / 32)

**Most Effective Remedy**:
- Writ petition to **High Court** (Article 226) or **Supreme Court** (Article 32)
- For **immediate release** if detention illegal
- Court issues writ directing production of person
- If detention found illegal, person released immediately

**Grounds**:
- Arrest without authority
- Detention beyond 24 hours without remand
- Violation of arrest procedure
- Mala fide arrest

**Landmark Case**: **Rudul Sah v. State of Bihar (1983)**
- Person detained **14 years after acquittal**
- Supreme Court ordered release and awarded **compensation**

### 2. Bail Application

- **Anticipatory bail** (Section 438 CrPC / 482 BNSS): Before arrest
- **Regular bail** (Section 437 CrPC / 483 BNSS): After arrest
- **Default bail** (Section 167(2)): If charge sheet not filed in 60/90 days

**See Protocol**: IL-CRIM-BAIL.md

### 3. Complaint to Senior Officers

- SP/Commissioner/DIG
- Complaint of illegal arrest, torture
- Departmental inquiry against erring officer

### 4. Complaint to Magistrate (Section 155(2) CrPC / 174(2) BNSS)

- Non-cognizable offence complaint
- Magistrate can order investigation

### 5. Criminal Prosecution of Police Officer

**Offences by Police**:
- **IPC Section 166 / BNS 199**: Public servant disobeying law (illegal arrest)
- **IPC Section 220 / BNS 212**: Illegal detention
- **IPC Section 342 / BNS 127**: Wrongful confinement
- **IPC Section 348 / BNS 133**: Wrongful confinement with threat
- **IPC Section 323-326 / BNS 115-121**: Assault (if force used)
- **IPC Section 330 / BNS 127**: Voluntarily causing hurt to extort confession

**Requirement**: Generally, sanction required for prosecution of public servant (Section 197 CrPC / 218 BNSS)

### 6. Civil Suit for Damages

**Malicious Prosecution**:
- Suit for damages in civil court
- Must prove: (1) prosecution initiated, (2) without reasonable cause, (3) maliciously, (4) prosecution terminated in accused's favor

**False Imprisonment**:
- Tort action for unlawful detention
- Compensation for loss of liberty

**Landmark Case**: **Bhim Singh v. State of J&K (1986)**
- MLA illegally arrested and detained
- Supreme Court awarded **Rs. 50,000 compensation** for violation of fundamental rights

---

## Custodial Deaths and Torture

### Constitutional and Legal Framework

**Article 21**: Protection of life and personal liberty includes protection from custodial torture

**IPC Sections** (now BNS):
- **Section 330 / BNS 127**: Voluntarily causing hurt to extort confession
- **Section 331 / BNS 128**: Voluntarily causing grievous hurt to extort confession

### D.K. Basu Safeguards

(Already covered above - 11 commandments)

### National Human Rights Commission (NHRC)

**NHRC Act 1993**:
- Mandatory reporting of **custodial deaths** to NHRC within 24 hours
- NHRC inquiry and recommendations
- Compensation to victims

### Landmark Cases

#### 1. **D.K. Basu v. State of West Bengal (1997)**
- Leading case on custodial torture
- 11 safeguards laid down (detailed above)

#### 2. **Nilabati Behera v. State of Orissa (1993)**
- Youth died in police custody
- Supreme Court awarded **Rs. 1.5 lakh compensation** to mother
- Under **public law remedy** (Article 32)

#### 3. **Sheela Barse v. State of Maharashtra (1983)**
- Women in police lock-ups
- Directions for protection of women in custody

#### 4. **Khatri v. State of Bihar (1981)** - Bhagalpur Blinding Case
- Police blinded under-trial prisoners with acid
- Supreme Court ordered compensation and prosecution of officers
- Emphasized 24-hour production rule

---

## Arrest Under Special Statutes

### Differences from CrPC/BNSS

Many special statutes have **stricter provisions** for arrest and bail:

| Statute | Arrest Provision | Bail Provision |
|---------|------------------|----------------|
| **NDPS Act 1985** (Drugs) | Section 41, 42, 43 | Section 37 - **twin conditions** for bail |
| **PMLA 2002** (Money Laundering) | Section 19 | Section 45 - **very strict** twin conditions |
| **UAPA 1967** (Terrorism) | Section 43 | Section 43D - restrictive bail |
| **PCA 1988** (Corruption) | No special provision | Regular CrPC bail applies |
| **POCSO Act 2012** (Child Sexual Abuse) | CrPC applies | Anticipatory bail **generally not granted** |

**Twin Conditions** (NDPS Section 37, PMLA Section 45):
For bail, court must be satisfied:
1. There are **reasonable grounds** to believe accused **not guilty**, AND
2. Accused **not likely to commit** any offence while on bail

**Effect**: Very difficult to get bail in these cases

**Landmark Case**: **Nikesh Tarachand Shah v. Union of India (2018)**
- Supreme Court upheld constitutional validity of Section 45 PMLA
- Stringent bail conditions justified due to seriousness of money laundering

---

## CrPC to BNSS Transition (Arrest Provisions)

### Arrest Procedure: CrPC 1973 vs BNSS 2023

| Aspect | CrPC 1973 | BNSS 2023 | Change |
|--------|-----------|-----------|---------|
| **Arrest without warrant** | Section 41 | Section 41 | Same conditions |
| **Notice of Appearance** | Section 41A | Section 41A | Same (Arnesh Kumar principle continues) |
| **Rights of arrested person** | Section 41B | Section 41B | Same |
| **Procedure of arrest** | Sections 46-60 | Sections 45-60 | Largely same |
| **Female arrest** | Section 46(4) - not after sunset | Section 45(4) - same | **No change** |
| **Information to person** | Section 50A | Section 49A | Same (inform nominated person) |
| **Medical examination** | Sections 53, 54 | Sections 52, 53 | Same |
| **24-hour production** | Section 56, 57 | Section 55, 56 | Same constitutional mandate |
| **Remand** | Section 167 | Section 187 | **Section number changed; 60/90 day rule continues** |
| **Handcuffing** | Not specifically addressed | Section 35(3) - **new explicit provision** | **Enhancement** |

### Key BNSS Enhancement: Handcuffing Provision

**Section 35(3) BNSS**:
- Explicit provision on **use of handcuffs**
- Can be used only if:
  1. Person has committed **heinous crime** (punishable with death or life), AND
  2. **Reasons recorded in writing** by police officer
- Codifies Prem Shankar Shukla judgment

**Impact**: Statutory restriction on handcuffing (previously only judicial precedent)

---

## Practical Compliance Checklist

### For Police Officers (Arrest Procedure)

#### Before Arrest
- [ ] Verify if offence is cognizable
- [ ] Check if offence punishable with <7 years (if yes, issue notice under Section 41A instead of arrest unless exceptional)
- [ ] Assess **necessity** of arrest (Joginder Kumar / Arnesh Kumar principle)
- [ ] Record reasons for arrest in writing (Section 41(2))
- [ ] If female, ensure arrest not after sunset/before sunrise (except with Magistrate permission)
- [ ] Prepare to inform grounds of arrest

#### At Time of Arrest
- [ ] **Inform** person of **grounds of arrest** (Section 50 / Article 22(1))
- [ ] Prepare **arrest memo** with details (D.K. Basu)
- [ ] Get counter-signature of arrested person (or record refusal)
- [ ] Attest by one witness (preferably family member)
- [ ] **Inform** one friend/relative/known person about arrest and place of detention
- [ ] Enter details in **station diary**
- [ ] If bailable offence, inform person of **right to bail**
- [ ] Show **identity card** if not in uniform (Section 42A)

#### Immediately After Arrest
- [ ] Conduct **medical examination** by registered medical practitioner (Section 54)
- [ ] Record all injuries (pre-existing)
- [ ] Give copy of medical report to arrested person or relative
- [ ] Search arrested person (Section 51) with two witnesses; if female, search by female officer
- [ ] Seize articles and prepare inventory
- [ ] Dispatch **letter to nominated person** within 8-12 hours (Section 50A)

#### Within 24 Hours
- [ ] **Produce** arrested person before **Magistrate within 24 hours** (Article 22(3) + Section 57)
- [ ] 24 hours excludes journey time
- [ ] Prepare case diary entry

#### During Custody
- [ ] If remand sought, file application with grounds
- [ ] Magistrate to record satisfaction
- [ ] Police custody maximum 15 days in stages
- [ ] Ensure no torture, no third degree (D.K. Basu safeguards)
- [ ] Provide food, clothing, sanitation
- [ ] Regular medical check-ups if needed
- [ ] No handcuffing unless heinous crime and reasons recorded (Section 35(3) BNSS)

#### Before Release
- [ ] Conduct **medical examination** again (Section 54)
- [ ] Compare with arrest-time medical report
- [ ] Give copy to person released
- [ ] Return personal belongings

### For Arrested Person (Know Your Rights)

#### At Time of Arrest
- [ ] Ask for **grounds of arrest** in writing
- [ ] Request to **inform** one family member/friend
- [ ] Ask for **medical examination** (right under Section 54)
- [ ] Cooperate but do not self-incriminate (Article 20(3))
- [ ] **Sign arrest memo** or refuse (your choice)

#### Immediately After
- [ ] Insist on medical examination to record any injuries
- [ ] Get copy of medical report
- [ ] Contact **lawyer** (right under Article 22(1))
- [ ] If indigent, request legal aid

#### Production Before Magistrate (Within 24 Hours)
- [ ] You **must be produced** within 24 hours
- [ ] Inform Magistrate if tortured or threatened
- [ ] Request Magistrate to record your statement (D.K. Basu right)
- [ ] Apply for bail if applicable
- [ ] If police seeking remand, you can oppose

#### During Custody
- [ ] Insist on human treatment
- [ ] No torture - report immediately if occurs
- [ ] Request lawyer visit
- [ ] Keep track of custody period (60/90 days)
- [ ] Apply for **default bail** if charge sheet not filed within 60/90 days

### For Family/Friends

- [ ] If informed about arrest, note down:
  - Time of arrest
  - Place of custody (which police station)
  - Arresting officer's name
- [ ] Contact lawyer immediately
- [ ] Visit police station to verify arrest
- [ ] Ensure person medically examined
- [ ] File **habeas corpus** if detention illegal or person not produced within 24 hours
- [ ] Apply for anticipatory bail if arrest apprehended
- [ ] Apply for regular bail after arrest

---

## Related Protocols and Cross-References

### Directly Related
- **IL-CRIM-FIR**: First Information Report - triggers investigation leading to arrest
- **IL-CRIM-INVESTIGATION**: Investigation process during which arrest may occur
- **IL-CRIM-BAIL**: Bail procedures (anticipatory, regular, default)
- **IL-CRIM-TRIAL**: Trial process post-arrest and charge sheet

### Rights Related
- **IL-CONSTITUTIONAL-RIGHTS**: Article 21, 22 fundamental rights
- **IL-CRIM-EVIDENCE**: Evidence during investigation, statements

### Offence-Specific
- **IL-CRIM-[SPECIFIC-OFFENCES]**: Arrest procedures vary by offence
- **IL-CONSUMER-CYBER-CRIME**: IT Act arrests
- **IL-CORP-FRAUD**: Economic offences arrests (ED, CBI)

---

## Conclusion

Arrest and custody are **necessary evils** in the criminal justice system. While necessary to ensure:
- Presence of accused during investigation and trial
- Prevention of further crimes
- Protection of witnesses and evidence

They also represent **severe curtailment of personal liberty**, which is a fundamental right under Article 21.

**Key Principles**:
1. **Arrest is the exception, not the rule** (Joginder Kumar)
2. **No automatic arrest for offences <7 years** (Arnesh Kumar)
3. **Strict procedural safeguards** (D.K. Basu)
4. **Production within 24 hours** (Article 22(3))
5. **Default bail if charge sheet not filed** in 60/90 days (Section 167(2))
6. **No torture, human treatment** (Article 21)
7. **Right to legal aid and fair trial**

The transition from CrPC to BNSS continues these safeguards with **explicit provisions on handcuffing** (Section 35(3)) - codifying Supreme Court judgments.

**Bottom Line**: Liberty is precious. Arrest must be justified, procedurally correct, and subject to strict judicial oversight.

---

## Document Control

**Version History**:
- v1.0.0 (January 2025): Initial protocol creation

**Review Schedule**: Annual review; immediate update for landmark judgments or amendments

**Feedback**: Submit to protocol administrator

**Disclaimer**: Informational purposes only. Consult qualified legal professionals for specific cases.

---

**End of Protocol: IL-CRIM-ARREST-CUSTODY**
