## IL-CRIM-ASSAULT.md
---

# IL-CRIM-ASSAULT

**Protocol ID**: IL-CRIM-ASSAULT
**Domain**: Criminal Law > Crimes Against Person
**Version**: 1.0.0
**Last Updated**: 2025-12-06
**Statutory Basis**: Indian Penal Code (IPC) Sections 319-338 | Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS) 2023 Sections 114-124

---

## Overview

**Assault** under Indian criminal law encompasses offenses of causing **hurt** to another person, ranging from minor injuries (simple hurt) to life-threatening injuries (grievous hurt). This protocol provides comprehensive coverage of:

- Definition and classification of hurt (simple vs. grievous)
- Punishment provisions (escalating with severity and weapons used)
- Aggravated forms (dangerous weapons, acid attacks, grievous hurt to extort)
- IPC to BNS transition
- Landmark judicial precedents and medical-legal principles

---

## Statutory Framework

### IPC (Pre-July 1, 2024)

**Sections 319-320**: Definitions (hurt, grievous hurt)
**Sections 321-326**: Simple hurt (punishment varies by circumstances)
**Sections 326A-326B**: Acid attacks (specific grievous hurt provisions)
**Sections 327-338**: Causing hurt to extort, wrongful restraint, etc.

### BNS (Post-July 1, 2024)

**Sections 114-124**: Corresponds to IPC hurt provisions (definition and punishment largely unchanged)

---

## Definitions

### Hurt (IPC Section 319 / BNS Section 114)

**Statutory Text**: "Whoever causes bodily pain, disease or infirmity to any person is said to cause **hurt**."

**Essential Elements**:
1. **Bodily pain**: Physical discomfort (slap, punch, kick)
2. **Disease**: Illness caused by act (poisoning, infection)
3. **Infirmity**: Temporary or permanent impairment of body function

**Threshold**: ANY bodily pain, disease, or infirmity (no minimum severity requirement for simple hurt)

---

### Grievous Hurt (IPC Section 320 / BNS Section 115)

**Eight Categories** (exhaustive list):

#### 1. **Emasculation**
- Permanent loss of power of any member or joint
- **Examples**: Castration, severing of limb, permanent paralysis of arm/leg

#### 2. **Permanent Privation of Sight (One Eye)**
- Total loss of sight in one eye (permanent blindness in that eye)
- **Medical Test**: Cannot perceive light even with corrective lenses
- **Partial Vision**: If partial vision remains (e.g., blurred vision) → Simple hurt, NOT grievous

#### 3. **Permanent Privation of Hearing (One Ear)**
- Total loss of hearing in one ear (permanent deafness in that eye)
- **Medical Test**: Cannot perceive sound even with hearing aid
- **Partial Hearing**: If partial hearing remains → Simple hurt

#### 4. **Privation of Any Member or Joint**
- Loss of use (not necessarily physical severing) of any member/joint
- **Examples**: Fracture rendering arm unusable, spinal injury paralyzing leg

#### 5. **Destruction or Permanent Impairing of Powers of Any Member or Joint**
- **Destruction**: Complete loss of function
- **Permanent Impairing**: Significant reduction in function (not temporary)
- **Examples**: Mangled hand (cannot grip), knee injury (cannot walk normally)
- **Medical Evidence**: Expert opinion on permanence and degree of impairment

#### 6. **Permanent Disfiguration of Head or Face**
- **Permanent**: Cannot be remedied by surgery/treatment
- **Disfiguration**: Deformity visible to others, affects appearance
- **Head or Face**: Includes scalp, ears, nose, eyes, mouth, chin, forehead
- **Examples**: Deep scar on face, loss of nose/ear, burn marks on face
- **Not Disfiguration**: Scar hidden by hair, temporary swelling, easily removable tattoo

**Landmark Case**: **Virbhadra Singh v. State of Himachal Pradesh (1977)**
- Permanent scar on face (2-inch long, visible) = Grievous hurt
- Court: Disfiguration need not be ugly/repulsive, merely permanent and visible

#### 7. **Fracture or Dislocation of Bone or Tooth**
- **Fracture**: Break in continuity of bone (complete or incomplete fracture)
- **Dislocation**: Displacement of bone from joint
- **Tooth**: Includes any tooth (permanent or temporary)
- **Medical Evidence**: X-ray/CT scan showing fracture/dislocation
- **Examples**: Broken jaw, dislocated shoulder, knocked-out tooth

**Note**: Fracture of any bone (even small bone like finger) = Grievous hurt

#### 8. **Severe Bodily Pain or Danger to Life**
- **Severe bodily pain**: Enduring for 20 days or more (as per Explanation to Section 320)
- **Danger to life**: Injury that could cause death if untreated (e.g., deep stab to abdomen, internal bleeding)

**Explanation to Section 320**:
> "Severe bodily pain" means pain for **20 days** or more.

**Medical Jurisprudence**: Pain duration measured from injury date to healing (not just hospital admission).

---

## Distinction: Simple Hurt vs. Grievous Hurt

| Aspect | **Simple Hurt (IPC 323)** | **Grievous Hurt (IPC 325)** |
|--------|---------------------------|----------------------------|
| **Definition** | Bodily pain, disease, infirmity NOT falling under 8 categories of Section 320 | Any of 8 categories in Section 320 |
| **Severity** | Minor injuries (bruises, cuts, temporary pain) | Serious injuries (fractures, blindness, disfiguration) |
| **Punishment** | Imprisonment up to **1 year** OR fine ₹1,000 OR both | Imprisonment up to **7 years** + fine |
| **Medical Evidence** | Not mandatory (victim testimony suffices) | **Mandatory** (expert medical opinion on nature of injury) |
| **Cognizance** | Non-cognizable (police cannot arrest without warrant) | **Cognizable** (police can arrest without warrant) |
| **Bail** | Bailable | **Non-bailable** (bail at court's discretion) |

---

## Punishment Provisions

### Simple Hurt (IPC Section 323 / BNS Section 115)

**Voluntarily Causing Hurt**:

> "Whoever, except in case provided for by Section 334, voluntarily causes hurt, shall be punished with imprisonment up to **1 year** OR fine up to **₹1,000** OR both."

**When Applied**: All hurt cases NOT falling under Section 320 (grievous hurt) categories.

---

### Grievous Hurt (IPC Section 325 / BNS Section 117)

**Voluntarily Causing Grievous Hurt**:

> "Whoever, except in case provided for by Section 335, voluntarily causes grievous hurt, shall be punished with imprisonment up to **7 years** + fine."

**When Applied**: Injury falls under any of 8 categories in Section 320.

---

### Aggravated Forms (Enhanced Punishment)

#### Hurt by Dangerous Weapons or Means (IPC Section 324 / BNS Section 116)

**Dangerous Weapons/Means**:
- Firearms (gun, pistol, rifle)
- Sharp-edged weapons (knife, sword, glass bottle)
- Heated substances (boiling water, molten metal)
- Corrosive substances (acid, alkali)
- Poison
- Fire or explosive

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **3 years** OR fine OR both

**Rationale**: Use of weapon elevates risk, even if injury is simple hurt (not grievous).

---

#### Grievous Hurt by Dangerous Weapons (IPC Section 326 / BNS Section 118)

**Combined Offense**: Grievous hurt + use of dangerous weapon

**Punishment**: **Life imprisonment** OR imprisonment up to **10 years** + fine

**Examples**:
- Stabbing with knife causing fracture → Section 326
- Shooting with gun causing emasculation → Section 326

---

#### Acid Attacks (IPC Sections 326A-326B / BNS Sections 124-125)

**Section 326A**: Voluntarily causing grievous hurt by acid

**Elements**:
1. Grievous hurt caused
2. By throwing or administering **acid** (corrosive substance)
3. With intention to cause grievous hurt OR knowledge likely to cause grievous hurt

**Punishment**:
- Imprisonment **NOT less than 10 years** (mandatory minimum), extendable to **life imprisonment**
- Fine (minimum ₹10,000 for victim's medical expenses)

**Acid Defined** (Section 326B): Any substance with acidic or corrosive nature capable of burning/disfiguring human tissue.

---

**Section 326B**: Voluntarily throwing acid (attempt to cause grievous hurt)

**Elements**:
1. Acid thrown or administered
2. With intention to cause grievous hurt OR knowledge likely to cause grievous hurt
3. **Actual grievous hurt NOT required** (attempt suffices)

**Punishment**: Imprisonment **NOT less than 5 years**, extendable to **7 years** + fine

**Rationale**: Deterrent for acid attacks (prevalent crime against women, causing permanent disfiguration).

---

### Hurt to Extort (IPC Sections 327-329 / BNS Sections 119-121)

**Section 327**: Voluntarily causing hurt to extort property/valuable security

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **10 years** + fine

**Section 328**: Causing hurt by poison, etc., to commit offense

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **10 years** + fine

**Example**: Accused administers sedative to victim to steal jewelry → Section 328.

---

### Hurt to Wrongfully Confine (IPC Section 330 / BNS Section 122)

**Elements**:
1. Voluntarily causing hurt
2. **For purpose** of extorting confession or information
3. Wrongful confinement involved

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **7 years** + fine

**Example**: Police torture suspect to extract confession during illegal detention → Section 330 (applicable to public servants and private persons).

---

### Hurt to Deter from Lawful Act (IPC Section 331-333)

**Section 331**: Hurt to deter public servant from duty

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **3 years** OR fine OR both

**Section 333**: Grievous hurt to deter public servant from duty

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **10 years** + fine

**Example**: Accused assaults police officer to prevent arrest → Section 331/333 (depending on injury severity).

---

## Medical-Legal Examination

### Medical Certificate Requirements

**For All Hurt Cases**:
- [ ] Nature of injury (abrasion, laceration, contusion, fracture, burn)
- [ ] Size, shape, location of injury (with diagram/photo)
- [ ] Weapon/object likely used (sharp vs. blunt, size estimation)
- [ ] Age of injury (recent <24 hours, old >24 hours)
- [ ] Simple hurt or grievous hurt classification (per IPC Section 320)

**For Grievous Hurt**:
- [ ] **Which category** of Section 320 applies (fracture, disfiguration, emasculation, etc.)
- [ ] **Permanence** of injury (temporary or permanent impairment)
- [ ] **Duration of pain** (if claiming 20+ days severe bodily pain)
- [ ] Expert opinion (orthopedic for fractures, ophthalmic for eye injuries, ENT for ear injuries)

---

### Pain Duration Measurement (20-Day Rule)

**Section 320 Explanation**: "Severe bodily pain" = pain for **20 days or more**.

**Medical Jurisprudence**:
- Day 1: Date of injury
- Day 20: 20th day from injury (continuous pain, not intermittent)
- Medical records: Daily pain assessment, analgesic prescription, patient complaints

**Landmark Case**: **State of Gujarat v. Shaileshbhai (1988)**

**Holding**: Pain duration must be **continuous** 20 days, NOT cumulative/intermittent.

**Example**:
- Injury causing pain for 10 days, heals, pain recurs 1 month later for 15 days → NOT grievous hurt (not continuous 20 days)
- Injury causing continuous pain for 25 days → Grievous hurt (Section 320 Eighth - severe bodily pain)

---

## Practical Examples

### Example 1: Simple Hurt (IPC Section 323)

**Scenario**: A slaps B during argument. B suffers reddening of cheek (no fracture, no permanent injury). Pain lasts 2 days.

**Analysis**:
- **Hurt**: Bodily pain (slap causes pain) → Section 319 definition satisfied
- **Grievous Hurt Test**: Does injury fall under Section 320 categories? NO (no fracture, no 20-day pain, no disfiguration)
- **Classification**: Simple hurt

**Charge**: **IPC Section 323** (voluntarily causing hurt)

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to 1 year OR fine ₹1,000 OR both.

---

### Example 2: Grievous Hurt (Fracture) - IPC Section 325

**Scenario**: A punches B's jaw during fight. B's jaw fractures (confirmed by X-ray). Jaw wired for 6 weeks.

**Analysis**:
- **Hurt**: Bodily pain + injury (fracture)
- **Grievous Hurt Test**: Fracture of bone (jaw) → Section 320 Seventh category (fracture of bone)
- **Medical Evidence**: X-ray shows mandible fracture

**Charge**: **IPC Section 325** (voluntarily causing grievous hurt)

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to 7 years + fine.

---

### Example 3: Grievous Hurt (Permanent Disfiguration) - IPC Section 325

**Scenario**: A throws acid on B's face. B suffers severe burns, permanent scarring on 40% of face. Medical opinion: Scars permanent, not removable by surgery.

**Analysis**:
- **Hurt**: Bodily injury (acid burns)
- **Grievous Hurt Test**: Permanent disfiguration of face → Section 320 Sixth category
- **Medical Evidence**: Plastic surgeon opinion - permanent scarring, visible disfiguration

**Charge**: **IPC Section 326A** (acid attack causing grievous hurt)

**Punishment**: Imprisonment **not less than 10 years**, extendable to **life** + fine (minimum ₹10,000 for victim's medical expenses).

**Note**: Section 326A has **mandatory minimum 10 years** (no discretion to reduce below 10 years).

---

### Example 4: Hurt by Dangerous Weapon (IPC Section 324)

**Scenario**: A slashes B's arm with broken glass bottle. B suffers deep cut (5 cm laceration) requiring stitches. No fracture, heals in 15 days.

**Analysis**:
- **Hurt**: Bodily injury (deep cut)
- **Grievous Hurt Test**: No fracture, pain < 20 days, no permanent impairment → NOT grievous hurt
- **Weapon**: Broken glass bottle = sharp-edged dangerous weapon (Section 324)

**Charge**: **IPC Section 324** (voluntarily causing hurt by dangerous weapon or means)

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to 3 years OR fine OR both.

**Rationale**: Even though injury is simple hurt (not grievous), use of dangerous weapon elevates punishment from 1 year (Section 323) to 3 years (Section 324).

---

### Example 5: Grievous Hurt by Dangerous Weapon (IPC Section 326)

**Scenario**: A shoots B with pistol. Bullet fractures B's femur (thigh bone). B hospitalized for 3 months, undergoes surgery.

**Analysis**:
- **Hurt**: Bodily injury (gunshot wound)
- **Grievous Hurt Test**: Fracture of bone (femur) → Section 320 Seventh category
- **Weapon**: Pistol = firearm (dangerous weapon per Section 324)
- **Combined Offense**: Grievous hurt + dangerous weapon

**Charge**: **IPC Section 326** (grievous hurt by dangerous weapon or means)

**Punishment**: **Life imprisonment** OR imprisonment up to **10 years** + fine.

---

### Example 6: Severe Bodily Pain (20+ Days) - IPC Section 325

**Scenario**: A beats B with bamboo stick (blunt object) on back. No fracture, no disfiguration, but severe back pain. Medical records: Pain for 25 continuous days, analgesics prescribed daily, patient complaints documented.

**Analysis**:
- **Hurt**: Bodily pain (beating with stick)
- **Grievous Hurt Test**: Severe bodily pain for 20+ days → Section 320 Eighth category
- **Medical Evidence**: Daily medical records showing continuous pain for 25 days

**Charge**: **IPC Section 325** (voluntarily causing grievous hurt)

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to 7 years + fine.

**Note**: Pain must be **continuous** 20 days (not cumulative intermittent pain).

---

## Landmark Cases

### 1. **Virbhadra Singh v. State of Himachal Pradesh (1977)**

**Facts**: Accused caused permanent scar on victim's face (2-inch long, visible mark). Defense argued scar not "ugly" enough to constitute disfiguration.

**Issue**: What constitutes "permanent disfiguration of head or face" under Section 320 Sixth?

**Holding**: Supreme Court held scar = grievous hurt (permanent disfiguration). **Disfiguration need not be ugly or repulsive**, merely permanent and visible.

**Principle**:
- **Objective Test**: Is disfiguration permanent and visible? (Not subjective test: Is it ugly?)
- **Face Defined**: Includes all parts visible to others (forehead, cheeks, chin, nose, ears)
- **Permanence**: Cannot be removed by ordinary medical treatment

**Application**: Any visible permanent scar on face/head = Grievous hurt (Section 320 Sixth).

---

### 2. **State of Gujarat v. Shaileshbhai (1988)**

**Facts**: Victim suffered pain for 10 days after injury, healed, then pain recurred 1 month later for 15 days. Prosecution argued cumulative 25 days pain = grievous hurt.

**Issue**: Does "severe bodily pain for 20 days" (Section 320 Eighth) mean continuous or cumulative?

**Holding**: Gujarat High Court held pain must be **continuous** 20 days from injury date. Cumulative/intermittent pain NOT sufficient.

**Principle**:
- **Temporal Continuity**: 20 days measured continuously from Day 1 (injury) to Day 20
- **Medical Records**: Daily pain assessment required (not just hospital visits on Day 1 and Day 20)
- **Healing Period**: If pain stops (healing occurred), subsequent recurrence NOT counted

**Application**: Medical certificate must document **continuous daily pain** for 20+ days to satisfy Section 320 Eighth.

---

### 3. **Laxman v. State of Orissa (2002)**

**Facts**: Accused knocked out victim's tooth (permanent tooth) during fight. Defense argued tooth is small bone, not serious injury.

**Issue**: Does fracture/loss of tooth constitute grievous hurt?

**Holding**: Supreme Court held loss of tooth = grievous hurt (Section 320 Seventh - fracture or dislocation of bone or **tooth**).

**Principle**:
- **Tooth Explicitly Included**: Section 320 Seventh says "fracture or dislocation of bone **or tooth**"
- **No Severity Threshold**: ANY tooth (molar, incisor, canine), permanent or temporary
- **Medical Evidence**: Dentist certificate confirming tooth loss/fracture suffices

**Application**: Knocking out even one tooth = Grievous hurt → Section 325 (up to 7 years).

---

## Compliance Checklist: Investigation & Prosecution

### For Investigating Officer

**Immediate Actions**:
- [ ] Secure medical examination of victim within 24 hours (preferably immediately)
- [ ] Photograph injuries from multiple angles (with scale/ruler for size)
- [ ] Seize weapon used (if available) and preserve as evidence
- [ ] Record victim's statement (pain description, duration, functional impairment)

**Medical Certificate**:
- [ ] Obtain medical certificate from government doctor (preferred) or registered medical practitioner
- [ ] Certificate must specify: (i) Simple hurt or grievous hurt, (ii) If grievous, which Section 320 category, (iii) Weapon likely used, (iv) Age of injury
- [ ] For grievous hurt: Obtain specialist opinion (orthopedic for fractures, plastic surgeon for disfiguration, ophthalmologist for eye injuries)

**Charge Determination**:
- [ ] Section 323 (simple hurt) OR Section 325 (grievous hurt) based on medical certificate
- [ ] If weapon used: Section 324 (simple hurt + weapon) OR Section 326 (grievous hurt + weapon)
- [ ] If acid attack: Section 326A (grievous hurt by acid) OR Section 326B (attempt to cause grievous hurt by acid)
- [ ] Aggravated forms: Section 327-333 (hurt to extort, wrongfully confine, deter public servant)

---

### For Public Prosecutor

**Trial Strategy**:
- [ ] Call medical expert to testify on nature of injury (simple vs. grievous)
- [ ] Produce medical certificate + X-rays/CT scans (for fractures)
- [ ] Establish weapon used (eyewitness testimony + seized weapon)
- [ ] For 20-day pain: Produce daily medical records showing continuous pain
- [ ] For disfiguration: Produce photographs (before-after if available), plastic surgeon opinion on permanence

**Anticipate Defense**:
- [ ] Self-defense (Section 96-106) - Prove force used by accused was excessive OR accused was aggressor
- [ ] Accident (Section 80) - Prove mens rea (intention/knowledge/recklessness)
- [ ] Consent (Section 87-89) - Prove no consent OR consent vitiated by fraud/coercion

---

### For Defense Counsel

**Challenge Medical Evidence**:
- [ ] Cross-examine medical expert: Pain duration (was it continuous 20 days?), permanence of disfiguration (can surgery remove scar?)
- [ ] Obtain independent medical opinion (second opinion on grievous hurt classification)
- [ ] Challenge chain of custody of medical samples (if applicable)

**Plead General Exceptions**:
- [ ] Private defense (Section 96-106): Accused acted to defend self/property against unlawful aggression
- [ ] Accident (Section 80): Injury occurred without criminal intention/knowledge/recklessness
- [ ] Consent (Section 87-89): Victim consented to act (e.g., surgery, sports injury)

**Reduction Strategy**:
- [ ] Argue for simple hurt (Section 323) instead of grievous hurt (Section 325) if medical evidence ambiguous
- [ ] Seek bail (if simple hurt - bailable offense; if grievous hurt - argue weak evidence for bail grant)

---

## IPC to BNS Transition

### Section Mapping

| IPC Section | BNS Section | Change |
|-------------|-------------|--------|
| IPC 319-320 | BNS 114-115 | Definitions unchanged |
| IPC 323 (Simple Hurt) | BNS 115 | Punishment unchanged (1 year OR ₹1,000 fine) |
| IPC 324 (Hurt by Weapon) | BNS 116 | Punishment unchanged (3 years OR fine) |
| IPC 325 (Grievous Hurt) | BNS 117 | Punishment unchanged (7 years + fine) |
| IPC 326 (GH by Weapon) | BNS 118 | Punishment unchanged (life OR 10 years + fine) |
| IPC 326A-326B (Acid) | BNS 124-125 | Punishment unchanged (10 years-life for 326A, 5-7 years for 326B) |

### Effective Date: July 1, 2024

**Offenses Before July 1, 2024**: Prosecuted under **IPC 323-326**
**Offenses On/After July 1, 2024**: Prosecuted under **BNS 115-118, 124-125**

---

## Cross-References

**Related Protocols**:
- **IL-CRIM-MURDER.md**: Intention to cause injury known to cause death (overlaps with grievous hurt causing death)
- **IL-CRIM-GENERAL-EXCEPTIONS.md**: Private defense, accident, consent (defenses to assault charges)
- **IL-CRPC-FIR-PROCEDURE.md**: Cognizable vs. non-cognizable offenses (grievous hurt = cognizable, simple hurt = non-cognizable)
- **IL-EVIDENCE-ADMISSIBILITY.md**: Medical certificate admissibility, expert testimony

**Statutes Referenced**:
- Indian Penal Code (IPC) 1860: Sections 319-338 (Hurt and Grievous Hurt)
- Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS) 2023: Sections 114-124
- Code of Criminal Procedure (CrPC) 1973: Section 155(2) (non-cognizable offenses require magisterial order for investigation)

---

## Version History

- **v1.0.0** (2025-12-06): Initial protocol - Comprehensive coverage of IPC hurt provisions, medical-legal principles

---

## Keywords

`assault` `hurt` `grievous hurt` `IPC 319` `IPC 323` `IPC 325` `IPC 326` `IPC 326A` `BNS 114` `BNS 115` `BNS 117` `fracture` `disfiguration` `acid attack` `simple hurt` `dangerous weapon` `medical evidence` `20-day rule` `criminal law`

## IL-CRIM-BAIL.md
---

# Bail - India Legal Protocol

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-CRIM-BAIL
- **Version**: 1.0.0
- **Effective Date**: January 2025
- **Last Updated**: January 2025
- **Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)
- **Domain**: Criminal Law
- **Subdomain**: Criminal Procedure
- **IPC Provisions**: N/A
- **BNS 2023 Provisions**: N/A
- **CrPC Provisions**: Sections 436-450
- **BNSS 2023 Provisions**: Sections 482-496
- **Related Protocols**: IL-CRIM-ARREST-CUSTODY, IL-CRIM-INVESTIGATION, IL-CRIM-TRIAL

---

## Overview and Purpose

### What is Bail?

**Bail** is the **temporary release of an accused person** from custody, pending investigation or trial, usually on the condition that a sum of money (bail bond) or surety is deposited to guarantee their appearance before the court.

### Etymology and Meaning

**Origin**: From Old French "bailler" meaning "to give" or "to deliver"

**Legal Meaning**: Release from custody with undertaking to appear before court as and when required

### Purpose of Bail

1. **Presumption of Innocence**: Accused is innocent until proven guilty
2. **Personal Liberty**: Article 21 - liberty is the rule, detention is exception
3. **Ensure Appearance**: Accused will appear for trial
4. **Prevent Escape**: Conditions can be imposed
5. **Public Interest**: Balance between individual liberty and societal safety
6. **Fair Trial**: Accused can prepare defense better when free

### Bail vs Bond

| Term | Meaning |
|------|---------|
| **Bail** | Release from custody |
| **Bond** | Written undertaking/guarantee (with or without surety) to pay specific amount if accused doesn't appear |
| **Surety** | Third party who guarantees accused's appearance; liable to pay if accused absconds |

---

## Legal Framework and Statutory Basis

### Constitutional Foundation

#### Article 21: Right to Life and Personal Liberty
- Liberty is fundamental right
- Bail is manifestation of personal liberty
- Deprivation of liberty must be in accordance with fair procedure

**"Bail is the Rule, Jail is the Exception"**

### Primary Legislation

#### Code of Criminal Procedure (CrPC) 1973
- **Chapter XXXIII**: Provisions as to Bail and Bonds (Sections 436-450)

#### Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita (BNSS) 2023
- **Chapter XXXIV**: Provisions as to Bail and Bonds (Sections 482-496)
- Effective from **July 1, 2024**
- Substantively similar to CrPC with some enhancements

### Supporting Legislation

1. **Special Statutes with Restrictive Bail Provisions**:
   - NDPS Act 1985 - Section 37 (twin conditions)
   - PMLA 2002 - Section 45 (very strict twin conditions)
   - UAPA 1967 - Section 43D (terrorism cases)
   - MCOCA 1999 - Section 21 (organized crime)
   - POCSO Act 2012 - Anticipatory bail generally not granted

---

## Types of Bail

### 1. Regular Bail (Section 437 CrPC / 483 BNSS)

**When**: After arrest and production before Magistrate

**Who Can Grant**:
- Magistrate (for offences triable by Magistrate or during investigation)
- Sessions Court (for sessions triable offences)
- High Court (inherent powers)

**Applicable To**: Both bailable and non-bailable offences

### 2. Anticipatory Bail (Section 438 CrPC / 482 BNSS)

**When**: **Before arrest** - when person has reason to believe they may be arrested

**Who Can Grant**:
- **Sessions Court** (within local jurisdiction)
- **High Court** (anywhere in state)

**Not Applicable**: Some statutes exclude anticipatory bail (e.g., POCSO in some circumstances)

### 3. Interim Bail

**When**: Temporary bail for short period

**Purpose**:
- To hear bail application (time to decide regular bail)
- For specific purpose (medical treatment, funeral, marriage, exams)
- Till final decision on regular/anticipatory bail

**Duration**: Limited period as specified by court

### 4. Default Bail (Statutory Bail) - Section 167(2) CrPC / 187(2) BNSS

**When**: Investigation not completed and charge sheet not filed within statutory period

**Conditions**:
- Accused arrested and in custody
- Charge sheet not filed within:
  - **60 days** (for offences punishable with <10 years)
  - **90 days** (for offences punishable with death, life, or ≥10 years)

**Nature**: **Indefeasible statutory right** (mandatory, not discretionary)

**See Protocol**: IL-CRIM-ARREST-CUSTODY.md (Section 167 analysis)

---

## Bailable vs Non-Bailable Offences

### Bailable Offence

**Definition** (Section 2(a) CrPC / 2(a) BNSS):
Offence shown as **bailable** in First Schedule OR made bailable by any other law

**Bail is a Right**:
- Accused has **right to be released on bail**
- Court **must grant** bail
- Only surety/bond amount to be decided

**Section 436 CrPC / 482 BNSS**: Right to bail in bailable offences

**Examples**:
- Simple hurt (IPC 323 / BNS 115)
- Assault (IPC 352 / BNS 74)
- Defamation (IPC 500 / BNS 356)
- Theft <Rs. 5,000 (some circumstances)

### Non-Bailable Offence

**Definition** (Section 2(a) CrPC / 2(a) BNSS):
Offence shown as **non-bailable** in First Schedule OR made non-bailable by law

**Bail is Discretionary**:
- Court **may** grant bail (not mandatory)
- Court considers various factors
- Prosecution can oppose

**Section 437 CrPC / 483 BNSS**: Discretionary bail for non-bailable offences

**Examples**:
- Murder (IPC 302 / BNS 103)
- Rape (IPC 376 / BNS 64)
- Robbery (IPC 392 / BNS 309)
- Corruption (PCA 1988 Section 7, 13)
- Drug trafficking (NDPS Act)

---

## Regular Bail (Section 437 CrPC / 483 BNSS)

### Who Can Apply?

Any person accused of non-bailable offence who is:
- Arrested and in custody, OR
- Anticipates arrest

### Who Can Grant?

**Magistrate or Sessions Judge**:
- Offence triable by that court
- During investigation: Magistrate
- After committal to Sessions: Sessions Judge

**High Court**: Inherent powers (Section 482 CrPC / 528 BNSS)

### Conditions for Granting Bail (Section 437(1))

**Magistrate or Sessions Judge may release on bail** if satisfied:

#### General Considerations:
1. **Nature and gravity** of offence
2. **Character** of accused (previous criminal record)
3. **Severity of punishment** on conviction
4. **Reasonable grounds** to believe accused not guilty
5. **Not likely to commit** offence while on bail

#### Specific Restrictions: Section 437(1) Proviso

**CANNOT grant bail if**:

##### (i) Reasonable Grounds to Believe Accused Guilty
- Of offence punishable with **death or life imprisonment**
- Unless:
  - Accused is **woman**, OR
  - Accused is **child** (below 16 years), OR
  - Accused is **sick or infirm**

##### (ii) Accused Previously Convicted
- Of offence punishable with **death, life, or ≥7 years**
- Unless court satisfied **special reasons** exist (to be recorded in writing)

##### (iii) Reasonable Apprehension
- Accused will **tamper with evidence**, OR
- **Influence witnesses**, OR
- **threaten witnesses**

### Bail Conditions (Section 440 CrPC / 486 BNSS)

Court can impose conditions:

1. **Personal Bond** (with or without sureties)
2. **Amount of Bond**: Determined by court (reasonable, not excessive)
3. **Residence**: Reside at particular place
4. **Reporting**: Report to police station periodically (e.g., weekly, fortnightly)
5. **Surrender Passport**: For offences involving flight risk
6. **Not Leave Country**: Without court permission
7. **Not Contact Witnesses**: Especially in sensitive cases
8. **Not Commit Similar Offence**
9. **Cooperate with Investigation**: Appear when called
10. **Cash Deposit**: In certain cases

**Important**: Conditions must be **reasonable** and **not onerous**

**Landmark Case**: **Moti Ram v. State of M.P. (1978)**
- Bail conditions must not be oppressive
- Should not nullify grant of bail
- Economic capacity of accused to be considered

---

## Anticipatory Bail (Section 438 CrPC / 482 BNSS)

### What is Anticipatory Bail?

**Pre-arrest bail**: Bail granted **before arrest** to person who has **reason to believe** they may be arrested for non-bailable offence

### Who Can Grant?

1. **Sessions Court** (within its jurisdiction)
2. **High Court** (throughout the state)

**NOT Magistrate** (no power to grant anticipatory bail)

### When Can Be Granted?

**Section 438(1)**: If person has **reason to believe** arrest may be made for:
- Non-bailable offence
- Accusation has been made OR
- Credible information exists

### Grounds/Considerations

Court considers:
1. **Nature and gravity** of accusation
2. **Antecedents** of applicant (character, past record)
3. **Possibility** of applicant fleeing
4. **Nature of evidence** (prima facie case)

**No Specific Criteria**: Unlike Section 437, Section 438 doesn't have rigid criteria; purely discretionary

### Landmark Judgments on Anticipatory Bail

#### 1. **Gurbaksh Singh Sibbia v. State of Punjab (1980)**

**Issue**: Constitutional validity of Section 438 CrPC

**Held** (Constitution Bench):
- Section 438 is **constitutionally valid**
- Anticipatory bail is in aid of **liberty** (Article 21)
- **No absolute rule** against anticipatory bail for serious offences
- Each case to be decided on facts
- Economic offences: anticipatory bail can be refused
- But blanket ban not permissible

**Key Observations**:
- Personal liberty is precious
- Arrest is humiliating; false accusations can ruin reputation
- Anticipatory bail prevents "arrest first, investigate later" approach

#### 2. **Sushila Aggarwal v. State (NCT of Delhi) (2020)**

**Issue**: Whether anticipatory bail can be cancelled when regular FIR filed

**Held** (3-Judge Bench):
- Once anticipatory bail granted, it **continues** even after arrest
- **No need for regular bail** application after arrest
- Anticipatory bail operates till trial concludes (unless cancelled)
- Protection continues despite change in circumstances

**Impact**: Clarified that anticipatory bail survives actual arrest

#### 3. **Arnab Goswami v. State of Maharashtra (2020)**

**Issue**: High Court denying anticipatory bail in abetment to suicide case

**Held**:
- **Liberty is sacrosanct**
- High Court should not mechanically reject anticipatory bail
- "Bail is rule, jail is exception" applies even to serious offences
- Personal liberty cannot be sacrificed at altar of majoritarian view

### Conditions for Anticipatory Bail (Section 438(2))

Court may impose conditions:
1. **Make himself available** for interrogation
2. **Not directly/indirectly** make any inducement, threat to witnesses
3. **Not leave India** without court permission
4. **Such other conditions** as court deems fit

**Section 438(3)**: Court can modify conditions or cancel bail

### Duration of Anticipatory Bail

**Earlier Ambiguity**: Whether interim or permanent

**Clarified in Sushila Aggarwal (2020)**:
- Anticipatory bail **continues till conclusion of trial**
- Unless specifically limited by court
- Survives actual arrest

### When Anticipatory Bail Not Granted?

**General Refusal Situations**:
1. **Serious economic offences** (Sibbia exception)
2. **Prima facie case** is very strong
3. **Possibility of tampering** with evidence
4. **Habitual offender**
5. **Likely to abscond**

**Statutory Exclusions**:
Some statutes **exclude anticipatory bail**:

| Statute | Provision | Anticipatory Bail |
|---------|-----------|-------------------|
| **POCSO Act 2012** | - | Generally not granted (rape/sexual assault of children) |
| **SC/ST (Prevention of Atrocities) Act 1989** | Section 18 | **Prohibited** (subject to safeguards added in 2018 amendment) |

**Note on SC/ST Act**: 2018 amendment allowed preliminary inquiry before arrest; reduces need for anticipatory bail but doesn't explicitly permit it

---

## Bail for Women, Children, and Special Categories

### Women

**Section 437(1)(i) Proviso**: Even if reasonable grounds to believe guilty of offence punishable with death/life, **woman can be granted bail**

**Rationale**: Societal vulnerability, pregnancy, children

**Not Absolute**: Court still has discretion; if woman is habitual offender or poses danger, bail can be refused

### Children (Juveniles)

**Juvenile Justice (Care and Protection of Children) Act 2015**:
- **Child in Conflict with Law** (below 18 years)
- Bail is the **norm**
- Incarceration is **exception**
- Grant bail unless **exceptional circumstances** (serious offence + likelihood of influencing witnesses)

**Principle**: Best interest of child paramount

### Sick or Infirm Persons

**Section 437(1)(i) Proviso**: Can be granted bail even in death/life imprisonment cases

**Medical Grounds**:
- Serious illness requiring hospitalization
- Terminal disease
- Old age with health issues
- Disability

**Landmark Case**: **Dataram Singh v. State of U.P. (2018)**
- Accused had terminal cancer
- Supreme Court granted bail on humanitarian grounds
- Even for serious offence (murder)

---

## Bail in Special Statutes (Restrictive Bail Provisions)

### 1. NDPS Act 1985 - Section 37

**Twin Conditions** for bail:

Court must be satisfied:
1. **Reasonable grounds** to believe accused **not guilty**, AND
2. Accused **not likely to commit** any offence while on bail

**Effect**: Very difficult to get bail; prosecution can easily oppose by showing prima facie case

**Landmark Case**: **Union of India v. Rattan Mallik (2009)**
- Supreme Court held Section 37 conditions are **strict**
- Mere probability or doubt is enough for prosecution
- Accused must show they are **probably not guilty**

**Recent**: **Toofan Singh v. State of Tamil Nadu (2021)**
- Reiterated stringent nature of Section 37
- Commercial quantity presumption under Section 37 upheld

### 2. PMLA 2002 - Section 45

**Strictest Bail Provisions**:

Twin conditions (**very stringent**):
1. **Public Prosecutor** must be given opportunity to oppose bail, AND
2. Court must be satisfied **reasonable grounds** exist to believe accused **not guilty**, AND
3. Accused **not likely to commit** offence while on bail

**Additional**: Court must record reasons in writing

**Landmark Case**: **Nikesh Tarachand Shah v. Union of India (2018)**
- Constitutional validity of Section 45 upheld
- Held: Stringent conditions justified given gravity of money laundering
- Special category of economic offences

**Effect**: Bail in PMLA cases is extremely rare; most accused remain in custody throughout trial

### 3. UAPA 1967 - Section 43D

**Terrorism Cases**:

Twin conditions:
1. Public Prosecutor to be heard
2. Court satisfied **reasonable grounds** to believe accusation is **prima facie true**, AND
3. Accused **not likely to commit** offence on bail

**Plus**: If offence entails death penalty, even more restrictive

**Landmark Case**: **Watali v. NIA (2020)**
- Supreme Court held Section 43D conditions must be applied strictly
- Liberty cannot override national security
- But procedural fairness must be ensured

### 4. MCOCA 1999 - Section 21

**Organized Crime**:

Similar twin conditions:
1. Public Prosecutor heard
2. Reasonable grounds to believe not guilty
3. Not likely to commit offence on bail

**Plus**: Court must record findings in writing

**Effect**: Difficult to obtain bail in organized crime cases

### 5. POCSO Act 2012

**No Specific Bail Provision**: CrPC/BNSS provisions apply

**But**: Judiciary generally **does not grant anticipatory bail** in child sexual abuse cases

**Reason**: Seriousness of offence, protection of child victim

**Regular Bail**: Can be granted under Section 437, but very rare

---

## Cancellation of Bail (Section 437(5) CrPC / 483(5) BNSS)

### When Can Bail Be Cancelled?

Court can **cancel bail** and direct accused to be arrested if:

1. **Absconding/Not Appearing**: Accused doesn't appear on specified dates
2. **Breach of Bail Conditions**: Violates any condition imposed
3. **Committing Further Offence**: While on bail, commits another offence
4. **Tampering with Evidence**: Despite conditions, tampers with evidence or threatens witnesses
5. **Misrepresentation**: Bail obtained through fraud, concealment, misrepresentation
6. **Change of Circumstances**: Material change in circumstances (e.g., fresh evidence showing stronger case)

### Procedure for Cancellation

**Application by**:
- Prosecution
- Investigating agency
- Complainant/victim
- Court suo motu

**Notice**: Accused must be given opportunity to be heard (principles of natural justice)

**Effect of Cancellation**:
- Bail bond stands cancelled
- Accused to be arrested
- Fresh bail application can be filed

**Landmark Case**: **Dolat Ram v. State of Haryana (1995)**
- Bail cancellation is discretionary power
- Should be exercised cautiously
- Accused should not be deprived of liberty lightly
- Grounds must be substantial

### Cancellation vs Rejection

- **Cancellation**: Bail already granted, now revoked
- **Rejection**: Bail application dismissed (bail never granted)

---

## Procedure for Bail Application

### Filing Bail Application

**Contents**:
1. **Details of accused**: Name, age, address
2. **Offence**: What offence accused of
3. **FIR details**: FIR number, police station, date
4. **Grounds**: Why bail should be granted
5. **Undertaking**: To abide by conditions
6. **Proposed sureties**: Details of sureties (if applicable)

**Where to File**:
- **Anticipatory bail**: Sessions Court or High Court
- **Regular bail**: Court where case is pending (Magistrate/Sessions/High Court)

### Hearing

**Notice to Prosecution**: Opportunity to oppose

**Arguments**:
- **Accused's side**: No criminal record, roots in society, will not abscond, weak case
- **Prosecution's side**: Serious offence, strong evidence, may tamper with evidence, flight risk

**Court's Decision**:
- Grant bail (with or without conditions)
- Reject bail
- Defer decision (call for more information)

**Timeline**: Should be decided expeditiously (bail applications are urgent)

### Execution of Bail Bond (Section 441 CrPC / 487 BNSS)

**After Bail Granted**:
1. **Bail bond** to be executed
2. **Surety** (if required) to execute bond
3. **Amount deposited** (if cash bail)
4. **Documents verified** (identity, address of sureties)

**Then**: Accused released from custody

**Verification of Sureties** (Section 441A CrPC / 487A BNSS):
- Police to verify antecedents and property of sureties
- Ensure they are solvent and reliable

---

## Factors Considered for Granting Bail

### Judicial Guidelines

Courts consider multiple factors (**not exhaustive**):

#### 1. Nature and Gravity of Offence
- **Serious offences** (murder, rape, terrorism): Less likely to get bail
- **Bailable offences**: Bail is right
- **Non-bailable but not serious** (e.g., cheating, theft): More likely

#### 2. Severity of Punishment
- **Death/Life imprisonment**: Strict scrutiny
- **Less than 7 years**: More liberal

#### 3. Character and Antecedents of Accused
- **Previous convictions**: Against bail
- **Habitual offender**: Strong ground for refusal
- **Clean record**: Favors bail

#### 4. Strength of Prosecution Case
- **Strong prima facie case**: May refuse bail
- **Weak case/false implication**: Favors bail

**Important**: At bail stage, detailed appreciation of evidence NOT required (State of Kerala v. Raneef, 2011)

#### 5. Possibility of Accused Fleeing
- **Roots in society**: Favors bail (family, property, business, job)
- **Flight risk**: Against bail (no local ties, history of absconding, passport issues)

#### 6. Possibility of Tampering with Evidence
- **May influence witnesses**: Against bail
- **May destroy evidence**: Refuse bail
- **Evidence already collected**: Favors bail

#### 7. Period of Detention Already Undergone
- **Long detention without trial**: Favors bail
- **Undertrials languishing**: Liberal approach

**Landmark Case**: **Hussainara Khatoon v. State of Bihar (1979)** (Undertrials Crisis)
- Supreme Court held prolonged detention without trial violates Article 21
- Bail should be granted to undertrials who have already served substantial period
- Especially if period exceeds possible sentence on conviction

#### 8. Likelihood of Trial Completion
- **Trial likely to take years**: Favors bail
- **Speedy trial expected**: Neutral

#### 9. Age, Health, Gender of Accused
- **Women**: Section 437 special provision
- **Children**: JJ Act liberal provisions
- **Sick/infirm**: Humanitarian grounds

#### 10. Economic Offences
- **Large-scale fraud, money laundering**: Stringent approach (Sibbia case exception)
- **Small economic disputes**: Liberal

---

## Bail Jurisprudence: Key Supreme Court Principles

### 1. "Bail is the Rule, Jail is the Exception"

**Gudikanti Narasimhulu v. Public Prosecutor (1978)**:
- Personal liberty is fundamental right
- Curtailment must be minimal
- Pre-trial detention should be exception

**Applied**: Universally in bail matters

### 2. Purpose of Bail

**Babu Singh v. State of U.P. (1978)**:
- Purpose of bail is to **secure appearance** of accused
- NOT to punish accused before conviction
- Pre-trial detention is not punishment

### 3. No Detailed Appreciation of Evidence

**State of Kerala v. Raneef (2011)**:
- At bail stage, court should **not minutely analyze** evidence
- Only **prima facie consideration**
- Detailed appreciation only at trial

### 4. Prolonged Detention

**Hussainara Khatoon v. State of Bihar (1979)**:
- Undertrials who have undergone substantial detention should be released
- Especially if detention period approaching likely sentence

### 5. Economic Offences Exception

**Gurbaksh Singh Sibbia v. State of Punjab (1980)**:
- Anticipatory bail can be refused in **serious economic offences**
- Magnitude of offence affects liberty considerations

### 6. Sushila Aggarwal Principle (2020)

- Anticipatory bail **survives actual arrest**
- Continues till trial conclusion unless cancelled

### 7. Arnab Goswami Principle (2020)

- Liberty is sacrosanct
- Courts should not deny bail mechanically
- Personal liberty cannot be sacrificed

---

## Default Bail (Section 167(2) CrPC / 187(2) BNSS)

### Statutory Provision

**If investigation not completed** and charge sheet not filed within:
- **60 days** (offences punishable with <10 years)
- **90 days** (offences punishable with death, life, or ≥10 years)

**Then**: Accused entitled to be released on **default bail**

### Nature of Right

**Indefeasible Statutory Right**:
- **Mandatory**, not discretionary
- Court **must** grant
- Cannot be refused

**Landmark Case**: **Sanjay Dutt v. State (1994)**
- Supreme Court held default bail is **absolute right**
- Once period expires, accused must be released
- Doesn't matter how serious the offence is

### Conditions

1. **Accused must be in custody** (arrested and detained)
2. **Charge sheet not filed** within 60/90 days
3. **Application must be made** (in some cases, courts release suo motu)

**Important**: 60/90 days counted from **date of arrest**, not from FIR date

### Effect of Filing Charge Sheet

**If charge sheet filed within 60/90 days**:
- No default bail
- Accused must apply for regular bail under Section 437

**If charge sheet filed on 61st/91st day**:
- Still entitled to default bail if application made before charge sheet filed
- Timing is crucial

### Computation of Period

**Exclude**:
- Period accused was not in custody (if released and re-arrested)
- Sundays/holidays if court closed (conflicting judgments; generally included)

**Include**:
- All days from arrest till filing of charge sheet

**Landmark Case**: **Rakesh Kumar Paul v. State of Assam (2017)**
- Computation should include all calendar days
- Not only working days

**See Protocol**: IL-CRIM-ARREST-CUSTODY.md for detailed analysis

---

## Bail Amount and Sureties

### Determination of Bail Amount (Section 440 CrPC / 486 BNSS)

**Factors**:
1. **Nature of offence**
2. **Economic capacity** of accused
3. **Ensuring appearance**

**Principle**: Bail amount should not be **excessive** or **oppressive**

**Landmark Case**: **Moti Ram v. State of M.P. (1978)**
- Bail amount must be **reasonable**
- Should not nullify grant of bail
- Should consider accused's financial status

**Example**:
- Daily wage laborer accused of theft: Rs. 5,000-10,000 bail
- Businessman accused of fraud: Rs. 50,000-5 lakh bail

### Sureties (Section 441 CrPC / 487 BNSS)

**What is Surety?**
- Third party who guarantees accused's appearance
- Executes bond promising to pay specified amount if accused absconds

**Requirements**:
- **Solvent**: Must have property/assets to cover bond amount
- **Reliable**: Good character
- **Verification**: Police to verify surety's credentials

**Number**: Usually 1-2 sureties required

**Local Surety**: Generally from local area (easier verification)

### Personal Bond

**Without Sureties**: Accused executes bond personally
- Promise to pay amount if doesn't appear
- Signed by accused

**With Sureties**: Accused + sureties execute bond
- Joint liability

**Preference**: Courts prefer sureties for non-bailable offences

---

## CrPC to BNSS Transition (Bail Provisions)

### Bail: CrPC 1973 vs BNSS 2023

| Aspect | CrPC 1973 | BNSS 2023 | Change |
|--------|-----------|-----------|---------|
| **Bailable offences** | Section 436 | Section 482 | Section number changed; **substance same** |
| **Regular bail** | Section 437 | Section 483 | Section number changed; **conditions same** |
| **Anticipatory bail** | Section 438 | Section 484 | Section number changed; **Sushila Aggarwal principle continues** |
| **Bail conditions** | Section 440 | Section 486 | Same |
| **Cancellation** | Section 437(5) | Section 483(5) | Same |
| **Default bail** | Section 167(2) | Section 187(2) | **60/90 day rule continues** |
| **Amount of bond** | Section 440 | Section 486 | Same (not excessive) |

### Key Observations

**No Material Change**: BNSS 2023 **largely retains** CrPC bail provisions
- Section numbers changed
- Substance remains same
- Judicial precedents continue to apply

**Sushila Aggarwal (2020) Continues**: Anticipatory bail survives arrest

**Default Bail**: 60/90 day rule continues unchanged

---

## Practical Compliance Checklist

### For Accused/Defense Lawyers

#### Anticipatory Bail Application
- [ ] File in **Sessions Court or High Court** (not Magistrate)
- [ ] Show **reason to believe** arrest may be made
- [ ] Provide **clean antecedents** (if applicable)
- [ ] Show **roots in society** (residence, family, job)
- [ ] Argue **prima facie weak case** or **false implication**
- [ ] Undertake to **cooperate with investigation**
- [ ] Offer to **join investigation** as and when required
- [ ] Propose **reasonable conditions**

#### Regular Bail Application
- [ ] File in court where case pending
- [ ] If Magistrate rejects, appeal to **Sessions Court**
- [ ] If Sessions rejects, revision to **High Court** (Section 482 / 528 BNSS)
- [ ] Show grounds:
  - [ ] Not likely to abscond
  - [ ] No tampering with evidence
  - [ ] Clean record
  - [ ] Weak prosecution case (prima facie)
  - [ ] Already undergone substantial detention
  - [ ] Medical/humanitarian grounds (if applicable)
- [ ] Arrange **sureties** (verified, solvent)
- [ ] Propose **reasonable bail amount**

#### Default Bail Application
- [ ] Check if **60/90 days expired**
- [ ] Calculate from **date of arrest**
- [ ] File application immediately
- [ ] Assert **indefeasible statutory right** (Sanjay Dutt principle)
- [ ] If court delays, file writ petition (habeas corpus)

#### After Bail Granted
- [ ] Execute **bail bond** promptly
- [ ] Sureties to execute bonds
- [ ] Verify surety documents (property papers, ID)
- [ ] Comply with **all bail conditions** strictly:
  - [ ] Appear on all dates
  - [ ] Report to police station (if condition imposed)
  - [ ] Do not contact witnesses
  - [ ] Do not leave country without permission
  - [ ] Surrender passport (if condition)
- [ ] Maintain low profile (avoid publicity that may prejudice case)
- [ ] Prepare defense effectively

### For Prosecution/Police

#### Opposing Bail Application
- [ ] File **reply/counter-affidavit**
- [ ] Highlight **gravity of offence**
- [ ] Show **strength of evidence** (prima facie case)
- [ ] Demonstrate **accused is flight risk**:
  - No local residence
  - History of absconding
  - Passport, foreign assets
- [ ] Show **tampering possibility**:
  - May influence witnesses
  - Evidence not yet collected
- [ ] Cite **previous criminal record** (if any)
- [ ] Argue **special statute restrictions** (NDPS Section 37, PMLA Section 45)
- [ ] Request **stringent conditions** if bail granted

#### If Bail Granted
- [ ] Ensure **sureties verified** properly
- [ ] Monitor **compliance with conditions**
- [ ] If violation, file **cancellation application**:
  - Non-appearance on dates
  - Tampering with evidence
  - Threatening witnesses
  - Committing fresh offence
- [ ] Expedite **investigation and trial** to minimize release period

### For Courts/Magistrates

#### Bail Hearing
- [ ] Hear both sides (accused and prosecution)
- [ ] Consider **relevant factors**:
  - Nature of offence
  - Severity of punishment
  - Strength of evidence (prima facie only)
  - Character of accused
  - Flight risk
  - Tampering possibility
- [ ] Apply **"bail is rule, jail is exception"** principle
- [ ] If serious offence (death/life), apply Section 437(1) proviso
- [ ] If special statute (NDPS, PMLA, UAPA), apply twin conditions
- [ ] Record **reasons** in writing (especially if refusing bail)

#### Imposing Bail Conditions
- [ ] Conditions must be **reasonable**, not oppressive
- [ ] Consider **economic capacity** of accused (Moti Ram principle)
- [ ] Tailor conditions to case:
  - Reporting to police station
  - Surrender passport
  - Not leave country
  - Not contact witnesses
  - Reside at specified address
- [ ] Amount of bond: **not excessive** (Section 440 / 486)

#### Default Bail
- [ ] If 60/90 days expired and no charge sheet filed:
  - [ ] **Must grant** default bail (mandatory)
  - [ ] Indefeasible statutory right (cannot refuse)
- [ ] Calculate period from **date of arrest**
- [ ] If application made, grant immediately

---

## Common Bail Mistakes and Pitfalls

### For Accused

1. **Not filing anticipatory bail in time**: Delay leads to arrest first
2. **Absconding after bail**: Leads to cancellation and arrest warrant
3. **Violating bail conditions**: Non-compliance causes cancellation
4. **Not appearing on dates**: Bail cancelled, arrested
5. **Contacting witnesses**: Breach of condition
6. **Providing false sureties**: Fraud, bail cancelled

### For Prosecution

1. **Not opposing bail properly**: Weak opposition leads to grant
2. **Not monitoring compliance**: Accused violates conditions without consequence
3. **Not filing cancellation promptly**: Delay weakens case

### For Courts

1. **Excessive bail amount**: Violates Moti Ram principle
2. **Mechanical rejection**: Not applying "bail is rule, jail is exception"
3. **Detailed evidence appreciation**: Should not do mini-trial at bail stage
4. **Refusing default bail**: Violates statutory right (Section 167(2))

---

## Related Protocols and Cross-References

### Directly Related
- **IL-CRIM-ARREST-CUSTODY**: Arrest, remand, and default bail (Section 167)
- **IL-CRIM-INVESTIGATION**: Investigation process leading to need for bail
- **IL-CRIM-TRIAL**: Trial procedure; bail status during trial

### Rights Related
- **IL-CONSTITUTIONAL-RIGHTS**: Article 21 (liberty), Article 22 (arrest safeguards)

### Special Statute Related
- **IL-CRIM-MONEY-LAUNDERING**: PMLA Section 45 (very strict bail)
- **IL-CRIM-CORRUPTION**: PCA 1988 bail provisions
- **IL-CONSUMER-CYBER-CRIME**: IT Act offences bail

---

## Conclusion

Bail is a **delicate balance** between:
- **Individual liberty** (Article 21 - fundamental right)
- **Societal interest** (ensuring accused's appearance, preventing further crimes)

**Core Principles**:
1. **"Bail is the Rule, Jail is the Exception"** (Gudikanti Narasimhulu)
2. **Presumption of Innocence**: Accused innocent till proven guilty
3. **Pre-trial detention is not punishment** (Babu Singh)
4. **Personal liberty is precious** (Arnab Goswami)
5. **No detailed evidence appreciation** at bail stage (Raneef)
6. **Economic capacity must be considered** (Moti Ram)
7. **Default bail is indefeasible right** (Sanjay Dutt)
8. **Anticipatory bail survives arrest** (Sushila Aggarwal)

**Special Statutes** like NDPS, PMLA, UAPA have **stringent bail conditions** (twin conditions), making bail very difficult - justified by gravity of offences (drugs, money laundering, terrorism).

The transition from CrPC to BNSS **retains bail provisions** with minimal changes - section numbers updated, but substance and judicial precedents continue.

**Ultimate Goal**: Ensure accused's presence at trial while respecting fundamental right to liberty until conviction.

---

## Document Control

**Version History**:
- v1.0.0 (January 2025): Initial protocol creation

**Review Schedule**: Annual review; immediate update for landmark judgments or statutory amendments

**Feedback**: Submit to protocol administrator

**Disclaimer**: Informational purposes only. Consult qualified legal professionals for specific cases.

---

**End of Protocol: IL-CRIM-BAIL**


---
## IL-CRIM-BURDEN-PROOF.md
---

# Burden of Proof and Presumptions - India Legal Protocol

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-CRIM-BURDEN-PROOF
- **Version**: 1.0.0
- **Effective Date**: January 2025
- **Last Updated**: January 2025
- **Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)
- **Domain**: Criminal Law
- **Subdomain**: Evidence and Defenses
- **IPC Provisions**: Various sections with reverse burden (e.g., 304B, 306, 498A)
- **BNS 2023 Provisions**: Corresponding BNS sections
- **Evidence Act Provisions**: Sections 101-114A (Burden of Proof, Presumptions)
- **BSA 2023 Provisions**: Sections 101-114A (Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam)
- **Related Protocols**: IL-CRIM-TRIAL, IL-CRIM-EVIDENCE, IL-CRIM-GENERAL-EXCEPTIONS

---

## Overview and Purpose

### What is Burden of Proof?

**Burden of Proof** is the **obligation to prove or disprove** a disputed fact or issue in a legal proceeding. In criminal law, it determines **who must prove what** to establish guilt or innocence.

### Purpose

1. **Allocate Responsibility**: Clarify which party bears duty to prove facts
2. **Protect Innocence**: Prevent wrongful conviction by requiring prosecution to prove guilt
3. **Ensure Fairness**: Balance between state's resources and accused's rights
4. **Guide Adjudication**: Help court determine when case is proved or not

### Two Aspects of Burden

#### 1. Burden of Proof (Legal Burden)
**Who must prove** the fact to win the case

**In Criminal Law**: Generally on **prosecution** to prove guilt beyond reasonable doubt

#### 2. Burden of Producing Evidence (Evidential Burden)
**Who must produce evidence** on a particular issue

**May Shift**: During trial, burden of producing evidence may shift, but burden of proof generally does not

---

## Legal Framework and Statutory Basis

### Primary Legislation

#### Indian Evidence Act 1872
- **Chapter VII**: Of the Burden of Proof (Sections 101-114A)
- Defines burden of proof, burden of producing evidence, presumptions

#### Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam (BSA) 2023
- **Chapter VII**: Of the Burden of Proof (Sections 101-114A)
- Effective from **July 1, 2024**
- **Substantively identical** to Evidence Act 1872
- Section numbers same; language modernized

### Constitutional Foundation

#### Article 20(3): Right Against Self-Incrimination
- No person can be compelled to be witness against himself
- Burden cannot be placed on accused to incriminate self

#### Article 21: Right to Fair Trial
- Presumption of innocence is part of fair trial
- Burden on prosecution to prove guilt

---

## Fundamental Principle: Burden on Prosecution

### Section 101 Evidence Act / BSA

**"Whoever desires any Court to give judgment as to any legal right or liability dependent on the existence of facts which he asserts, must prove that those facts exist."**

**In Criminal Cases**:
- **Prosecution asserts** accused committed offence
- **Prosecution must prove** those facts

### Golden Thread of Criminal Jurisprudence

**Woolmington v. DPP (1935)** (English case adopted in India):

> "Throughout the web of the English Criminal Law one golden thread is always to be seen—that it is the duty of the prosecution to prove the prisoner's guilt..."

**Principle**: **Burden on prosecution to prove guilt beyond reasonable doubt; burden NEVER shifts to accused** (except specific statutory exceptions)

**Indian Adoption**: This principle is **foundation of Indian criminal law**

**Landmark Case**: **State of West Bengal v. Mir Mohammad Omar (2000)**
- Supreme Court held: Burden is on prosecution to prove guilt beyond reasonable doubt
- Accused need not prove innocence
- If prosecution fails, accused entitled to acquittal

---

## Standard of Proof in Criminal Cases

### "Beyond Reasonable Doubt"

**Criminal Standard**: Prosecution must prove guilt **beyond reasonable doubt**

**NOT**:
- Beyond all doubt (impossible standard)
- Balance of probabilities (civil standard)

**Reasonable Doubt**:
- Doubt for which **reason can be assigned**
- Not fanciful, imaginary, or hypothetical doubt
- Based on evidence or lack thereof

**Landmark Case**: **Sharad Birdhichand Sarda v. State of Maharashtra (1984)**
- Proof beyond reasonable doubt means:
  - Moral certainty (not mathematical certainty)
  - Degree of proof which satisfies conscience of court
  - Evidence so cogent that court can safely act upon it

### Benefit of Doubt

**If Reasonable Doubt Exists**: Accused entitled to **benefit of doubt** and must be **acquitted**

**Landmark Case**: **Kali Ram v. State of H.P. (1973)**
- Benefit of doubt is not to be given lightly
- But if prosecution fails to prove guilt beyond reasonable doubt, acquittal mandatory

---

## Sections 101-114A Evidence Act / BSA: Detailed Analysis

### Section 101: Burden of Proof

**"Whoever desires any Court to give judgment as to any legal right or liability dependent on the existence of facts which he asserts, must prove that those facts exist."**

**Application**:
- **Prosecution asserts** accused guilty
- **Prosecution proves** commission of offence

**Burden Never Shifts**: In criminal cases, burden of proving guilt never shifts to accused (except statutory exceptions)

### Section 102: On Whom Burden of Proof Lies

**"The burden of proof in a suit or proceeding lies on that person who would fail if no evidence at all were given on either side."**

**Application in Criminal Cases**:
- If no evidence presented by either side, prosecution would **fail**
- Therefore, **burden on prosecution**

**Illustration**: If accused charged with theft, and no evidence presented:
- Prosecution fails (cannot prove theft)
- Accused acquitted (presumption of innocence)

### Section 103: Burden of Proof as to Particular Fact

**"The burden of proof as to any particular fact lies on that person who wishes the Court to believe in its existence, unless it is provided by any law that the proof of that fact shall lie on any particular person."**

**Application**:
- **General Rule**: Party asserting fact must prove it
- **Exception**: Unless statute provides otherwise (reverse burden)

**Criminal Law Example**:
- Prosecution asserts accused committed murder (fact)
- Prosecution must prove: (1) death occurred, (2) accused caused it, (3) with intention/knowledge
- If accused claims alibi (fact that he was elsewhere), accused must produce evidence (evidential burden)

### Section 104: Burden of Proving Fact to Be Proved to Make Evidence Admissible

**Preliminary facts** for admissibility must be proved by party relying on evidence

**Example**: If prosecution wants to use confession:
- Must prove confession was voluntary (not coerced)
- Burden on prosecution

### Section 105: Burden of Proving Exception

**"When a person is accused of any offence, the burden of proving the existence of circumstances bringing the case within any of the General Exceptions in the Indian Penal Code, or within any special exception or proviso contained in any other part of the same Code, or in any law defining the offence, is upon him, and the Court shall presume the absence of such circumstances."**

**Key Provision**: **Exception to "burden on prosecution" rule**

**Application**:
- If accused claims benefit of **general exception** (e.g., self-defense, insanity, mistake of fact)
- **Burden on accused** to prove existence of exception

**Standard for Accused**: **Preponderance of probabilities** (not beyond reasonable doubt)
- Accused need not prove beyond reasonable doubt
- Only needs to show more probable than not

**Landmark Case**: **Dahyabhai Chhaganbhai Thakker v. State of Gujarat (1964)**
- Supreme Court held Section 105 is exception to general rule
- Burden on accused to prove general exceptions
- But standard is preponderance of probabilities, not beyond reasonable doubt

**Examples of General Exceptions** (IPC Sections 76-106 / BNS Sections 6-26):
- Section 79 IPC / BNS 6: Act done by person justified by law
- Section 81 IPC / BNS 8: Act likely to cause harm done without criminal intent for lawful object
- Section 82-83 IPC / BNS 10-11: Act of child below 7 years / above 7 below 12 years
- Section 84 IPC / BNS 13: Act of person of unsound mind
- Section 96-106 IPC / BNS 14-24: Private defense of body and property

**See Protocol**: IL-CRIM-GENERAL-EXCEPTIONS.md for detailed analysis

### Section 106: Burden of Proving Fact Especially Within Knowledge

**"When any fact is especially within the knowledge of any person, the burden of proving that fact is upon him."**

**Rationale**: If fact is within accused's exclusive knowledge, accused must explain

**Landmark Case**: **State of West Bengal v. Mir Mohammad Omar (2000)**
- Section 106 does not shift burden of proof
- Only shifts **burden of producing evidence** (evidential burden)
- Prosecution still bears burden of proving guilt beyond reasonable doubt

**Application**:
- Used when accused has exclusive or special knowledge of fact
- Accused must offer explanation

**Example**: **Sharad Birdhichand Sarda v. State of Maharashtra (1984)**
- Wife went missing after being last seen with husband
- Husband did not explain what happened
- Section 106 applied: Husband had special knowledge of wife's whereabouts
- Failure to explain is circumstance against him

**Limitation**: Section 106 is **rebuttable presumption**, not conclusive
- Accused's failure to explain is one circumstance
- Does not automatically prove guilt
- Prosecution must still prove guilt beyond reasonable doubt

### Sections 107-114: Specific Presumptions

#### Section 107: Burden of Proving Death Within 30 Years
- Burden on person asserting death occurred within 30 years
- If neither party proves, presumption that person is **alive**

#### Section 108: Burden of Proving Death of Person Known to Be Alive Within 30 Years
- If person was alive within 30 years and not heard of for 7 years by those who would naturally hear
- **Presumption**: Person is dead

#### Section 109: Burden of Proof as to Relationship
- Burden on person asserting relationship exists

#### Section 110: Burden of Proof as to Ownership
- Burden on person asserting ownership

#### Section 111: Proof of Good Faith in Transactions Where One Party is in Relation of Active Confidence
- If transaction between parties in fiduciary relationship
- Burden on beneficiary to prove good faith

#### Section 111A: Presumption as to Electronic Records
- In any proceedings involving secure electronic record:
  - Presumed that electronic signature is affixed by subscriber with intent
  - Secure electronic record has not been altered
  - **Added by IT Act 2000**

#### Section 112: Birth During Marriage - Legitimacy
- Child born during valid marriage is **presumed legitimate**
- Burden on person denying legitimacy

#### Section 113: Proof of Cession of Territory
- Burden on person asserting cession

#### Section 113A: Presumption as to Abetment of Suicide by Married Woman
**Specific to Dowry Cases**:
- If woman commits suicide within 7 years of marriage
- If husband/relatives subjected her to cruelty
- **Presumption**: Husband/relatives abetted suicide

**Applicability**: IPC Section 306 / BNS Section 104 (Abetment of Suicide)

**Standard**: Rebuttable presumption
- Prosecution must prove: (1) suicide, (2) within 7 years of marriage, (3) cruelty
- If proved, presumption arises
- Accused can rebut

**Landmark Case**: **Ude Singh v. State of Haryana (2019)**
- Section 113A is rebuttable presumption
- Prosecution must first prove ingredients
- Then presumption aids prosecution

#### Section 113B: Presumption as to Dowry Death
**Dowry Death Cases**:
- If woman dies within 7 years of marriage (otherwise than under normal circumstances)
- If shown she was subjected to cruelty or harassment for dowry before death
- **Presumption**: Husband/relatives caused dowry death

**Applicability**: IPC Section 304B / BNS Section 80 (Dowry Death)

**Effect**: **Reverse burden** - accused must prove innocence

**Landmark Case**: **Kaliyaperumal v. State of Tamil Nadu (2004)**
- Section 113B creates strong presumption
- Once prosecution proves: (1) death within 7 years, (2) cruelty/harassment for dowry
- Presumption arises; accused must rebut

**Critical Distinction**:
- **Section 113A** (Abetment of Suicide): Rebuttable presumption (aids prosecution)
- **Section 113B** (Dowry Death): Stronger presumption (near-reverse burden)

#### Section 114: Court May Presume Existence of Certain Facts

**Discretionary Presumptions**: Court **may presume** (not must):

**Illustrations** (from Section 114):

##### (a) Judicial and Official Acts Regular
- Court may presume judicial and official acts have been regularly performed
- Example: Magistrate's order presumed to be within jurisdiction

##### (b) Common Course of Business
- Court may presume common course of business has been followed
- Example: Cheque sent by post presumed to be received

##### (c) Evidence Withheld
- If party could produce evidence and deliberately withholds it:
- **Presumption**: Evidence, if produced, would be unfavorable to that party

**Landmark Case**: **Ramesh Chandra Agarwal v. Regency Hospital Ltd. (2009)**
- Adverse inference can be drawn against party who withholds evidence
- But discretionary; court considers facts

##### (d) Accomplice is Unworthy of Credit
- Evidence of accomplice should be treated with caution
- But can be basis of conviction if corroborated

**Landmark Case**: **State of Punjab v. Balwinder Singh (2012)**
- Accomplice evidence must be corroborated
- Conviction on sole testimony of accomplice risky (but legally permissible)

##### (e) Altered Documents
- If document appears to be altered:
- Presumption: Alteration made after execution

##### (f) Tampering
- If document appears tampered:
- Presumption: Tampering done for improper purpose

##### (g) Property Stolen
- If person in possession of recently stolen property:
- **Presumption**: Person either stole it or received it knowing it was stolen

**Illustration**: Accused found with stolen goods within short time after theft
- Section 114(a) presumption: Accused either thief or receiver

**Landmark Case**: **Rama Shankar v. State of U.P. (2015)**
- Possession of recently stolen property gives rise to presumption under Section 114
- Accused must explain possession
- If fails to explain satisfactorily, can be convicted

---

## Reverse Burden: Statutory Exceptions

### When Burden Shifts to Accused

**General Rule**: Burden on prosecution

**Exception**: Specific statutes create **reverse burden** (accused must prove innocence or exception)

### Examples of Reverse Burden Provisions

#### 1. Dowry Death (IPC Section 304B / BNS Section 80 + Evidence Act Section 113B)
- Prosecution proves: Death within 7 years of marriage + Cruelty/harassment for dowry
- **Presumption**: Accused caused dowry death
- **Burden shifts**: Accused must prove innocence

#### 2. Abetment of Suicide (IPC Section 306 / BNS Section 104 + Evidence Act Section 113A)
- Prosecution proves: Suicide within 7 years + Cruelty
- **Presumption**: Accused abetted suicide
- Accused must rebut

#### 3. Prevention of Corruption Act 1988
**Section 20 PCA** (now Section 24 after 2018 amendment):
- If accused accepted gratification (bribe) other than legal remuneration:
- **Presumption**: Gratification was for corrupt motive
- **Burden on accused** to prove it was not corrupt

**Landmark Case**: **Neeraj Dutta v. State (NCT of Delhi) (2023)**
- Supreme Court held Section 20 PCA (pre-2018) created mandatory presumption
- Once prosecution proved acceptance of gratification, presumption arose
- Accused must rebut

#### 4. Prevention of Money Laundering Act (PMLA) 2002
**Section 24 PMLA** (Pre-2024 Amendment):
- **Reverse burden**: Accused must prove property not proceeds of crime

**Controversial**: Constitutional validity challenged

**Vijay Madanlal Choudhary v. Union of India (2022)**:
- Supreme Court **upheld** constitutional validity of Section 24 PMLA
- Held reverse burden justified given nature of money laundering

**2024 Amendment**: Section 24 PMLA **struck down** by Supreme Court in subsequent decision
- Reverse burden now removed in PMLA cases
- **Update**: Check latest position post-2024

#### 5. Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic Substances (NDPS) Act 1985
**Section 35 NDPS**:
- Presumption of **culpable mental state** (mens rea)
- If accused found in possession of drugs:
- **Presumption**: Accused possessed knowingly and intentionally
- Burden on accused to prove lack of knowledge/intent

**Section 54 NDPS** (Reverse Burden in Bail):
- While not direct reverse burden of guilt, bail provisions (Section 37) create **twin conditions**
- Accused must show grounds to believe not guilty (quasi-reverse burden)

#### 6. Information Technology Act 2000
**Section 79 IT Act**:
- If electronic signature verified:
- **Presumption**: Document was signed by person whose signature it purports to be

#### 7. Arms Act 1959
**Section 25 Arms Act**:
- If accused found in possession of arms/ammunition:
- **Presumption**: Possession with intent to use for purposes prejudicial to public peace
- Burden on accused to prove lawful purpose

---

## Presumptions: Rebuttable vs Irrebuttable

### Rebuttable Presumptions (Most Presumptions)

**Can be Rebutted**: Accused can produce evidence to disprove presumption

**Examples**:
- Section 105 (General Exceptions): Accused can prove exception
- Section 106 (Special Knowledge): Accused can explain
- Section 113A (Abetment of Suicide): Accused can rebut
- Section 113B (Dowry Death): Accused can prove innocence
- Section 114 (Discretionary Presumptions): Can be rebutted

**Standard for Rebuttal**: **Preponderance of probabilities** (not beyond reasonable doubt)

### Irrebuttable Presumptions (Conclusive)

**Cannot be Rebutted**: Once proved, conclusive

**Examples** (in Evidence Act):
- **Section 41**: Judgments in rem (conclusive as to status)
- **Section 112**: Legitimacy of child (very difficult to rebut; DNA evidence may rebut)

**Criminal Law**: Very few irrebuttable presumptions (fairness requires rebuttal opportunity)

---

## Burden of Proving General Exceptions (Section 105)

### General Exceptions in IPC (Sections 76-106) / BNS (Sections 6-26)

**See Protocol**: IL-CRIM-GENERAL-EXCEPTIONS.md for detailed analysis

**Burden**: Section 105 places burden on accused to prove general exception

**Standard**: **Preponderance of probabilities** (not beyond reasonable doubt)

### Examples

#### 1. Self-Defense (IPC Sections 96-106 / BNS Sections 14-24)
- If accused claims killed in self-defense:
- **Burden on accused** to prove: (1) imminent threat, (2) no safe retreat, (3) force proportionate
- Standard: Preponderance of probabilities

**Landmark Case**: **Darshan Singh v. State of Punjab (2010)**
- Accused claiming self-defense must prove it
- But standard is preponderance of probabilities
- If accused shows more likely than not, entitled to acquittal

#### 2. Insanity (IPC Section 84 / BNS Section 13)
- If accused claims was insane at time of offence:
- **Burden on accused** to prove insanity
- Standard: Preponderance of probabilities

**Landmark Case**: **Surendra Mishra v. State of Jharkhand (2011)**
- Burden on accused to prove unsound mind at time of offence
- Medical evidence helpful but not mandatory
- Conduct before/after offence relevant

#### 3. Mistake of Fact (IPC Section 76, 79 / BNS Section 6, 9)
- If accused claims acted under bona fide mistake of fact:
- Burden on accused to prove mistake
- Must show belief was reasonable

#### 4. Intoxication (IPC Section 85-86 / BNS Section 18-19)
- If accused claims was intoxicated (involuntary):
- Burden on accused to prove involuntary intoxication
- Voluntary intoxication generally not defense (except for specific intent crimes)

---

## Practical Application: Burden in Different Stages of Trial

### 1. Framing of Charges / Discharge

**Standard**: **Prima facie case**

**Burden**: On prosecution to show prima facie case

**Landmark Case**: **State of Bihar v. Ramesh Singh (1977)**
- At discharge stage, court does not require proof beyond reasonable doubt
- Only prima facie case
- If two views possible, one leading to conviction and other to discharge, benefit to accused

### 2. Trial - Prosecution Evidence

**Burden**: On prosecution to prove guilt beyond reasonable doubt

**Procedure**:
- Prosecution examines witnesses
- Prosecution produces documentary/physical evidence
- Establishes corpus delicti (body of crime)
- Connects accused to crime

**If Prosecution Fails**: Acquittal at this stage (no defense needed)

**Landmark Case**: **State of W.B. v. Orilal Jaiswal (1994)**
- If prosecution evidence so weak that no case made out:
- Accused can be acquitted without calling defense
- Section 232 CrPC / 256 BNSS

### 3. Section 313 Statement

**Not Evidence**: Accused's statement under Section 313 not evidence

**Purpose**: Opportunity to explain incriminating circumstances

**Burden**: No burden on accused to explain (right to silence)
- But failure to explain can be circumstance against accused (not determinative)

### 4. Defense Evidence

**No Burden**: Accused has **no burden to prove innocence**

**But**: If accused claims exception (e.g., self-defense), burden on accused to prove exception (Section 105)

**Procedure**:
- Accused may remain silent (Article 20(3))
- OR produce defense evidence:
  - Alibi witnesses
  - Character witnesses
  - Documentary evidence
- OR testify himself (subject to cross-examination)

### 5. Final Arguments

**Prosecution**: Summarizes evidence, argues guilt proved beyond reasonable doubt

**Defense**: Challenges prosecution evidence, highlights reasonable doubt, argues exceptions/defenses

### 6. Judgment

**Court Considers**:
- Has prosecution proved guilt beyond reasonable doubt?
- Has accused proved any exception (if claimed)?

**If Reasonable Doubt**: Acquittal (benefit of doubt to accused)

---

## Landmark Supreme Court Cases on Burden of Proof

### 1. Woolmington v. DPP (1935) (English; Adopted in India)
- **Golden thread of criminal law**: Burden on prosecution to prove guilt
- Burden never shifts (except specific statutory exceptions)

### 2. State of West Bengal v. Mir Mohammad Omar (2000)
- Burden on prosecution to prove guilt beyond reasonable doubt
- Accused need not prove innocence
- Section 106 does not shift burden of proof, only evidential burden

### 3. Dahyabhai Chhaganbhai Thakker v. State of Gujarat (1964)
- Section 105: Burden on accused to prove general exceptions
- But standard is preponderance of probabilities, not beyond reasonable doubt

### 4. Sharad Birdhichand Sarda v. State of Maharashtra (1984)
- Proof beyond reasonable doubt means moral certainty
- Circumstantial evidence must exclude all hypotheses except guilt
- Section 106 (special knowledge) applied to husband's failure to explain wife's disappearance

### 5. Kaliyaperumal v. State of Tamil Nadu (2004)
- Section 113B (dowry death) creates strong presumption
- Once prosecution proves death within 7 years + cruelty, presumption arises
- Accused must rebut

### 6. State of Punjab v. Balwinder Singh (2012)
- Accomplice evidence must be corroborated (Section 114 Illustration (d))
- Conviction on sole testimony risky but legally permissible

### 7. Rama Shankar v. State of U.P. (2015)
- Section 114 Illustration (a): Possession of recently stolen property
- Presumption accused either stole or received knowing it stolen
- Accused must explain; failure to explain satisfactorily can lead to conviction

### 8. Neeraj Dutta v. State (NCT of Delhi) (2023)
- Section 20 PCA (pre-2018): Mandatory presumption of corrupt motive if gratification accepted
- Accused must rebut

### 9. Vijay Madanlal Choudhary v. Union of India (2022)
- Section 24 PMLA (reverse burden): Upheld constitutional validity
- Reverse burden justified given nature of money laundering
- **Note**: Subsequent developments may have altered this (check 2024 updates)

---

## Burden vs Standard: Key Distinctions

### Burden of Proof

**Who must prove**: Prosecution (generally)

**Exceptions**: Accused must prove general exceptions (Section 105), rebut presumptions

### Standard of Proof

| Party | Standard | Explanation |
|-------|----------|-------------|
| **Prosecution** (proving guilt) | **Beyond reasonable doubt** | Moral certainty; high standard |
| **Accused** (proving exception/rebuttal) | **Preponderance of probabilities** | More likely than not; lower standard |
