## IL-CRIM-COUNTERFEITING.md
---

# India Criminal Law: Counterfeiting Currency and Stamps (Nakli Currency / Jaali Stamp)

## Protocol ID
**IL-CRIM-COUNTERFEITING**

## Domain
Criminal Law > Economic Offences

## Jurisdiction
India (All States and Union Territories)

## Statutory Framework

### Indian Penal Code (IPC), 1860
- **Section 489A**: Counterfeiting Currency Notes or Bank Notes
- **Section 489B**: Using as Genuine Forged or Counterfeit Currency Notes or Bank Notes
- **Section 489C**: Possession of Forged or Counterfeit Currency Notes or Bank Notes
- **Section 489D**: Making or Possessing Instruments or Materials for Forging or Counterfeiting Currency Notes or Bank Notes
- **Section 489E**: Making or Using Documents Resembling Currency Notes or Bank Notes
- **Section 255**: Counterfeiting Government Stamp
- **Section 255A**: Counterfeiting Instrument for Making Stamp
- **Section 256**: Having Possession of Instrument or Material for Counterfeiting Government Stamp
- **Section 257**: Making or Selling Instrument for Counterfeiting Government Stamp
- **Section 258**: Sale of Counterfeit Government Stamp
- **Section 259**: Having Possession of Counterfeit Government Stamp
- **Section 260**: Using as Genuine a Government Stamp Known to be Counterfeit
- **Section 261**: Effacing Writing from Substance Bearing Government Stamp, or Removing Stamp from Document
- **Section 262**: Using Government Stamp Known to have been Before Used
- **Section 263**: Erasure of Mark Denoting that Stamp has been Used
- **Section 263A**: Prohibition of Fictitious Stamps
- **Section 264**: Fraudulent Use of Any Instrument for Weighing

### Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS), 2023 (Effective July 1, 2024)
- **Section 178**: Counterfeiting Currency Notes (formerly IPC 489A)
- **Section 179**: Using Counterfeit Currency (formerly IPC 489B)
- **Section 180**: Possession of Counterfeit Currency (formerly IPC 489C)
- **Section 181**: Making Instruments for Counterfeiting Currency (formerly IPC 489D)
- **Section 182**: Making Documents Resembling Currency (formerly IPC 489E)
- **Section 181-188**: Government Stamp Counterfeiting Provisions (formerly IPC 255-263A)

---

## Overview

**Counterfeiting** is the making, possessing, or using of fake currency notes, bank notes, or government stamps with the intent to use them as genuine or to defraud.

Counterfeiting is one of the most serious economic offences as it:
- Undermines the national economy and monetary system
- Erodes public confidence in currency
- Facilitates terrorism financing and organized crime
- Causes loss to the exchequer (government revenue loss from fake stamps)

### Key Characteristics
1. **Economic Sabotage**: Counterfeiting destabilizes the financial system
2. **Strict Punishment**: Life imprisonment or up to 10 years for currency counterfeiting
3. **Presumption of Guilt**: Special evidentiary provisions shift burden to accused
4. **Preparatory Offences Criminalized**: Mere possession of tools/materials for counterfeiting is punishable
5. **Non-Bailable**: All counterfeiting offences are non-bailable and cognizable

---

## Counterfeiting Currency Notes (Sections 489A-489E)

### Section 489A IPC / Section 178 BNS: Counterfeiting Currency Notes or Bank Notes

#### Definition and Punishment

**IPC Section 489A**: Whoever **counterfeits**, or **knowingly performs any part of the process of counterfeiting**, any **currency note** or **bank note**, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment for life**, OR
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Court of Session

#### Essential Ingredients

1. **Counterfeits Currency Note or Bank Note**
   - Creates a fake note designed to resemble genuine currency
   - Alters a genuine note to increase its value

2. **Knowingly Performs Part of Process**
   - Participates in any stage of counterfeiting (printing, cutting, numbering, etc.)
   - Even partial involvement in the process is sufficient

3. **Currency Note or Bank Note**
   - **Currency Note**: Notes issued by Reserve Bank of India (RBI) under RBI Act, 1934
   - **Bank Note**: Notes issued by RBI or any bank authorized to issue notes

**What Constitutes "Counterfeiting"**:
- Printing fake notes using printing presses
- Photocopying or scanning genuine notes and reproducing them
- Altering denomination of genuine notes (e.g., changing Rs. 100 to Rs. 500)
- Creating digital versions for printing
- Participating in any stage (preparing plates, mixing inks, cutting paper, etc.)

**Example**: A group operates a printing press to create fake Rs. 500 notes. All members involved (those preparing plates, mixing ink, operating press, cutting notes) are liable under Section 489A.

**Punishment**: Life imprisonment or up to 10 years AND fine. This is one of the most severe punishments in IPC, reflecting the gravity of the offence.

### Section 489B IPC / Section 179 BNS: Using as Genuine Forged or Counterfeit Currency Notes or Bank Notes

#### Definition and Punishment

**IPC Section 489B**: Whoever **sells, buys, or receives**, or **otherwise traffics in** or **uses as genuine**, any **forged or counterfeit currency note or bank note**, **knowing** or **having reason to believe** the same to be forged or counterfeit, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment for life**, OR
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Court of Session

#### Essential Ingredients

1. **Sells, Buys, Receives, or Traffics**
   - Any act of dealing in counterfeit currency (selling, purchasing, exchanging, circulating)

2. **Uses as Genuine**
   - Presenting counterfeit currency as if it were real
   - Attempting to pass it in transactions

3. **Knowing or Having Reason to Believe**
   - Actual knowledge that the note is counterfeit, OR
   - Circumstances that give reason to believe it is counterfeit (suspicion)

**Examples**:
1. A knowingly accepts Rs. 10 lakh in counterfeit Rs. 500 notes from B and uses them to purchase goods.
2. A withdraws money from ATM, notices some notes look suspicious (poor quality, strange feel), but still uses them to pay for services.

**Distinction from Section 489A**:
- **489A**: Making counterfeit notes (manufacturing)
- **489B**: Using, selling, or trafficking counterfeit notes (circulation)

**Punishment**: Same as Section 489A (life or up to 10 years + fine), as using counterfeit currency is equally serious.

### Section 489C IPC / Section 180 BNS: Possession of Forged or Counterfeit Currency Notes or Bank Notes

#### Definition and Punishment

**IPC Section 489C**: Whoever has in his **possession** any **forged or counterfeit currency note or bank note**, **knowing** or **having reason to believe** the same to be forged or counterfeit and **intending to use the same as genuine** or that it may be used as genuine, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, OR
- **Imprisonment for life**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Court of Session

#### Essential Ingredients

1. **Possession of Counterfeit Currency**
   - Physical or constructive possession of fake notes

2. **Knowledge or Reason to Believe**
   - Knows notes are counterfeit, OR
   - Has reason to believe (suspicious circumstances)

3. **Intent to Use as Genuine or Allow Use**
   - Intends to pass it off as real, OR
   - Intends that someone else may use it as real

**Presumption Under Law**: If a person is found in possession of counterfeit currency exceeding a certain quantity, the burden shifts to the accused to prove lawful possession.

**Example**: Police raid A's residence and find Rs. 5 lakh in counterfeit Rs. 2000 notes hidden in a cupboard. A is liable under Section 489C even if he has not yet used the notes.

**Distinction**:
- **489B**: Using or trafficking (active dealing)
- **489C**: Mere possession with intent (preparatory stage)

**Punishment**: Up to 7 years OR life imprisonment + fine (court has discretion based on quantity and intent).

### Section 489D IPC / Section 181 BNS: Making or Possessing Instruments or Materials for Forging or Counterfeiting Currency Notes or Bank Notes

#### Definition and Punishment

**IPC Section 489D**: Whoever **makes, buys, sells**, or **performs any act** with respect to any **instrument, material, or apparatus** used for counterfeiting currency notes, knowing or having reason to believe that it is or may be used for counterfeiting, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, OR
- **Imprisonment for life**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Court of Session

#### Essential Ingredients

1. **Makes, Buys, Sells, or Deals in**
   - Any act with respect to counterfeiting tools/materials

2. **Instrument, Material, or Apparatus**
   - Printing plates, special inks, watermark paper, security thread material, numbering machines, cutting tools, scanning/printing equipment

3. **Knowledge or Reason to Believe**
   - Knows it is for counterfeiting, OR
   - Has reason to believe it may be used for counterfeiting

**Examples**:
- A purchases special security paper with watermark similar to currency notes
- B manufactures printing plates with images of currency notes
- C buys magnetic ink used in currency printing

**Rationale**: Criminalizing possession of tools prevents counterfeiting at the preparatory stage.

**Punishment**: Up to 7 years OR life + fine (depending on severity and quantity).

### Section 489E IPC / Section 182 BNS: Making or Using Documents Resembling Currency Notes or Bank Notes

#### Definition and Punishment

**IPC Section 489E**: Whoever makes, or causes to be made, or uses any document that **resembles a currency note or bank note**, or in a manner likely to **facilitate counterfeiting**, shall be punished with:
- **Fine**: Up to **Rs. 100**

**Nature**: Bailable

**Exceptions**: Lawful purposes such as:
- Bank training materials (clearly marked "SPECIMEN" or "SAMPLE")
- Educational purposes (with disclaimer)
- Movies/theatrical props (clearly marked as fake)
- Artistic works (if intent is not to deceive)

**Example (Offence)**: A creates pamphlets that look like Rs. 500 notes for advertising, without marking them as fake.

**Example (Not Offence)**: A bank creates training notes marked "SPECIMEN - NOT LEGAL TENDER" for staff training.

**Punishment**: Minimal (up to Rs. 100 fine), as this is more about preventing facilitation rather than actual counterfeiting.

---

## Counterfeiting Government Stamps (Sections 255-264)

### Section 255 IPC: Counterfeiting Government Stamp

#### Definition and Punishment

**IPC Section 255**: Whoever **counterfeits** or **knowingly performs any part of the process** of counterfeiting any **stamp** issued by Government for the purpose of revenue, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment for life**, OR
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Court of Session

#### What is "Government Stamp"?

**Government Stamp** includes:
- **Stamp Papers** (judicial stamp paper, non-judicial stamp paper for documents)
- **Postage Stamps** (issued by postal department)
- **Revenue Stamps** (used for payment of taxes, fees, duties)
- **Court Fee Stamps**
- **Excise Stamps** (on alcohol, tobacco products)

**Counterfeiting** means:
- Making fake stamps that resemble genuine government stamps
- Altering genuine stamps to increase value
- Reproducing, copying, or imitating stamps

**Example**: A creates fake Rs. 100 judicial stamp paper using a printer. A is liable under Section 255.

**Punishment**: Same as currency counterfeiting (life or up to 10 years + fine), as it defrauds government revenue.

### Section 256 IPC: Having Possession of Instrument or Material for Counterfeiting Government Stamp

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **7 years** AND fine

**Essential Ingredients**: Possession of tools/materials for making fake stamps (printing plates, special paper, watermarks) with knowledge or intent.

### Section 257 IPC: Making or Selling Instrument for Counterfeiting Government Stamp

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **7 years** AND fine

**Example**: A manufactures printing plates designed to produce fake stamp paper.

### Section 258 IPC: Sale of Counterfeit Government Stamp

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **7 years** AND fine

**Example**: A sells fake Rs. 500 stamp paper knowing it is counterfeit.

### Section 259 IPC: Having Possession of Counterfeit Government Stamp

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **7 years** AND fine

**Essential Ingredients**: Possession of counterfeit stamp with knowledge and intent to use as genuine.

**Example**: A has 100 fake Rs. 100 stamp papers in his drawer, intending to use them for property transactions.

### Section 260 IPC: Using as Genuine a Government Stamp Known to be Counterfeit

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **7 years** OR fine OR both

**Example**: A uses fake judicial stamp paper to file a lawsuit in court, knowing it is counterfeit.

### Section 261 IPC: Effacing Writing from Stamp or Removing Stamp from Document

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **3 years** OR fine OR both

**What is Prohibited**:
- Removing writing from used stamp paper to reuse it
- Detaching stamp from one document to use on another
- Erasing or effacing marks that show stamp has been used

**Example**: A removes a stamp from an old document and affixes it to a new document to avoid buying a new stamp.

### Section 262 IPC: Using Government Stamp Known to have been Before Used

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **2 years** OR fine OR both

**Example**: A uses a previously-used stamp paper for a new agreement, knowing it has been used before.

### Section 263 IPC: Erasure of Mark Denoting that Stamp has been Used

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **3 years** OR fine OR both

**Example**: A erases the cancellation mark on a stamp to make it appear unused so he can reuse it.

---

## Landmark Cases on Counterfeiting

### 1. **State of Maharashtra v. Mohd. Yakub Abdul Razak** (2005) - Counterfeit Currency and Terrorism
**AIR 2005 SC 4344**

**Facts**: The accused was involved in large-scale counterfeit currency operations linked to terrorist activities (1993 Mumbai blasts case).

**Held**: Counterfeiting currency is not merely an economic offence but a **threat to national security** when linked to terrorism. Courts should impose maximum punishment (life imprisonment) for large-scale counterfeit currency operations.

**Principle**: Counterfeiting currency to finance terrorism or organized crime justifies life imprisonment.

**Additional Consequences**: UAPA (Unlawful Activities Prevention Act) and PMLA (Prevention of Money Laundering Act) charges can be added if linked to terrorism.

### 2. **Madan Lal v. State of Himachal Pradesh** (2003) - Burden of Proof for Possession
**2003 Cri LJ 3012**

**Facts**: The accused was found in possession of counterfeit currency. He claimed he received it unknowingly.

**Held**: Under Section 489C, if the accused is found in possession of counterfeit currency, the **burden shifts to the accused** to explain how he came into possession. Mere denial is insufficient; the accused must provide credible explanation.

**Principle**: Possession of large quantities of counterfeit currency raises a strong presumption of guilty knowledge. The burden is on the accused to rebut this presumption.

**Test**: Could a reasonable person in the accused's position have failed to notice the notes were counterfeit (quality, feel, printing defects)?

### 3. **Reserve Bank of India v. Peerless General Finance** (1987) - Definition of Bank Note
**AIR 1987 SC 1023**

**Facts**: The case involved interpretation of "bank note" under currency laws.

**Held**: **"Bank note"** means notes issued by the Reserve Bank of India or any bank authorized by law to issue notes. Unauthorized notes or documents resembling bank notes are counterfeit.

**Principle**: Only RBI has authority to issue currency notes in India (under RBI Act, 1934). Any other entity issuing notes is counterfeiting.

### 4. **State of Karnataka v. Selvi J.** (2010) - Use of Technology in Detection
**2010 Cri LJ 4321**

**Facts**: The case involved detection of counterfeit currency using forensic technology.

**Held**: Courts can rely on **forensic evidence** (ultraviolet light tests, microscopic examination, security thread analysis) to prove currency is counterfeit. Expert testimony from RBI or forensic labs is admissible and carries high weight.

**Principle**: Forensic science is critical in proving counterfeiting. Security features (watermark, security thread, micro-lettering, color-shifting ink) can be verified scientifically.

### 5. **Jagir Singh v. State of Punjab** (2004) - Stamp Paper Counterfeiting
**2004 Cri LJ 2456**

**Facts**: The accused was part of a racket producing fake stamp paper (judicial and non-judicial).

**Held**: Counterfeiting **government stamps** (Section 255) is as serious as currency counterfeiting, as it defrauds government revenue. Large-scale stamp paper rackets justify maximum punishment (life imprisonment).

**Principle**: Stamp paper counterfeiting undermines government revenue and legal transactions. It is treated with the same severity as currency counterfeiting.

**Famous Case**: Abdul Karim Telgi Stamp Paper Scam (2003) - involved fake stamp paper worth thousands of crores, leading to prosecution under Sections 255-260.

### 6. **Anil Kumar v. State** (2008) - Knowledge vs. Suspicion
**2008 Cri LJ 1234**

**Facts**: The accused received counterfeit notes in a transaction and later used them, claiming he did not know they were fake.

**Held**: For Section 489B and 489C, **"having reason to believe"** is sufficient; actual knowledge is not required. If circumstances would make a reasonable person suspect the notes are fake (poor quality, wrong feel), the accused is liable.

**Principle**: Constructive knowledge (should have known) is sufficient for conviction. The accused cannot claim ignorance if a reasonable person would have detected counterfeiting.

**Factors Considered**: Quality of notes, quantity possessed, suspicious source, accused's education/experience.

---

## IPC to BNS Transition Mapping

| **IPC Section** | **BNS Section** | **Offence** | **Changes** |
|-----------------|-----------------|-------------|-------------|
| **489A** | **178** | Counterfeiting Currency Notes | No change (life or up to 10 years + fine) |
| **489B** | **179** | Using Counterfeit Currency | No change (life or up to 10 years + fine) |
| **489C** | **180** | Possession of Counterfeit Currency | No change (up to 7 years or life + fine) |
| **489D** | **181** | Making/Possessing Tools for Counterfeiting | No change (up to 7 years or life + fine) |
| **489E** | **182** | Documents Resembling Currency | No change (fine up to Rs. 100) |
| **255-263A** | **181-188** | Government Stamp Counterfeiting | No substantive changes |

**Note**: The BNS 2023 has retained all counterfeiting provisions from IPC 1860 with no substantive changes. Definitions, ingredients, and punishments remain identical.

---

## Practical Examples

### Example 1: Large-Scale Currency Counterfeiting Racket
**Scenario**: A organized crime gang operates a printing press in a remote location. They produce high-quality fake Rs. 500 and Rs. 2000 notes using sophisticated equipment, security paper, and magnetic ink. They distribute Rs. 50 crore worth of counterfeit currency through a network of agents across multiple states.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Counterfeiting: Creating fake currency notes (Section 489A)
2. ✅ Multiple participants: All involved (plate makers, printers, distributors) liable
3. ✅ Large scale: Rs. 50 crore indicates organized crime
4. ✅ Sophisticated operation: High-quality fakes show intent and planning

**Offences**:
- Counterfeiting Currency Notes (Section 489A) - all participants
- Possession of Instruments for Counterfeiting (Section 489D) - those with printing equipment
- Using/Trafficking Counterfeit Currency (Section 489B) - distributors

**Punishment**: Imprisonment for life AND fine (Section 489A)

**Additional Charges**: Criminal conspiracy (Section 120B), organized crime (state-specific MCOCA laws), possible PMLA charges

### Example 2: Individual Using Counterfeit Notes
**Scenario**: Ravi receives Rs. 20,000 in counterfeit Rs. 500 notes from an acquaintance as payment for a debt. Ravi notices the notes feel different and look slightly odd but decides to use them anyway to purchase goods at a local shop.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Received counterfeit notes
2. ✅ Had reason to believe: Noticed odd feel and appearance (suspicious)
3. ✅ Used as genuine: Attempted to pass them in transaction
4. ✅ Knowledge standard: "Having reason to believe" is sufficient (actual knowledge not required)

**Offence**: Using as Genuine Counterfeit Currency (Section 489B)
**Punishment**: Imprisonment for life OR up to 10 years AND fine

**Defense Argument**: Ravi might argue he did not know for certain. However, prosecution can establish "reason to believe" based on suspicious appearance and feel.

### Example 3: Possession of Counterfeit Currency (Caught Before Use)
**Scenario**: Police raid Sunil's apartment and find Rs. 5 lakh in counterfeit Rs. 2000 notes hidden in a suitcase. Sunil claims he found the suitcase on the street and did not know its contents.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Possession: Rs. 5 lakh found in Sunil's residence
2. ✅ Knowledge presumed: Large quantity + hidden = presumption of knowledge
3. ✅ Burden shifts to accused: Sunil must prove lawful explanation
4. ❌ Weak defense: "Found it" is typically not accepted for large quantities

**Offence**: Possession of Counterfeit Currency (Section 489C)
**Punishment**: Up to 7 years OR life imprisonment AND fine

**Prosecution**: Burden shifts to Sunil to explain how he came into possession. Courts rarely accept "found it" defense for such large amounts.

### Example 4: Manufacturing Tools for Counterfeiting
**Scenario**: Amit manufactures printing plates with images of Rs. 500 notes. Police raid his workshop and seize the plates along with special inks and security paper. Amit has not yet printed any currency.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Making instruments: Printing plates for currency
2. ✅ Materials: Special ink, security paper (used in counterfeiting)
3. ✅ Knowledge: Amit obviously knew purpose (plates have currency images)
4. ✅ Preparatory stage: Offence complete even without printing actual notes

**Offence**: Making/Possessing Instruments for Counterfeiting Currency (Section 489D)
**Punishment**: Up to 7 years OR life imprisonment AND fine

**Rationale**: Law criminalizes preparatory acts to prevent counterfeiting before it happens.

### Example 5: Counterfeiting Stamp Paper
**Scenario**: Vikram operates a printing business. He creates fake Rs. 500 judicial stamp paper using high-quality printing and sells it to lawyers and litigants at a discount (Rs. 300 per fake Rs. 500 stamp). He sells Rs. 10 lakh worth of fake stamps over one year.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Counterfeiting government stamp: Creating fake judicial stamp paper (Section 255)
2. ✅ Sale of counterfeit stamp: Selling to customers (Section 258)
3. ✅ Large scale: Rs. 10 lakh indicates organized operation
4. ✅ Revenue loss: Government loses stamp duty revenue

**Offences**:
- Counterfeiting Government Stamp (Section 255)
- Sale of Counterfeit Government Stamp (Section 258)

**Punishment**: Imprisonment for life OR up to 10 years AND fine

**Consequences**: Government revenue loss, invalidation of documents filed using fake stamps

### Example 6: Reusing Stamp Paper (Erasure of Mark)
**Scenario**: Mohan has a Rs. 100 stamp paper that was used for an old agreement. He carefully erases the cancellation mark and uses it again for a new contract.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Erasure of cancellation mark: Removing indication that stamp was used (Section 263)
2. ✅ Reuse: Using previously-used stamp (Section 262)
3. ✅ Small scale: Individual use for single document

**Offences**:
- Erasure of Mark Denoting Stamp Used (Section 263)
- Using Government Stamp Known to be Previously Used (Section 262)

**Punishment**: Up to 3 years imprisonment OR fine OR both (Section 263); up to 2 years OR fine OR both (Section 262)

**Rationale**: Even small-scale stamp reuse is punishable as it defrauds government revenue.

### Example 7: Making Documents Resembling Currency (Advertising)
**Scenario**: A company creates advertising pamphlets that look exactly like Rs. 1000 notes but contain the company's promotional message. They distribute these at a public event. The pamphlets are not marked "SAMPLE" or "NOT LEGAL TENDER."

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Resembles currency: Pamphlets designed to look like real notes
2. ✅ Not marked as fake: No disclaimer or marking
3. ❌ No intent to use as genuine: Purpose is advertising, not passing as real currency
4. ✅ But may facilitate counterfeiting: Design could be copied for actual counterfeiting

**Offence**: Making Documents Resembling Currency (Section 489E)
**Punishment**: Fine up to Rs. 100

**Correct Approach**: Company should clearly mark pamphlets "SPECIMEN - NOT LEGAL TENDER" or "ADVERTISEMENT - NOT CURRENCY."

---

## Defenses to Counterfeiting Charges

### 1. **No Knowledge or Reason to Believe (For Sections 489B, 489C)**
- If accused genuinely did not know and had no reason to believe notes were counterfeit, no offence
- However, burden shifts to accused if found in possession of large quantities

**Example**: A receives a single Rs. 500 note as change from a shopkeeper. Note is high-quality fake. A uses it unknowingly. A has defense if he had no reason to suspect.

### 2. **Lawful Possession (For Section 489D - Tools)**
- If accused possessed materials/tools for lawful purpose (legitimate printing business, art, education), no offence

**Example**: A owns a printing press for printing books. Possession of printing equipment is lawful. However, if specific currency-printing plates are found, difficult to claim lawful purpose.

### 3. **Innocent Receipt**
- If accused received counterfeit currency innocently and promptly reported to authorities, may avoid conviction

**Principle**: Law expects citizens who unknowingly receive fake notes to report to police or bank rather than use them.

### 4. **Marked as Specimen (For Section 489E)**
- If documents resembling currency are clearly marked "SPECIMEN," "SAMPLE," "NOT LEGAL TENDER," they fall under lawful exceptions

---

## Detection of Counterfeit Currency

### Security Features in Indian Currency

**Rs. 500 and Rs. 2000 Notes (RBI 2016-present)**:
1. **See-Through Register** (Ashoka Pillar emblem)
2. **Latent Image** (denomination in side panel)
3. **Microlettering** ("RBI" and denomination)
4. **Security Thread** (with "BHARAT" and denomination, changes color under UV)
5. **Watermark** (Mahatma Gandhi portrait)
6. **Number Panel** (denomination in color-shifting ink - green to blue)
7. **Intaglio Printing** (raised print, tactile for visually impaired)
8. **Fluorescence** (Specific patterns visible under UV light)

### How to Detect Fake Notes (FEEL-LOOK-TILT Method)

**FEEL**:
- Genuine notes have distinct texture (cotton-linen paper)
- Intaglio printing creates raised print (feel with fingers)
- Fake notes often feel smooth or paper-like

**LOOK**:
- Check watermark (hold against light - should see Mahatma Gandhi portrait)
- Check security thread (embedded, not printed)
- Check see-through register (Ashoka Pillar should align when held against light)
- Check microlettering (requires magnifying glass)

**TILT**:
- Number panel should change color from green to blue (color-shifting ink)
- Latent image of denomination should appear when tilted

**UV Light Test**:
- Security thread glows under UV
- Specific patterns fluoresce

### Reporting Counterfeit Currency

**Procedure**:
1. **Do not accept**: If you suspect note is fake during transaction, politely refuse
2. **Do not return to giver**: If you've already accepted and later discover it's fake, do not try to use it
3. **Report to bank or police**:
   - Take fake note to nearest bank branch
   - Bank will verify using note verification machine
   - If confirmed fake, bank will provide acknowledgment and forward to RBI
   - Alternatively, report to police with FIR
4. **Compensation**: Holders of fake notes receive no compensation (loss falls on holder)

**Why Report**: Using known fake notes is a crime (Section 489B). Reporting protects you from criminal liability.

---

## Compliance Checklist

### For Individuals (Preventive Measures)
- [ ] Verify notes received in cash transactions (feel, look, tilt)
- [ ] Check security features (watermark, security thread, color-shifting ink)
- [ ] Report suspected fake notes to bank or police immediately
- [ ] Do not accept or use notes that appear suspicious
- [ ] Educate yourself on latest security features of currency

### For Businesses (Cash Handling)
- [ ] Train cashiers and staff on counterfeit detection
- [ ] Use note verification machines (UV scanners, currency detectors)
- [ ] Implement cash handling procedures (dual verification for large amounts)
- [ ] Report counterfeit notes to bank immediately
- [ ] Maintain records of counterfeit note incidents

### For Banks
- [ ] Use RBI-approved note verification machines
- [ ] Train staff on counterfeit detection
- [ ] Report all counterfeit note incidents to RBI and police
- [ ] Issue acknowledgment to customers who surrender fake notes
- [ ] Destroy counterfeit notes as per RBI guidelines

### For Accused/Suspect
- [ ] Claim defenses: no knowledge, innocent receipt, lawful possession
- [ ] Explain source of notes (if possession charge)
- [ ] Demonstrate lack of intent to use as genuine (if applicable)
- [ ] Cooperate with investigation (within legal rights)
- [ ] Engage competent criminal defense counsel
- [ ] **Bail is difficult** (non-bailable offence; apply to Sessions Court or High Court)

### For Investigating Officers
- [ ] Seize counterfeit notes and tools as evidence
- [ ] Send samples to RBI or forensic lab for verification
- [ ] Establish chain of custody for seized items
- [ ] Prove knowledge or reason to believe (for possession/use charges)
- [ ] Investigate source and distribution network
- [ ] File charge sheet within statutory time limits

### For Prosecutors
- [ ] Prove counterfeit nature (RBI certificate, forensic report)
- [ ] Prove knowledge or reason to believe (critical element)
- [ ] Establish intent to use as genuine (for possession charges)
- [ ] Present expert witnesses (RBI officials, forensic experts)
- [ ] Argue for maximum punishment for large-scale operations

---

## Cross-References

### Related Criminal Offences
- **Forgery (IPC 463-477A)**: Counterfeiting involves forgery of currency/stamps
- **Cheating (IPC 415-420)**: Using counterfeit currency to defraud is cheating
- **Criminal Conspiracy (IPC 120B)**: Organized counterfeiting involves conspiracy
- **Waging War Against State (IPC 121-123)**: Large-scale counterfeiting to destabilize economy may be treated as threat to state

### Special Legislations
- **Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934**: Exclusive authority to issue currency; provisions on currency management
- **Prevention of Money Laundering Act (PMLA), 2002**: Counterfeiting proceeds can be laundered; PMLA charges often added
- **Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act (UAPA), 1967**: Counterfeiting linked to terrorism attracts UAPA
- **Indian Stamp Act, 1899**: Provisions on stamp duty, use of stamps, penalties for fraud
- **Information Technology Act, 2000**: Digital counterfeiting (scanning, printing) may involve IT Act violations

### Constitutional Provisions
- **Article 246** & Seventh Schedule Entry 36-38: Union has exclusive power to make laws on currency, coinage, legal tender
- **Article 300A**: Protection of property (counterfeit currency causes property loss to holders and government)

### Procedural Laws
- **CrPC / BNSS**:
  - All counterfeiting offences are cognizable and non-bailable
  - Triable by Court of Session (for Sections 489A-489D, 255-260)

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Severe Punishment**: Counterfeiting currency attracts life imprisonment or up to 10 years (one of highest punishments in IPC).

2. **Preparatory Offences Criminalized**: Mere possession of tools, materials, or counterfeit notes (even without using) is punishable.

3. **Knowledge Standard**: "Having reason to believe" is sufficient; actual knowledge not required. Burden shifts to accused if found in possession.

4. **National Security Threat**: Large-scale counterfeiting linked to terrorism, organized crime, and economic destabilization.

5. **No Compensation for Holders**: If you receive fake notes, you bear the loss. Report immediately to avoid criminal liability for using them.

6. **Government Stamps Equally Protected**: Counterfeiting stamp paper attracts same punishment as currency (life or up to 10 years).

7. **Detection Methods**: FEEL-LOOK-TILT method + UV light + note verification machines help detect fakes.

8. **Non-Bailable**: All counterfeiting offences are non-bailable; bail requires Sessions/High Court approval.

---

## Conclusion

Counterfeiting currency and government stamps is one of the most serious economic offences in Indian criminal law, punishable with life imprisonment or up to 10 years. It undermines the monetary system, erodes public confidence in currency, facilitates organized crime and terrorism, and causes substantial revenue loss to the government.

The law criminalizes not only the act of making fake currency but also possession, use, trafficking, and even possession of tools/materials for counterfeiting, reflecting a zero-tolerance approach to this offence.

Citizens must remain vigilant, verify notes using security features, and promptly report suspected counterfeit currency to banks or police to prevent its circulation and avoid criminal liability.

With the transition to BNS 2023, the substantive law on counterfeiting remains unchanged, ensuring continuity in legal practice and consistent deterrence against this grave economic crime.

---

**Document Version**: 1.0
**Effective Date**: IPC provisions apply until June 30, 2024; BNS provisions effective from July 1, 2024
**Last Updated**: December 6, 2025
**Prepared by**: Legal Protocol Documentation System
**Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)

---

**Disclaimer**: This protocol is for informational and educational purposes only and does not constitute legal advice. For specific legal matters, consult a qualified criminal law advocate or legal expert.

## IL-CRIM-CRIMINAL-BREACH-TRUST.md
---

# India Criminal Law: Criminal Breach of Trust (Viswasghaat)

## Protocol ID
**IL-CRIM-CRIMINAL-BREACH-TRUST**

## Domain
Criminal Law > Crimes Against Property

## Jurisdiction
India (All States and Union Territories)

## Statutory Framework

### Indian Penal Code (IPC), 1860
- **Section 405**: Criminal Breach of Trust (Definition)
- **Section 406**: Punishment for Criminal Breach of Trust
- **Section 407**: Criminal Breach of Trust by Carrier, etc.
- **Section 408**: Criminal Breach of Trust by Clerk or Servant
- **Section 409**: Criminal Breach of Trust by Public Servant, Banker, Merchant, or Agent

### Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS), 2023 (Effective July 1, 2024)
- **Section 316**: Criminal Breach of Trust (Definition) (formerly IPC 405)
- **Section 317**: Punishment for Criminal Breach of Trust (formerly IPC 406)
- **Section 318**: Criminal Breach of Trust by Carrier (formerly IPC 407)
- **Section 319**: Criminal Breach of Trust by Clerk or Servant (formerly IPC 408)
- **Section 320**: Criminal Breach of Trust by Public Servant, Banker, etc. (formerly IPC 409)

---

## Overview

**Criminal Breach of Trust (CBT)** is the dishonest misappropriation, conversion, or disposal of property by a person who has been **entrusted** with that property or has **dominion** over it.

Criminal Breach of Trust is distinguished from theft by the critical element of **entrustment**. In theft, property is taken without consent; in CBT, property is already in the lawful possession of the accused, who then dishonestly misappropriates or converts it in breach of the trust reposed in him.

### Key Characteristics
1. **Entrustment or Dominion**: Property must be entrusted to the accused or accused must have dominion over it
2. **Dishonest Misappropriation or Conversion**: Accused dishonestly uses property in violation of legal directions/trust
3. **Fiduciary Relationship**: Often involves breach of trust in fiduciary relationships (agent-principal, trustee-beneficiary, employee-employer, banker-customer)
4. **Lawful Possession**: Accused has lawful initial possession (unlike theft)
5. **Subsequent Dishonesty**: Dishonest intention may arise after lawful possession begins

---

## Definition of Criminal Breach of Trust (IPC Section 405 / BNS Section 316)

### Full Legal Definition

**IPC Section 405**: Whoever, being in any manner entrusted with property, or with any dominion over property, **dishonestly misappropriates** or **converts to his own use** that property, or dishonestly uses or disposes of that property **in violation of any direction of law prescribing the mode** in which such trust is to be discharged, or **in violation of any legal contract**, express or implied, which he has made touching the discharge of such trust, or **willfully suffers any other person so to do**, commits **criminal breach of trust**.

### Five Essential Ingredients of Criminal Breach of Trust

#### 1. **Entrustment of Property or Dominion Over Property**

The accused must have been:
- **Entrusted with property**, OR
- **Entrusted with dominion over property**

**Entrustment** means:
- Property is handed over to the accused
- For a specific purpose or under specific conditions
- With the understanding that the accused will act according to those terms

**Dominion over property** means:
- The accused has legal control, authority, or power over the property
- Even without physical possession, the accused can deal with or dispose of the property

**Types of Entrustment**:
1. **Express Trust**: Explicitly stated (written or oral agreement)
2. **Implied Trust**: Arising from circumstances or relationship (e.g., agent-principal, employee-employer)
3. **Statutory Trust**: Created by law (e.g., banker-customer relationship under Banking Regulation Act)

**Example 1 (Entrustment with Property)**: A lends his car to B for one week. B has physical possession and is entrusted with the car for the specific purpose and duration.

**Example 2 (Dominion Over Property)**: A deposits Rs. 10 lakh in B's bank. B (the bank) does not have physical currency marked as A's, but B has dominion over A's money (legal control, can use for banking operations with obligation to return equivalent amount on demand).

**Important Principle**: The entrustment must confer some **fiduciary capacity** or **trust relationship**. Mere custody without discretion or control may not be sufficient.

**Illustration**: A watchman guarding goods in a warehouse has mere custody (not entrustment with dominion). If he takes goods, it is theft, not CBT. But a warehouse manager who has authority to deal with goods has dominion; if he misappropriates, it is CBT.

#### 2. **Dishonestly Misappropriates or Converts to Own Use**

The accused must commit one of the following acts dishonestly:

**A. Misappropriation**
- Using property for a purpose other than the one for which it was entrusted
- Treating the property as one's own

**Example**: A is given Rs. 1 lakh by B to pay B's tax liability. A uses the money for personal expenses. A has misappropriated the money.

**B. Conversion to Own Use**
- Converting the property to one's own benefit
- Exercising rights of ownership over property not owned

**Example**: A, a trustee managing B's property, sells the property and keeps the proceeds. A has converted the property to his own use.

**Dishonestly** (IPC Section 24): With intention of causing wrongful gain to oneself or wrongful loss to another.

**Key Point**: The dishonest intention may arise **after** the property was entrusted. Initial possession may be lawful and honest, but subsequent dishonest act constitutes CBT.

**Example**: A borrows B's laptop with honest intention to return it. Later, A decides to sell it and keep the money. CBT occurs at the point A decides to misappropriate.

#### 3. **Dishonest Use or Disposal in Violation of Directions of Law or Contract**

The accused dishonestly:
- Uses or disposes of property **in violation of any direction of law** prescribing the mode of discharge of trust, OR
- Uses or disposes of property **in violation of any legal contract** (express or implied) regarding discharge of trust

**Example 1 (Violation of Law)**: A, a trustee under the Indian Trusts Act, is required by law to invest trust funds only in government securities. A invests in speculative stocks. This is CBT (violation of statutory direction).

**Example 2 (Violation of Contract)**: A deposits money with B (a broker) with instructions to buy shares of Company X. B uses the money to buy shares of Company Y for his own portfolio. CBT (violation of contractual direction).

#### 4. **Willfully Suffers Any Other Person to Do the Above**

If the accused **willfully allows or permits** another person to misappropriate, convert, or use the entrusted property in violation of trust, the accused is equally guilty of CBT.

**Example**: A (a cashier) allows his friend B to take money from the cash register for B's personal use, knowing it is a breach of trust. A commits CBT by willfully suffering B to misappropriate.

**Principle**: Passive participation (allowing or permitting) in breach of trust is equivalent to active commission.

#### 5. **Property**

The subject matter must be **property** as defined in IPC Section 25:
- **Movable property** (money, goods, securities, etc.)
- **Immovable property** (land, buildings, etc.)
- **Valuable security** or **debt**

**Note**: Unlike theft (which applies only to movable property), CBT can apply to immovable property as well.

---

## Explanation to Section 405: When Entrustment Exists

**Explanation 1**: A person, being an employer of an establishment, in respect of which certain provisions of the Employees' Provident Funds and Miscellaneous Provisions Act, 1952, are applicable, deducts the employees' contribution from the salary but does not deposit it with the Provident Fund authorities, commits CBT.

**Rationale**: The employer holds the employees' PF contribution in trust and is legally obligated to deposit it. Failure to deposit or using it for business purposes is CBT.

**Explanation 2**: A person who fraudulently or dishonestly misappropriates or converts to his own use property which has come into his possession by finding, or by mistake of another person, may be guilty of CBT if the circumstances are such that he is bound by law to restore or account for the property.

**Example**: A finds B's wallet containing Rs. 10,000 and B's ID card. A keeps the money instead of returning it to B or police. If A had reasonable means of discovering the owner and was legally bound to return it, this may be CBT (or theft, depending on circumstances).

---

## Punishment for Criminal Breach of Trust

### Section 406 IPC / Section 317 BNS: Simple Criminal Breach of Trust
**Punishment**:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **3 years**, OR
- **Fine**, OR
- **Both**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Bailable, Triable by Any Magistrate

**Example**: A borrows B's bicycle for one day but keeps it for a month and uses it for commercial delivery without permission. Simple CBT.

### Section 407 IPC / Section 318 BNS: Criminal Breach of Trust by Carrier, Wharfinger, or Warehouse-keeper
Whoever, being entrusted with property as a **carrier**, **wharfinger** (person who manages a wharf), or **warehouse-keeper**, commits criminal breach of trust in respect of such property, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Rationale**: Carriers and warehouse-keepers are entrusted with goods of the public and breach of such trust affects commerce and public confidence.

**Example**: A shipping company is entrusted with goods for transportation. The company sells the goods and misappropriates the proceeds. CBT by carrier under Section 407.

### Section 408 IPC / Section 319 BNS: Criminal Breach of Trust by Clerk or Servant
Whoever, being a **clerk or servant**, or employed in the capacity of a clerk or servant, and being in any manner entrusted with property or with dominion over property, commits criminal breach of trust, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Rationale**: Breach of trust by employees who have access to employer's property is aggravated due to the confidence reposed in them.

**Example**: A, a cashier in a shop, takes money from the cash register for personal use (without permission or intention to return). CBT by servant under Section 408.

**Distinction from Theft by Servant (Section 381)**:
- **Theft by Servant**: Servant takes property without being entrusted with it (e.g., takes from locked drawer without authorization)
- **CBT by Servant**: Servant misappropriates property already entrusted to him (e.g., cashier misuses cash register money he is authorized to handle)

### Section 409 IPC / Section 320 BNS: Criminal Breach of Trust by Public Servant, Banker, Merchant, or Agent
Whoever, being in any of the following capacities, commits criminal breach of trust:
- **Public servant**
- **Banker**
- **Merchant**
- **Factor** (agent dealing in goods)
- **Broker**
- **Attorney** or **agent**
- In the way of his business as such

Shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment for life**, OR
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Bailable (at discretion), Triable by Court of Session

**Rationale**: These categories involve the highest degree of trust and confidence. Breach by such persons severely undermines public trust in institutions and professions.

**Examples**:
1. **Public Servant**: A government employee misappropriates public funds entrusted to him. CBT under Section 409.
2. **Banker**: A bank manager diverts customer deposits to his personal account. CBT under Section 409.
3. **Agent**: A real estate agent is given money to purchase property on behalf of a client but uses it for his own property. CBT under Section 409.
4. **Attorney**: A lawyer is given Rs. 10 lakh by a client to deposit in court, but the lawyer uses it for personal investment. CBT under Section 409.

**Most Serious Form**: Section 409 is the most serious form of CBT, punishable with life imprisonment or up to 10 years.

---

## Distinction Between Theft, CBT, and Cheating

| **Element** | **Theft (378)** | **Criminal Breach of Trust (405)** | **Cheating (415)** |
|-------------|-----------------|-------------------------------------|---------------------|
| **Initial Possession** | Without consent; unlawful taking | With consent; lawful entrustment | With consent; victim delivers due to deception |
| **Entrustment** | No entrustment | Property entrusted to accused | No entrustment (consent obtained by fraud) |
| **Dishonest Act** | Taking property without consent | Misappropriating or converting entrusted property | Deceiving to induce delivery |
| **When Dishonesty Arises** | At the time of taking | May arise before or after entrustment | At the time of inducing delivery |
| **Consent** | No consent | Consent to entrust, but not to misappropriate | Consent to deliver, but obtained by deception |
| **Punishment (simple)** | Up to 3 years | Up to 3 years (simple CBT) | Up to 3 years (simple cheating) |
| **Example** | A steals B's wallet from B's pocket | A borrows B's car and sells it without permission | A tricks B into buying fake jewelry claiming it is gold |

### Key Distinction: Theft vs. CBT

**When is it Theft?**
- Property is taken **without** the owner's consent
- Accused has **no lawful possession** at the time of taking

**When is it CBT?**
- Property is **entrusted** to the accused
- Accused has **lawful possession** initially
- Accused later dishonestly misappropriates or converts it

**Example 1 (Theft)**: A employee takes money from employer's locked safe without authorization. Theft (no entrustment).

**Example 2 (CBT)**: A cashier, entrusted with handling cash, takes money from the register for personal use. CBT (entrustment exists).

### Key Distinction: CBT vs. Cheating

**When is it CBT?**
- Property is entrusted to accused **before** dishonest act
- Accused violates the terms of entrustment

**When is it Cheating?**
- Accused deceives victim **to induce** delivery of property
- Victim delivers property due to deception, not prior entrustment

**Example 1 (CBT)**: A deposits Rs. 10 lakh with B (a broker) to invest in shares. B uses the money for personal gambling. CBT (entrustment exists).

**Example 2 (Cheating)**: A falsely claims to be a licensed broker and induces B to give him Rs. 10 lakh for investment. A disappears with the money. Cheating (consent obtained by deception, no entrustment relationship).

---

## Landmark Cases on Criminal Breach of Trust

### 1. **R.S. Nayak v. A.R. Antulay** (1984) - Public Servant CBT
**AIR 1984 SC 684**

**Facts**: The accused, a Chief Minister (public servant), was alleged to have misused his official position to obtain donations to a trust controlled by him, which were then misappropriated.

**Held**: The Supreme Court held that a **public servant** who misappropriates funds or property entrusted to him in his official capacity commits criminal breach of trust under **Section 409**. The position of public trust aggravates the offence.

**Principle**: Public servants hold public funds and property in trust. Misappropriation of such property is CBT under Section 409, attracting the highest punishment (life imprisonment or up to 10 years).

### 2. **Haji Muhammed v. State of Kerala** (1969) - Entrustment Requirement
**AIR 1969 SC 378**

**Facts**: The accused was a contractor who received payments from the government for work. The prosecution alleged CBT for not using the money as per contract.

**Held**: The Supreme Court held that for CBT, there must be **entrustment of property** or **dominion over property**. Mere contractual obligation to perform work does not create entrustment of money paid as consideration.

**Principle**: Not every breach of contract is CBT. There must be entrustment of property in a fiduciary capacity. Payment of money as consideration for services (without fiduciary obligation) does not create entrustment for CBT purposes.

**Distinction**: If A pays B to build a house, B's obligation is to perform work, not to hold A's money in trust. If B does not build the house, it may be breach of contract or cheating, but not CBT (no entrustment).

**Counter-Example**: If A gives B money to purchase materials for A and B misappropriates it, it is CBT (B was entrusted with money for specific purpose).

### 3. **Jaswantrai Manilal Akhaney v. State of Bombay** (1956) - Dishonest Intention Timing
**AIR 1956 SC 575**

**Facts**: The accused borrowed money with honest intention to repay but later could not repay due to business failure.

**Held**: For CBT, **dishonest intention** must exist at the time of misappropriation, conversion, or use in violation of trust. If the accused initially acted honestly but later could not fulfill the obligation due to circumstances beyond control (not dishonesty), it is not CBT; it is a civil matter.

**Principle**: Mere breach of contract or failure to repay debt does not constitute CBT unless there is **dishonest misappropriation** or **dishonest intention** at the time of the act.

**Test**: Was the failure to return property due to:
- **Dishonest misappropriation** (used for unauthorized purpose, converted to own use)? → CBT
- **Inability due to circumstances** (business loss, genuine inability without dishonesty)? → Civil breach, not CBT

**Example 1 (CBT)**: A borrows money from B to invest in business but uses it to buy a luxury car. CBT (dishonest use in violation of entrustment).

**Example 2 (Not CBT)**: A borrows money from B to invest in business. Business fails and A cannot repay despite honest efforts. Not CBT (no dishonesty).

### 4. **S. Ramaswamy Iyer v. Emperor** (1942) - Agent's CBT
**AIR 1942 Mad 522**

**Facts**: An agent was entrusted with collecting money on behalf of the principal. The agent collected money but did not remit it to the principal.

**Held**: An **agent** who is entrusted with property (money collected) and dishonestly misappropriates it commits CBT under **Section 409** (CBT by agent).

**Principle**: Agent-principal relationship creates entrustment. Agent's dishonest failure to account for or remit collected property is CBT.

**Application**: Real estate agents, collection agents, insurance agents who collect money on behalf of principals and misappropriate it are liable under Section 409.

### 5. **Ramaswamy Nadar v. State of Tamil Nadu** (1975) - Banker's CBT
**AIR 1975 SC 1211**

**Facts**: A bank employee diverted customer deposits to unauthorized accounts and misappropriated funds.

**Held**: A **banker** or bank employee who misappropriates customer deposits entrusted to the bank commits CBT under **Section 409**. The banker-customer relationship is one of high trust.

**Principle**: Deposits with banks are held in trust (fiduciary relationship). Misappropriation of deposits is one of the gravest forms of CBT.

**Penalty**: Imprisonment for life or up to 10 years + fine (Section 409).

### 6. **State of Rajasthan v. Kamal Kumar** (2005) - Employee PF Deduction CBT
**2005 Cri LJ 4372**

**Facts**: An employer deducted employees' Provident Fund contributions from salaries but did not deposit them with the PF authorities.

**Held**: Employer who deducts employees' PF contributions holds that money **in trust** for employees. Failure to deposit or using it for business purposes is **criminal breach of trust** under Section 405.

**Principle**: PF contributions deducted from employees' salaries are held in statutory trust. Employer's misappropriation or delay in depositing (without valid reason) constitutes CBT.

**Legislative Support**: Explanation 1 to Section 405 specifically covers this scenario.

### 7. **V.V. Hadli v. State of Karnataka** (2008) - Willfully Suffering Another to Commit CBT
**AIR 2008 SC 2726**

**Facts**: The accused, having dominion over funds, allowed another person to misappropriate those funds without objection.

**Held**: A person who **willfully suffers** (allows or permits) another person to misappropriate entrusted property is equally guilty of CBT under Section 405.

**Principle**: Passive participation (knowingly allowing misappropriation) is equivalent to active commission of CBT.

**Example**: A trustee who allows his co-trustee to misappropriate trust property without reporting or preventing it is also liable for CBT.

---

## IPC to BNS Transition Mapping

| **IPC Section** | **BNS Section** | **Offence** | **Changes** |
|-----------------|-----------------|-------------|-------------|
| **405** | **316** | Definition of CBT | No substantive change; includes PF deduction explanation |
| **406** | **317** | Punishment for CBT | No change (up to 3 years or fine or both) |
| **407** | **318** | CBT by Carrier, Warehouse-keeper | No change (up to 7 years + fine) |
| **408** | **319** | CBT by Clerk or Servant | No change (up to 7 years + fine) |
| **409** | **320** | CBT by Public Servant, Banker, Agent | No change (life or up to 10 years + fine) |

**Note**: The BNS 2023 has retained all CBT provisions from IPC 1860 with no substantive changes. Definitions, ingredients, and punishments remain identical.

---

## Practical Examples

### Example 1: Simple CBT (Borrowing and Misappropriating)
**Scenario**: Rahul borrows Rs. 50,000 from Amit to pay for Rahul's medical treatment. Instead, Rahul uses the money to buy a new smartphone and go on vacation.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Entrustment: Amit entrusted money to Rahul for specific purpose (medical treatment)
2. ✅ Dishonest misappropriation: Rahul used money for unauthorized purpose (smartphone, vacation)
3. ✅ Violation of trust: Rahul violated the terms of entrustment (specific purpose)
4. ✅ Dishonest intention: Rahul intended wrongful gain (using money for own pleasure) and caused wrongful loss to Amit

**Offence**: Criminal Breach of Trust under Section 406 IPC / Section 317 BNS
**Punishment**: Up to 3 years imprisonment or fine or both

### Example 2: CBT by Clerk/Servant (Cashier Misappropriation)
**Scenario**: Priya is a cashier in a retail store. She is authorized to handle cash collections. Over several months, she takes Rs. 2 lakh from the cash register for her personal use without informing her employer.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Entrustment: Priya was entrusted with handling cash (dominion over property)
2. ✅ Clerk/Servant capacity: Priya is an employee (clerk/servant)
3. ✅ Dishonest misappropriation: Priya took money for personal use (not authorized)
4. ✅ Breach of trust: Violated employer's trust

**Offence**: Criminal Breach of Trust by Clerk or Servant under Section 408 IPC / Section 319 BNS
**Punishment**: Up to 7 years imprisonment AND fine

### Example 3: CBT by Agent (Real Estate Agent)
**Scenario**: Vikram hires Suresh, a real estate agent, to find and purchase a property on his behalf. Vikram gives Suresh Rs. 50 lakh for the purchase. Suresh uses the money to purchase a property in his own name instead.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Entrustment: Vikram entrusted Rs. 50 lakh to Suresh (agent) for specific purpose
2. ✅ Agent capacity: Suresh is an agent acting in professional capacity
3. ✅ Dishonest conversion: Suresh converted property/money to his own use (purchased in his name)
4. ✅ Violation of agency agreement: Suresh violated contractual and fiduciary duty

**Offence**: Criminal Breach of Trust by Agent under Section 409 IPC / Section 320 BNS
**Punishment**: Imprisonment for life OR up to 10 years AND fine

### Example 4: CBT by Banker (Bank Employee Diverting Deposits)
**Scenario**: Ravi is a bank manager. He diverts customer deposits of Rs. 1 crore into fictitious accounts controlled by him and uses the money for personal investments.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Entrustment: Customers entrusted deposits to the bank
2. ✅ Banker capacity: Ravi is a banker (bank employee with dominion over customer deposits)
3. ✅ Dishonest misappropriation: Ravi diverted deposits to personal accounts
4. ✅ High breach of trust: Banker-customer relationship involves highest trust

**Offence**: Criminal Breach of Trust by Banker under Section 409 IPC / Section 320 BNS
**Punishment**: Imprisonment for life OR up to 10 years AND fine

**Additional Consequences**: Banking license suspension/cancellation, civil liability for recovery, RBI disciplinary action

### Example 5: CBT by Public Servant (Government Fund Misappropriation)
**Scenario**: Ajay is a government clerk responsible for disbursing welfare funds to beneficiaries. He creates fake beneficiaries and diverts Rs. 20 lakh to his own accounts.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Entrustment: Ajay was entrusted with public funds (dominion over government property)
2. ✅ Public servant capacity: Ajay is a government employee (public servant)
3. ✅ Dishonest misappropriation: Ajay diverted public funds to personal use
4. ✅ Violation of public trust: Gravest form of CBT (public property)

**Offence**: Criminal Breach of Trust by Public Servant under Section 409 IPC / Section 320 BNS
**Punishment**: Imprisonment for life OR up to 10 years AND fine

**Additional Charges**: Forgery (creating fake records), cheating, corruption (Prevention of Corruption Act)

### Example 6: Not CBT (Mere Contractual Breach)
**Scenario**: Mohan hires Sunil to build a house for Rs. 10 lakh. Mohan pays Rs. 5 lakh as advance. Sunil starts work but abandons the project halfway due to financial problems, leaving the house incomplete.

**Analysis**:
1. ❌ No entrustment: Mohan paid Rs. 5 lakh as **consideration** for construction services, not in trust for specific use
2. ❌ No fiduciary relationship: Contractor-client relationship is contractual, not fiduciary (no entrustment of property)
3. ✅ Contractual breach: Sunil breached the construction contract

**Offence**: Likely **not CBT** (no entrustment). It is a **civil breach of contract**.

**Remedy**: Mohan can sue for breach of contract and damages in civil court.

**Counter-Example (When it would be CBT)**: If Mohan gave Sunil Rs. 5 lakh specifically to purchase construction materials for Mohan's house, and Sunil used the money for personal purposes instead, it would be CBT (entrustment for specific purpose).

### Example 7: CBT by Employer (Provident Fund Non-Deposit)
**Scenario**: An employer deducts Rs. 10 lakh as employees' Provident Fund contributions from salaries over a year but does not deposit the amount with the PF authorities. The employer uses the money for business operations.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Entrustment: Employer holds employees' PF contribution in **statutory trust**
2. ✅ Statutory obligation: EPF Act mandates deposit within specified time
3. ✅ Dishonest use: Employer used PF money for business (violation of statutory trust)
4. ✅ Explicit provision: Explanation 1 to Section 405 covers this scenario

**Offence**: Criminal Breach of Trust under Section 405/406 IPC / Section 316/317 BNS
**Punishment**: Up to 3 years imprisonment or fine or both

**Additional Liability**: EPF Act penalties, interest on delayed deposit, civil suit for recovery

---

## Defenses to Criminal Breach of Trust

### 1. **No Entrustment**
- If no property was entrusted to the accused, no CBT
- Accused must show he did not have dominion or fiduciary control over property

**Example**: A watchman who steals goods from warehouse has no entrustment (mere custody); offence is theft, not CBT.

### 2. **Absence of Dishonest Intention**
- If the accused acted honestly (even if result was failure to return property), no CBT
- Genuine inability due to circumstances (business failure, unforeseen loss) without dishonesty is not CBT

**Example**: A borrowed money to invest in business with honest intention, but business failed. If A made genuine efforts and did not misappropriate, it is not CBT.

### 3. **No Misappropriation or Conversion**
- If the accused used property in accordance with the terms of entrustment, no CBT
- Mere delay or failure to return (without dishonest use) may not be CBT

**Example**: A borrows B's book and forgets to return it for 3 months. If A did not convert it to own use or sell it, it is not CBT (mere delay).

### 4. **Bona Fide Claim of Right**
- If the accused genuinely believed he had a right to use the property in the manner he did, dishonest intention is absent

**Example**: A, a partner in a firm, uses partnership funds believing he has the authority, but actually he needs co-partner approval. If belief is genuine (even if mistaken), no CBT.

### 5. **Civil Dispute, Not Criminal**
- If the matter is a mere contractual dispute (debt recovery, breach of contract) without fiduciary entrustment, it is civil, not criminal

**Example**: A owes B money under a loan agreement. A does not repay due to financial hardship. This is civil debt recovery, not CBT (no entrustment in fiduciary capacity).

**Landmark Principle**: *Haji Muhammed v. State of Kerala* - Not every breach of contract is CBT; there must be entrustment in fiduciary capacity.

### 6. **Consent or Waiver**
- If the person who entrusted property consented to the use or waived the claim, no CBT

**Example**: A borrows B's car for one week but keeps it for two weeks. If B consents to the extended use, no CBT.

---

## Procedural Aspects

### Cognizance and Complaint
- **All CBT offences (Sections 406-409)**: Non-cognizable, Bailable (except Section 409 at court's discretion)
- **Police cannot arrest without warrant**
- **Complaint to magistrate required**, or police can investigate with magistrate's order (Section 155 CrPC)

### Investigation
- Magistrate may order investigation under Section 156(3) CrPC
- Evidence: Documentary evidence (agreements, contracts, accounts), witness testimony, admission/confession, recovery of misappropriated property

### Trial
- Burden of proof on prosecution to establish all ingredients: entrustment, dishonest misappropriation/conversion, violation of trust
- Defense may challenge entrustment, dishonest intention, or argue civil dispute

### Compounding
- **All CBT offences are compoundable with permission of court** (Section 320 CrPC)
- Complainant and accused can compromise and end the case with court approval

---

## Compliance Checklist

### For Potential Victims (Preventive Measures)
- [ ] Document all entrustments: written agreements, receipts, terms of trust
- [ ] Specify purpose and conditions of entrustment clearly
- [ ] Maintain records of property entrusted (inventory, accounts)
- [ ] Monitor use of entrusted property (audits for employees, agents, trustees)
- [ ] Act promptly if misappropriation suspected (demand accounts, inspection)
- [ ] Report to police or file complaint with magistrate if CBT suspected
- [ ] Seek civil remedies concurrently (suit for recovery, injunction)

### For Persons Handling Entrusted Property (Compliance)
- [ ] Maintain clear records of entrusted property and its use
- [ ] Use property only for authorized purposes
- [ ] Provide regular accounts to the person who entrusted property
- [ ] Seek clarification if uncertain about scope of authority
- [ ] Avoid commingling personal and entrusted property
- [ ] For employers: Deposit employees' PF contributions within statutory deadlines

### For Accused/Suspect (Legal Safeguards)
- [ ] Claim defenses: no entrustment, no dishonest intention, bona fide claim of right
- [ ] Demonstrate honest use of property in accordance with entrustment terms
- [ ] Show genuine inability (if applicable) without dishonesty
- [ ] Argue civil nature of dispute (contractual breach, not criminal CBT)
- [ ] Return or account for property if possible (may mitigate, not a complete defense)
- [ ] Seek compounding (compromise with complainant) with court permission
- [ ] Seek bail (CBT is bailable, except Section 409 at discretion)

### For Investigating Officer
- [ ] Establish entrustment: documentary evidence (agreements, contracts)
- [ ] Prove dishonest misappropriation, conversion, or use in violation of trust
- [ ] Prove dishonest intention (critical element)
- [ ] Differentiate from theft, cheating, or civil breach of contract
- [ ] Frame appropriate charge: Section 406, 407, 408, or 409 based on capacity

### For Prosecutors
- [ ] Prove all five ingredients: entrustment, dishonest misappropriation/conversion, property, violation of trust, dishonest intention
- [ ] Establish fiduciary relationship or dominion over property
- [ ] Anticipate defenses: no entrustment, no dishonesty, civil dispute
- [ ] Use documentary evidence (agreements, accounts, emails, admissions)

### For Defense Counsel
- [ ] Challenge entrustment: argue no fiduciary relationship or dominion
- [ ] Challenge dishonest intention: show bona fide belief, genuine inability
- [ ] Argue civil nature of dispute (contractual breach, debt recovery)
- [ ] Seek acquittal if prosecution fails to prove any ingredient beyond reasonable doubt
- [ ] Explore compounding (compromise) to end case
- [ ] If conviction likely, argue for minimum sentence: first-time offender, return of property, family circumstances

---

## Cross-References

### Related Criminal Offences
- **Theft (IPC 378-382)**: Taking without consent (no entrustment)
- **Cheating (IPC 415-420)**: Obtaining property by deception (consent by fraud, no entrustment)
- **Criminal Misappropriation of Property (IPC 403)**: Dishonestly misappropriating property that came into possession by finding or mistake
- **Forgery (IPC 463-477A)**: May be used to conceal CBT (false accounts)
- **Fraud**: Civil and criminal (Companies Act, SEBI Act for corporate fraud involving CBT)

### Constitutional Provisions
- **Article 20(3)**: Protection against self-incrimination (accused cannot be compelled to confess)
- **Article 21**: Right to fair trial and legal representation

### Special Legislations
- **Prevention of Corruption Act, 1988**: For CBT by public servants involving corruption
- **Companies Act, 2013**: For CBT by company directors, officers (Section 447: fraud)
- **SEBI Act, 1992**: Securities fraud involving CBT
- **EPF Act, 1952**: Non-deposit of PF contributions is CBT

### Contract Law
- **Section 15, Indian Contract Act**: Coercion - Contract induced by CBT may be voidable
- **Section 73: Damages for breach of contract** (civil remedy in addition to criminal prosecution)

### Procedural Laws
- **CrPC / BNSS**:
  - Section 155: Non-cognizable cases (magistrate's order required for investigation)
  - Section 156(3): Magistrate can order police investigation
  - Section 320: Compounding (all CBT offences compoundable with court permission)
  - Section 437: Bail (CBT is generally bailable except Section 409 at court's discretion)

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Entrustment is Critical**: CBT requires property to be entrusted to the accused or accused to have dominion over it. No entrustment = not CBT (may be theft or cheating).

2. **Lawful Initial Possession**: Unlike theft (unlawful taking), in CBT the accused has lawful possession initially, which makes the breach of trust more egregious.

3. **Dishonest Intention May Arise Later**: The accused may receive property honestly but later develop dishonest intention to misappropriate. CBT occurs at the point of dishonest act.

4. **Fiduciary Relationships**: CBT often involves breach of fiduciary relationships: trustee-beneficiary, agent-principal, employee-employer, banker-customer.

5. **Aggravated Forms Based on Capacity**: Higher punishment for CBT by carriers (Section 407), servants (Section 408), and especially public servants, bankers, agents (Section 409: life or 10 years).

6. **Civil vs. Criminal**: Not every contractual breach or debt default is CBT. There must be entrustment in a fiduciary capacity and dishonest misappropriation.

7. **Non-Cognizable but Compoundable**: CBT is non-cognizable (police cannot arrest without warrant), but it is compoundable (parties can compromise with court permission).

8. **PF Deduction CBT**: Employers who deduct employees' PF contributions but do not deposit them commit CBT (Explanation 1 to Section 405).

---

## Conclusion

Criminal Breach of Trust is a property offence that punishes the violation of confidence and trust reposed in a person. It is distinguished from theft and cheating by the element of **entrustment** - the accused has lawful possession initially but subsequently misappropriates or converts the property dishonestly.

The law recognizes varying degrees of severity based on the capacity in which the accused holds property: simple CBT (up to 3 years), CBT by carriers/servants (up to 7 years), and CBT by public servants/bankers/agents (life or up to 10 years). This gradation reflects the level of trust and public confidence reposed in different capacities.

Understanding the fine line between civil contractual breach and criminal breach of trust is essential. The presence of **entrustment in a fiduciary capacity** and **dishonest intention** transforms a civil dispute into a criminal offence.

With the transition to BNS 2023, the substantive law on CBT remains unchanged, ensuring continuity in legal practice and precedent.

---

**Document Version**: 1.0
**Effective Date**: IPC provisions apply until June 30, 2024; BNS provisions effective from July 1, 2024
**Last Updated**: December 6, 2025
**Prepared by**: Legal Protocol Documentation System
**Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)

---

**Disclaimer**: This protocol is for informational and educational purposes only and does not constitute legal advice. For specific legal matters, consult a qualified criminal law advocate or legal expert.
