## IL-CRIM-EXTORTION.md
---

# India Criminal Law: Extortion (Jabar-Vusuli / Bhatta)

## Protocol ID
**IL-CRIM-EXTORTION**

## Domain
Criminal Law > Crimes Against Property

## Jurisdiction
India (All States and Union Territories)

## Statutory Framework

### Indian Penal Code (IPC), 1860
- **Section 383**: Definition of Extortion
- **Section 384**: Punishment for Extortion
- **Section 385**: Putting Person in Fear of Injury to Commit Extortion
- **Section 386**: Extortion by Putting a Person in Fear of Death or Grievous Hurt
- **Section 387**: Putting Person in Fear of Death or Grievous Hurt to Commit Extortion
- **Section 388**: Extortion by Threat of Accusation of an Offence Punishable with Death, etc.
- **Section 389**: Putting Person in Fear of Accusation of Offence to Commit Extortion

### Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS), 2023 (Effective July 1, 2024)
- **Section 308**: Definition of Extortion (formerly IPC 383)
- **Section 309**: Punishment for Extortion (formerly IPC 384)
- **Section 310**: Putting in Fear of Injury to Commit Extortion (formerly IPC 385)
- **Section 311**: Extortion by Fear of Death/GH (formerly IPC 386)
- **Section 312**: Putting in Fear of Death/GH to Commit Extortion (formerly IPC 387)
- **Section 313**: Extortion by Threat of Accusation (formerly IPC 388)
- **Section 314**: Putting in Fear of Accusation to Commit Extortion (formerly IPC 389)

---

## Overview

**Extortion** is the act of intentionally putting a person in fear of injury to himself or any other person, or to the reputation of himself or any other person, in order to dishonestly induce that person to deliver any property or valuable security.

Extortion is a property offence characterized by the use of **threat** or **fear** to obtain property. Unlike theft (where property is taken without consent) or robbery (where instant violence is used), extortion involves **obtaining property with consent, but that consent is procured by fear**.

### Key Characteristics
1. **Consent Under Fear**: Victim delivers property voluntarily but under fear
2. **Threat Element**: Fear is induced by threat of injury (physical, reputational, or to property)
3. **Broad Scope of Injury**: Injury includes harm to person, reputation, or property
4. **Future or Immediate**: Threat may be of immediate or future harm (unlike robbery which requires instant harm)
5. **Delivery Induced**: Victim must deliver or transfer property due to the fear

---

## Definition of Extortion (IPC Section 383 / BNS Section 308)

### Full Legal Definition

**IPC Section 383**: Whoever intentionally puts any person in fear of any injury to that person, or to any other person, or to the reputation of that person or of any other person, and thereby dishonestly induces the person so put in fear to deliver to any person any property or valuable security, or anything signed or sealed which may be converted into a valuable security, commits **extortion**.

### Seven Essential Ingredients of Extortion

#### 1. **Intentionally Putting in Fear**
- The accused must **intentionally** create fear in the victim's mind
- Accidental or unintentional fear does not constitute extortion
- The act must be deliberate and purposeful

**Example**: A tells B, "If you don't pay me Rs. 10 lakh, I will publish this compromising video of you." A intentionally puts B in fear of reputational injury.

#### 2. **Fear of Injury**
The fear must be of **injury** to:
- **The person himself** (physical harm to victim)
- **Any other person** (physical harm to victim's family, friends, etc.)
- **The reputation of the person** (defamation, dishonor of victim)
- **The reputation of any other person** (defamation of victim's family, etc.)

**Meaning of "Injury"** (IPC Section 44):
- Any harm whatever illegally caused to any person
- In body, mind, reputation, or property

**Types of Injury**:
1. **Physical Injury**: Threat of death, hurt, wrongful restraint, wrongful confinement
2. **Reputational Injury**: Threat of defamation, exposure of secrets, false accusations
3. **Property Injury**: Threat of damage to property, destruction of business

**Example 1 (Physical)**: A threatens to kill B's son unless B pays Rs. 5 lakh. Fear of physical injury to another person.

**Example 2 (Reputational)**: A threatens to expose B's extramarital affair unless B transfers property. Fear of reputational injury to B.

**Example 3 (Property)**: A threatens to burn down B's shop unless B pays monthly protection money. Fear of property injury.

#### 3. **Dishonestly Inducing Delivery**
- The fear must **induce** the victim to deliver property
- The induction must be **dishonest** (with intention of wrongful gain or wrongful loss)
- There must be a causal link between the fear and the delivery

**Dishonest** (IPC Section 24): Intention to cause wrongful gain to one person or wrongful loss to another

**Example**: If A threatens B and B pays money due to that threat, the delivery is induced by fear. But if B pays for some other reason (e.g., genuine debt repayment), no extortion.

#### 4. **Delivery of Property or Valuable Security**
The victim must deliver:
- **Property** (movable or immovable)
- **Valuable security** (documents of title, promissory notes, cheques, etc.)
- **Anything signed or sealed** that may be converted into a valuable security (blank cheques, signed documents)

**Note**: Unlike theft (which applies only to movable property), extortion can apply to immovable property as well (e.g., forcing someone to transfer land by threat).

**Example**: A threatens B and induces B to sign a deed transferring B's house to A. This is extortion of immovable property.

#### 5. **Delivery to Any Person**
- The property need not be delivered to the accused himself
- It may be delivered to any person designated by the accused

**Example**: A threatens B and induces B to transfer money to C's account (C is A's associate). A commits extortion even though property was delivered to C.

#### 6. **Actual Delivery Required**
- For completed extortion, the victim must actually deliver the property
- If property is not delivered (victim refuses or police intervene), it is **attempt to commit extortion** (Section 385 or 387 or 389)

#### 7. **No Consent Defense**
- The victim's consent to deliver property is **irrelevant** if it is obtained by fear
- Consent procured by threat is not free consent

---

## Punishment for Extortion

### Section 384 IPC / Section 309 BNS: Simple Extortion
**Punishment**:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **3 years**, OR
- **Fine**, OR
- **Both**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Bailable, Triable by Any Magistrate

**Example**: A threatens to report B to the police for a minor traffic violation unless B pays Rs. 5,000. B pays. A commits extortion.

### Section 385 IPC / Section 310 BNS: Putting Person in Fear of Injury to Commit Extortion
Whoever, in order to commit extortion, puts any person in fear of any injury, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **2 years**, OR
- **Fine**, OR
- **Both**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Bailable, Triable by Any Magistrate

**This is ATTEMPT to commit extortion**: The threat is made, but property is not delivered.

**Example**: A sends a threatening letter to B demanding money, but B refuses to pay and reports to police. A is liable under Section 385 (attempt).

### Section 386 IPC / Section 311 BNS: Extortion by Putting Person in Fear of Death or Grievous Hurt
Whoever commits extortion by putting any person in fear of **death** or **grievous hurt** to that person or any other, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Aggravating Circumstance**: Threat of death or grievous hurt (most serious form of physical harm)

**Example**: A threatens to kill B's daughter unless B pays Rs. 50 lakh. B pays. A commits aggravated extortion under Section 386.

### Section 387 IPC / Section 312 BNS: Putting Person in Fear of Death or Grievous Hurt to Commit Extortion
Whoever, in order to commit extortion, puts any person in fear of **death** or **grievous hurt**, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**This is ATTEMPT to commit aggravated extortion**: Threat of death/GH is made, but property not delivered.

**Example**: A threatens B with death unless B transfers property. B refuses and reports to police. A is liable under Section 387 (attempt).

### Section 388 IPC / Section 313 BNS: Extortion by Threat of Accusation of an Offence Punishable with Death or Imprisonment for Life
Whoever commits extortion by putting any person in fear of an **accusation** against that person or any other person of having committed or attempted to commit:
- An offence **punishable with death**, OR
- An offence **punishable with imprisonment for life**, OR
- An offence **punishable with imprisonment for 10 years**, OR
- Of having **attempted to commit suicide** (before IPC amendment decriminalizing suicide attempt)

shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Aggravating Circumstance**: Threat of serious criminal accusation (blackmail)

**Example**: A threatens to file a false case of rape (punishable with life imprisonment) against B unless B pays Rs. 10 lakh. B pays. A commits extortion under Section 388.

### Section 389 IPC / Section 314 BNS: Putting Person in Fear of Accusation of Offence to Commit Extortion
Whoever, in order to commit extortion, puts any person in fear of an accusation against that person or any other of having committed or attempted to commit an offence punishable with death, life, or 10 years imprisonment, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**This is ATTEMPT to commit extortion by accusation**: Threat made, property not delivered.

**Example**: A threatens to accuse B of murder unless B pays money. B refuses and reports to police. A is liable under Section 389 (attempt).

---

## Distinction Between Theft, Robbery, and Extortion

| **Element** | **Theft (378)** | **Robbery (390)** | **Extortion (383)** |
|-------------|-----------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| **Consent** | Without consent | Without consent (or consent under instant fear) | With consent (but obtained by fear) |
| **Taking vs. Delivery** | Offender takes property | Offender takes property (theft-robbery) OR victim delivers under instant fear (extortion-robbery) | Victim delivers property |
| **Violence/Threat** | No violence or threat | Violence OR threat of **instant** death/hurt/restraint | Threat of injury (may be **future** or immediate) |
| **Timing** | No time restriction | Instant harm required | Harm may be future or immediate |
| **Presence** | Offender need not be present | For extortion-robbery: offender must be present | Offender need not be present (e.g., threatening letter) |
| **Type of Property** | Movable property only | Movable property only | Movable **or immovable** property |
| **Punishment (simple)** | Up to 3 years | Minimum 1 year, up to 10 years (RI) | Up to 3 years (simple extortion) |
| **Nature** | Cognizable, Non-bailable (simple) | Cognizable, Non-bailable | Non-cognizable (simple), Cognizable (aggravated) |

### Key Distinction: Robbery vs. Extortion

**When Extortion Becomes Robbery** (Section 390 Explanation):
1. Offender must be **in the presence** of the victim
2. Fear must be of **instant** death, hurt, or wrongful restraint
3. Victim delivers property **then and there** due to instant fear

**Example 1 (Extortion, not Robbery)**: A sends a letter to B threatening to kill B's son if B does not pay Rs. 10 lakh within a week. B pays. This is **extortion** (threat of future harm, offender not present).

**Example 2 (Robbery by Extortion)**: A points a gun at B and says, "Give me your wallet now or I will shoot you." B hands over the wallet. This is **robbery** (threat of instant death, offender present, immediate delivery).

**Example 3 (Extortion, not Robbery)**: A calls B on the phone and threatens to kill B if B does not transfer Rs. 5 lakh to A's account by tomorrow. B transfers the money. This is **extortion** (threat of future harm, not instant).

---

## Landmark Cases on Extortion

### 1. **Romesh Chandra Mehta v. State of West Bengal** (1969) - Fear of Injury
**AIR 1970 SC 940**

**Facts**: The accused threatened to initiate false criminal proceedings against the victim unless the victim paid money.

**Held**: The Supreme Court held that **fear of injury to reputation** (including fear of false accusation) is sufficient for extortion under Section 383. The injury need not be physical; reputational harm qualifies as "injury" within the meaning of Section 44.

**Principle**: Injury includes harm to reputation. Threatening to file false cases or defame a person to extract money is extortion.

### 2. **State of M.P. v. Dhani Ram** (2004) - Dishonest Intention
**2004 Cri LJ 4816**

**Facts**: The accused threatened the victim to pay money, but the prosecution could not prove that the accused had dishonest intention (wrongful gain).

**Held**: For extortion, **dishonest intention** (intention to cause wrongful gain or wrongful loss) is an essential ingredient. If the accused had a bona fide claim of right (e.g., recovering a legitimate debt), dishonest intention may be absent.

**Principle**: Extortion requires dishonest intention. If the accused genuinely believes he has a right to the money (e.g., debt recovery), and the means used are not illegal, it may not be extortion.

**Caution**: However, using threats to recover even a legitimate debt may still be illegal (e.g., criminal intimidation under Section 503).

### 3. **Bhuvan Mohan Singh v. State of U.P.** (1963) - Threat of Accusation
**AIR 1963 All 101**

**Facts**: The accused threatened to file a false rape complaint against the victim unless the victim paid money.

**Held**: Threatening to make a false accusation of a serious offence (rape, punishable with life imprisonment) to extract money constitutes extortion under **Section 388** (extortion by threat of accusation of offence punishable with death/life/10 years).

**Principle**: False accusations of serious crimes used as leverage for extortion fall under the aggravated form of extortion (Section 388), attracting up to 10 years imprisonment.

### 4. **Harman Singh Sidhu v. State of Punjab** (2007) - Inducement and Delivery
**2007 Cri LJ 2873**

**Facts**: The accused threatened the victim, but the victim did not deliver any property. The prosecution charged the accused with extortion (Section 384).

**Held**: For **completed extortion** (Section 384), there must be actual delivery of property induced by the threat. If no property is delivered, the offence is **attempt to commit extortion** (Section 385, 387, or 389), not completed extortion.

**Principle**: Extortion (Section 384) requires actual delivery. If threat is made but property not delivered, charge Section 385/387/389 (attempt).

### 5. **Ram Narain v. State of Delhi** (1953) - Delivery to Any Person
**AIR 1953 SC 277**

**Facts**: The accused threatened the victim and induced the victim to deliver money to a third person (the accused's associate).

**Held**: Extortion is complete even if the property is delivered to a person other than the accused, as long as the delivery is induced by the accused's threat. Section 383 explicitly states "deliver to **any person**."

**Principle**: The property need not be delivered to the accused personally. Delivery to any person at the accused's behest completes the offence.

### 6. **Abdul Sattar v. State of Mysore** (1956) - Fear of Future vs. Instant Harm
**AIR 1956 SC 168**

**Facts**: The accused threatened the victim with harm at a future time unless money was paid. The prosecution charged robbery.

**Held**: For **robbery**, the fear must be of **instant** death, hurt, or wrongful restraint. If the threat is of harm at a future time, it is **extortion**, not robbery.

**Principle**: The critical distinction between robbery and extortion is the **timing** of the threatened harm. Instant harm = robbery; future harm = extortion.

**Test**: Was the victim in fear of harm "right now" or "then and there"? If yes, robbery (if other conditions met). Was the fear of harm "later" or "if you don't pay within X days"? If yes, extortion.

### 7. **State of Bihar v. Rajendra Prasad** (2000) - Attempt to Extort
**2000 Cri LJ 3421**

**Facts**: The accused sent threatening letters demanding money, but the victim refused to pay and reported to police.

**Held**: The offence is **attempt to commit extortion** (Section 385) because the threat was made (putting in fear) but no property was delivered.

**Principle**: Sections 385, 387, and 389 criminalize the **attempt** to extort (making threats without successful delivery of property).

**Sentencing**: Attempt carries lower punishment (2-7 years depending on type) compared to completed extortion (3-10 years).

---

## IPC to BNS Transition Mapping

| **IPC Section** | **BNS Section** | **Offence** | **Changes** |
|-----------------|-----------------|-------------|-------------|
| **383** | **308** | Definition of Extortion | No substantive change |
| **384** | **309** | Punishment for Extortion | No change (up to 3 years or fine or both) |
| **385** | **310** | Putting in Fear to Commit Extortion (Attempt) | No change (up to 2 years or fine or both) |
| **386** | **311** | Extortion by Fear of Death/GH | No change (up to 10 years + fine) |
| **387** | **312** | Putting in Fear of Death/GH (Attempt) | No change (up to 7 years + fine) |
| **388** | **313** | Extortion by Threat of Accusation | No change (up to 10 years + fine) |
| **389** | **314** | Putting in Fear of Accusation (Attempt) | No change (up to 10 years + fine) |

**Note**: The BNS 2023 has retained all extortion provisions from IPC 1860 with no substantive changes. Definitions, ingredients, and punishments remain identical.

---

## Practical Examples

### Example 1: Simple Extortion by Physical Threat
**Scenario**: Ravi owes Amit Rs. 50,000 (a legitimate debt). Amit sends Ravi a message: "Pay me the Rs. 50,000 within 7 days or I will break your legs." Ravi, in fear, pays the money.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Intentionally putting in fear: Amit threatens physical harm
2. ✅ Fear of injury: Threat of physical injury to Ravi
3. ✅ Dishonestly inducing delivery: Even though the debt is legitimate, using threats to recover it is dishonest (wrongful means)
4. ✅ Delivery of property: Ravi pays money
5. ✅ Causal link: Ravi paid due to fear of threat

**Offence**: Extortion under Section 384 IPC / Section 309 BNS
**Punishment**: Up to 3 years imprisonment or fine or both

**Note**: Even if Amit had a legitimate claim (debt recovery), using threats of violence is illegal and constitutes extortion. Legal remedies (civil suit) should be used.

### Example 2: Extortion by Threat to Reputation (Blackmail)
**Scenario**: Sneha has compromising photographs of Vikram. She threatens to publish them on social media unless Vikram pays her Rs. 10 lakh. Vikram, fearing reputational damage, pays.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Intentionally putting in fear: Sneha threatens to publish photos
2. ✅ Fear of injury to reputation: Vikram fears defamation and dishonor
3. ✅ Dishonestly inducing delivery: Sneha has no right to the money; wrongful gain
4. ✅ Delivery of property: Vikram pays Rs. 10 lakh
5. ✅ Causal link: Payment induced by fear of reputational harm

**Offence**: Extortion under Section 384 IPC / Section 309 BNS
**Punishment**: Up to 3 years imprisonment or fine or both

**Additional Charges**: Criminal intimidation (Section 503-506 IPC), possibly defamation or IT Act violations

### Example 3: Aggravated Extortion by Threat of Death
**Scenario**: A gang threatens a businessman: "Pay us Rs. 50 lakh protection money or we will kill you and your family." The businessman, in fear of his life, pays.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Intentionally putting in fear: Threat of death to victim and family
2. ✅ Fear of death (injury to person): Most serious form of physical harm
3. ✅ Dishonestly inducing delivery: Gang has no right to money
4. ✅ Delivery of property: Businessman pays Rs. 50 lakh

**Offence**: Extortion by Putting in Fear of Death under Section 386 IPC / Section 311 BNS (aggravated form)
**Punishment**: Up to 10 years imprisonment AND fine (cognizable, non-bailable)

**Additional Charges**: Criminal conspiracy (Section 120B), organized crime (MCOCA, if applicable)

### Example 4: Extortion by Threat of False Criminal Accusation
**Scenario**: Priya threatens Rohit: "Transfer your property to me or I will file a false rape case against you (punishable with life imprisonment)." Rohit, fearing the accusation and social stigma, transfers the property.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Intentionally putting in fear: Threat of serious criminal accusation
2. ✅ Fear of accusation of offence punishable with life: Rape is punishable with life imprisonment
3. ✅ Fear of injury to reputation: False accusation would destroy reputation
4. ✅ Dishonestly inducing delivery: Priya has no right to property
5. ✅ Delivery: Rohit transfers property

**Offence**: Extortion by Threat of Accusation under Section 388 IPC / Section 313 BNS (aggravated form)
**Punishment**: Up to 10 years imprisonment AND fine (cognizable, non-bailable)

**Additional Charges**: Criminal intimidation (Section 506), defamation (if accusation is published), falsely charging with intent to injure (Section 211)

### Example 5: Attempt to Commit Extortion (No Delivery)
**Scenario**: Ajay sends a threatening email to a company demanding Rs. 1 crore, threatening to leak confidential data. The company refuses and reports to police. No money is paid.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Intentionally putting in fear: Threat to leak confidential data
2. ✅ Fear of injury: Reputational and business injury
3. ❌ No delivery: Company refused to pay

**Offence**: Putting Person in Fear to Commit Extortion (Attempt) under Section 385 IPC / Section 310 BNS
**Punishment**: Up to 2 years imprisonment or fine or both

**Additional Charges**: IT Act violations (unauthorized access, data theft), criminal intimidation

### Example 6: Not Extortion (Lack of Dishonest Intention)
**Scenario**: Mohan genuinely believes that Suresh owes him Rs. 1 lakh for a business transaction. Mohan tells Suresh, "Pay me my Rs. 1 lakh or I will file a civil suit against you." Suresh pays.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Putting in fear: Threat of civil litigation
2. ✅ Delivery: Suresh pays
3. ❌ No dishonest intention: If Mohan has a bona fide belief that the money is owed to him, and he is merely asserting his legal right through lawful means (civil suit), there is no dishonest intention

**Offence**: Likely no offence (bona fide claim of right, lawful means)

**Caution**: However, if Mohan threatened criminal prosecution or violence (unlawful means) to recover even a legitimate debt, it could still be extortion or criminal intimidation.

**Principle**: Dishonest intention is essential. Bona fide assertion of legal rights through lawful means is not extortion.

### Example 7: Extortion of Immovable Property
**Scenario**: A threatens to burn down B's factory unless B transfers ownership of a plot of land to A. B, fearing destruction of his business, executes a sale deed transferring the land to A.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Intentionally putting in fear: Threat to destroy factory (property injury)
2. ✅ Dishonestly inducing delivery: A has no right to the land
3. ✅ Delivery of immovable property: B transfers land ownership
4. ✅ Note: Extortion applies to immovable property (unlike theft which applies only to movable property)

**Offence**: Extortion under Section 384 IPC / Section 309 BNS
**Punishment**: Up to 3 years imprisonment or fine or both

**Additional Charges**: Criminal intimidation (Section 506)

**Civil Remedy**: B can also file a civil suit to set aside the sale deed on grounds of coercion (Section 15, Indian Contract Act).

---

## Defenses to Extortion

### 1. **Absence of Dishonest Intention**
- If the accused had a bona fide claim of right to the property (e.g., recovering a legitimate debt), dishonest intention may be absent
- The means used must also be lawful; threatening violence or false accusations is still illegal even for debt recovery

**Example**: If A genuinely believes B owes him money and threatens to file a civil suit (lawful means), it may not be extortion.

### 2. **No Fear Induced**
- If the victim was not actually put in fear, or the so-called threat was not perceived as a threat, no extortion
- The fear must be subjectively felt by the victim and objectively reasonable

**Example**: If A jokingly says to B, "Give me Rs. 100 or I'll complain to your wife," and B laughs and refuses, no fear was induced.

### 3. **Voluntary Payment (No Causal Link)**
- If the victim paid voluntarily for reasons other than the threat, no extortion
- The delivery must be causally linked to the fear

**Example**: If A threatens B, but B pays because he genuinely owes the money (not due to the threat), no extortion.

### 4. **Lawful Threat**
- If the threat is of lawful action (e.g., "Pay your debt or I will sue you in court"), it may not constitute extortion if the claim is bona fide
- However, threatening criminal prosecution to recover civil debt is generally not lawful

**Example**: A tells B, "Pay the rent you owe or I will file an eviction suit." If the rent is genuinely due, this is a lawful threat, not extortion.

### 5. **No Delivery**
- If no property was delivered, the offence is attempt to extort (Sections 385/387/389), not completed extortion (Section 384)
- Defense may argue for lesser charge

### 6. **Entrapment**
- If the alleged extortion was induced by police or undercover agents (entrapment), it may be a defense, though Indian law has limited recognition of entrapment

---

## Procedural Aspects

### Cognizance and Complaint
- **Simple Extortion (Section 384)**: Non-cognizable, Bailable, Triable by Any Magistrate
  - Police cannot arrest without warrant
  - Complaint to magistrate or police with warrant required
- **Aggravated Extortion (Sections 386, 388)**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class
  - Police can arrest without warrant
  - Bail requires court approval

### Investigation
- For cognizable offences: FIR registration, arrest without warrant, investigation
- For non-cognizable offences: Complaint to magistrate, magistrate may direct investigation
- Evidence: threatening letters/emails/messages, witness testimony, evidence of delivery (bank transfers, property documents)

### Trial
- Burden of proof on prosecution to establish all ingredients: intentional fear, dishonest intention, delivery induced by fear
- Defense may challenge dishonest intention, claim of right, or causal link

### Compounding
- **Simple Extortion (Section 384)**: Compoundable with permission of court (Section 320 CrPC)
- **Aggravated Extortion (Sections 386, 388)**: Non-compoundable

---

## Compliance Checklist

### For Potential Victims (Preventive Measures)
- [ ] Do not yield to threats; report immediately to police
- [ ] Preserve evidence: threatening letters, emails, messages, call recordings (where lawful)
- [ ] Document all communications and demands
- [ ] Seek legal advice before making any payments or transfers
- [ ] File FIR or complaint promptly (for aggravated extortion, file FIR)
- [ ] Consider civil remedies: set aside contracts obtained by coercion (Section 15, Contract Act)

### For Accused/Suspect (Legal Safeguards)
- [ ] Claim defenses: bona fide claim of right, no dishonest intention, lawful threat
- [ ] Demonstrate that any payment was voluntary (not induced by threat)
- [ ] Show lawful means were used (e.g., civil suit, not threats)
- [ ] Seek bail (for simple extortion, bail is available as of right; for aggravated, apply to court)
- [ ] Engage competent defense counsel
- [ ] Explore compounding (for simple extortion only)

### For Investigating Officer
- [ ] Register FIR or complaint
- [ ] Preserve evidence: threatening communications, evidence of delivery
- [ ] Record statements of victim and witnesses
- [ ] Prove all ingredients: intentional fear, dishonest intention, delivery
- [ ] Differentiate from theft, robbery, or criminal intimidation
- [ ] File charge sheet with appropriate section (384, 386, or 388 based on facts)

### For Prosecutors
- [ ] Frame appropriate charge: Section 384, 386, or 388 based on type of threat
- [ ] Prove dishonest intention (critical element)
- [ ] Prove causal link between threat and delivery
- [ ] Establish fear was induced (victim's testimony)
- [ ] Anticipate defenses (claim of right, voluntary payment)

### For Defense Counsel
- [ ] Challenge dishonest intention: establish bona fide claim of right
- [ ] Challenge causal link: payment was voluntary, not due to threat
- [ ] Argue lawful means: threat of lawful action (civil suit) is not extortion
- [ ] Challenge delivery: if no property delivered, reduce to attempt charge
- [ ] Seek acquittal if prosecution fails to prove all ingredients beyond reasonable doubt
- [ ] If conviction likely, argue for leniency: first-time offender, return of property, compromise

---

## Cross-References

### Related Criminal Offences
- **Theft (IPC 378-382)**: Taking without consent (no threat element)
- **Robbery (IPC 390-402)**: Theft/extortion with instant violence or fear
- **Criminal Intimidation (IPC 503-506)**: Threat to cause injury (may or may not involve property)
- **Cheating (IPC 415-420)**: Obtaining property by deception (not threat)
- **Criminal Breach of Trust (IPC 405-409)**: Dishonest misappropriation of entrusted property
- **Abduction (IPC 362-369)**: May be used in conjunction with extortion (kidnapping for ransom)

### Constitutional Provisions
- **Article 21**: Right to Life and Personal Liberty (protection from threats and coercion)
- **Article 300A**: Right to Property (protection from unlawful deprivation)

### Contract Law
- **Section 15, Indian Contract Act, 1872**: Coercion - A contract induced by coercion is voidable at the option of the party whose consent was coerced
- Victim can seek to set aside any contract or transfer obtained by extortion

### Procedural Laws
- **CrPC / BNSS**:
  - Section 154: FIR registration (for cognizable offences)
  - Section 155: Non-cognizable offences (for simple extortion)
  - Section 320: Compounding (simple extortion is compoundable with court permission)
  - Section 437: Bail for bailable offences (simple extortion)

### Evidence Law
- **Indian Evidence Act / BSA**:
  - Section 8: Motive, preparation, and conduct (relevant for proving dishonest intention)
  - Electronic evidence: Emails, messages, call recordings (subject to IT Act compliance)

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Consent Under Fear**: Extortion involves obtaining property with the victim's consent, but that consent is procured by fear of injury

2. **Broad Scope of Injury**: Injury includes physical harm, reputational harm (defamation), and property harm

3. **Future or Immediate Threat**: Unlike robbery (which requires instant fear), extortion can involve threats of future harm

4. **Offender Need Not Be Present**: Extortion can be committed remotely (letters, emails, phone calls), unlike robbery which requires presence

5. **Immovable Property**: Extortion applies to both movable and immovable property, unlike theft (movable only)

6. **Aggravated Forms**: Higher punishment (up to 10 years) for extortion involving death/grievous hurt threats or threats of serious criminal accusations

7. **Dishonest Intention Essential**: Bona fide claim of right (even if asserted forcefully) may negate dishonest intention, but using unlawful means (threats) is still illegal

8. **Attempt Criminalized**: Even if property is not delivered, putting in fear to commit extortion is punishable (Sections 385, 387, 389)

---

## Conclusion

Extortion is a serious property offence that violates both property rights and personal liberty (freedom from fear and coercion). The law distinguishes between simple extortion (threats of injury) and aggravated forms (threats of death, grievous hurt, or serious criminal accusations), with correspondingly higher punishments for the latter.

Understanding the distinction between extortion, robbery, theft, and criminal intimidation is critical for proper charge framing and legal remedies. While extortion involves obtaining property through threats, robbery involves instant violence, theft involves taking without consent, and criminal intimidation may involve threats without property transfer.

With the transition to BNS 2023, the substantive law on extortion remains unchanged, ensuring continuity in legal practice and precedent.

---

**Document Version**: 1.0
**Effective Date**: IPC provisions apply until June 30, 2024; BNS provisions effective from July 1, 2024
**Last Updated**: December 6, 2025
**Prepared by**: Legal Protocol Documentation System
**Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)

---

**Disclaimer**: This protocol is for informational and educational purposes only and does not constitute legal advice. For specific legal matters, consult a qualified criminal law advocate or legal expert.

## IL-CRIM-FIR.md
---

# India Criminal Law: First Information Report (FIR) (Pratham Soochna Riport)

## Protocol ID
**IL-CRIM-FIR**

## Domain
Criminal Law > Criminal Procedure

## Jurisdiction
India (All States and Union Territories)

## Statutory Framework

### Code of Criminal Procedure (CrPC), 1973
- **Section 154**: Information in Cognizable Cases (FIR)
- **Section 155**: Information as to Non-Cognizable Cases
- **Section 156**: Police Officer's Power to Investigate Cognizable Case
- **Section 157**: Procedure for Investigation
- **Section 173**: Report of Police Officer on Completion of Investigation

### Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita (BNSS), 2023 (Effective July 1, 2024)
- **Section 173**: Information in Cognizable Cases (formerly CrPC 154)
- **Section 174**: Information as to Non-Cognizable Cases (formerly CrPC 155)
- **Section 175**: Police Officer's Power to Investigate (formerly CrPC 156)
- **Section 176**: Procedure for Investigation (formerly CrPC 157)
- **Section 193**: Investigation Report (formerly CrPC 173)

---

## Overview

**First Information Report (FIR)** is the written document prepared by police when they receive information about the commission of a **cognizable offence** (serious crime for which police can arrest without warrant).

The FIR is the **foundation of criminal justice system** - it sets in motion the machinery of investigation, arrest, and prosecution. It is the first step taken by law enforcement upon receiving information about a crime.

### Key Characteristics
1. **"First" Information**: Must be the first information received by police about the offence
2. **Cognizable Offences Only**: FIR is registered only for cognizable offences (non-cognizable offences have different procedure)
3. **Mandatory Registration**: Police must register FIR if information relates to cognizable offence (no discretion to refuse)
4. **Written Document**: Reduced to writing and signed by informant
5. **Copy to Informant**: Free copy given to informant immediately
6. **Sets Investigation in Motion**: Once FIR registered, investigation begins

---

## Definition and Legal Basis (Section 154 CrPC / Section 173 BNSS)

### Section 154 CrPC: Information in Cognizable Cases

**Every information relating to the commission of a cognizable offence, if given orally to an officer in charge of a police station, shall be reduced to writing by him or under his direction, and be read over to the informant; and every such information, whether given in writing or reduced to writing as aforesaid, shall be signed by the person giving it, and the substance thereof shall be entered in a book to be kept by such officer in such form as the State Government may prescribe in this behalf.**

### Essential Elements of FIR

#### 1. **Information**
- Any knowledge or intelligence about commission of a cognizable offence
- Can be given by: victim, witness, or any person with knowledge
- Can be oral or written
- Can be given in person, by phone, email, or through online portals

**Who Can Give Information?**
- **Victim** (most common)
- **Witness** (person who saw the crime)
- **Informant** (any person with knowledge, even if not victim/witness)
- **Anonymous** (though police may verify before registering)

**Example**: A is assaulted by B. A goes to police station and narrates the incident. This is "information."

#### 2. **Relating to Commission of Cognizable Offence**

**Cognizable Offence** (Section 2(c) CrPC / BNSS): Offence for which police can arrest **without warrant** and start investigation **without magistrate's permission**.

**Examples of Cognizable Offences**:
- Murder (IPC 302)
- Rape (IPC 376)
- Kidnapping (IPC 363)
- Robbery (IPC 392)
- Dacoity (IPC 395)
- Cheating (IPC 420)
- Forgery (IPC 467-469 for certain types)
- Mischief causing damage Rs. 50+ (IPC 427)

**Non-Cognizable Offences** (require different procedure under Section 155):
- Simple hurt (IPC 323)
- Defamation (IPC 500)
- Simple assault (IPC 352)
- Simple mischief (IPC 426)

**How to Check?**: First Schedule to CrPC/BNSS lists all offences and specifies whether cognizable or non-cognizable.

#### 3. **Officer in Charge of Police Station**

FIR must be given to:
- **Station House Officer (SHO)** / Officer In-Charge (OIC) of police station, OR
- Any officer **authorized by State Government** to receive FIR

**Territorial Jurisdiction**: FIR should be lodged at police station having jurisdiction over the place where the crime occurred.

**Exception**: If crime occurred in multiple jurisdictions, FIR can be lodged at any such police station. Also, in cases of urgency or if victim is unable to reach the jurisdictional police station, FIR can be lodged elsewhere (police will transfer to appropriate jurisdiction).

#### 4. **Reduced to Writing**

- If information is given **orally**, police officer must **reduce it to writing**
- If given in **writing**, accepted as is
- Information must be **read over to informant** to ensure accuracy
- Informant must **sign** the FIR

**Language**: FIR can be given in any language. If informant doesn't know English, FIR can be in vernacular language. Translation may be provided if needed.

#### 5. **Entry in Daily Diary / Station Diary**

- **Substance (gist) of information** must be entered in **Station Diary / General Diary (GD Entry)**
- Diary maintained as per prescribed format

#### 6. **Copy to Informant**

**Section 154(2)**: A **copy of the information as recorded** shall be given **forthwith, free of cost**, to the informant.

**Importance**: Copy serves as proof that FIR was registered. Informant can use it to ensure police action is taken.

---

## Mandatory Registration of FIR (Supreme Court Directions)

### Lalita Kumari v. Govt. of U.P. (2014) - Landmark Judgment
**AIR 2014 SC 187**

**Held**: **Registration of FIR is mandatory under Section 154 CrPC if the information discloses commission of a cognizable offence.** No preliminary inquiry is permissible in such cases.

**Key Principles**:

1. **Mandatory Registration**: If information relates to cognizable offence, police **must** register FIR immediately. No discretion.

2. **No Preliminary Inquiry**: Police cannot refuse to register FIR on grounds that they want to conduct "preliminary inquiry" or "verification."

3. **Exceptions** (where preliminary inquiry allowed before FIR):
   - **Matrimonial disputes / family disputes**: To verify if offence actually occurred
   - **Commercial / civil disputes**: Where criminal complaint is merely to pressurize other party
   - **Medical negligence**: Requires expert opinion
   - **Corruption by public servants**: Requires verification
   - **Cases under Section 498A (cruelty by husband/in-laws)**: Verification may be done to prevent misuse

4. **Preliminary Inquiry Time-Bound**: If preliminary inquiry is conducted (in exceptions), must be completed within **7 days**. If cognizable offence is made out, FIR must be registered.

5. **No Endorsement Technique**: Police cannot merely record information and "endorse" to another station or department without registering FIR.

6. **Accountability**: If FIR is not registered without lawful reason, the police officer can be prosecuted under Section 166A IPC (Public Servant disobeying law to cause injury - up to 1 year imprisonment).

**Impact**: Landmark judgment curbing police tendency to refuse FIR registration. Informants can cite *Lalita Kumari* if police refuse to register FIR.

---

## Procedure for Registering FIR (Step-by-Step)

### Step 1: Informant Approaches Police Station

- Victim / witness / informant goes to police station (or calls, emails, uses online portal)
- Narrates incident to officer in charge

### Step 2: Information Recorded in Writing

**If Oral**:
- Officer writes down information (in own words or informant's words)
- Reads it back to informant to verify accuracy

**If Written**:
- Informant provides written complaint
- Officer accepts and attaches to FIR

### Step 3: Informant Signs FIR

- Informant signs the FIR after confirming contents are accurate

### Step 4: FIR Number Assigned

- Each FIR is assigned a **unique FIR number** (consists of: number/year/police station code)

**Example**: FIR No. 234/2025 at Kotwali Police Station

### Step 5: Entry in Station Diary / GD

- Substance of FIR entered in General Diary (maintained in chronological order)

### Step 6: Copy Given to Informant

- **Free copy** of FIR given to informant immediately (mandatory)

### Step 7: Information Sent to Magistrate

- Copy of FIR sent to **Magistrate** having jurisdiction (Section 157 CrPC)

### Step 8: Investigation Begins

- Police officer proceeds to investigate under Section 156 CrPC (for cognizable offences)

---

## Contents of FIR

An FIR typically contains:

1. **Date and Time** of registration
2. **Place of Occurrence** (where crime occurred)
3. **Name and Address of Informant**
4. **Name and Address of Accused** (if known; otherwise "unknown persons")
5. **Description of Offence**: Narrative of what happened
6. **Details of Stolen/Damaged Property** (if applicable)
7. **Names of Witnesses** (if any)
8. **Sections of Law** under which offence falls (IPC sections, other laws)
9. **Signature of Informant**
10. **Signature/Seal of Police Officer** who recorded FIR

**FIR Number and Date** are crucial for tracking the case.

---

## Zero FIR (Anywhere in India)

### Concept

**Zero FIR**: FIR that can be registered at **any police station**, regardless of jurisdiction. It is later transferred to the police station having territorial jurisdiction.

**Why "Zero"?**: Initially, it is given a temporary number "0" (zero). After transfer, the receiving police station assigns a regular FIR number.

**When Used?**:
- **Emergency situations**: Victim is unable to reach the jurisdictional police station (injured, urgent help needed)
- **Crime occurred while traveling**: Victim doesn't know exact jurisdiction
- **Convenience**: Allows victim to lodge complaint at nearest police station

**Example**: A woman is raped in Delhi while traveling from Mumbai. She lodges Zero FIR at Mumbai police station. Mumbai police register it as Zero FIR and transfer to Delhi police (where crime occurred). Delhi police assign regular FIR number and investigate.

**Legal Basis**: Not explicitly mentioned in CrPC, but recognized by Supreme Court and police practice.

---

## Online FIR / e-FIR

Many states have introduced **online FIR** filing for certain categories of offences:

**Offences Covered** (varies by state):
- Theft of mobile phone, vehicle, documents
- Loss of property
- Motor vehicle accidents (non-injury cases)

**Procedure**:
1. Victim visits state police website or mobile app
2. Fills online form with details of offence
3. Submits online
4. FIR number generated
5. Victim may be called to police station later for verification / investigation

**Benefits**: Convenience, 24/7 availability, reduces rush at police stations

**Limitations**: Not available for all offences (serious crimes still require physical presence)

---

## Refusal to Register FIR: Remedies

### What if Police Refuse to Register FIR?

Despite *Lalita Kumari* judgment, police may still refuse to register FIR. **Remedies**:

#### 1. **Request in Writing**

- Give written complaint to police
- Take acknowledgment (diary entry number/receipt)
- If FIR not registered, written complaint serves as evidence of police refusal

#### 2. **Approach Senior Officer**

- Complaint to **Superintendent of Police (SP)** / **Deputy Commissioner of Police (DCP)**
- SP/DCP can direct subordinate police station to register FIR

#### 3. **File Application under Section 156(3) CrPC / Section 175(3) BNSS**

**Section 156(3)**: **Magistrate** can order police to register FIR and investigate.

**Procedure**:
- File application before Magistrate (Chief Judicial Magistrate or any Magistrate)
- Magistrate examines application
- If satisfied that information discloses cognizable offence, magistrate directs police to register FIR and investigate

**Advantage**: Magistrate's order is binding on police. Failure to comply can lead to contempt.

**Timeline**: Magistrate should dispose application expeditiously (typically within a few weeks).

#### 4. **File Writ Petition in High Court (Article 226)**

- Approach High Court with writ petition
- High Court can direct police to register FIR
- Used when lower remedies fail or in urgent/sensitive cases

**Example**: *Arnab Goswami v. State of Maharashtra* (2020) - Supreme Court intervened when Maharashtra police allegedly refused to transfer FIR to different jurisdiction (jurisdiction dispute).

#### 5. **Complaint to State Human Rights Commission / National Human Rights Commission**

- File complaint alleging human rights violation (right to justice)
- Commission can recommend action against erring officers

#### 6. **Private Complaint before Magistrate (Section 190 CrPC / Section 210 BNSS)**

- If all else fails, victim can directly approach Magistrate with **private complaint** (bypassing police)
- Magistrate can take cognizance and issue process (summons/warrant) to accused
- Investigation may be ordered, or trial may proceed directly

**Difference**:
- **FIR route**: Police investigate first, then file charge sheet
- **Private complaint route**: Magistrate takes cognizance; accused summoned directly; no police investigation initially (though magistrate can order investigation)

---

## FIR vs. Complaint

| **Aspect** | **FIR** | **Complaint** |
|------------|---------|---------------|
| **Legal Basis** | Section 154 CrPC (cognizable offences) | Section 190 CrPC (any offence, filed before Magistrate) |
| **Filed With** | Police Station | Magistrate |
| **Offences** | Cognizable offences only | Any offence (cognizable or non-cognizable) |
| **Investigation** | Police investigate automatically | Magistrate may order investigation or proceed directly |
| **Copy to Informant** | Mandatory free copy | No such provision |
| **Who Files** | Victim / witness / any informant | Victim / complainant |

**Both can lead to prosecution**, but FIR is more common for serious crimes as police investigation happens automatically.

---

## Importance of FIR

### 1. **Starting Point of Criminal Justice**
- FIR triggers investigation, arrest, and prosecution machinery

### 2. **Evidence**
- FIR can be used as **evidence** in court (though not substantive evidence of facts stated, but relevant for:
  - **Corroboration**: If informant's testimony in court matches FIR, it strengthens prosecution
  - **Contradiction**: If informant's court testimony differs from FIR, defense can use FIR to impeach credibility

### 3. **Protection from False Implication**

- If accused is named in FIR from the beginning, it is **stronger evidence** against him
- If accused's name is added later (during investigation), it raises suspicion of afterthought / false implication

**Example**: FIR names A, B, C as accused. Later, during investigation, police add D's name. D can argue he was falsely implicated later (FIR didn't name him initially).

### 4. **Basis for Bail**

- Bail applications often refer to FIR (nature of accusation, sections charged)

### 5. **Time Stamp**

- FIR establishes when information was first received by police
- Delay in lodging FIR can weaken prosecution case (defense may argue: if crime was serious, why delay in reporting?)

**Example**: A alleges assault by B on January 1, but FIR is lodged on January 15. Defense can question 15-day delay.

**However**: Delay alone doesn't discredit case if satisfactorily explained (victim was hospitalized, threatened, etc.)

---

## Quashing of FIR (Section 482 CrPC / Section 528 BNSS)

### When Can FIR Be Quashed?

**High Court** has inherent power under **Section 482 CrPC** to quash FIR if:

1. **No cognizable offence is made out**: Even if allegations in FIR are taken as true, no offence is committed

**Example**: FIR alleges civil dispute (property disagreement) without any criminal element. High Court can quash.

2. **Abuse of process of court**: FIR filed with malicious intent to harass accused

**Example**: False FIR filed by business rival to harass competitor.

3. **Settlement between parties** (in compoundable offences)

**Example**: Parties to a cheating case settle dispute amicably. High Court may quash FIR if offence is compoundable.

4. **Continuation would be abuse of justice**

**Example**: FIR based on civil contract dispute; no criminal intent; allowing it to continue would abuse justice.

**Limitations**:
- High Court exercises this power **sparingly**
- If there are disputed questions of fact, High Court won't quash (trial should decide)
- In serious offences (murder, rape, dacoity), quashing is rare (even if parties settle)

---

## Landmark Cases on FIR

### 1. **Lalita Kumari v. Govt. of U.P. (2014)** - Mandatory FIR Registration
**AIR 2014 SC 187**

**Held**: FIR registration is **mandatory** if information discloses cognizable offence. Police have no discretion. Preliminary inquiry allowed only in exceptions (matrimonial disputes, commercial disputes, medical negligence, corruption).

**Impact**: Strengthened citizens' rights; curbed police tendency to refuse FIR.

### 2. **State of Haryana v. Bhajan Lal (1992)** - Quashing of FIR
**AIR 1992 SC 604**

**Held**: High Court can quash FIR under Section 482 in following situations:
- FIR doesn't disclose cognizable offence
- Allegations are absurd/inherently improbable
- Criminal proceeding is abuse of process
- FIR is to satisfy personal vendetta

**Guidelines**: Court laid down guidelines for exercise of inherent powers to quash FIR/criminal proceedings.

### 3. **Arnesh Kumar v. State of Bihar (2014)** - Arrest Guidelines
**AIR 2014 SC 2756**

**Held**: Police should not arrest mechanically in all cognizable offences. Arrest only if:
- Necessary to prevent further offence
- Necessary for proper investigation
- Accused may flee
- Accused may tamper with evidence/witnesses

**Relation to FIR**: Even if FIR is registered for cognizable offence, arrest is not automatic. Police must justify need for arrest.

**Impact**: Curbed arbitrary arrests based on FIR.

### 4. **T.T. Antony v. State of Kerala (2001)** - Delayed FIR
**AIR 2001 SC 2637**

**Held**: Delay in lodging FIR can affect credibility of prosecution case, but delay alone doesn't discredit case if **satisfactorily explained** (victim was injured, threatened, etc.).

**Principle**: Courts examine reasons for delay; each case decided on facts.

---

## CrPC to BNSS Transition Mapping

| **CrPC Section** | **BNSS Section** | **Provision** | **Changes** |
|------------------|------------------|---------------|-------------|
| **154** | **173** | Information in Cognizable Cases (FIR) | No substantive change; e-FIR provisions may be added |
| **155** | **174** | Information in Non-Cognizable Cases | No substantive change |
| **156** | **175** | Power to Investigate Cognizable Case | No substantive change |
| **157** | **176** | Procedure for Investigation | No substantive change |
| **173** | **193** | Police Report on Investigation | No substantive change |

**Note**: BNSS 2023 retains FIR provisions largely unchanged. May introduce e-FIR as standard procedure in all states.

---

## Practical Guidance

### For Victims / Informants

**Before Going to Police Station**:
- [ ] Gather facts: Date, time, place, what happened, who was involved
- [ ] Identify witnesses (if any)
- [ ] Collect evidence: Photos, videos, documents, medical reports (if injured)
- [ ] Note down names/badge numbers of accused (if known)

**At Police Station**:
- [ ] Narrate incident clearly and chronologically
- [ ] Ensure all important facts are recorded in FIR
- [ ] Read FIR carefully before signing (verify accuracy)
- [ ] Collect **free copy** of FIR (mandatory - Section 154(2))
- [ ] Note down **FIR number** (crucial for tracking)

**If Police Refuse to Register FIR**:
- [ ] Give written complaint; take acknowledgment
- [ ] Approach SP/DCP
- [ ] File application under Section 156(3) before Magistrate
- [ ] If urgent, approach High Court (writ petition)

### For Accused

**If Named in FIR**:
- [ ] Obtain copy of FIR (through advocate or from police)
- [ ] Analyze sections charged and potential punishment
- [ ] Engage criminal defense lawyer immediately
- [ ] Assess bail prospects (bailable vs. non-bailable offence)
- [ ] Prepare defense strategy
- [ ] If FIR is false/mala fide, consider quashing petition in High Court (Section 482)

**Do Not**:
- ❌ Approach complainant or witnesses to settle/threaten (can lead to additional charges)
- ❌ Destroy evidence (Section 201 IPC - destruction of evidence)
- ❌ Abscond (arrest warrant will be issued; bail becomes difficult)

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **FIR is Mandatory**: If information discloses cognizable offence, police must register FIR (*Lalita Kumari*). No discretion.

2. **Free Copy to Informant**: Informant entitled to free copy of FIR immediately (Section 154(2)).

3. **Zero FIR**: Can be lodged at any police station in emergency; later transferred to jurisdictional police.

4. **Remedies for Refusal**: Section 156(3) application to Magistrate, complaint to SP, writ petition in High Court.

5. **FIR is Evidence**: Can be used for corroboration or contradiction of informant's testimony; establishes timeline.

6. **Delayed FIR**: Delay weakens prosecution if not explained, but not fatal if satisfactory reason exists.

7. **Quashing**: High Court can quash FIR if no cognizable offence, abuse of process, or settlement (Section 482).

8. **No Automatic Arrest**: Even if FIR registered, arrest not automatic (*Arnesh Kumar*); police must justify need.

---

## Conclusion

The First Information Report (FIR) is the cornerstone of criminal justice in India. It is the citizen's first interface with law enforcement and the official trigger for investigation and prosecution machinery.

The landmark *Lalita Kumari* judgment (2014) strengthened citizens' rights by making FIR registration mandatory, curbing decades of police discretion and refusals. However, challenges persist: reluctance to register FIRs (especially against influential persons), delayed registration, improper recording of facts.

For victims, understanding the FIR process—knowing it's their legal right, obtaining a free copy, and using remedies if refused—is crucial. For the accused, the FIR is often the first indication of criminal proceedings and requires immediate legal response.

With the transition to BNSS 2023, the substantive law on FIR remains unchanged, but technological advancements (e-FIR, online complaints) are making the system more accessible and accountable.

---

**Document Version**: 1.0
**Effective Date**: CrPC provisions apply until June 30, 2024; BNSS provisions effective from July 1, 2024
**Last Updated**: December 6, 2025
**Prepared by**: Legal Protocol Documentation System
**Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)

---

**Disclaimer**: This protocol is for informational and educational purposes only and does not constitute legal advice. For specific matters involving FIR or criminal proceedings, consult a qualified criminal defense advocate.


---
## IL-CRIM-FORGERY.md
---

# India Criminal Law: Forgery (Jaalsaazi / Kooti Rachna)

## Protocol ID
**IL-CRIM-FORGERY**

## Domain
Criminal Law > Economic Offences

## Jurisdiction
India (All States and Union Territories)

## Statutory Framework

### Indian Penal Code (IPC), 1860
- **Section 463**: Forgery (Definition)
- **Section 464**: Making a False Document
- **Section 465**: Punishment for Forgery
- **Section 466**: Forgery of Record of Court or Public Register
- **Section 467**: Forgery of Valuable Security, Will, or Authority to Make or Transfer Property
- **Section 468**: Forgery for Purpose of Cheating
- **Section 469**: Forgery for Purpose of Harming Reputation
- **Section 470**: Forged Document
- **Section 471**: Using as Genuine a Forged Document
- **Section 472**: Making or Possessing Counterfeit Seal, etc., with Intent to Commit Forgery
- **Section 473**: Making or Possessing Counterfeit Seal, etc., with Intent to Commit Forgery of Valuable Security
- **Section 474**: Having Possession of Document Described in Section 466 or 467, Knowing it to be Forged
- **Section 475**: Counterfeiting Device or Mark Used for Authenticating Documents
- **Section 476**: Counterfeiting Device or Mark Used for Authenticating Documents (Valuable Security)
- **Section 477**: Fraudulent Cancellation, Destruction, etc., of Will, Authority to Adopt, or Valuable Security
- **Section 477A**: Falsification of Accounts

### Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS), 2023 (Effective July 1, 2024)
- **Section 336**: Forgery (Definition) (formerly IPC 463)
- **Section 337**: Making False Document (formerly IPC 464)
- **Section 338**: Punishment for Forgery (formerly IPC 465)
- **Section 339**: Forgery of Court Record (formerly IPC 466)
- **Section 340**: Forgery of Valuable Security/Will (formerly IPC 467)
- **Section 341**: Forgery for Cheating (formerly IPC 468)
- **Section 342**: Forgery for Harming Reputation (formerly IPC 469)
- **Section 343**: Forged Document (formerly IPC 470)
- **Section 344**: Using Forged Document as Genuine (formerly IPC 471)
- **Section 345**: Making/Possessing Counterfeit Seal (formerly IPC 472)
- **Section 346**: Counterfeit Seal for Valuable Security (formerly IPC 473)
- **Section 347**: Possessing Forged Document (Sections 339-340) (formerly IPC 474)
- **Section 348**: Counterfeiting Device/Mark (formerly IPC 475)
- **Section 349**: Counterfeiting Device for Valuable Security (formerly IPC 476)
- **Section 350**: Fraudulent Cancellation of Will/Valuable Security (formerly IPC 477)
- **Section 351**: Falsification of Accounts (formerly IPC 477A)

---

## Overview

**Forgery** is the making of a false document or electronic record with the intent to cause damage or injury to the public or any person, or to support any claim or title, or to cause any person to part with property.

Forgery strikes at the reliability and authenticity of documents, which are essential for commerce, administration of justice, property rights, and trust in written records. It is both a property offence and an offence against public justice.

### Key Characteristics
1. **False Document**: Making a document or electronic record that purports to be genuine but is not
2. **Intent to Defraud or Injure**: Specific intention to cause damage, support false claim, or induce delivery of property
3. **Document-Centric**: Offence relates to documents (paper or electronic records)
4. **Preparatory Offence**: Even mere making of false document (without using it) is punishable
5. **Wide Application**: Applies to all types of documents (wills, contracts, court records, securities, accounts, etc.)

---

## Definition of Forgery (IPC Section 463 / BNS Section 336)

### Full Legal Definition

**IPC Section 463**: Whoever makes any **false document or false electronic record** or **part of a document or electronic record**, with **intent to cause damage or injury** to the public or to any person, or to support any claim or title, or to cause any person to part with property, or to enter into any express or implied contract, or with intent to commit fraud or that fraud may be committed, commits **forgery**.

### Essential Ingredients of Forgery

#### 1. **Making a False Document or Electronic Record**

**False Document** is defined in **Section 464**. A person makes a false document if he:

**A. Dishonestly or Fraudulently Makes, Signs, Seals, or Executes a Document**
- Creates a document that appears to be genuine but is not
- Makes it with dishonest or fraudulent intention

**B. Makes Any Material Alteration in a Document**
- Changes a genuine document in a material (significant) way
- The alteration must affect the legal effect or meaning of the document

**Examples of Making False Document**:

1. **Creating Entirely Fake Document**
   - A creates a fake land sale deed claiming B sold property to A

2. **Forging Signature**
   - A signs B's name on a cheque without B's authorization

3. **Altering Existing Document**
   - A changes the amount on a genuine cheque from Rs. 5,000 to Rs. 50,000

4. **Backdating or Misdating Document**
   - A creates a contract dated one year ago to give false impression of priority

5. **Forging Seal or Stamp**
   - A creates a fake court seal and affixes it to a document

6. **Electronic Record Forgery**
   - A creates a fake digital signature or alters an electronic document

**Material Alteration**: The alteration must be significant enough to affect the document's legal meaning or effect. Mere corrections or immaterial changes are not forgery.

**Example (Material)**: Changing beneficiary name in a will is material alteration (affects inheritance).

**Example (Immaterial)**: Correcting a typo in a non-essential clause is not material forgery.

#### 2. **Intent to Cause Damage or Injury**

The accused must have one of the following intentions:

**A. Intent to Cause Damage or Injury to Public or Any Person**
- Intention to harm public interest or injure any individual

**Example**: A forges a court order to illegally confine B, causing injury to B's liberty.

**B. Intent to Support Any Claim or Title**
- Intention to use the forged document to assert a false claim or right

**Example**: A forges a will to claim he is the rightful heir to property.

**C. Intent to Cause Any Person to Part with Property**
- Intention to induce someone to transfer property based on the forged document

**Example**: A forges B's signature on a sale deed to fraudulently acquire B's land.

**D. Intent to Cause Any Person to Enter into Contract**
- Intention to induce someone to enter into a contract (express or implied) based on the forged document

**Example**: A forges a reference letter from a reputable company to get a job contract.

**E. Intent to Commit Fraud or That Fraud May Be Committed**
- General fraudulent intention

**Example**: A forges a university degree certificate to commit fraud in job applications.

**Important Principle**: The **intent** at the time of making the false document is crucial. If the document is made with innocent intention but later used fraudulently, the forgery occurs at the time of making (if fraudulent intent existed then) or using (if intent arose later).

#### 3. **Document or Electronic Record**

**Document** (Section 29 IEA): Any matter expressed or described on any substance by means of letters, figures, marks, or symbols, or by more than one of those means, intended to be used for recording that matter.

**Electronic Record** (IT Act, 2000): Data generated, image or sound stored, received or sent in an electronic form, or micro film or computer-generated micro fiche.

**Types of Documents Subject to Forgery**:
- **Wills and testaments**
- **Deeds and conveyances** (sale deeds, lease agreements)
- **Contracts and agreements**
- **Valuable securities** (promissory notes, cheques, share certificates)
- **Court records and judgments**
- **Government records** (birth certificates, passports, licenses)
- **Educational certificates**
- **Invoices, bills, and accounts**
- **Electronic records** (emails, digital signatures, electronic contracts)

---

## What Constitutes a "False Document" (Section 464 / BNS Section 337)

### Section 464: Making a False Document

A person is said to make a **false document** who:

#### 1. **Dishonestly or Fraudulently Makes, Signs, Seals, or Executes a Document or Electronic Record**

**A. Makes or Executes a Document Purporting to Be Made by Another**
- Creating a document and falsely claiming someone else made it

**Example**: A creates a power of attorney claiming B authorized it, but B never did.

**B. Makes or Executes a Document Purporting to Be Made by Authority of Person Who Did Not Give Authority**
- Creating a document claiming authorization that does not exist

**Example**: A creates a letter of recommendation claiming it's from Professor X, who never authorized it.

**C. Makes or Executes a Document Purporting to Be Made at a Time/Place When/Where It Was Not Made**
- Misdating or misrepresenting the time/place of execution

**Example**: A creates a contract backdated to avoid a legal deadline or tax liability.

**D. Makes or Executes a Document Purporting to Be a Copy of Another Document When No Such Document Exists**
- Creating a fake copy of a non-existent original

**Example**: A creates a "copy" of a will claiming the original was destroyed, but no such will ever existed.

#### 2. **Makes Any Material Alteration in a Document or Electronic Record**

**A. Dishonestly or Fraudulently Alters a Genuine Document**
- Changing any material part of a genuine document

**Example**: A changes the amount on a genuine cheque from Rs. 1,000 to Rs. 10,000.

**B. Adds or Removes Content from a Document**
- Inserting false content or deleting genuine content

**Example**: A adds an extra clause to a signed contract, creating a false term.

**Material vs. Immaterial Alteration**:
- **Material**: Affects legal meaning, rights, or obligations (forgery)
- **Immaterial**: Minor corrections or non-essential changes (not forgery)

**Explanation 1 to Section 464**: A person commits forgery even if the document purports to be made by a **fictitious person** (non-existent person).

**Example**: A creates a recommendation letter from "Dr. John Smith, Harvard University" when no such person exists. This is forgery.

**Explanation 2**: A person commits forgery even if the false document is **not made in the name of any person**, if it is made with fraudulent intent.

**Example**: A creates a fake invoice without any issuer's name but uses it to defraud. This is forgery.

**Explanation 3**: A person commits forgery even if the false document is **not complete** or does not purport to be on oath.

**Example**: A creates a half-completed fake contract. Even if incomplete, it is forgery if made with fraudulent intent.

---

## Forged Document (Section 470 / BNS Section 343)

### Definition

**IPC Section 470**: A **false document made wholly or in part by forgery** is called a **forged document**.

**Principle**: Once a document is created through forgery, it becomes a "forged document" which can then be the subject of other offences (e.g., using forged document under Section 471, possessing forged document under Section 474).

---

## Punishment for Forgery

### Section 465 IPC / Section 338 BNS: Simple Forgery
**Punishment**:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **2 years**, OR
- **Fine**, OR
- **Both**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Bailable, Triable by Any Magistrate

**Applies to**: Forgery not covered by aggravated forms (Sections 466-469).

**Example**: A forges a letter of recommendation to apply for a job. Simple forgery under Section 465.

### Section 466 IPC / Section 339 BNS: Forgery of Record of Court or Public Register
Whoever forges a document purporting to be a **record or proceeding of a court of justice**, or a **register of birth, baptism, marriage, or burial**, or a register kept by a public servant, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Rationale**: Court records and public registers are relied upon by the public and judicial system. Forging such documents undermines trust in the administration of justice and public records.

**Examples**:
- Forging a court judgment or order
- Creating a fake birth certificate
- Altering marriage register entries

### Section 467 IPC / Section 340 BNS: Forgery of Valuable Security, Will, or Authority to Make/Transfer Property
Whoever forges a document which purports to be a **valuable security** or a **will**, or an **authority to adopt a son**, or which purports to give authority to any person to make or transfer any valuable security, or to receive any property, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment for life**, OR
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Court of Session

**Rationale**: Valuable securities (shares, bonds, promissory notes, wills, property transfer authorities) involve high-value property. Forging them can cause massive financial loss and disrupt property rights.

**Examples**:
- Forging a will to claim false inheritance
- Creating a fake share certificate
- Forging a power of attorney to transfer property
- Forging a cheque or promissory note

**Most Serious Form**: Section 467 carries the highest punishment (life imprisonment or up to 10 years) for forgery offences.

### Section 468 IPC / Section 341 BNS: Forgery for Purpose of Cheating
Whoever commits forgery with the **intent that the forged document shall be used for the purpose of cheating**, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Rationale**: Forgery combined with intent to cheat is aggravated.

**Example**: A forges a fake invoice to overcharge a client (cheating). Forgery for cheating under Section 468.

**Distinction from Section 467**: Section 468 applies when the forged document is intended for cheating but is not a valuable security or will. If it is a valuable security, Section 467 applies (higher punishment).

### Section 469 IPC / Section 342 BNS: Forgery for Purpose of Harming Reputation
Whoever commits forgery with the intent to cause **damage to the reputation** of any party, or knowing that it is likely to be used for that purpose, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **3 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Bailable, Triable by Any Magistrate

**Example**: A forges a compromising letter purporting to be written by B, intending to defame B's reputation. Forgery for harming reputation under Section 469.

---

## Using Forged Document (Section 471 / BNS Section 344)

### Definition and Punishment

**IPC Section 471**: Whoever **fraudulently or dishonestly uses as genuine any document or electronic record which he knows or has reason to believe to be a forged document or record**, shall be punished in the **same manner as if he had forged such document or record**.

**Punishment**: Same as the underlying forgery offence (Section 465, 466, 467, 468, or 469 depending on type of document).

**Essential Ingredients**:
1. **Document is forged** (made by someone through forgery)
2. **Accused knows or has reason to believe** it is forged
3. **Accused uses it as genuine** (presents it as if it were authentic)
4. **Use is fraudulent or dishonest**

**Principle**: Even if the accused did not forge the document himself, **using a forged document knowing it is forged** is equivalent to forgery.

**Example**: A obtains a forged degree certificate from B. A submits it in a job application knowing it is forged. A commits offence under Section 471 (using forged document), punishable same as Section 465 or 467 (depending on document type).

**Distinction**:
- **Section 463-469**: Making a false document (forgery itself)
- **Section 471**: Using a forged document made by another (or by oneself), knowing it is forged

---

## Possession of Forged Documents (Sections 472-474)

### Section 472 IPC / Section 345 BNS: Making or Possessing Counterfeit Seal with Intent to Commit Forgery
Whoever **makes or counterfeits any seal, plate, or other instrument** for making an impression, **intending** that the same shall be used for the purpose of committing any forgery, or who **possesses any such counterfeit seal or instrument** with such intent, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Rationale**: Possession of tools for forgery (fake seals, stamps, plates) is preparatory to forgery and is criminalized to prevent future forgeries.

**Example**: A manufactures a fake government seal intending to use it to forge documents. A is liable under Section 472 even before using the seal.

### Section 473 IPC / Section 346 BNS: Making or Possessing Counterfeit Seal for Valuable Security
Whoever makes or counterfeits any seal or instrument for making an impression, intending that the same shall be used for forging **valuable securities**, or who possesses such counterfeit seal or instrument knowing the same to be counterfeit and intending to use it, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment for life**, OR
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Court of Session

**Rationale**: Possession of tools to forge valuable securities (shares, bonds, wills) is the most serious preparatory offence.

### Section 474 IPC / Section 347 BNS: Having Possession of Forged Document (Court Record or Valuable Security)
Whoever has in his possession any document or electronic record, knowing the same to be forged, and intending that it shall be fraudulently or dishonestly used as genuine, shall, if the document is one of the description mentioned in **Section 466** (court record) or **Section 467** (valuable security), be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Example**: A has a forged will in his possession, knowing it is forged, and intends to use it to claim inheritance. A is liable under Section 474.

---

## Counterfeiting Devices for Authentication (Sections 475-476)

### Section 475 IPC / Section 348 BNS: Counterfeiting Device or Mark Used for Authenticating Documents
Whoever counterfeits upon or in the substance of any material any **device or mark** used for the purpose of authenticating any document (other than a stamp issued by government), intending that such device or mark shall be used for forging documents, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Examples**: Creating fake company seals, university stamps, notary seals.

### Section 476 IPC / Section 349 BNS: Counterfeiting Device for Authenticating Valuable Securities
Whoever counterfeits any device or mark used for authenticating **documents prescribed by law to be stamped** (e.g., stamp paper, court fees), or valuable securities (other than currency notes or bank notes), with intent to forge, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment for life**, OR
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Example**: Creating fake stamp paper watermarks or security features.

---

## Fraudulent Cancellation or Destruction (Section 477 / BNS Section 350)

### Definition and Punishment

**IPC Section 477**: Whoever **fraudulently or dishonestly**, or with intent to cause damage or injury to the public or any person, **cancels, destroys, or defaces**, or attempts to cancel, destroy, or deface, or **secretes** (hides):
- Any **will**
- Any **authority to adopt a son**
- Any **valuable security**
- Or causes any of the above to be cancelled, destroyed, defaced, or secreted

With intent to cause damage or injury, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment for life**, OR
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Rationale**: Destroying or hiding wills, adoption authorities, or valuable securities can cause severe property loss and legal consequences.

**Example**: A destroys B's will (in which A is not a beneficiary) so that B's property will devolve according to intestate succession (benefiting A). A is liable under Section 477.

---

## Falsification of Accounts (Section 477A / BNS Section 351)

### Definition and Punishment

**IPC Section 477A**: Whoever, being a **clerk, officer, or servant**, or employed in that capacity, **willfully and with intent to defraud**:
- Destroys, alters, mutilates, or falsifies any **book, paper, writing, valuable security, or account** belonging to his employer
- Makes or abets making of any false entry in any such book, account, etc.
- Omits or abets omission of any material particular from any such book, account, etc.

Shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, OR
- **Fine**, OR
- **Both**

**Nature**: Non-cognizable, Bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Rationale**: Employees who falsify accounts betray their employer's trust and facilitate fraud.

**Examples**:
- An accountant alters company books to hide embezzlement
- A clerk makes false entries in ledgers to show fake expenses
- An employee destroys invoices to conceal theft

**Distinction from Forgery**:
- **Forgery (Section 463)**: Making a false document with intent to defraud
- **Falsification of Accounts (Section 477A)**: Specifically applies to employees who falsify their employer's accounts

---

## Landmark Cases on Forgery

### 1. **State of Maharashtra v. Dr. Praful B. Desai** (2003) - Intent Essential
**AIR 2003 SC 2053**

**Facts**: The accused, a doctor, was alleged to have forged medical certificates. The prosecution could not prove fraudulent intent.

**Held**: For forgery under Section 463, **fraudulent or dishonest intent** at the time of making the false document is essential. If the document was made with innocent intention (e.g., genuine mistake, no intent to defraud), forgery is not established.

**Principle**: **Mens rea** (guilty mind) is the essence of forgery. Mere making of a false document without fraudulent intent is not forgery.

**Test**: Was the document made with intent to cause damage, support false claim, or commit fraud? If no such intent, no forgery.

### 2. **Gopal Lal v. State of Rajasthan** (1979) - Material Alteration
**AIR 1979 SC 824**

**Facts**: The accused made minor corrections in a document. The prosecution alleged forgery.

**Held**: For forgery, the alteration must be **material** (affecting the legal meaning or effect of the document). **Immaterial or minor alterations** (e.g., correcting typos, non-essential changes) do not constitute forgery.
