**Principle**: Not every alteration is forgery. Only material alterations that change the legal character or effect of the document constitute forgery.

**Example (Material)**: Changing the beneficiary name in a will is material forgery.

**Example (Immaterial)**: Correcting a spelling mistake in a witness's name (if it does not affect identity) is not material forgery.

### 3. **K. Chinnaswamy Reddy v. State of A.P.** (1962) - Using Forged Document
**AIR 1962 SC 1788**

**Facts**: The accused used a forged document (made by another person) in a legal proceeding, knowing it was forged.

**Held**: Under **Section 471**, **using a forged document as genuine**, knowing it to be forged, is punishable in the same manner as forgery itself. The accused need not have forged the document himself.

**Principle**: Liability extends to users of forged documents, not just makers. Knowingly using a forged document is equivalent to forgery.

**Application**: If A forges a document and gives it to B, and B uses it knowing it is forged, both A (forger) and B (user) are liable.

### 4. **Bishwanath Prasad v. State of Bihar** (1974) - Signature Forgery
**AIR 1974 SC 1717**

**Facts**: The accused forged another person's signature on a deed to fraudulently transfer property.

**Held**: **Forging a signature** is one of the most common forms of forgery. If a person signs another's name without authorization, with intent to defraud or cause damage, it is forgery under Section 463.

**Principle**: Signature forgery is classic forgery if done with fraudulent intent.

**Evidence**: Forensic handwriting analysis is critical in proving signature forgery (expert witnesses compare disputed signature with genuine samples).

### 5. **State of Rajasthan v. Nand Lal** (2003) - Backdating of Documents
**2003 Cri LJ 2945**

**Facts**: The accused created a document backdated to give false impression of priority over competing claims.

**Held**: **Backdating a document** with fraudulent intent (to create false priority, avoid legal deadlines, or defraud) constitutes forgery under Section 464 (making document purporting to be made at a time when it was not made).

**Principle**: Misdating (backdating or post-dating) with intent to defraud is forgery.

**Application**: Backdating contracts to avoid stamp duty, tax liability, or legal deadlines is forgery.

### 6. **Dalmia Cement Ltd. v. Galaxy Traders** (2001) - Corporate Forgery
**AIR 2001 SC 1325**

**Facts**: Corporate documents (invoices, bills) were forged to facilitate tax evasion and financial fraud.

**Held**: Forgery in corporate context (fake invoices, false accounts, forged resolutions) is punishable under Section 463 (forgery) and Section 477A (falsification of accounts).

**Principle**: Corporate entities and their officers can be prosecuted for forgery of business documents.

**Additional Liability**: Companies Act, 2013 Section 447 (fraud) provides additional punishment for corporate fraud involving forgery.

### 7. **Vijay Kumar Ghai v. State of Punjab** (2007) - Electronic Forgery
**AIR 2007 SC 2817**

**Facts**: The accused altered electronic records (email contents) to defraud.

**Held**: **Electronic records** are subject to forgery provisions under IPC (as amended by IT Act, 2000). Altering, creating fake, or manipulating electronic records with fraudulent intent is forgery.

**Principle**: Forgery laws apply to electronic documents, digital signatures, emails, and computer-generated records.

**Application**: Email forgery, fake digital signatures, manipulated PDFs, altered databases are all punishable as forgery.

---

## IPC to BNS Transition Mapping

| **IPC Section** | **BNS Section** | **Offence** | **Changes** |
|-----------------|-----------------|-------------|-------------|
| **463** | **336** | Definition of Forgery | No substantive change; includes electronic records |
| **464** | **337** | Making False Document | No substantive change |
| **465** | **338** | Punishment for Forgery | No change (up to 2 years or fine or both) |
| **466** | **339** | Forgery of Court Record | No change (up to 7 years + fine) |
| **467** | **340** | Forgery of Valuable Security/Will | No change (life or up to 10 years + fine) |
| **468** | **341** | Forgery for Cheating | No change (up to 7 years + fine) |
| **469** | **342** | Forgery for Harming Reputation | No change (up to 3 years + fine) |
| **470** | **343** | Forged Document | No substantive change |
| **471** | **344** | Using Forged Document | No change (punished same as forgery) |
| **472** | **345** | Possessing Counterfeit Seal | No change (up to 7 years + fine) |
| **473** | **346** | Counterfeit Seal for Valuable Security | No change (life or up to 7 years + fine) |
| **474** | **347** | Possessing Forged Document (466/467) | No change (up to 7 years + fine) |
| **475** | **348** | Counterfeiting Authentication Device | No change (up to 7 years + fine) |
| **476** | **349** | Counterfeiting Device for Valuable Security | No change (life or up to 7 years + fine) |
| **477** | **350** | Fraudulent Cancellation of Will/Security | No change (life or up to 7 years + fine) |
| **477A** | **351** | Falsification of Accounts | No change (up to 7 years or fine or both) |

**Note**: The BNS 2023 has retained all forgery provisions from IPC 1860 with no substantive changes. The provisions are modernized to explicitly include electronic records (already covered by IPC as amended by IT Act 2000).

---

## Practical Examples

### Example 1: Forging Signature on Cheque
**Scenario**: Ramesh steals a blank cheque from Priya's cheque book. He forges Priya's signature and writes a cheque for Rs. 50,000 payable to himself. He attempts to cash it at the bank.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Making false document: Ramesh forged Priya's signature on the cheque
2. ✅ Fraudulent intent: Ramesh intended to cause Priya to part with Rs. 50,000 (fraudulently)
3. ✅ Valuable security: Cheque is a valuable security (negotiable instrument)

**Offence**: Forgery of Valuable Security under Section 467 IPC / Section 340 BNS
**Punishment**: Imprisonment for life OR up to 10 years AND fine

**Additional Charges**: Theft (stealing cheque book), cheating (Section 420), offences under Negotiable Instruments Act

### Example 2: Forging Educational Certificate
**Scenario**: Sunil creates a fake MBA degree certificate from a prestigious university to apply for a job. The certificate claims Sunil graduated in 2020, but he never attended the university. He submits it with his job application.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Making false document: Sunil created a fake degree certificate
2. ✅ Fraudulent intent: Sunil intended to commit fraud (obtain job by false credentials)
3. ✅ Not valuable security or will: Degree certificate is a regular document (not valuable security)

**Offence**:
- Forgery under Section 465 IPC / Section 338 BNS (for making the certificate)
- Using Forged Document under Section 471 (for submitting it)
**Punishment**: Up to 2 years imprisonment or fine or both

**Alternative Charge**: Forgery for Purpose of Cheating (Section 468) if prosecution emphasizes intent to cheat employer - up to 7 years + fine

### Example 3: Forging Will
**Scenario**: After his uncle's death, Vikram forges a will claiming the uncle left all property to Vikram. In reality, the uncle died intestate (without a will). Vikram presents the forged will in court to claim inheritance.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Making false document: Vikram created a fake will
2. ✅ Fraudulent intent: Vikram intended to support false claim (inheritance claim)
3. ✅ Will: The document is a will (specifically mentioned in Section 467)
4. ✅ Using forged document: Vikram presented it in court

**Offence**:
- Forgery of Will under Section 467 IPC / Section 340 BNS
- Using Forged Document under Section 471
**Punishment**: Imprisonment for life OR up to 10 years AND fine

**Additional Charges**: Cheating (if property is obtained), fraud

### Example 4: Forging Court Order
**Scenario**: A creates a fake court order directing the release of B (who is in police custody). A presents the forged order to the police, and B is released.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Making false document: A created a fake court order
2. ✅ Fraudulent intent: A intended to cause injury to public (obstruct justice) and injure administration of law
3. ✅ Court record: The document purports to be a court order (record of court)

**Offence**: Forgery of Record of Court under Section 466 IPC / Section 339 BNS
**Punishment**: Up to 7 years imprisonment AND fine

**Additional Charges**: Obstruction of justice, aiding escape of prisoner, forgery for purpose of cheating (Section 468)

### Example 5: Falsification of Company Accounts
**Scenario**: Mohan, an accountant at XYZ Ltd, makes false entries in the company's books to hide embezzlement of Rs. 10 lakh. He creates fake invoices and alters ledger entries.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Clerk/officer/servant: Mohan is an employee (accountant)
2. ✅ Willfully and with intent to defraud: Mohan intentionally falsified accounts to hide embezzlement
3. ✅ Belonging to employer: The books belong to XYZ Ltd (employer)
4. ✅ False entries and alterations: Created fake invoices, altered ledgers

**Offence**: Falsification of Accounts under Section 477A IPC / Section 351 BNS
**Punishment**: Up to 7 years imprisonment OR fine OR both

**Additional Charges**: Criminal breach of trust (Section 408 - by servant), cheating, forgery (Section 463)

### Example 6: Using Forged Document (Not the Forger)
**Scenario**: Ravi obtains a forged property deed from his friend Amit (who forged it). Ravi knows the deed is forged but uses it to claim ownership of land in a legal dispute.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Document is forged: The deed was forged by Amit
2. ✅ Ravi knows it is forged: Ravi is aware the deed is fake
3. ✅ Uses as genuine: Ravi presents it in legal proceedings as if genuine
4. ✅ Fraudulent/dishonest use: Ravi intends to fraudulently claim land

**Offence**: Using Forged Document as Genuine under Section 471 IPC / Section 344 BNS
**Punishment**: Same as if Ravi had forged the document himself (Section 467 applies since it's a property transfer document - life or up to 10 years + fine)

**Note**: Amit (the forger) is liable under Section 467. Ravi (the user) is liable under Section 471.

### Example 7: Backdating Contract for Tax Evasion
**Scenario**: A company enters into a contract with a vendor on April 15, 2024. To claim tax benefit available only for contracts signed before March 31, 2024, the company backdates the contract to March 30, 2024.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Making false document: Backdating creates a false impression of timing
2. ✅ Fraudulent intent: Intent to defraud government (claim false tax benefit)
3. ✅ Purporting to be made at different time: Contract falsely dated March 30

**Offence**: Forgery under Section 464/465 IPC (making false document purporting to be made at different time)
**Punishment**: Up to 2 years imprisonment or fine or both

**Additional Charges**: Tax evasion under Income Tax Act / GST Act, criminal breach of trust (if public funds involved)

---

## Defenses to Forgery

### 1. **No Fraudulent Intent**
- If the document was made without intent to defraud, cause damage, or support false claim, no forgery
- Genuine mistake or innocent creation of document is not forgery

**Example**: A signs B's name on a document with B's verbal authorization (but not written authorization). If A genuinely believed authorization existed, intent to forge may be absent.

### 2. **Immaterial Alteration**
- If the alteration to a document is immaterial (does not affect legal meaning or effect), not forgery

**Example**: Correcting a typo in a non-essential clause of a contract is not forgery.

### 3. **Authorization or Consent**
- If the person whose signature was used or document was altered gave authorization, no forgery

**Example**: B authorizes A to sign B's name on documents. A's signature is not forgery (authorized).

### 4. **Lack of Knowledge (For Section 471 - Using Forged Document)**
- If the accused did not know and had no reason to believe the document was forged, Section 471 does not apply

**Example**: A receives a certificate from a university. A submits it in a job application. Later it is discovered to be forged by the university (not by A). If A had no knowledge it was forged, A is not liable under Section 471.

### 5. **No Use as Genuine (For Section 471)**
- If the forged document was not used or presented as genuine, Section 471 does not apply

**Example**: A possesses a forged document but never uses it. Possession alone may not be Section 471 (but could be Section 474 if intent to use exists).

---

## Procedural Aspects

### Cognizance and Complaint
- **Simple Forgery (465, 469)**: Non-cognizable, Bailable
- **Aggravated Forgery (466, 467, 468)**: Non-cognizable, Non-bailable
- **Possession of Counterfeit Seal (472, 473)**: Cognizable, Non-bailable
- **Falsification of Accounts (477A)**: Non-cognizable, Bailable

### Investigation
- For non-cognizable offences: Complaint to magistrate; investigation with magistrate's order
- Evidence: Forensic document examination (handwriting analysis, ink analysis, paper analysis), expert testimony, comparison with genuine documents

### Trial
- Burden of proof on prosecution to establish: making of false document, fraudulent intent, materiality of alteration
- Expert evidence critical for proving forgery (handwriting experts, forensic document examiners)

### Compounding
- **Forgery offences are generally non-compoundable** (except Section 465 - simple forgery, which is compoundable with court permission)

---

## Compliance Checklist

### For Individuals and Organizations (Preventive Measures)
- [ ] Verify authenticity of documents (check seals, signatures, watermarks, security features)
- [ ] Use secure storage for important documents (wills, deeds, securities)
- [ ] Implement digital security for electronic records (encryption, digital signatures, access controls)
- [ ] Conduct background verification before accepting documents (especially employment certificates, degrees)
- [ ] Report suspected forgery immediately to police or authorities
- [ ] Preserve original documents and avoid giving originals to untrusted parties

### For Businesses (Compliance)
- [ ] Implement internal controls for document authentication (approval processes, dual signatures)
- [ ] Conduct audits to detect account falsification (Section 477A)
- [ ] Use secure systems for financial records (tamper-proof accounting software)
- [ ] Train employees on forgery prevention and detection
- [ ] Report forgery incidents to law enforcement and regulatory authorities

### For Accused/Suspect (Legal Safeguards)
- [ ] Claim defenses: no fraudulent intent, authorization, immaterial alteration, lack of knowledge
- [ ] Demonstrate genuine mistake or innocent creation of document
- [ ] Return or surrender forged document if applicable
- [ ] Seek compounding (for simple forgery only) with court permission
- [ ] Engage forensic experts to challenge prosecution's handwriting/document analysis
- [ ] Seek bail (bailable for simple forgery; apply to court for aggravated forms)

### For Investigating Officer
- [ ] Secure original documents as evidence
- [ ] Engage forensic document examiner (handwriting analysis, ink dating, paper analysis)
- [ ] Obtain exemplar (sample) signatures/handwriting of suspected forger for comparison
- [ ] Establish fraudulent intent (motive, statements, circumstances)
- [ ] Frame appropriate charge based on type of document (Section 465, 466, 467, or 468)

### For Prosecutors
- [ ] Prove making of false document (forensic evidence, expert testimony)
- [ ] Prove fraudulent intent (critical element)
- [ ] Prove materiality of alteration (if applicable)
- [ ] Present expert witnesses (handwriting experts, forensic document examiners)
- [ ] Anticipate defenses (authorization, immaterial alteration, lack of intent)

### For Defense Counsel
- [ ] Challenge authenticity of prosecution's expert analysis (cross-examine forensic experts)
- [ ] Argue lack of fraudulent intent or genuine mistake
- [ ] Challenge materiality of alteration
- [ ] Seek acquittal if prosecution fails to prove intent or making of false document beyond reasonable doubt
- [ ] If conviction likely, argue for minimum sentence: first-time offender, return of documents, no actual harm caused

---

## Cross-References

### Related Criminal Offences
- **Cheating (IPC 415-420)**: Forgery is often used to facilitate cheating
- **Criminal Breach of Trust (IPC 405-409)**: Employees may forge documents to conceal CBT
- **Fraud (Companies Act 2013, Section 447)**: Corporate forgery and fraud
- **Counterfeiting Currency (IPC 489A-489E)**: Specialized form of forgery

### Special Legislations
- **Information Technology Act, 2000**: Cyber forgery, tampering with electronic records (Section 65), electronic signature forgery
- **Companies Act, 2013**: Fraud involving forgery of corporate documents (Section 447)
- **Prevention of Corruption Act, 1988**: Forgery by public servants
- **Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881**: Forged cheques, promissory notes
- **Copyright Act, 1957 / Trade Marks Act, 1999**: Forgery of intellectual property documents

### Evidence Law
- **Indian Evidence Act, 1872 / BSA**:
  - Section 45: Opinion of expert witnesses (handwriting experts, forensic examiners)
  - Section 47: Comparison of signature/handwriting
  - Section 73: Court may compare disputed writing with admitted or proved writing

### Constitutional Provisions
- **Article 21**: Right to fair trial, protection against wrongful conviction based on forged evidence
- **Article 20(3)**: Protection against self-incrimination (accused cannot be compelled to provide handwriting samples in certain circumstances)

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Making False Document is Forgery**: Creating, altering, backdating, or signing another's name on a document with fraudulent intent is forgery.

2. **Intent is Critical**: Fraudulent or dishonest intent at the time of making the document is essential. Innocent mistake or immaterial alteration is not forgery.

3. **Material Alteration**: Only material alterations (affecting legal meaning or effect) constitute forgery. Minor corrections are not forgery.

4. **Using Forged Document = Forgery**: Under Section 471, using a forged document knowing it is forged carries the same punishment as forging it.

5. **Graduated Punishment Based on Document Type**:
   - Simple forgery: up to 2 years
   - Court records: up to 7 years
   - Valuable securities/wills: life or up to 10 years
   - For cheating: up to 7 years

6. **Electronic Records Covered**: Forgery laws apply to electronic documents, emails, digital signatures, and computer-generated records.

7. **Preparatory Offences Criminalized**: Possessing counterfeit seals, tools for forgery, or forged documents with intent to use is punishable even before actual use.

8. **Forensic Evidence Critical**: Handwriting analysis, ink dating, and document examination by experts are crucial for proving forgery.

---

## Conclusion

Forgery strikes at the foundation of trust in written and electronic records, which are essential for commerce, property rights, and administration of justice. The law provides stringent punishment for forgery, with the most serious forms (forgery of wills, valuable securities) punishable with life imprisonment.

Understanding the distinction between making a false document (forgery) and using a forged document (Section 471), as well as the importance of fraudulent intent and material alteration, is critical for proper charge framing and defense.

With the transition to BNS 2023, the substantive law on forgery remains unchanged, ensuring continuity in legal practice while explicitly recognizing electronic records in the digital age.

---

**Document Version**: 1.0
**Effective Date**: IPC provisions apply until June 30, 2024; BNS provisions effective from July 1, 2024
**Last Updated**: December 6, 2025
**Prepared by**: Legal Protocol Documentation System
**Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)

---

**Disclaimer**: This protocol is for informational and educational purposes only and does not constitute legal advice. For specific legal matters, consult a qualified criminal law advocate or legal expert.


---
## IL-CRIM-FRAUD.md
---

# India Criminal Law: Fraud (Ghotala / Dhokha)

## Protocol ID
**IL-CRIM-FRAUD**

## Domain
Criminal Law > Economic Offences

## Jurisdiction
India (All States and Union Territories)

## Statutory Framework

### Companies Act, 2013
- **Section 447**: Punishment for Fraud

### Indian Penal Code (IPC), 1860
- **Section 420**: Cheating and Dishonestly Inducing Delivery of Property
- **Section 415**: Cheating (Definition)
- **Section 463**: Forgery (often accompanies fraud)

### Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) Act, 1992
- **Section 24**: Fraudulent and Unfair Trade Practices
- **Section 12A**: SEBI Power to Issue Directions in Cases of Fraud

### Insurance Act, 1938 & Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority (IRDA) Act, 1999
- Provisions against insurance fraud

### Banking Regulation Act, 1949
- Provisions against banking fraud

### Information Technology Act, 2000
- **Section 66C**: Identity Theft
- **Section 66D**: Cheating by Personation Using Computer Resource
- **Cyber Fraud Provisions**

---

## Overview

**Fraud** in Indian criminal law is the intentional deception or dishonest act to secure unfair or unlawful gain, or to deprive another of a legal right. While the IPC covers general fraud through cheating provisions (Section 420), specialized statutes address corporate fraud, securities fraud, insurance fraud, banking fraud, and cyber fraud.

Fraud is distinguished from mere breach of contract by the element of **criminal intent** (mens rea) - the deliberate intention to deceive from the inception.

### Key Characteristics
1. **Deception**: Fraudulent misrepresentation or concealment of material facts
2. **Criminal Intent**: Dishonest intention from the beginning (not mere failure to perform)
3. **Economic Loss**: Usually involves financial loss to victim or wrongful gain to perpetrator
4. **Multiple Statutes**: Fraud is covered by IPC (general), Companies Act (corporate), SEBI Act (securities), IT Act (cyber fraud)
5. **Severe Punishment**: Up to 10 years imprisonment for corporate fraud; varying penalties for other forms

---

## Types of Fraud

### 1. Corporate Fraud (Companies Act, 2013 - Section 447)

#### Definition

**Section 447**: Any person found to be **guilty of fraud** involving an amount of at least **Rs. 10 lakh** or **1% of the turnover** of the company (whichever is lower) shall be punishable with imprisonment from **6 months to 10 years** AND fine (at least the amount involved, may extend to 3 times the amount).

If fraud involves amount less than Rs. 10 lakh or 1% turnover, imprisonment up to **5 years** OR fine up to Rs. 50 lakh OR both.

#### What Constitutes Fraud (Explanation to Section 447)

**"Fraud"** includes any act, omission, concealment, or abuse of position committed by any person with intent to **deceive**, to gain **undue advantage**, or to **injure the interests** of:
- The company
- Shareholders
- Creditors
- Any other person

Whether or not there is any wrongful gain or wrongful loss.

#### Common Forms of Corporate Fraud

**A. Financial Statement Fraud**
- Manipulating financial statements to show false profits/losses
- Overstating assets or understating liabilities
- Creating fictitious revenues or hiding expenses

**Example**: Satyam Computer Services scandal (2009) - Chairman Ramalinga Raju admitted to inflating cash and bank balances by Rs. 5,040 crore, overstating revenues, and creating fictitious assets.

**B. Asset Misappropriation**
- Embezzlement of company funds
- Theft of company assets (inventory, equipment, intellectual property)
- Fraudulent expense claims

**Example**: Director of a company diverts Rs. 50 crore from company accounts to personal offshore accounts.

**C. Corruption and Bribery**
- Kickbacks in procurement contracts
- Bribes to obtain government contracts or licenses
- Conflict of interest transactions

**Example**: Company pays bribes to government officials to secure telecom licenses (2G spectrum scam).

**D. Securities Fraud (Insider Trading, Market Manipulation)**
- Covered primarily by SEBI Act (discussed below)
- But may also constitute corporate fraud under Companies Act

**E. Fraudulent Related-Party Transactions**
- Directors awarding contracts to related entities at inflated prices
- Transferring company assets to promoter-owned firms at undervalued prices

**Example**: Company sells land worth Rs. 100 crore to a promoter's relative for Rs. 10 crore (fraudulent undervaluation).

#### Punishment for Corporate Fraud

**If amount involved ≥ Rs. 10 lakh or 1% turnover**:
- **Imprisonment**: 6 months to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**: At least the amount involved in fraud, may extend to **3 times** the amount

**If amount < Rs. 10 lakh or 1% turnover**:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **5 years**, OR
- **Fine**: Up to **Rs. 50 lakh**, OR
- **Both**

**Nature**: Cognizable (if amount ≥ Rs. 10 lakh), Non-bailable

**Additional Consequences**:
- **Disqualification from being director** (Section 164(2)) - 5 years
- **Debarment from managing companies**
- **Criminal liability for directors, auditors, executives**
- **Civil liability**: Shareholders/creditors can sue for damages
- **Disgorgement of profits**: Court may order return of fraudulently obtained gains

#### Who Can Be Prosecuted?

**Section 447 applies to**:
- **Directors** (executive and non-executive)
- **Key Managerial Personnel** (CEO, CFO, Company Secretary)
- **Officers in default** (those responsible for company compliance)
- **Auditors** (if connived in fraud)
- **Promoters and shareholders** (if they perpetrated fraud)
- **Company itself** (through directors/officers)

**Vicarious Liability**: If company commits fraud, every person who was responsible and consented/connived is deemed guilty.

---

### 2. Securities Fraud (SEBI Act, 1992)

#### Section 12A SEBI Act: Fraudulent and Unfair Trade Practices

SEBI has power to investigate and prosecute fraudulent and unfair trade practices in securities markets, including:

**A. Insider Trading**
- Trading in securities while in possession of **unpublished price-sensitive information** (UPSI)

**Definition (SEBI Insider Trading Regulations, 2015)**:
- **Insider**: Person with access to UPSI (directors, employees, connected persons)
- **UPSI**: Information not available to public, which if published would likely affect price of securities

**Example**: CFO of Company X knows (before public announcement) that company will report huge quarterly losses. CFO sells his shares at current high price before announcement. After announcement, share price crashes. CFO committed insider trading.

**Punishment**:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years** (under SEBI Act and Companies Act)
- **Fine**: Up to **Rs. 25 crore** or **3 times the profit** made (whichever is higher)
- **Disgorgement**: Return of illegal profits

**Famous Cases**:
- Rajat Gupta case (2012): Former McKinsey head and Goldman Sachs director convicted in US for insider trading; also prosecuted in India by SEBI
- Rajesh Sharma case (2014): SEBI imposed Rs. 15 crore penalty for insider trading

**B. Market Manipulation**
- **Price Rigging**: Artificially inflating or deflating stock prices
- **Front Running**: Broker trading for own account before executing large client orders
- **Pump and Dump**: Artificially inflating stock price through false recommendations, then selling holdings at inflated price
- **Circular Trading**: Creating artificial volumes through synchronized buy-sell orders among connected parties

**Example (Pump and Dump)**: A stock operator spreads false rumors that Company Y will be acquired by a multinational, causing share price to jump from Rs. 50 to Rs. 200. Operator sells his holdings at Rs. 200 and exits. When truth emerges, price crashes to Rs. 30. Innocent investors lose money.

**Punishment**:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years**
- **Fine**: Up to **Rs. 25 crore** or **3 times the profit** made
- **Market ban**: SEBI can debar from trading for specified period

**Famous Case**: Harshad Mehta Scam (1992) - Stock market manipulation involving fake bank receipts and circular trading; Mehta artificially inflated stock prices causing market crash.

**C. Ponzi Schemes and Fraudulent Collective Investment Schemes**
- Promising high returns but using new investors' money to pay old investors (unsustainable)
- Not genuine investment schemes

**Example**: Saradha chit fund scam (2013) - Rs. 2,500 crore Ponzi scheme in West Bengal; promised high returns, collapsed leaving lakhs of investors defrauded.

**Punishment**: Companies Act fraud provisions + SEBI penalties + IPC cheating (Section 420)

---

### 3. Banking Fraud

#### Definition (RBI Guidelines & Banking Regulation Act)

**Bank fraud** includes:
- **Loan fraud**: Obtaining loans by submitting false documents, inflating assets, or diverting loan funds
- **Credit card fraud**: Unauthorized use of credit cards, card cloning
- **Account fraud**: Forged cheques, unauthorized withdrawals
- **Letter of credit fraud**: Presenting forged documents to claim payment

**Section 135 of Banking Regulation Act**: Punishment for bank fraud up to **3 years** imprisonment OR fine OR both.

**IPC Provisions**: Cheating (Section 420), forgery (Section 463-477), criminal breach of trust (Section 405-409)

#### Common Forms of Banking Fraud

**A. Non-Performing Asset (NPA) Fraud / Wilful Default**
- **Wilful Default**: Borrower has capacity to repay but deliberately defaults
- Diversion of loan funds to unauthorized purposes
- Siphoning off funds through related entities

**Example**: Vijay Mallya case - Kingfisher Airlines defaulted on Rs. 9,000 crore loans. Alleged wilful default, diversion of funds, fraudulent documentation. Mallya fled India; declared fugitive economic offender.

**Consequences**:
- **Criminal prosecution**: Cheating, fraud, criminal breach of trust
- **Civil recovery**: SARFAESI Act (bank can auction collateral without court)
- **Fugitive Economic Offenders Act, 2018**: Confiscation of all assets if declared fugitive

**B. Letter of Credit (LC) Fraud**
- Presenting forged/fake documents to claim LC payment
- Inflating invoice values

**Example**: Nirav Modi-PNB Fraud (2018) - Rs. 14,000 crore fraud involving fraudulent Letters of Undertaking (LoUs) issued by PNB officials to facilitate import payments. Nirav Modi and associates siphoned funds through shell companies.

**Charges**: Companies Act fraud, cheating, criminal conspiracy, money laundering (PMLA)

**C. Credit Card Fraud**
- Card cloning (skimming devices at ATMs/POS)
- Phishing (stealing card details through fake websites/emails)
- Unauthorized transactions

**Punishment**: IT Act provisions (Section 66C - identity theft, Section 66D - cheating by personation) + IPC cheating/theft

---

### 4. Insurance Fraud

#### Definition

**Insurance fraud** includes:
- **False claims**: Claiming insurance for non-existent loss, accident, or death
- **Staged accidents**: Creating fake accidents to claim insurance
- **Inflated claims**: Exaggerating value of loss
- **Fraudulent policies**: Taking policies on fictitious persons or with false information

**Example**: A sets fire to his own property and claims fire insurance of Rs. 50 lakh (arson + insurance fraud).

#### Punishment

- **IPC Section 420**: Cheating (up to 7 years + fine)
- **Insurance Act provisions**: Cancellation of policy, denial of claim
- **IRDA regulations**: Penalties by regulator

**Famous Case**: Motor insurance fraud rackets - Staged road accidents with inflated medical bills to claim insurance; several gangs busted in major cities.

---

### 5. Cyber Fraud

#### IT Act, 2000 Provisions

**Section 66C**: **Identity Theft**
- Fraudulently using another person's electronic signature, password, or unique identification
- **Punishment**: Up to **3 years** imprisonment AND fine up to **Rs. 1 lakh**

**Section 66D**: **Cheating by Personation Using Computer Resource**
- Impersonating another person online to cheat or defraud
- **Punishment**: Up to **3 years** imprisonment AND fine up to **Rs. 1 lakh**

**Section 43**: **Penalty for Damage to Computer Systems, Data Theft**
- Unauthorized access, data theft, virus introduction
- **Compensation**: Up to **Rs. 1 crore**

#### Common Forms of Cyber Fraud

**A. Phishing**
- Fake emails/websites mimicking banks, e-commerce sites to steal login credentials, card details

**Example**: Email claiming to be from State Bank asks user to "verify account" by clicking link and entering credentials. Link leads to fake website; credentials stolen.

**B. Online Shopping Fraud**
- Fake e-commerce sites taking payment but not delivering goods
- Selling counterfeit/substandard products

**C. UPI/Digital Wallet Fraud**
- Tricking victims into sharing OTP or QR code to steal money from digital wallets

**Example**: Scammer calls victim claiming to be from bank, asks for OTP "to reverse wrong transaction." Victim shares OTP; money is debited from account.

**D. Job/Lottery/Prize Scams**
- Fake job offers demanding "registration fee"
- Fake lottery wins asking for "processing fee"

**E. Romance/Social Media Scams**
- Fake profiles on dating apps/social media to gain trust and extract money

#### Reporting and Remedies

**Report to**:
- **Cybercrime Police** (dedicated cyber cells in every state)
- **National Cybercrime Reporting Portal**: cybercrime.gov.in
- **Bank/Payment Gateway**: For transaction reversals

---

## Distinction Between Fraud and Civil Breach of Contract

| **Aspect** | **Criminal Fraud** | **Civil Breach of Contract** |
|------------|--------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Intent** | **Dishonest intent from beginning** (mens rea) | Genuine contract, later inability or unwillingness to perform |
| **Deception** | Fraudulent misrepresentation or concealment | No deception; honest agreement |
| **Remedy** | **Criminal prosecution** (imprisonment + fine) | **Civil suit** for damages or specific performance |
| **Standard of Proof** | Beyond reasonable doubt (higher standard) | Preponderance of probabilities (lower standard) |
| **Example** | A takes advance for construction with no intention to build | A genuinely enters contract to build but fails due to business loss |

**Supreme Court Principle** (*K. Nagireddy v. State of A.P.*, 1975): Mere breach of contract does not constitute fraud unless there was **criminal intent from inception**.

---

## Landmark Cases on Fraud

### 1. **Satyam Computer Services Fraud** (2009) - Corporate Fraud
**Case**: Ramalinga Raju (Chairman) v. SEBI & State

**Facts**: Raju admitted to manipulating Satyam's balance sheet:
- Inflated cash and bank balances by Rs. 5,040 crore
- Inflated revenues, understated liabilities
- Fictitious assets and interest income

**Charges**:
- Companies Act: Fraud (Section 447 equivalent)
- IPC: Cheating, forgery, criminal conspiracy
- SEBI: Market manipulation, misleading investors

**Outcome**:
- Raju and associates convicted; sentenced to 7 years imprisonment
- Rs. 1,849 crore fine imposed
- Debarred from holding director position
- Civil suits by shareholders for damages

**Significance**: Landmark case exposing corporate governance failures; led to stricter auditing norms and SEBI regulations.

### 2. **Nirav Modi - Punjab National Bank Fraud** (2018) - Banking Fraud
**Amount**: Rs. 14,000 crore

**Facts**:
- Fraudulent Letters of Undertaking (LoUs) issued by PNB officials to Nirav Modi's companies
- Funds used for import payments, but imports either didn't happen or were overvalued
- Money siphoned through shell companies to offshore accounts
- Nirav Modi fled India

**Charges**:
- Companies Act: Fraud
- IPC: Cheating, criminal conspiracy, criminal breach of trust
- PMLA: Money laundering
- Fugitive Economic Offenders Act: Declared fugitive; assets confiscated

**Status**: Nirav Modi arrested in UK (2019); extradition proceedings ongoing; assets worth thousands of crores attached.

**Significance**: India's largest banking fraud; exposed vulnerabilities in banking systems; led to Fugitive Economic Offenders Act, 2018.

### 3. **Harshad Mehta Scam** (1992) - Securities Fraud
**Amount**: Rs. 5,000 crore (1992 values)

**Facts**:
- Stock broker Harshad Mehta manipulated stock market through:
  - Fake Bank Receipts (BRs) - forged documents to borrow from banks
  - Circular trading - artificially inflating stock prices
  - Pump and dump scheme
- Sensex crashed from 4,500 to 2,500 after fraud exposed

**Charges**: IPC cheating, forgery, criminal breach of trust; SEBI violations

**Outcome**: Mehta arrested; died in custody before trial completion; associates prosecuted.

**Significance**: Led to major reforms in securities regulation, establishment of NSE, strengthening of SEBI powers.

### 4. **Sahara Group Case** (2011-ongoing) - Ponzi Scheme
**Amount**: Rs. 24,000 crore

**Facts**:
- Sahara companies raised Rs. 24,000 crore from 3 crore investors through Optionally Fully Convertible Debentures (OFCDs)
- SEBI found it was a collective investment scheme (Ponzi) operating without regulatory approval
- Sahara failed to refund investors

**Charges**: SEBI violations, Companies Act fraud

**Outcome**:
- Supreme Court ordered Sahara to refund Rs. 24,000 crore + interest to SEBI for investor protection
- Sahara chief Subrata Roy jailed for contempt (released on bail after partial payment)
- Refund process ongoing

**Significance**: Regulatory action against unregulated collective investment schemes.

### 5. **Vijay Mallya - Kingfisher Airlines Default** (2012-ongoing) - Wilful Default
**Amount**: Rs. 9,000 crore

**Facts**:
- Kingfisher Airlines defaulted on bank loans
- Alleged wilful default - Mallya had capacity to repay but diverted funds
- Mallya fled India (2016)

**Charges**:
- IPC: Cheating, criminal conspiracy
- PMLA: Money laundering
- Fugitive Economic Offenders Act: Declared fugitive (2019)

**Outcome**:
- Mallya arrested in UK (2017); extradition proceedings
- Assets worth Rs. 12,900 crore confiscated
- SEBI banned from securities market

**Significance**: Led to Fugitive Economic Offenders Act, 2018 enabling confiscation of assets of economic offenders who flee.

### 6. **Fortis Healthcare Fraud** (2018) - Corporate Fraud by Promoters
**Case**: Malvinder & Shivinder Singh (promoters) v. Shareholders

**Facts**:
- Promoters allegedly siphoned Rs. 500 crore from Fortis Healthcare through related-party transactions
- Fraudulent loans to shell companies controlled by promoters
- Funds diverted to personal investments

**Charges**: Companies Act fraud, criminal breach of trust, cheating

**Outcome**: SEBI investigation; criminal prosecution ongoing; promoters arrested.

**Significance**: Highlighted need for stricter oversight of related-party transactions.

---

## Preventive Measures and Compliance

### For Companies (Corporate Governance)

**Mandatory Compliance**:
- [ ] Appoint independent directors (at least 1/3 of board)
- [ ] Establish Audit Committee with independent directors
- [ ] Conduct statutory audits by qualified auditors
- [ ] Implement whistle-blower policy for reporting fraud
- [ ] Maintain internal controls and risk management systems
- [ ] Comply with disclosure norms (quarterly results, material events)
- [ ] Ensure related-party transactions are at arm's length and disclosed

**Best Practices**:
- Periodic internal audits and forensic audits
- Fraud risk assessment
- Ethics training for employees
- Separation of duties (prevent single person control over funds)
- Use of technology (data analytics to detect anomalies)

### For Investors (Due Diligence)

- [ ] Verify company credentials (CIN, registration) on MCA portal
- [ ] Check SEBI warnings/alerts about fraudulent schemes
- [ ] Review financial statements and audit reports
- [ ] Beware of unrealistic return promises (Ponzi schemes)
- [ ] Diversify investments (don't put all funds in one scheme)
- [ ] Deal only with SEBI-registered intermediaries (brokers, advisors)

### For Individuals (Cyber Fraud Prevention)

- [ ] Never share OTP, passwords, CVV with anyone (banks never ask)
- [ ] Verify website URLs before entering credentials (check https:// and domain)
- [ ] Use strong, unique passwords; enable two-factor authentication
- [ ] Install antivirus software and keep systems updated
- [ ] Report suspicious emails/messages to bank/authorities
- [ ] Monitor bank statements regularly for unauthorized transactions
- [ ] Use credit cards (better fraud protection than debit cards) for online transactions

---

## Reporting Fraud

### Corporate Fraud
- **Registrar of Companies (ROC)**: File complaint under Companies Act
- **SEBI**: For listed companies, securities fraud
- **Economic Offences Wing (EOW)**: State police specialized in economic crimes
- **Serious Fraud Investigation Office (SFIO)**: Central agency for complex corporate frauds

### Banking Fraud
- **Bank's fraud monitoring cell**: Report immediately
- **RBI Banking Ombudsman**: For grievance redressal
- **Police/EOW**: File FIR for criminal prosecution

### Cyber Fraud
- **National Cybercrime Reporting Portal**: cybercrime.gov.in
- **Local cybercrime police station**
- **Bank/Payment gateway**: For transaction reversal/freeze

### Securities Fraud
- **SEBI Complaints Redress System (SCORES)**: scores.gov.in
- **Stock exchanges**: NSE, BSE investor grievance cells

---

## Punishment Summary

| **Type of Fraud** | **Statute** | **Punishment** | **Nature** |
|-------------------|-------------|----------------|------------|
| **Corporate Fraud (≥ Rs. 10L)** | Companies Act 447 | 6 months - 10 years + fine (≥ fraud amount, up to 3x) | Cognizable, Non-bailable |
| **Corporate Fraud (< Rs. 10L)** | Companies Act 447 | Up to 5 years OR fine up to Rs. 50L OR both | Non-cognizable, Bailable |
| **Insider Trading** | SEBI Act + Companies Act | Up to 10 years + fine up to Rs. 25 crore or 3x profit | Cognizable, Non-bailable |
| **Cheating (General)** | IPC 420 | Up to 7 years + fine | Cognizable, Non-bailable |
| **Banking Fraud** | Banking Regulation Act + IPC | Up to 3 years (BRA) + 7 years (IPC 420) | Cognizable, Non-bailable |
| **Cyber Fraud (Identity Theft)** | IT Act 66C | Up to 3 years + fine up to Rs. 1 lakh | Cognizable, Bailable |
| **Cyber Fraud (Cheating by Personation)** | IT Act 66D | Up to 3 years + fine up to Rs. 1 lakh | Cognizable, Bailable |

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Fraud Requires Criminal Intent**: Mere breach of contract or business failure is not fraud; there must be dishonest intent from inception.

2. **Multiple Statutes**: Fraud is prosecuted under Companies Act (corporate), SEBI Act (securities), IT Act (cyber), IPC (general), and specialized laws.

3. **Severe Punishment**: Corporate fraud can result in up to 10 years imprisonment + fines up to 3 times fraud amount.

4. **Director Liability**: Directors, KMPs, and auditors can be personally prosecuted for corporate fraud (vicarious liability).

5. **Fugitive Economic Offenders Act**: Economic offenders who flee India can have all assets confiscated without trial.

6. **Investor Protection**: SEBI has strong powers to investigate, prosecute, and penalize securities fraud; disgorgement of profits.

7. **Cyber Fraud Rising**: Online fraud (phishing, identity theft, UPI fraud) is increasing; awareness and reporting critical.

8. **Civil and Criminal Remedies**: Fraud victims can pursue both criminal prosecution (imprisonment) and civil suits (damages).

---

## Conclusion

Fraud is a pervasive economic crime that undermines trust in financial systems, markets, and commerce. India has developed a comprehensive anti-fraud framework across multiple statutes - Companies Act for corporate fraud, SEBI Act for securities fraud, IT Act for cyber fraud, and IPC for general fraud.

Recent landmark cases (Satyam, Nirav Modi, Vijay Mallya) have exposed systemic vulnerabilities and led to legislative reforms: stricter auditing standards, Fugitive Economic Offenders Act, enhanced SEBI powers, and mandatory corporate governance norms.

However, enforcement challenges persist: complex investigations, lengthy trials, accused fleeing jurisdiction, and proving criminal intent. Effective fraud prevention requires a multi-pronged approach: robust regulatory oversight, corporate governance, investor awareness, technological safeguards, and a swift justice system.

As India's economy grows and digital transactions proliferate, vigilance against fraud - from boardroom conspiracies to WhatsApp scams - is more critical than ever.

---

**Document Version**: 1.0
**Effective Date**: Current as of December 6, 2025
**Last Updated**: December 6, 2025
**Prepared by**: Legal Protocol Documentation System
**Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)

---

**Disclaimer**: This protocol is for informational and educational purposes only and does not constitute legal advice. For specific legal matters involving fraud, consult a qualified advocate specializing in corporate law, securities law, or economic offences.

## IL-CRIM-GENERAL-EXCEPTIONS.md
---

# General Exceptions to Criminal Liability - India Legal Protocol

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-CRIM-GENERAL-EXCEPTIONS
- **Version**: 1.0.0
- **Effective Date**: January 2025
- **Last Updated**: January 2025
- **Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)
- **Domain**: Criminal Law
- **Subdomain**: Evidence and Defenses
- **IPC Provisions**: Sections 76-106 (Chapter IV - General Exceptions)
- **BNS 2023 Provisions**: Sections 6-26 (Chapter III - General Exceptions)
- **Evidence Act Provisions**: Section 105 (Burden of proving exception)
- **Related Protocols**: IL-CRIM-BURDEN-PROOF, IL-CRIM-TRIAL, IL-CRIM-IPC-BNS-TRANSITION

---

## Overview and Purpose

### What are General Exceptions?

**General Exceptions** are **circumstances** or **conditions** that **exempt a person from criminal liability** even though they committed an act that would otherwise constitute an offence.

### Purpose

1. **Recognize Absence of Mens Rea**: No guilty mind due to mistake, coercion, insanity
2. **Justify Conduct**: Act done in lawful exercise of right (e.g., self-defense, official duty)
3. **Balance Rights**: Individual's right to defend vs state's power to punish
4. **Fairness**: Don't punish those who lack culpability

### Burden of Proof (Section 105 Evidence Act / BSA)

**Burden on Accused**: If accused claims benefit of general exception, burden on **accused** to prove exception exists

**Standard**: **Preponderance of probabilities** (not beyond reasonable doubt)

**See Protocol**: IL-CRIM-BURDEN-PROOF.md

---

## Legal Framework

### IPC 1860 - Chapter IV: General Exceptions (Sections 76-106)

**Old Law**: Effective until June 30, 2024

### BNS 2023 - Chapter III: General Exceptions (Sections 6-26)

**New Law**: Effective from July 1, 2024

**Material Changes**: Minimal; mostly renumbering and language modernization

### Evidence Act Section 105 / BSA Section 105

**Burden of Proving Exception**:
"When a person is accused of any offence, the burden of proving the existence of circumstances bringing the case within any of the General Exceptions... is upon him..."

---

## Classification of General Exceptions

### Category 1: Absence of Criminal Intent (Mens Rea)

**Principle**: No guilty mind, no crime

**Exceptions**:
1. Act of judge (Section 77-78 / BNS 7)
2. Mistake of fact (Section 76, 79 / BNS 6, 9)
3. Accident (Section 80 / BNS 10)
4. Infancy (Sections 82-83 / BNS 11-12)
5. Insanity (Section 84 / BNS 13)
6. Intoxication (Sections 85-86 / BNS 18-19)

### Category 2: Acts Done Under Compulsion

**Principle**: Person coerced, no free will

**Exceptions**:
7. Necessity (Section 81 / BNS 8)
8. Duress (Section 94 / BNS 20)

### Category 3: Acts Justified by Law

**Principle**: Act done in lawful exercise of authority/right

**Exceptions**:
9. Judicial acts (Section 77 / BNS 7)
10. Acts under authority of law (Section 76 / BNS 6)
11. Acts done by consent (Sections 87-93 / BNS 21-22)
12. Communication in good faith (Section 79 / BNS 9)

### Category 4: Right of Private Defense

**Principle**: Inherent right to protect self and property

**Exceptions**:
13. Private defense of body (Sections 96-106 / BNS 14-24)
14. Private defense of property (Sections 96-106 / BNS 14-24)

---

## Section-by-Section Analysis

### Category 1: Absence of Criminal Intent

#### Section 76 IPC / BNS Section 6: Act Done by Person Bound by Law

**Provision**: Nothing is an offence which is done by a person who is **bound by law** to do it.

**Rationale**: Person obeying law cannot be penalized

**Example**: Police officer arresting person on warrant from court
- Officer bound by law to execute warrant
- Protected by Section 76

**Limitation**: Protection only if act truly mandated by law

**Landmark Case**: **Emperor v. Khwaja Nazir Ahmad (1945)**
- Official acting in good faith belief of legal duty protected
- But if patently illegal, no protection

#### Section 77 IPC / BNS Section 7: Act of Judge When Acting Judicially

**Provision**: Nothing is an offence which is done by a Judge when acting **judicially** in exercise of powers believed in good faith to be within his authority.

**Rationale**: Judicial immunity - judges must decide without fear of prosecution

**Conditions**:
1. Act done by **Judge**
2. When acting **judicially**
3. In exercise of powers **believed in good faith** to be within authority

**Limitation**: Does not protect **malicious** or **corrupt** acts

**Example**: Judge sentences accused to imprisonment; later sentence found to be beyond jurisdiction
- If done in good faith, judge protected by Section 77

**Landmark Case**: **Tukaram Ganpat v. State of Maharashtra (1979)**
- Judicial immunity extends to orders passed in good faith
- Does not cover acts done maliciously or in bad faith

#### Section 78 IPC (Merged into Section 77 BNS 7): Act Done Pursuant to Judgment of Court

**Provision**: Nothing is an offence done in pursuance of judgment or order of court, even if court lacked jurisdiction, if person believed in good faith court had jurisdiction.

**Rationale**: Person executing court order should not be penalized for court's jurisdictional error

**Example**: Bailiff executes attachment order; later court found to lack jurisdiction
- Bailiff protected if acted in good faith

#### Section 79 IPC / BNS Section 9: Act Done in Good Faith Belief of Justification by Law

**Provision**: Nothing is an offence done by person who is **justified by law** or who **believes in good faith** he is justified by law.

**Conditions**:
1. Act done in **good faith**
2. **Belief** that law justifies act
3. Belief is **reasonable**

**Example**: Police officer arrests person mistakenly believing arrest warrant valid
- If belief in good faith and reasonable, officer protected

**Landmark Case**: **State of Orissa v. Ram Bahadur Thapa (1960)**
- Good faith means honesty of intention
- Mistake must be reasonable

#### Section 80 IPC / BNS Section 10: Accident in Doing Lawful Act by Lawful Means

**Provision**: Nothing is an offence which is done by **accident** or **misfortune**, without criminal intention or knowledge, in doing **lawful act** by **lawful means** with proper care and caution.

**Five Essential Ingredients**:
1. Act done by **accident or misfortune**
2. Without **criminal intention** or **knowledge**
3. Act was **lawful**
4. Means were **lawful**
5. Proper **care and caution** exercised

**Example**: Person driving carefully; suddenly dog runs on road; person swerves but hits pedestrian
- Lawful act (driving), lawful means (following traffic rules), proper care
- Accident protected by Section 80

**Limitation**: **No protection if negligence**

**Landmark Case**: **Tulsidas Kanolkar v. State of Goa (2003)**
- Accused driving rashly; hit pedestrian
- NOT protected by Section 80 (rash and negligent driving not "proper care")

**Difference from Section 79**:
- **Section 79**: Mistake of law/fact
- **Section 80**: Pure accident (no fault at all)

#### Sections 82-83 IPC / BNS Sections 11-12: Act of Child

##### Section 82 IPC / BNS Section 11: Act of Child Under Seven Years
**"Nothing is an offence which is done by a child **under seven years** of age."**

**Absolute Immunity**: Child below 7 years **incapable** of committing crime (doli incapax)

**Rationale**: Child lacks capacity to understand nature and consequences of act

**No Exceptions**: Applies to **all offences** (including murder)

**Example**: 6-year-old child fires gun, killing person
- Child protected by Section 82; cannot be prosecuted

##### Section 83 IPC / BNS Section 12: Act of Child Above Seven and Under Twelve Years
**"Nothing is an offence which is done by a child **above seven and under twelve years** who has not attained **sufficient maturity** of understanding to judge nature and consequences of conduct."**

**Conditional Immunity**: Child 7-12 years protected **unless** has maturity to understand

**Burden**: Prosecution must prove child had **sufficient maturity** to understand

**Factors**: Intelligence, education, environment, nature of act

**Landmark Case**: **Ulla Mahapatra v. King (1950)**
- 11-year-old boy pushed another child off moving train (causing death)
- Held: Boy had sufficient maturity to understand consequences
- Section 83 did not protect

**Juvenile Justice Act 2015**:
- Now special provisions for children below 18 years
- JJ Act overrides IPC/BNS for juveniles
- Section 82-83 still relevant for children 7-12 if JJ Act not applicable

#### Section 84 IPC / BNS Section 13: Act of Person of Unsound Mind

**"Nothing is an offence which is done by a person who, at the time of doing it, by reason of **unsoundness of mind**, is **incapable** of knowing nature of act OR that what he is doing is **wrong or contrary to law**."**

**Three Essential Elements**:
1. **Unsoundness of mind** at time of act
2. **Incapable** of knowing:
   - **Nature of act**, OR
   - That act is **wrong or contrary to law**

**Based on M'Naghten Rules (English law)**: "Disease of mind" preventing knowledge of nature/wrongfulness

**Burden**: On **accused** to prove insanity (Section 105 Evidence Act)

**Standard**: **Preponderance of probabilities**

**Presumption**: Every person is presumed **sane** until contrary proved

**Medical vs Legal Insanity**:
- **Medical insanity**: Broad (any mental disorder)
- **Legal insanity** (Section 84): Narrow (must be incapable of knowing nature/wrongfulness at time of act)

**Landmark Cases**:

##### Dahyabhai Chhaganbhai Thakker v. State of Gujarat (1964)
- Burden on accused to prove unsoundness of mind
- Must show unsoundness existed at time of offence
- Medical evidence helpful but not conclusive

##### Ratan Lal v. State of M.P. (1971)
- Insanity must be proved at **time of commission** of offence
- Post-offence insanity irrelevant
- Onus on accused; standard is preponderance

##### Surendra Mishra v. State of Jharkhand (2011)
- Conduct before and after offence relevant to prove insanity
- Sudden impulse without motive may indicate insanity
- But burden remains on accused

**Difference from Diminished Responsibility**:
- **Section 84**: Complete exemption (acquittal)
- **Diminished Responsibility**: Not recognized in India (unlike UK)

#### Sections 85-86 IPC / BNS Sections 18-19: Intoxication

##### Section 85 IPC / BNS Section 18: Act of Person Incapable of Judgment by Intoxication

**"Nothing is an offence done by person who, at time of doing it, is **incapable of knowing nature** of act OR that what doing is wrong or contrary to law, by reason of **intoxication** caused by **involuntary** consumption of intoxicating substance."**

**Conditions for Protection**:
1. **Intoxication** at time of act
2. **Incapable** of knowing nature or wrongfulness
3. Intoxication was **involuntary** (without knowledge or against will)

**Voluntary Intoxication**: **NO protection** under Section 85

**Rationale**: Person who voluntarily intoxicates cannot claim exemption

**Landmark Case**: **Basdev v. State of Pepsu (1956)**
- Accused murdered wife while drunk
- Voluntary intoxication; Section 85 not applicable
- Convicted

##### Section 86 IPC / BNS Section 19: Intoxication When Defense and When Not

**Provides nuance to Section 85**:

**Rule 1**: Voluntary intoxication is **defense** ONLY if offence requires **specific intent** (mens rea) AND intoxication prevented formation of that intent

**Example**: Murder requires **intention** to kill
- If voluntary intoxication prevented formation of intention
- May be convicted of **lesser offence** (e.g., culpable homicide not amounting to murder)

**Rule 2**: Voluntary intoxication is **NOT defense** if offence requires only **general intent** OR **knowledge** that act is dangerous

**Landmark Case**: **Toofan Singh v. State of Punjab (2008)**
- Accused voluntarily drunk; committed murder
- Held: Voluntary intoxication not defense for murder
- Intention inferred from act

**Involuntary Intoxication**:
- If truly involuntary (drugged without knowledge)
- Complete defense under Section 85

### Category 2: Acts Done Under Compulsion

#### Section 81 IPC / BNS Section 8: Act Likely to Cause Harm Done Without Criminal Intent for Lawful Object

**"Nothing is an offence by reason of any harm which it may cause to a person for whose benefit it is done in **good faith**, even without that person's consent, if circumstances are such that it is **impossible to obtain consent** OR person is **incapable of giving consent**, and person doing act **does not intend** to cause harm and **does not know** act is likely to cause harm other than harm which person intended to **prevent or avoid**."**

**Principle**: **Necessity** - lesser harm to prevent greater harm

**Example**: Surgeon amputates gangrenous leg to save patient's life (patient unconscious, cannot consent)
- Harm (amputation) done to prevent greater harm (death)
- Protected by Section 81

**Conditions**:
1. Act done in **good faith**
2. For **benefit** of person harmed
3. **Impossible to obtain consent** OR person **incapable** of consenting
4. No **intent** to cause harm beyond what necessary
5. **Lawful object** (preventing/avoiding harm)

**Landmark Case**: **Dr. Jacob Mathew v. State of Punjab (2005)**
- Medical negligence case
- Section 81 protects doctors acting in good faith, without gross negligence
- Simple lack of skill not criminal if good faith

#### Section 94 IPC / BNS Section 20: Act Compelled by Threats

**"Nothing is an offence if person coerced to do it by **threats** which reasonably cause **apprehension** of **instant death**."**

**Three Essential Elements**:
1. **Threat** of instant death
2. Threat creates **reasonable apprehension**
3. Person **compelled** to do act

**Exceptions** (Section 94 Explanation):
Does **NOT apply** to:
- Murder
- Offences against state (treason, waging war)

**Rationale**: Person acting under mortal fear lacks free will

**Landmark Case**: **Nathi Lal v. State of H.P. (1999)**
- Accused claimed acted under duress
- Court held threat must be **immediate** and **instant death**
- Remote or future threat not sufficient

**Limitation**: Very narrow scope
- Threat must be of **instant death** (not mere injury or future death)
- Not applicable to murder or state offences

### Category 3: Acts Justified by Law

#### Sections 87-93 IPC / BNS Sections 21-22: Consent

**General Principle**: Act not an offence if done with **valid consent**

##### Section 87 IPC / BNS Section 21: Act Not Intended to Cause Death, Done with Consent

**"Nothing which is not intended to cause death or grievous hurt, and which is not known to be likely to cause death or grievous hurt, is an offence **by reason of any harm** which it may cause with **consent** of person suffering harm."**

**Application**: Sports, medical procedures, tattooing

**Example**: Boxing match - punches cause hurt
- Both boxers consent
- No intent to cause death/grievous hurt
- Protected by Section 87

**Limitation**:
- **Death**: If death or grievous hurt intended/likely, consent irrelevant
- Cannot consent to being killed (euthanasia not legal in India)

##### Section 88 IPC / BNS Section 22: Act Not Intended to Cause Death, Done for Benefit with Consent

**Medical Procedures**: Act done in good faith for person's benefit with consent (even if causes harm)

**Example**: Surgery causing pain and scars
- Done for benefit (treatment)
- With consent
- Protected

**Landmark Case**: **Samira Kohli v. Dr. Prabha Manchanda (2008)**
- "Informed consent" necessary for medical procedures
- Patient must be informed of risks, alternatives
- Consent must be voluntary

##### Section 89 IPC: Act Done for Benefit of Child/Insane Person Without Consent

**Guardian's Consent**: Act done in good faith for benefit of child (<12) or insane person, without consent, by guardian

**Example**: Parent allowing surgery on child unable to consent

##### Section 90 IPC / BNS Section 23: Consent Not Given Freely

**Consent is **NOT valid** if**:
1. Given under **fear of injury** or **misconception of fact**
2. Person giving consent is **incapable** (child, insane, intoxicated)

**Example**: Sexual intercourse with woman by impersonating her husband
- Consent obtained by misconception of fact (she thought partner was husband)
- NOT valid consent; rape (IPC 375 / BNS 63)

**Landmark Case**: **State of Karnataka v. Manjanna (2000)**
- Sexual intercourse on pretext of marriage
- Consent obtained by misconception
- Rape

##### Sections 91-93 IPC: Exclusions from Consent Protection

Acts where **consent does NOT protect**:
- **Section 91**: Consent to suffer harm from act not known/believed to cause death/grievous hurt (but does cause it) - **NO protection**
- **Section 92**: Act done in good faith for benefit WITHOUT consent (if consent could have been obtained) - **NO protection**
- **Section 93**: Communication made in good faith - **protected**

### Category 4: Right of Private Defense

**Sections 96-106 IPC / BNS Sections 14-24**

**Fundamental Right**: Every person has **inherent right** to defend:
1. **Self** (private defense of body)
2. **Property** (private defense of property)

#### Section 96 IPC / BNS Section 14: Right of Private Defense

**"Nothing is an offence which is done in exercise of **right of private defense**."**

**Two Types**:
1. Private defense of **body** (Sections 97-106)
2. Private defense of **property** (Sections 97, 99-106)

**Rationale**: State cannot be everywhere; individuals must protect themselves from immediate threats

**Constitutional Right**: Included in **right to life** (Article 21)

#### Section 97 IPC / BNS Section 15: When Right Extends

**Right of private defense extends** against acts which are **offences** affecting:
- Human body (assault, wrongful restraint, etc.)
- Property (theft, robbery, mischief, trespass)

**Limitations**:
- Does not extend against **public servants** acting in good faith (even if technically beyond powers)
- Does not extend if reasonable opportunity to seek protection from public authorities

**Example**: Cannot use private defense against police officer arresting (even if arrest unlawful) - must challenge in court

#### Section 99 IPC / BNS Section 16: Restrictions on Right of Private Defense

**Six Restrictions** (Right does **NOT extend** to):

##### 1. Acts Not Reasonably Apprehended to Cause Harm
- If no reasonable apprehension of danger, no right to defend
- Example: Person jokingly approaches; no actual threat - no right to use force

##### 2. Time for Seeking Public Protection Available
- If time to seek police/public authorities, must do so instead of using force
- Example: Person threatens to attack tomorrow - seek police protection, don't attack preemptively

##### 3. Risk of Harm to Innocent Person
- If defending likely to harm innocent bystander, better to refrain
- Balance harm to self vs harm to innocent

##### 4. More Harm Than Necessary
- Can use **only force necessary** to repel attack
- **Proportionality** principle
- Cannot use excessive force

**Landmark Case**: **Darshan Singh v. State of Punjab (2010)**
- Accused shot attacker with gun when pushed
- Excessive force; not proportionate to attack
- Right of private defense not available

##### 5. Against Public Servant Acting in Good Faith
- Cannot defend against public servant (police, etc.) acting within authority, even if minor excess
- Exception: If public servant acts in **bad faith** or **grossly exceeds** authority

##### 6. Not Commenced or Continued After Reasonable Apprehension Ceases
- Right exists **only while danger exists**
- Once danger passed, cannot continue attacking
- Example: Attacker knocked unconscious; cannot continue beating - danger ceased

#### Section 100 IPC / BNS Section 17: When Right of Private Defense of Body Extends to Causing Death

**Right to cause death** in private defense of body extends if offence:
1. Reasonably causes **apprehension of death**
2. Reasonably causes **apprehension of grievous hurt**
3. **Assault with intent** to commit rape
4. **Assault with intent** to gratify unnatural lust
5. **Assault with intent** to kidnap or abduct
6. **Acid attack** or attempt
7. **Wrongful confinement** if apprehension cannot otherwise be removed

**Principle**: Life-threatening attack justifies lethal force in defense

**Landmark Case**: **Vidhya Singh v. State of M.P. (1971)**
- Accused shot deceased who attacked with lathi (stick)
- Held: Grievous hurt apprehended; right to cause death applied
- Acquitted

#### Section 101 IPC / BNS Section 18: When Right Begins and Continues

**Right begins**: As soon as **reasonable apprehension** of danger arises

**Right continues**: Until danger ceases

**Important**: Need not wait for actual attack; reasonable apprehension sufficient

**Example**: Person advancing with weapon, threatening to kill
- Right begins when reasonable apprehension arises (when person starts advancing)
- Need not wait for first blow

#### Section 102 IPC / BNS Section 19: Right of Private Defense Against Death

**Right extends to causing death** if offense reasonably causes apprehension of death or grievous hurt

**Applies to**: Assault, kidnapping, rape, acid attack, wrongful confinement (as per Section 100)

#### Section 103 IPC / BNS Section 20: Right of Private Defense of Body Against Acts Not Covered by Section 100

**For offences NOT covered by Section 100**:
- Right to private defense exists
- But **NOT to cause death**
- Can cause **any harm other than death**

**Example**: Person pushes you (simple assault, no grievous hurt apprehended)
- Right to push back, restrain
- But NOT to kill

#### Sections 97-106 IPC / BNS Sections 15-24: Private Defense of Property

**Similar Principles** apply to property defense:

##### Section 105 IPC / BNS Section 23: Right to Cause Death in Defense of Property

**When extends to causing death**:
1. **Robbery** (property taken with violence)
2. **House-breaking at night**
3. **Mischief by fire** (arson) to dwelling/place of worship
4. **Theft, mischief, trespass** if offender causes death/grievous hurt/wrongful restraint to prevent defense

**Rationale**: Serious attacks on property (especially home at night) justify lethal defense

##### Section 106 IPC / BNS Section 24: When Right Begins and Continues (Property)

**Right begins**: When reasonable apprehension of danger to property arises

**Right continues**: Until danger ceases

**Example**: Person breaking into house at night
- Right to defend begins when breaking in starts
- Continues until person leaves or subdued

---

## Burden of Proof for General Exceptions

**Section 105 Evidence Act / BSA**:
"Burden of proving the existence of circumstances bringing the case within any of the General Exceptions... is upon [the accused]."

**Standard**: **Preponderance of probabilities** (not beyond reasonable doubt)

**Procedure**:
1. **Prosecution proves** commission of offence beyond reasonable doubt
2. **Accused claims** general exception applies (e.g., self-defense, insanity)
3. **Accused must prove** exception on preponderance

**If Accused Proves Exception**: Acquittal

**If Accused Fails to Prove**: Conviction (if prosecution proved guilt)

**Landmark Case**: **Dahyabhai Chhaganbhai Thakker v. State of Gujarat (1964)**
- Burden on accused to prove exception
- But standard is preponderance, not beyond reasonable doubt

**See Protocol**: IL-CRIM-BURDEN-PROOF.md

---

## IPC to BNS Transition: General Exceptions

### Comparative Table

| Exception | IPC Section | BNS Section | Material Change? |
|-----------|-------------|-------------|------------------|
| **Act bound by law** | 76 | 6 | **No change** |
| **Judicial acts** | 77-78 | 7 | **Merged into single section** |
| **Mistake of fact / good faith belief** | 79 | 9 | **No change** |
| **Accident** | 80 | 10 | **No change** |
| **Necessity** | 81 | 8 | **No change** |
| **Child <7 years** | 82 | 11 | **No change** |
| **Child 7-12 years** | 83 | 12 | **No change** |
| **Insanity** | 84 | 13 | **No change** |
| **Intoxication (involuntary)** | 85 | 18 | **Section number changed; substance same** |
| **Intoxication (nuanced)** | 86 | 19 | **No change** |
| **Consent** | 87-93 | 21-23 | **Consolidated; substance same** |
| **Duress** | 94 | 20 | **No change** |
| **Private defense of body** | 96-106 | 14-24 | **Section numbers changed; principles same** |
| **Private defense of property** | 97-106 | 15-24 | **Section numbers changed; principles same** |

### Key Observations

**Substantive Law**: **No material change** in general exceptions from IPC to BNS
- Principles remain same
- Conditions for exemption unchanged
- Judicial precedents continue to apply

**Structural Changes**:
- **Section numbers changed**
- **Some consolidation** (e.g., Sections 77-78 IPC merged into Section 7 BNS)
- **Language modernized**

**Practical Impact**: **Minimal**
- Lawyers/courts can rely on IPC-era precedents
- Same defenses available
- Same burden of proof

---

## Practical Compliance Checklist

### For Accused/Defense

#### Identifying Applicable Exception
- [ ] Analyze facts to determine if any exception applies:
  - [ ] Lack of capacity (child, insane, intoxicated)
  - [ ] Mistake of fact
  - [ ] Accident
  - [ ] Necessity
  - [ ] Duress
  - [ ] Consent
  - [ ] Self-defense

#### Building Exception Defense
- [ ] **Gather evidence** proving exception:
  - [ ] Witness testimony (eyewitnesses to self-defense, etc.)
  - [ ] Medical evidence (insanity, injury from attack)
  - [ ] Documentary evidence (age proof for child)
  - [ ] Expert evidence (psychiatrist for insanity, toxicologist for intoxication)

#### At Trial
- [ ] **Plead exception** clearly in statement/pleading
- [ ] **Produce evidence** during defense stage:
  - Defense witnesses
  - Medical/expert reports
  - Accused's testimony (if advisable)
- [ ] **Cross-examine** prosecution witnesses to elicit facts supporting exception
- [ ] In final arguments, show exception proved on **preponderance of probabilities**

#### Common Exceptions - Specific Preparation

**Self-Defense (Sections 96-106 / 14-24 BNS)**:
- [ ] Prove **imminent threat** (danger was immediate)
- [ ] Prove **no safe retreat** (couldn't escape or seek police)
- [ ] Prove **proportionate force** (used only necessary force)
- [ ] Show danger **ceased** (didn't continue attacking after threat ended)

**Insanity (Section 84 / 13 BNS)**:
- [ ] Medical evidence of **mental disorder**
- [ ] Expert testimony (psychiatrist)
- [ ] Evidence of **conduct before/after** offence (irrational behavior)
- [ ] Show **incapacity to know nature** of act OR that it was wrong

**Accident (Section 80 / 10 BNS)**:
- [ ] Show act was **lawful** (e.g., lawful driving)
- [ ] Show means were **lawful** (no rash/negligent driving)
- [ ] Show **proper care** exercised
- [ ] Show harm was **unforeseeable accident**

### For Prosecution

#### Countering Exception Defense
- [ ] **Challenge evidence** of exception:
  - Cross-examine defense witnesses
  - Present contradictory evidence
  - Show accused's conduct inconsistent with claimed exception

#### Specific Counters

**Self-Defense**:
- [ ] Show **no imminent threat** (accused was aggressor OR threat not immediate)
- [ ] Show **excessive force** used (disproportionate to attack)
- [ ] Show **continued after danger ceased** (retaliation, not defense)

**Insanity**:
- [ ] Challenge medical evidence (expert rebuttal)
- [ ] Show **conduct before/after** was rational (planned offence, concealed evidence)
- [ ] Show accused **knew nature** of act and that it was wrong

**Accident**:
- [ ] Show **rash or negligent** conduct (not "proper care")
- [ ] Show act or means were **unlawful**

#### In Final Arguments
- [ ] Show accused **failed to prove** exception on preponderance
- [ ] Highlight **inconsistencies** in exception defense
- [ ] Request conviction as prosecution proved guilt beyond reasonable doubt

### For Courts

#### Evaluating Exception Defense
- [ ] **Burden on accused** to prove exception (Section 105)
- [ ] **Standard**: Preponderance of probabilities (not beyond reasonable doubt)
- [ ] Consider **all evidence** (prosecution and defense)

#### If Exception Proved
- [ ] **Acquit** accused (if exception fully established)
- [ ] OR **Convict of lesser offence** (if exception partially applies)

**Example**: Murder charge; voluntary intoxication proved
- Section 86: May reduce murder to culpable homicide not amounting to murder
- If intoxication prevented formation of specific intent

#### If Exception Not Proved
- [ ] **Convict** if prosecution proved guilt beyond reasonable doubt
- [ ] Write **reasoned judgment** explaining why exception not accepted

---

## Common Errors and Misconceptions

### Errors

1. **Confusing burden**: Thinking prosecution must disprove exception (NO - burden on accused per Section 105)

2. **Confusing standard**: Thinking accused must prove exception beyond reasonable doubt (NO - only preponderance)

3. **Claiming multiple exceptions**: Claiming accident AND self-defense simultaneously (usually mutually exclusive)

4. **Excessive force in self-defense**: Using gun to defend against fist fight (disproportionate; Section 99)

5. **Continuing after danger ceased**: Continuing to beat attacker after they're unconscious (danger ceased; Section 99)

### Misconceptions

**Misconception 1**: "Voluntary intoxication is complete defense"
**Reality**: NO - Section 85 protects only **involuntary** intoxication; Section 86 allows voluntary intoxication as defense ONLY if offence requires specific intent AND intoxication prevented its formation

**Misconception 2**: "Can kill anyone in self-defense"
**Reality**: NO - Can cause death ONLY if offence falls under Section 100 / 17 BNS (apprehension of death, grievous hurt, rape, kidnapping, acid attack)

**Misconception 3**: "Child can never be prosecuted"
**Reality**: NO - Child <7 years has absolute immunity (Section 82); child 7-12 years protected ONLY if lacks maturity (Section 83); child >12 years can be prosecuted (but JJ Act applies if <18)

**Misconception 4**: "Consent always protects"
**Reality**: NO - Cannot consent to death or grievous hurt (Section 87 limitation); consent invalid if obtained by fear/misconception/from incapable person (Section 90)

**Misconception 5**: "Insanity = any mental illness"
**Reality**: NO - Legal insanity (Section 84) requires **incapacity to know nature of act OR that it was wrong**; mere mental illness insufficient

---

## Related Protocols and Cross-References

### Directly Related
- **IL-CRIM-BURDEN-PROOF**: Section 105 burden of proving exception
- **IL-CRIM-TRIAL**: Exception defense raised during trial
- **IL-CRIM-APPEALS**: Exception defense in appellate proceedings

### Offence-Specific
- **IL-CRIM-MURDER**: Section 100/102 private defense (causing death)
- **IL-CRIM-RAPE**: Section 90 invalid consent
- **IL-CRIM-ASSAULT**: Sections 87-89 consent to hurt

### Transition
- **IL-CRIM-IPC-BNS-TRANSITION**: Comprehensive IPC to BNS mapping

---

## Conclusion

**General Exceptions** are **crucial safeguards** ensuring:
- **No punishment without culpability**: Recognizing absence of mens rea
- **Protection of lawful conduct**: Judicial acts, official duty, consent
- **Right to defend**: Inherent right to protect self and property
- **Fairness**: Balancing state power with individual rights

**Key Principles**:
1. **Burden on accused** to prove exception (Section 105)
2. **Standard**: Preponderance of probabilities (not beyond reasonable doubt)
3. **Insanity** (Section 84/13 BNS): Incapacity to know nature or wrongfulness
4. **Self-Defense** (Sections 96-106 / 14-24 BNS): Imminent threat, proportionate force, danger must cease
5. **Consent** (Sections 87-93 / 21-23 BNS): Valid only if free, informed; cannot consent to death/grievous hurt
6. **Accident** (Section 80/10 BNS): Lawful act, lawful means, proper care
7. **Duress** (Section 94/20 BNS): Threat of instant death (NOT applicable to murder)
8. **Intoxication** (Sections 85-86 / 18-19 BNS): Only involuntary intoxication is complete defense

**IPC to BNS Transition**:
- **No material change** in general exceptions
- Section numbers changed (76-106 IPC → 6-26 BNS)
- Principles, conditions, burden - all same
- All IPC-era precedents continue to apply

**Practical Importance**:
- Defense lawyers must identify applicable exceptions early
- Gather evidence to prove exception on preponderance
- Courts must apply Section 105 correctly (burden on accused, preponderance standard)
- Prosecution must challenge exception evidence effectively

**Ultimate Safeguard**: General exceptions ensure criminal law punishes only those with **mens rea** (guilty mind) and protects those acting in **lawful exercise of rights** or under **disabling circumstances**.

---

## Document Control

**Version History**:
- v1.0.0 (January 2025): Initial protocol creation

**Review Schedule**: Annual review; immediate update for landmark judgments or amendments

**Feedback**: Submit to protocol administrator

**Disclaimer**: Informational purposes only. Consult qualified legal professionals for specific cases.

---

**End of Protocol: IL-CRIM-GENERAL-EXCEPTIONS**


---
## IL-CRIM-INVESTIGATION.md
---

# Criminal Investigation - India Legal Protocol

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-CRIM-INVESTIGATION
- **Version**: 1.0.0
- **Effective Date**: January 2025
- **Last Updated**: January 2025
- **Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)
- **Domain**: Criminal Law
- **Subdomain**: Criminal Procedure
- **IPC Provisions**: N/A
- **BNS 2023 Provisions**: N/A
- **CrPC Provisions**: Sections 2(h), 36, 154-157, 160-176
- **BNSS 2023 Provisions**: Sections 2(h), 35, 173-195
- **Related Protocols**: IL-CRIM-FIR, IL-CRIM-ARREST-CUSTODY, IL-CRIM-EVIDENCE

---

## Overview and Purpose

### What is Criminal Investigation?

Criminal investigation is the **systematic process of gathering, preserving, and analyzing evidence** to determine whether a crime has been committed, identify the perpetrator(s), and build a case for prosecution. Under Indian criminal law, investigation is a **police function** governed primarily by the Code of Criminal Procedure (CrPC) 1973 and the new Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita (BNSS) 2023.

### Definition Under Law

**CrPC Section 2(h) / BNSS Section 2(h)**:
