## IL-CRIM-ROBBERY-DACOITY.md
---

# India Criminal Law: Robbery and Dacoity (Loot aur Dakaiti)

## Protocol ID
**IL-CRIM-ROBBERY-DACOITY**

## Domain
Criminal Law > Crimes Against Property

## Jurisdiction
India (All States and Union Territories)

## Statutory Framework

### Indian Penal Code (IPC), 1860
- **Section 390**: Definition of Robbery
- **Section 391**: Definition of Dacoity
- **Section 392**: Punishment for Robbery
- **Section 393**: Attempt to Commit Robbery
- **Section 394**: Voluntarily Causing Hurt in Committing Robbery
- **Section 395**: Punishment for Dacoity
- **Section 396**: Dacoity with Murder
- **Section 397**: Robbery or Dacoity with Attempt to Cause Death or Grievous Hurt
- **Section 398**: Attempt to Commit Robbery or Dacoity When Armed with Deadly Weapon
- **Section 399**: Making Preparation to Commit Dacoity
- **Section 400**: Punishment for Belonging to Gang of Dacoits
- **Section 401**: Punishment for Belonging to Gang of Thieves
- **Section 402**: Assembling for Purpose of Committing Dacoity

### Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS), 2023 (Effective July 1, 2024)
- **Section 309**: Definition of Robbery (formerly IPC 390)
- **Section 310**: Definition of Dacoity (formerly IPC 391)
- **Section 311**: Punishment for Robbery (formerly IPC 392)
- **Section 312**: Attempt to Commit Robbery (formerly IPC 393)
- **Section 313**: Causing Hurt in Committing Robbery (formerly IPC 394)
- **Section 314**: Punishment for Dacoity (formerly IPC 395)
- **Section 315**: Dacoity with Murder (formerly IPC 396)
- **Section 316**: Robbery or Dacoity with Attempt to Cause Death/GH (formerly IPC 397)
- **Section 317**: Attempt to Commit Robbery/Dacoity When Armed (formerly IPC 398)
- **Section 318**: Making Preparation to Commit Dacoity (formerly IPC 399)
- **Section 319**: Belonging to Gang of Dacoits (formerly IPC 400)
- **Section 320**: Belonging to Gang of Thieves (formerly IPC 401)
- **Section 321**: Assembling for Dacoity (formerly IPC 402)

---

## Overview

**Robbery** is theft aggravated by the use of violence or putting a person in fear of instant death, hurt, or wrongful restraint, either during the commission of theft or to facilitate the retention of stolen property or to aid in escape.

**Dacoity** is robbery committed by five or more persons acting in concert.

These are among the most serious property offences in Indian criminal law because they combine the elements of property violation with violence or threat of violence against persons.

### Key Characteristics
1. **Robbery = Theft + Violence/Fear**: Robbery is an aggravated form of theft where violence or fear of instant harm is used
2. **Dacoity = Collective Robbery**: Dacoity requires five or more persons acting together
3. **Severe Punishment**: Both offences carry mandatory rigorous imprisonment, with minimum sentences
4. **Instant Harm Requirement**: The fear must be of **instant** death, hurt, or restraint (not future harm)
5. **Non-Bailable**: Both robbery and dacoity are non-bailable, cognizable offences

---

## Definition of Robbery (IPC Section 390 / BNS Section 309)

### Full Legal Definition

**IPC Section 390**: In all robbery there is either **theft** or **extortion**.

#### Two Types of Robbery

**Type 1: Robbery as Aggravated Theft**

**When theft is robbery**: Theft is robbery if, in order to the committing of the theft, or in committing the theft, or in carrying away or attempting to carry away property obtained by the theft, the offender, for that end:
- Voluntarily causes or attempts to cause to any person **death**, **hurt**, or **wrongful restraint**, OR
- Puts or attempts to put any person in **fear of instant death, hurt, or wrongful restraint**

**Type 2: Robbery as Aggravated Extortion**

**When extortion is robbery**: Extortion is robbery if the offender, at the time of committing the extortion:
- Is in the presence of the person put in fear, AND
- Commits the extortion by putting that person in fear of **instant death, instant hurt, or instant wrongful restraint** to that person or some other person, AND
- Thereby induces the person so put in fear then and there to deliver up the thing extorted

### Essential Ingredients of Robbery

#### For Robbery by Theft (Most Common Type)

1. **There must be theft** (all seven ingredients of IPC Section 378 must be present)
2. **Violence or fear must be used** in one of the following three stages:
   - **Before the theft**: In order to commit the theft
   - **During the theft**: While committing the theft
   - **After the theft**: In carrying away or attempting to carry away the stolen property
3. **The violence or fear must be of instant harm**: Death, hurt, or wrongful restraint must be instant, not future
4. **Purpose of violence/fear**: To facilitate the theft, retention of property, or escape

#### For Robbery by Extortion (Rare Type)

1. **There must be extortion** (IPC Section 383: intentionally putting person in fear to induce delivery of property)
2. **Offender must be in presence** of the victim at the time of committing extortion
3. **Fear must be of instant harm**: Instant death, instant hurt, or instant wrongful restraint
4. **Delivery induced by fear**: Victim delivers property then and there due to fear of instant harm

### Explanation 1 to Section 390: Use of Force
The offender is said to use force to any person if he uses force to that person in order to perpetrate the robbery, and if he uses force to any person in order to take or attempt to take away property, whether or not that person is in possession of the property.

**Example**: A attacks B and causes hurt to B in order to snatch B's bag. A commits robbery even though B may not have been holding the bag at that exact moment (bag was on a table nearby).

---

## Definition of Dacoity (IPC Section 391 / BNS Section 310)

### Full Legal Definition

**IPC Section 391**: When **five or more persons** conjointly commit or attempt to commit a **robbery**, or where the whole number of persons conjointly committing or attempting to commit a robbery, and persons present and aiding such commission or attempt, amount to five or more, every person so committing, attempting, or aiding, is said to commit **dacoity**.

### Essential Ingredients of Dacoity

1. **Five or more persons**: Minimum of five persons required
2. **Acting conjointly**: Must act in concert or common intention
3. **Committing or attempting robbery**: The act must constitute robbery (theft + violence/fear of instant harm)
4. **Includes aiders**: Persons present and aiding the commission also count toward the total of five

### Key Distinction: Robbery vs. Dacoity

| **Element** | **Robbery** | **Dacoity** |
|-------------|-------------|-------------|
| **Number of Offenders** | One or more (no minimum) | Minimum **five** persons |
| **Nature** | Individual or group offence | Collective offence only |
| **Punishment** | Minimum 1 year, up to 10 years RI | Minimum **7 years** RI, may extend to life |
| **Gravity** | Serious offence | More serious (collective violence) |

---

## Punishment for Robbery

### Section 392 IPC / Section 311 BNS: Simple Robbery
**Punishment**:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Minimum **1 year**, may extend to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Example**: A snatches B's purse from her hand with force. A commits robbery under Section 392.

### Section 393 IPC / Section 312 BNS: Attempt to Commit Robbery
**Punishment**:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Example**: A attempts to snatch B's chain but B resists and A fails to take the chain. A has attempted robbery.

### Section 394 IPC / Section 313 BNS: Voluntarily Causing Hurt in Committing Robbery
Whoever, in committing robbery, **voluntarily causes hurt**, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment for life**, OR
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Court of Session

**Example**: A snatches B's wallet and in the process punches B in the face, causing injury. A commits robbery with hurt under Section 394.

**Note**: "Hurt" means bodily pain, disease, or infirmity (IPC Section 319). If grievous hurt is caused, Section 397 applies.

---

## Punishment for Dacoity

### Section 395 IPC / Section 314 BNS: Simple Dacoity
**Punishment**:
- **Imprisonment for life**, OR
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Minimum **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Court of Session

**Example**: A gang of six persons robs a bank at gunpoint. All six commit dacoity under Section 395.

### Section 396 IPC / Section 315 BNS: Dacoity with Murder
If any one of **five or more persons**, who are conjointly committing dacoity, **commits murder** in so committing dacoity, **every one of those persons** shall be punished with:
- **Death**, OR
- **Imprisonment for life**, OR
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Court of Session

**Principle of Joint Liability**: Even if only one member of the gang commits murder, all members of the dacoity gang are liable for murder if the murder was committed in the course of dacoity.

**Example**: A gang of seven persons commits dacoity in a house. One member shoots and kills the owner during the robbery. All seven are liable for dacoity with murder under Section 396, even if only one fired the shot.

**Landmark Case**: *Mohan Singh v. State of Punjab* (1963) - Supreme Court held that all members of a dacoity gang are constructively liable for murder committed by any member during dacoity.

### Section 397 IPC / Section 316 BNS: Robbery or Dacoity with Attempt to Cause Death or Grievous Hurt
If at the time of committing robbery or dacoity, the offender uses any **deadly weapon**, or causes **grievous hurt** to any person, or **attempts to cause death or grievous hurt**, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment for life**, OR
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Minimum **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Court of Session

**Example**: A gang robs a jewelry shop while armed with guns and knives, and during the robbery, one robber slashes a customer with a knife causing grievous hurt. All are liable under Section 397.

### Section 398 IPC / Section 317 BNS: Attempt to Commit Robbery or Dacoity When Armed with Deadly Weapon
If at the time of **attempting to commit robbery or dacoity**, the offender is **armed with any deadly weapon**, the imprisonment shall be:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Minimum **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Court of Session

**Example**: A gang of five persons, armed with pistols, enters a bank intending to commit dacoity but is apprehended by police before taking any property. They are liable under Section 398 for armed attempt.

---

## Preparatory Offences Related to Dacoity

### Section 399 IPC / Section 318 BNS: Making Preparation to Commit Dacoity
Whoever makes any preparation for committing dacoity shall be punished with:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Example**: A group collects weapons, conducts surveillance of a target house, and plans the dacoity. Even if the dacoity is not attempted, preparation alone is punishable.

**Rationale**: Dacoity is so serious that even preparatory acts are criminalized to prevent the commission of the substantive offence.

### Section 400 IPC / Section 319 BNS: Punishment for Belonging to Gang of Dacoits
Whoever, at any time after the passing of this Act, shall belong to a **gang of persons associated for the purpose of habitually committing dacoity**, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment for life**, OR
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Up to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Court of Session

**Essential Ingredients**:
1. Gang of persons (association of persons)
2. Purpose: Habitually committing dacoity (regular criminal activity)
3. Accused belongs to such gang

**Example**: A is a member of a notorious gang that has committed 20 dacoities in the past year. Even if A did not participate in every dacoity, his membership in the gang is an offence under Section 400.

### Section 401 IPC / Section 320 BNS: Punishment for Belonging to Gang of Thieves
Whoever, at any time after the passing of this Act, shall belong to any **wandering or other gang of persons associated for the purpose of habitually committing theft or robbery**, and not being a gang of thugs or dacoits, shall be punished with:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Distinction from Section 400**:
- Section 400: Gang of dacoits (five or more committing robbery)
- Section 401: Gang of thieves or robbers (not necessarily five persons)

### Section 402 IPC / Section 321 BNS: Assembling for Purpose of Committing Dacoity
Whoever, at any time after the passing of this Act, shall be one of **five or more persons assembled** for the purpose of committing dacoity, shall be punished with:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

**Essential Ingredients**:
1. Five or more persons
2. Assembled (gathered together)
3. Purpose: To commit dacoity (specific intent)

**Example**: Five persons assemble at a hideout to plan and execute a dacoity. They are caught by police before leaving for the target. All five are liable under Section 402.

**Distinction from Section 399**:
- Section 399: Making preparation (individual act, any number of persons)
- Section 402: Assembling (collective act, minimum five persons)

---

## Distinction Between Theft, Extortion, and Robbery

| **Element** | **Theft (378)** | **Extortion (383)** | **Robbery (390)** |
|-------------|-----------------|---------------------|-------------------|
| **Taking** | Dishonestly taking movable property | Intentionally putting in fear to induce delivery | Theft or extortion + violence/fear of instant harm |
| **Consent** | Without consent | With consent (obtained by fear) | Without consent (theft) OR consent by fear of instant harm (extortion) |
| **Violence/Fear** | No violence or fear | Fear of injury (not necessarily instant) | Violence OR fear of **instant** death/hurt/restraint |
| **Delivery** | Offender takes property | Victim delivers property | Offender takes (theft-robbery) OR victim delivers under instant fear (extortion-robbery) |
| **Punishment** | Up to 3 years (simple) | Up to 3 years (simple) | Minimum 1 year, up to 10 years (RI mandatory) |

### Key Distinction: Extortion vs. Robbery

**Extortion becomes Robbery when**:
1. Offender is in the presence of the victim
2. Fear is of **instant** harm (not future harm)
3. Victim delivers property then and there

**Example 1 (Extortion, not Robbery)**: A sends a threatening letter to B demanding Rs. 10 lakh, threatening to kill B if he does not pay within 7 days. B pays. This is extortion (fear of future harm, offender not present at delivery).

**Example 2 (Robbery by Extortion)**: A points a gun at B and says "Give me your wallet now or I will shoot you." B hands over the wallet. This is robbery (fear of instant death, offender present, immediate delivery).

---

## Landmark Cases on Robbery and Dacoity

### 1. **Mohan Singh v. State of Punjab** (1963) - Dacoity with Murder
**AIR 1963 SC 174**

**Facts**: A gang of dacoits robbed a house. During the robbery, one member of the gang shot and killed the homeowner. Other members claimed they did not intend murder.

**Held**: The Supreme Court held that under **Section 396 IPC**, if any one member of a dacoity gang commits murder during the commission of dacoity, **all members of the gang are liable for murder**, even if they did not personally commit the act or intend to cause death.

**Principle**: **Constructive liability** for dacoity with murder. Common intention to commit dacoity makes all members vicariously liable for murder committed by any member in furtherance of the common intention.

**Reasoning**: Section 396 is based on the principle that when persons act conjointly to commit dacoity (a violent crime), they assume collective responsibility for all acts done in furtherance of that crime, including murder.

### 2. **Girja Prasad v. State of M.P.** (2007) - Robbery: Instant vs. Future Fear
**AIR 2007 SC 3088**

**Facts**: The accused threatened the victim with consequences if he did not hand over money. The victim, fearing harm, gave money to the accused.

**Held**: For an offence to be robbery (as opposed to extortion), the fear must be of **instant death, hurt, or wrongful restraint**. If the threat is of future harm, it is extortion, not robbery.

**Principle**: The critical distinction between robbery and extortion is the **immediacy of the threat**. Robbery requires instant fear; extortion may involve fear of future harm.

**Test**: Was the victim in fear of harm then and there (instant)? If yes, robbery. Was the fear of harm at some future time? If yes, extortion.

### 3. **Lakshmi Singh v. State of Bihar** (1976) - Robbery: Force Before, During, or After
**AIR 1976 SC 2263**

**Facts**: The accused snatched a purse from the victim and when the victim tried to resist, the accused pushed her, causing her to fall.

**Held**: Robbery can be committed by using force or putting in fear **before, during, or after** the theft. Even if force is used after snatching the property to facilitate escape or retention, it constitutes robbery.

**Principle**: The violence or fear element in robbery can occur at any of three stages:
1. Before: To facilitate the commission of theft
2. During: While committing the theft
3. After: To carry away or retain the stolen property or to escape

### 4. **Shaik Muneer v. State of A.P.** (2007) - Dacoity: Five Persons Requirement
**2007 Cri LJ 3725**

**Facts**: Four persons committed a robbery. The prosecution attempted to charge them with dacoity.

**Held**: Dacoity requires a **minimum of five persons** acting conjointly to commit robbery. If fewer than five persons are involved, the offence is robbery, not dacoity, regardless of the severity of violence.

**Principle**: The numerical requirement of five persons for dacoity is mandatory and cannot be waived. Courts cannot convict for dacoity if the number of offenders is less than five.

**Note**: However, if some offenders are not apprehended or identified, and evidence shows five or more participated, dacoity can still be established.

### 5. **Hari Kishan v. State of Haryana** (1975) - Robbery: Wrongful Restraint Sufficient
**AIR 1975 SC 1491**

**Facts**: The accused blocked the victim's path and demanded money, threatening to harm him if he tried to leave. The victim gave money.

**Held**: Robbery can be committed not only by causing hurt or death but also by **wrongful restraint**. If a person is put in fear of instant wrongful restraint (being prevented from moving) and induced to deliver property, it is robbery.

**Principle**: The fear element in robbery includes fear of instant death, hurt, **or wrongful restraint**. Physical violence is not always necessary; preventing freedom of movement is sufficient.

### 6. **Gurmukh Singh v. State of Haryana** (2009) - Attempt to Commit Robbery
**AIR 2009 SC 1471**

**Facts**: The accused surrounded a vehicle, attempted to snatch valuables, but were chased away before succeeding.

**Held**: For **attempt to commit robbery** (Section 393), it is sufficient if the accused took steps towards committing robbery and had the intention to commit robbery, even if the robbery was not completed due to intervention or resistance.

**Principle**: Attempt requires:
1. Intention to commit the offence
2. Some act done towards its commission (not mere preparation)
3. The act falls short of completion

### 7. **State of Maharashtra v. Daniel David** (1980) - Dacoity: Vicarious Liability
**AIR 1980 SC 990**

**Facts**: A gang of six persons committed dacoity. During the dacoity, one member caused grievous hurt to a victim. The other members claimed they did not participate in causing hurt.

**Held**: In dacoity, all members of the gang are **vicariously liable** for acts done by any member in furtherance of the common intention to commit dacoity. If grievous hurt is caused during dacoity, all members are liable under Section 397.

**Principle**: **Common intention** (Section 34 IPC) applies to dacoity. Each member is liable for the acts of other members done in furtherance of the dacoity.

---

## IPC to BNS Transition Mapping

| **IPC Section** | **BNS Section** | **Offence** | **Changes** |
|-----------------|-----------------|-------------|-------------|
| **390** | **309** | Definition of Robbery | No substantive change |
| **391** | **310** | Definition of Dacoity | No substantive change |
| **392** | **311** | Punishment for Robbery | No change (1-10 years RI + fine) |
| **393** | **312** | Attempt to Commit Robbery | No change (up to 7 years RI + fine) |
| **394** | **313** | Causing Hurt in Robbery | No change (life or 10 years RI + fine) |
| **395** | **314** | Punishment for Dacoity | No change (life or 7-10 years RI + fine) |
| **396** | **315** | Dacoity with Murder | No change (death or life or 10 years RI + fine) |
| **397** | **316** | Robbery/Dacoity with Attempt to Cause Death/GH | No change (life or 7 years RI minimum + fine) |
| **398** | **317** | Attempt to Rob/Dacoit When Armed | No change (7 years RI minimum + fine) |
| **399** | **318** | Making Preparation for Dacoity | No change (up to 10 years RI + fine) |
| **400** | **319** | Belonging to Gang of Dacoits | No change (life or 10 years RI + fine) |
| **401** | **320** | Belonging to Gang of Thieves | No change (up to 7 years RI + fine) |
| **402** | **321** | Assembling for Dacoity | No change (up to 7 years RI + fine) |

**Note**: The BNS 2023 has retained all robbery and dacoity provisions from IPC 1860 with no substantive changes. Definitions, ingredients, and punishments remain identical.

---

## Practical Examples

### Example 1: Simple Robbery (Snatching with Force)
**Scenario**: Rajesh is walking on a street when two men on a motorcycle approach him. One of them forcefully snatches Rajesh's mobile phone from his hand and they speed away.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Theft: Dishonest taking of movable property (mobile phone) without consent
2. ✅ Force used: Snatching with force constitutes violence
3. ✅ During theft: Force used while committing the theft
4. ✅ Instant harm: The act of snatching itself involves immediate force/violence
5. ❌ Not dacoity: Only two persons (less than five)

**Offence**: Robbery under Section 392 IPC / Section 311 BNS
**Punishment**: 1-10 years rigorous imprisonment + fine

### Example 2: Robbery with Hurt
**Scenario**: Three men enter a jewelry shop. One of them points a knife at the shopkeeper and demands jewelry. When the shopkeeper hesitates, another man punches the shopkeeper in the face, causing injury. They take jewelry worth Rs. 5 lakh and flee.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Theft: Taking jewelry without consent
2. ✅ Violence and fear: Knife threat (fear of instant hurt/death) + actual hurt (punch)
3. ✅ Hurt caused: Bodily pain/injury to shopkeeper
4. ❌ Not dacoity: Only three persons

**Offence**: Robbery with Voluntarily Causing Hurt under Section 394 IPC / Section 313 BNS
**Punishment**: Imprisonment for life OR up to 10 years rigorous imprisonment + fine

### Example 3: Dacoity (Five Persons)
**Scenario**: A gang of six persons, armed with sticks and knives, robs a bank. They threaten the staff and customers with weapons, tie them up, and take cash worth Rs. 20 lakh.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Robbery: Theft + violence/fear of instant harm (threats with weapons)
2. ✅ Five or more persons: Six persons involved
3. ✅ Acting conjointly: All acting with common intention
4. ✅ Armed: Sticks and knives (deadly weapons)

**Offence**: Dacoity under Section 395 IPC / Section 314 BNS
**Alternative charge**: Section 397 (Dacoity with deadly weapon)
**Punishment**: Imprisonment for life OR 7-10 years rigorous imprisonment + fine (Section 395)

### Example 4: Dacoity with Murder
**Scenario**: A gang of seven persons commits dacoity in a farmhouse. The owner tries to resist, and one gang member shoots and kills him. The others did not intend to kill anyone and did not fire any shots.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Dacoity: Five or more persons committing robbery
2. ✅ Murder committed: One member killed the owner during the dacoity
3. ✅ All liable: Section 396 imposes vicarious liability on all members

**Offence**: Dacoity with Murder under Section 396 IPC / Section 315 BNS (all seven members liable)
**Punishment**: Death OR imprisonment for life OR up to 10 years rigorous imprisonment + fine

**Principle**: All members of the dacoity gang are liable for murder committed by any one member during the commission of dacoity, even if they did not personally commit or intend the murder.

### Example 5: Extortion (Not Robbery - Future Threat)
**Scenario**: Anil sends an anonymous letter to Vikram demanding Rs. 10 lakh, threatening to kidnap Vikram's daughter if he does not pay within a week. Vikram pays the money through a bank transfer.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Fear induced: Vikram is in fear of harm to his daughter
2. ✅ Delivery of property: Vikram delivers money
3. ❌ Not instant fear: Threat is of future harm (within a week), not instant harm
4. ❌ Offender not present: Anonymous letter, offender not in presence during delivery

**Offence**: Extortion under Section 384 IPC / Section 308 BNS (NOT robbery)
**Punishment**: Up to 3 years imprisonment OR fine OR both

**Why not robbery**: Fear is of future harm, not instant harm, and offender is not in the victim's presence at the time of delivery.

### Example 6: Robbery by Extortion (Instant Fear, Presence)
**Scenario**: Four armed men stop Suresh's car on a highway. They point guns at him and say, "Give us all your cash and valuables right now or we will shoot you." Suresh, in fear of instant death, hands over Rs. 50,000 and his watch.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Extortion: Suresh delivers property due to fear
2. ✅ Offender in presence: Armed men are physically present
3. ✅ Fear of instant harm: Threat of instant death (guns pointed)
4. ✅ Immediate delivery: Suresh delivers then and there
5. ✅ Robbery by extortion: All conditions for robbery through extortion are met

**Offence**: Robbery under Section 392 IPC / Section 311 BNS
**Punishment**: 1-10 years rigorous imprisonment + fine

### Example 7: Attempt to Commit Dacoity When Armed
**Scenario**: A gang of eight persons, armed with pistols and knives, is caught by police while conducting surveillance of a bank. They have detailed plans, weapons, and getaway vehicles ready but have not yet entered the bank.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Five or more persons: Eight persons
2. ✅ Preparation/attempt: Surveillance, weapons, plans indicate attempt
3. ✅ Armed with deadly weapons: Pistols and knives
4. ✅ Intent to commit dacoity: Plans and preparation show intent
5. ❌ No actual dacoity committed: Caught before execution

**Offence**: Attempt to Commit Dacoity When Armed under Section 398 IPC / Section 317 BNS
**Alternative**: Making Preparation to Commit Dacoity (Section 399) or Assembling for Dacoity (Section 402)
**Punishment**: Minimum 7 years rigorous imprisonment + fine (Section 398)

---

## Defenses to Robbery and Dacoity

### 1. **Absence of Theft**
- If no theft occurred, there can be no robbery (robbery requires theft or extortion as base offence)
- If accused can show property was taken with consent or lawful authority, no robbery

### 2. **No Violence or Fear of Instant Harm**
- If no violence was used and no fear of instant harm was created, the offence may be theft or extortion, not robbery
- Fear must be of **instant** harm; fear of future harm does not constitute robbery

**Example**: If A threatens to harm B tomorrow unless B gives money today, and B gives money, it is extortion, not robbery (no instant fear).

### 3. **Self-Defense or Private Defense**
- If the accused used force in lawful exercise of the right of private defense (IPC Sections 96-106), no robbery
- The force must be proportionate and necessary for defense

**Example**: A attempts to snatch B's bag. B uses reasonable force to protect his property. B is not guilty of assault; A is guilty of attempted robbery.

### 4. **Lack of Common Intention (For Dacoity)**
- For dacoity, there must be common intention among five or more persons
- If accused can show he did not share common intention to commit robbery, he may not be liable for dacoity

**Example**: A is part of a group of six persons, but only three intended to commit robbery while others (including A) were unaware. A may not be liable for dacoity if lack of common intention is proved.

### 5. **Fewer Than Five Persons (For Dacoity)**
- If fewer than five persons committed the offence, it is robbery, not dacoity
- Accused may argue that number of offenders was less than five

### 6. **Alibi**
- If accused can prove he was elsewhere at the time of the offence, he cannot be convicted

### 7. **Mistaken Identity**
- If accused can show that he was wrongly identified as the offender (especially in cases with no direct evidence)

---

## Procedural Aspects

### Cognizance and Complaint
- **All robbery and dacoity offences**: Cognizable, Non-bailable
- **Triable by**:
  - Magistrate of First Class: Sections 392, 393, 399, 401, 402
  - Court of Session: Sections 394, 395, 396, 397, 398, 400

### Investigation
- Police can arrest without warrant (cognizable offences)
- FIR to be registered immediately
- Investigation includes: identification of accused, recovery of stolen property, recording witness statements, establishing number of offenders (for dacoity), proving violence/fear element

### Trial
- Serious offences (dacoity, robbery with hurt, etc.) tried by Sessions Court
- Burden of proof on prosecution to establish:
  - Theft (all seven ingredients)
  - Violence or fear of instant harm (critical element)
  - Number of offenders (for dacoity: five or more)
  - Common intention (for dacoity)

### Compounding
- **None of the robbery or dacoity offences are compoundable**
- Victim and accused cannot compromise to end the case

### Bail
- **All offences are non-bailable**
- Bail may be granted by Sessions Court or High Court in exceptional circumstances
- Factors considered: nature of offence, evidence, antecedents of accused, likelihood of fleeing

---

## Compliance Checklist

### For Potential Victims (Preventive Measures)
- [ ] Avoid carrying large amounts of cash or valuables in public places
- [ ] Be vigilant in isolated or poorly lit areas
- [ ] Install security systems (CCTV, alarms) in commercial establishments
- [ ] Employ security guards for high-risk locations (banks, jewelry shops)
- [ ] Maintain insurance for valuable property
- [ ] Report suspicious activity or persons to police
- [ ] Cooperate with police investigation if victimized

### For Investigating Officers
- [ ] Register FIR immediately upon complaint
- [ ] Secure crime scene and collect evidence (CCTV footage, eyewitness statements)
- [ ] Identify and apprehend suspects promptly
- [ ] Prove all ingredients: theft, violence/fear of instant harm, number of offenders (for dacoity)
- [ ] Recover stolen property and establish chain of custody
- [ ] Conduct identification parades if suspects are unknown to victims
- [ ] File charge sheet within statutory time limits

### For Prosecutors
- [ ] Frame appropriate charges: Section 392, 394, 395, 396, 397, or 398 based on facts
- [ ] Prove theft as foundational offence
- [ ] Prove violence or fear of instant harm (critical element)
- [ ] For dacoity: establish five or more persons acting conjointly
- [ ] For Section 396: prove murder was committed during dacoity (vicarious liability)
- [ ] Examine witnesses thoroughly; corroborate evidence
- [ ] Differentiate from theft, extortion, or other offences

### For Defense Counsel
- [ ] Challenge identification of accused (if case relies on eyewitness testimony)
- [ ] Argue absence of violence or instant fear (reduce charge to theft/extortion)
- [ ] For dacoity: challenge number of offenders or common intention
- [ ] Argue self-defense or private defense (if applicable)
- [ ] Seek acquittal if any essential ingredient is not proved beyond reasonable doubt
- [ ] If conviction likely, argue for minimum sentence: first-time offender, young age, return of property, family circumstances
- [ ] Seek bail from higher courts (Sessions/High Court)

---

## Cross-References

### Related Criminal Offences
- **Theft (IPC 378-382)**: Base offence for robbery
- **Extortion (IPC 383-389)**: Alternative base for robbery (if fear of instant harm in presence)
- **Wrongful Restraint (IPC 339-341)**: May be part of robbery (fear of instant restraint)
- **Assault and Hurt (IPC 319-338)**: Violence element in robbery
- **Murder (IPC 300-302)**: Dacoity with murder (Section 396)
- **Criminal Conspiracy (IPC 120A-120B)**: Planning and preparation for dacoity
- **Unlawful Assembly (IPC 141-149)**: Common object to commit dacoity

### Constitutional Provisions
- **Article 21**: Right to Life and Personal Liberty (protection from violent crime)
- **Article 300A**: Right to Property (protection from deprivation of property)

### Procedural Laws
- **CrPC / BNSS**:
  - Section 154: FIR registration
  - Section 41: Arrest without warrant for cognizable offences
  - Section 437: Bail (non-bailable offences, discretion of court)
  - Section 320: Compounding (robbery and dacoity are non-compoundable)

### Evidence Law
- **Indian Evidence Act / BSA**:
  - Section 27: Admissibility of information leading to discovery of stolen property
  - Section 8: Motive, preparation, and conduct (relevant for proving common intention)
  - Section 34: Common intention (for dacoity)

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Robbery = Theft + Instant Violence/Fear**: Robbery is an aggravated form of theft where violence or fear of instant harm is used before, during, or after the theft

2. **Instant vs. Future Harm**: The critical distinction between robbery and extortion is the **immediacy** of the threat. Robbery requires instant fear; extortion may involve future harm

3. **Dacoity = Collective Robbery (5+ Persons)**: Dacoity requires a minimum of five persons acting conjointly to commit robbery

4. **Severe Punishment**: Robbery and dacoity carry mandatory rigorous imprisonment with minimum sentences (1 year for robbery, 7 years for dacoity)

5. **Vicarious Liability in Dacoity**: All members of a dacoity gang are liable for acts done by any member in furtherance of the common intention, including murder (Section 396)

6. **Non-Bailable and Non-Compoundable**: All robbery and dacoity offences are non-bailable and non-compoundable, reflecting their seriousness

7. **Preparatory Offences**: The law criminalizes even preparation, assembling, and membership in dacoity gangs (Sections 399-402) to prevent commission of dacoity

8. **Three Stages of Violence**: Violence or fear in robbery can occur before, during, or after the theft

---

## Conclusion

Robbery and dacoity are among the most serious property offences in Indian criminal law due to their combination of property violation and violence against persons. The law imposes severe penalties, including life imprisonment and death (for dacoity with murder), to deter these crimes and protect public safety.

Understanding the fine distinctions between theft, extortion, and robbery—particularly the requirement of **instant harm** for robbery—is critical for proper charge framing and adjudication. The principle of vicarious liability in dacoity ensures that all participants in collective robbery bear responsibility for the acts of their co-offenders.

With the transition to BNS 2023, the substantive law on robbery and dacoity remains unchanged, ensuring continuity in legal practice and precedent.

---

**Document Version**: 1.0
**Effective Date**: IPC provisions apply until June 30, 2024; BNS provisions effective from July 1, 2024
**Last Updated**: December 6, 2025
**Prepared by**: Legal Protocol Documentation System
**Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)

---

**Disclaimer**: This protocol is for informational and educational purposes only and does not constitute legal advice. For specific legal matters, consult a qualified criminal law advocate or legal expert.

## IL-CRIM-TAX-EVASION.md
---

# India Criminal Law: Tax Evasion and Tax Fraud (Kar Chori)

## Protocol ID
**IL-CRIM-TAX-EVASION**

## Domain
Criminal Law > Economic Offences

## Jurisdiction
India (All States and Union Territories)

## Statutory Framework

### Income Tax Act, 1961
- **Section 276C**: Wilful Attempt to Evade Tax
- **Section 276CC**: Failure to Furnish Returns
- **Section 277**: False Statement in Verification
- **Section 278**: Abetment of False Return
- **Section 276B**: Failure to Pay Tax Deducted at Source (TDS)
- **Section 276BB**: Failure to Pay Tax Collected at Source (TCS)
- **Section 276D**: Failure to Produce Accounts and Documents
- **Section 270A**: Penalty for Underreporting and Misreporting Income

### Goods and Services Tax (GST) Act, 2017
- **Section 132**: Punishment for Certain Offences (Tax Evasion, Fake Invoices, Suppression)
- **Section 122**: Penalty for Certain Offences

### Customs Act, 1962
- **Section 135**: Evasion of Duty or Prohibitions
- **Section 132**: Punishment for Certain Offences

### Black Money (Undisclosed Foreign Income and Assets) and Imposition of Tax Act, 2015
- **Section 50**: Punishment for Wilful Attempt to Evade Tax on Foreign Assets
- **Section 51**: Punishment for Failure to Furnish Return for Foreign Assets

---

## Overview

**Tax evasion** is the illegal non-payment or underpayment of taxes through deliberate misrepresentation or concealment of income, assets, or transactions. It is distinguished from **tax avoidance** (legal minimization of tax liability through legitimate means) by the element of **fraud and illegality**.

Tax evasion:
- Deprives government of revenue needed for public services
- Creates unfair advantage for tax evaders over honest taxpayers
- Undermines public trust in tax system
- Facilitates other crimes (corruption, money laundering) by hiding illegal income

### Key Characteristics
1. **Wilful Intent**: Tax evasion requires deliberate intent to evade tax (not mere negligence or honest mistake)
2. **Multiple Tax Laws**: Income Tax, GST, Customs, Black Money Act all have criminal provisions
3. **Severe Punishment**: Imprisonment from 6 months to 7 years depending on amount and offence
4. **Prosecution Requires Sanction**: Chief Commissioner/Commissioner must sanction prosecution
5. **Burden of Proof**: Initially on prosecution, but presumptions assist prosecution in some cases

---

## Income Tax Evasion (Income Tax Act, 1961)

### Section 276C: Wilful Attempt to Evade Tax

#### Definition and Punishment

**Whoever wilfully attempts in any manner whatsoever to evade any tax, penalty, or interest** chargeable or imposable under the Income Tax Act shall be punishable with:

**If tax sought to be evaded exceeds Rs. 25 lakh**:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: 6 months to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**If tax sought to be evaded is less than Rs. 25 lakh but exceeds Rs. 1 lakh**:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: 3 months to **3 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**If tax sought to be evaded is up to Rs. 1 lakh**:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: 3 months to **2 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable (if tax evaded > Rs. 25 lakh), Non-bailable (if tax evaded > Rs. 50 lakh), Compoundable

#### Essential Ingredients

1. **Wilful Attempt**
   - Deliberate, intentional act (not mere negligence or error)
   - Conscious effort to evade tax

2. **To Evade Tax, Penalty, or Interest**
   - Evade: Avoid payment of lawful tax liability through illegal means
   - Includes concealing income, inflating expenses, false claims, suppressing facts

3. **In Any Manner Whatsoever**
   - Broad scope: any method of evasion
   - Includes false returns, forged documents, benami transactions, shell companies, offshore accounts

**Common Methods of Tax Evasion**:

**A. Concealment of Income**
- Not reporting cash income (especially in cash-intensive businesses like retail, real estate)
- Not declaring foreign income or assets
- Suppressing capital gains from property sales

**Example**: A shopkeeper has annual income of Rs. 50 lakh but reports only Rs. 10 lakh, paying tax on Rs. 10 lakh. Rs. 40 lakh income concealed.

**B. False/Inflated Expenses**
- Claiming fake business expenses to reduce taxable income
- Bogus purchases, non-existent employees on payroll

**Example**: A company claims Rs. 1 crore in "consulting fees" paid to a shell company (actually owned by promoter), reducing taxable profit.

**C. Fake or Bogus Transactions**
- Creating fictitious invoices (fake bills without actual transactions)
- Round-tripping (moving money through multiple entities and bringing it back as "investment")

**Example**: A obtains fake purchase invoices from paper companies to claim input tax credit and reduce income.

**D. Benami Transactions**
- Holding property or investments in names of family members, associates (benami) to hide true ownership and income

**Example**: A purchases property worth Rs. 5 crore in his wife's name using unaccounted money (black money).

**E. Offshore Tax Havens**
- Hiding income/assets in foreign accounts or shell companies in tax havens (countries with bank secrecy and no tax treaties)

**Example**: A stashes Rs. 100 crore in Swiss bank account and doesn't declare it in Indian tax returns.

### Section 276CC: Failure to Furnish Return of Income

**Whoever fails to furnish return of income** when required by law shall be punishable with:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: 3 months to **3 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Applies when**:
- Income exceeds maximum amount not chargeable to tax
- Assessee is required by law to file return but willfully fails to do so

**Example**: A has taxable income of Rs. 20 lakh but deliberately does not file ITR for 3 years to avoid detection.

### Section 277: False Statement in Verification

**Whoever makes a statement in any verification** (declaration in ITR or documents) which is false and which he knows or believes to be false shall be punishable with:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Up to **1 year**, OR
- **Fine**

**Example**: A signs ITR verification declaring "all particulars are true and correct" when he has concealed Rs. 50 lakh income.

### Section 278: Abetment of False Return

**Whoever abets or induces another person to make a false return** or statement shall be punishable with:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Up to **2 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Example**: Chartered Accountant B knowingly helps A prepare a false ITR showing fake expenses. B commits abetment.

### Section 276B: Failure to Pay Tax Deducted at Source (TDS)

**If employer or payer deducts TDS from employee/payee but fails to deposit it with government**, punishable with:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: 3 months to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Example**: Company deducts Rs. 10 lakh TDS from employees' salaries but does not deposit with Income Tax Department, using it for business.

---

## GST Evasion (GST Act, 2017)

### Section 132: Punishment for Certain Offences

**Whoever commits any of the following offences shall be punishable**:

**Offences** (Section 132(1)):
1. Issues invoice/bill without supply of goods/services (fake invoices)
2. Issues invoice/bill with intent to evade tax
3. Evades tax by suppressing turnover, issuing false invoices, misusing input tax credit
4. Takes/utilizes input tax credit without actual receipt of goods/services
5. Fraudulently obtains refund
6. Suppresses sales or purchases to evade tax

**Punishment**:

**If tax evaded or input tax credit wrongly availed or refund wrongly taken exceeds Rs. 5 crore**:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **5 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**If amount exceeds Rs. 2 crore but up to Rs. 5 crore**:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **3 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**If amount exceeds Rs. 1 crore but up to Rs. 2 crore**:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **1 year**, AND
- **Fine**

**If amount up to Rs. 1 crore**:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **1 year**, OR
- **Fine**

**Nature**: Cognizable (if tax evaded > Rs. 2.5 crore), Non-bailable (if tax evaded > Rs. 5 crore), Non-compoundable (if tax evaded > Rs. 5 crore)

### Common GST Frauds

**A. Fake Invoices and Input Tax Credit (ITC) Fraud**
- Creating fake purchase invoices to claim input tax credit without actual purchases
- Using invoices from bogus firms (firms that exist only on paper)

**Example**: A runs a manufacturing business. He obtains fake purchase invoices worth Rs. 10 crore from paper companies (non-existent suppliers), claims input tax credit of Rs. 1.8 crore (18% GST), reducing his tax liability.

**B. Suppression of Sales/Turnover**
- Not reporting actual sales to reduce tax liability
- Cash sales not recorded in books

**Example**: A jeweler sells Rs. 5 crore worth of jewelry in cash but reports only Rs. 2 crore in GST returns.

**C. Misclassification of Goods**
- Classifying goods under lower tax rate category to pay less GST

**Example**: A imports luxury cars (28% GST) but classifies them as "ambulances" (12% GST) to evade tax.

**D. Composition Scheme Fraud**
- Businesses with turnover below threshold (Rs. 1.5 crore) can opt for composition scheme (lower flat tax rate)
- Fraud: Suppressing actual turnover to remain in composition scheme

---

## Customs Duty Evasion (Customs Act, 1962)

### Section 135: Evasion of Duty or Prohibitions

**Whoever evades or attempts to evade any duty** or prohibitions on import/export shall be punishable with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to **7 years**, OR
- **Fine**, OR
- **Both**

**Common Methods**:
1. **Under-invoicing**: Declaring lower value of imported goods to pay less customs duty
2. **Mis-declaration**: Declaring goods under wrong category (lower duty)
3. **Smuggling**: Importing/exporting prohibited goods (gold, drugs, wildlife) without declaration

**Example**: A imports machinery worth Rs. 10 crore but declares value as Rs. 5 crore in customs documents, evading duty on Rs. 5 crore.

---

## Black Money Act (Undisclosed Foreign Assets), 2015

### Section 50: Wilful Attempt to Evade Tax on Foreign Assets

**Whoever wilfully attempts to evade tax in respect of any foreign income or foreign asset** shall be punishable with:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: 3 years to **10 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Section 51: Failure to Furnish Return for Foreign Assets**

**Failure to furnish return disclosing foreign income and assets** punishable with:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: 6 months to **7 years**, AND
- **Fine**

**Key Features**:
- **Higher Punishment**: Up to 10 years (vs. 7 years for domestic tax evasion)
- **Strict Liability**: No compounding allowed for foreign asset concealment
- **Rate of Tax**: 30% + 30% penalty = 60% on undisclosed foreign income/assets (plus 10% surcharge = 120% total tax on detection!)

**Rationale**: Deterring stashing of black money abroad; bringing back hidden foreign wealth.

**Example**: A has Rs. 500 crore in Swiss bank account and properties in Dubai. A does not declare in Indian tax returns. Penalty: 10 years imprisonment + Rs. 600 crore tax + fine.

---

## Defenses to Tax Evasion Charges

### 1. **Absence of Wilful Intent**
- If the underpayment was due to **bona fide error, mistake, or honest interpretation of law**, not wilful evasion
- Genuine disagreement over tax treatment of a transaction is not evasion

**Example**: A claimed a particular expense as deduction believing it was allowed. Tax officer disagrees. If A's belief was bona fide (even if wrong), not wilful evasion.

**Landmark Case** (*Amar Nath Chawla v. CIT*, 1973): Mere failure to file return or understatement of income does not amount to wilful evasion unless deliberate intent to evade is proved.

### 2. **Tax was Disputed / Subject to Interpretation**
- If there was genuine dispute over applicability of tax law, prosecution may not succeed

### 3. **Voluntary Disclosure and Payment**
- If assessee voluntarily discloses concealed income and pays tax before detection, may avoid prosecution (though not penalty)

### 4. **Technical Defenses**
- **Lack of Sanction**: Prosecution requires sanction from Chief Commissioner/Commissioner (Section 279). If sanction is not obtained or is defective, prosecution fails.
- **Limitation**: Prosecution must be launched within time limits prescribed

### 5. **Compounding**
- For offences that are compoundable, accused can pay prescribed amount and settle (avoid trial)

---

## Prosecution Procedure and Safeguards

### Sanction for Prosecution (Section 279 Income Tax Act)

**No prosecution for offences under Income Tax Act can be launched except with prior sanction** of:
- **Chief Commissioner of Income Tax**, OR
- **Commissioner of Income Tax**

**Rationale**: Protects taxpayers from frivolous or malicious prosecution.

**Sanction must be**:
- Based on application of mind to facts
- Specific to the offence and person
- In writing

**If sanction is defective or absent, prosecution is void.**

### Compounding of Offences (Section 279(2))

**Certain offences can be compounded** (settled by paying prescribed amount) either before or after institution of proceedings.

**Chief Commissioner may allow compounding on payment of**:
- **For offences not related to concealment**: Payment of tax + interest + penalty (typically 50-200% of tax)
- **For concealment offences**: Payment may be higher

**Benefits of Compounding**:
- Avoids criminal trial and imprisonment
- Settles matter by payment
- Record of conviction avoided

**Non-Compoundable**:
- Offences involving tax evasion exceeding certain amounts (varies, typically Rs. 1 crore+) may not be compoundable

---

## Landmark Cases on Tax Evasion

### 1. **K.C. Builders v. Asstt. CIT** (2004) - Wilful Evasion Requires Mens Rea
**AIR 2004 SC 2271**

**Held**: For prosecution under Section 276C (wilful evasion), the prosecution must prove **mens rea** (guilty mind) - deliberate, intentional attempt to evade tax. Mere understatement or delay in filing return without proof of wilful intent is insufficient.

**Principle**: Tax evasion is a criminal offence requiring proof of criminal intent beyond reasonable doubt. Honest disputes over tax liability are not crimes.

### 2. **Dilip N. Shroff v. Joint CIT** (2007) - Bona Fide Belief Defense
**288 ITR 294 (SC)**

**Held**: If the assessee had a **bona fide belief** (even if mistaken) about the tax treatment of a transaction, and disclosed all facts to the department, there is no wilful attempt to evade tax.

**Principle**: Good faith errors or disputes over interpretation of law do not constitute wilful evasion.

### 3. **Panama Papers and Paradise Papers** (2016-2017) - Offshore Tax Evasion
**Global Investigation**: Leaked documents exposed thousands of individuals and companies using offshore shell companies and tax havens to evade taxes.

**India's Response**:
- Income Tax Department initiated investigations against hundreds of Indians named
- Black Money Act prosecutions launched
- Notices issued for undisclosed foreign assets
- Several prosecutions ongoing

**Prominent Cases**:
- Amitabh Bachchan (actor) - named in Panama Papers (claimed legitimate holding, settled with tax payment)
- DLF promoters - offshore entities
- Several politicians, businessmen

**Outcome**: Enhanced scrutiny of foreign assets; tighter compliance requirements; information exchange agreements with tax havens.

### 4. **Sahara Group - Tax Evasion Allegations** (2014-ongoing)

**Alleged**: Sahara Group evaded taxes on Rs. 24,000 crore collected through illegal deposit schemes.

**Proceedings**: Income Tax Department assessed tax liability; criminal prosecution for evasion.

**Outcome**: Ongoing; Sahara contesting assessments and prosecutions.

### 5. **Reddy Brothers (Mining Scam) - Tax Evasion** (2011-2013)

**Context**: Illegal iron ore mining in Karnataka; alleged tax evasion on proceeds.

**Action**: Income Tax raids; assessment of undisclosed income; prosecution for tax evasion.

**Outcome**: Rs. 2,000+ crore tax demand; criminal prosecution ongoing.

---

## Practical Examples

### Example 1: Income Tax Evasion - Concealment of Cash Sales
**Scenario**: A runs a jewelry shop. He has annual sales of Rs. 10 crore (Rs. 6 crore in cash, Rs. 4 crore by card/cheque). He reports only Rs. 4 crore (card/cheque sales) in his ITR, concealing Rs. 6 crore cash sales. Tax evaded: Rs. 6 crore × 30% = Rs. 1.8 crore.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Wilful attempt: A deliberately concealed cash sales
2. ✅ To evade tax: Rs. 1.8 crore tax liability suppressed
3. ✅ Tax evaded < Rs. 25 lakh but > Rs. 1 lakh

**Offence**: Wilful Attempt to Evade Tax (Section 276C Income Tax Act)
**Punishment**: 3 months to 3 years RI + fine (since tax evaded is Rs. 1.8 crore, > Rs. 1 lakh but < Rs. 25 lakh threshold reached if the interpretation allows aggregation over years)

**Actually**: Tax evaded is Rs. 1.8 crore (> Rs. 25 lakh), so punishment is 6 months to 7 years RI + fine.

**Additional Consequences**: Penalty (50-200% of tax), interest, prosecution.

### Example 2: GST Fraud - Fake Input Tax Credit
**Scenario**: B operates a trading company. He obtains fake purchase invoices worth Rs. 20 crore from 10 bogus firms (paper companies that don't actually supply goods). He claims input tax credit of Rs. 3.6 crore (18% of Rs. 20 crore), reducing his GST liability.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Fake invoices without actual supply
2. ✅ Input tax credit fraudulently claimed: Rs. 3.6 crore
3. ✅ Intent to evade tax

**Offence**: Section 132 GST Act (fraudulent ITC, fake invoices)
**Punishment**: Since amount exceeds Rs. 2 crore but < Rs. 5 crore: Up to 3 years imprisonment + fine

**Nature**: Cognizable (since > Rs. 2.5 crore), Non-bailable (since > Rs. 5 crore)

**Wait**: Rs. 3.6 crore is < Rs. 5 crore, so bailable. But if exceeds Rs. 2.5 crore, cognizable.

### Example 3: Black Money Act - Undisclosed Foreign Assets
**Scenario**: C has Rs. 100 crore in Swiss bank account and properties in London worth Rs. 200 crore (total Rs. 300 crore foreign assets). C does not declare these in ITR or foreign asset disclosure forms.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Foreign income and assets undisclosed
2. ✅ Wilful evasion (C is aware of obligation to declare)

**Offence**:
- Section 50 Black Money Act (wilful evasion of tax on foreign assets)
- Section 51 (failure to furnish return)

**Punishment**:
- Section 50: 3-10 years RI + fine
- Section 51: 6 months to 7 years RI + fine

**Tax Liability**: Rs. 300 crore × 30% = Rs. 90 crore (tax) + Rs. 90 crore (penalty) = Rs. 180 crore + surcharge = approximately Rs. 200 crore+ total.

**Additional**: Prosecution not compoundable; assets may be confiscated.

### Example 4: Customs Duty Evasion - Under-Invoicing
**Scenario**: D imports electronics worth Rs. 50 crore but declares value as Rs. 20 crore in customs documents. Customs duty at 20%: Rs. 10 crore payable, but D pays only Rs. 4 crore (on declared Rs. 20 crore). Duty evaded: Rs. 6 crore.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Under-invoicing (false declaration of value)
2. ✅ Intent to evade customs duty

**Offence**: Section 135 Customs Act (evasion of duty)
**Punishment**: Up to 7 years imprisonment OR fine OR both

**Additional**: Goods may be confiscated; penalty up to 5 times duty evaded.

---

## Compliance and Best Practices

### For Individuals
- [ ] File ITR on time (even if no tax payable, if income exceeds threshold)
- [ ] Maintain proper records of income, expenses, investments (at least 6 years)
- [ ] Declare all income: salary, business, capital gains, foreign income
- [ ] Declare foreign assets (Form FA) if applicable
- [ ] Pay advance tax/self-assessment tax to avoid interest
- [ ] Respond to notices from tax authorities promptly
- [ ] Do not engage in cash transactions above prescribed limits (Rs. 2 lakh)
- [ ] Use legal tax-saving instruments (80C deductions, tax-free bonds) instead of evasion

### For Businesses
- [ ] Maintain proper books of accounts (statutory requirement)
- [ ] Conduct statutory audits (if turnover exceeds threshold)
- [ ] Deduct and deposit TDS/TCS on time
- [ ] File GST returns on time; reconcile with books
- [ ] Verify genuineness of suppliers (avoid fake invoice sellers)
- [ ] Comply with transfer pricing regulations (for international transactions)
- [ ] Implement internal controls to prevent fraud (segregation of duties, approvals)
- [ ] Train employees on tax compliance
- [ ] Use tax professionals (CAs, tax consultants) for complex matters

### For Tax Professionals (CAs, Advisors)
- [ ] Advise clients on legal tax planning (avoidance), not illegal evasion
- [ ] Refuse to prepare false returns or documents
- [ ] Maintain independence and professional ethics
- [ ] Document all advice given to clients
- [ ] Report suspicious activities if required (anti-money laundering norms)

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Wilful Intent Essential**: Tax evasion is a crime requiring deliberate intent to evade; honest mistakes or disputes are not crimes.

2. **Severe Punishment**: Imprisonment from 3 months to 10 years depending on amount and law; non-compoundable for large evasions.

3. **Multiple Taxes, Multiple Laws**: Income tax, GST, customs, black money - each has criminal provisions with varying punishments.

4. **Black Money Act Strictest**: Undisclosed foreign assets: 10 years imprisonment + 120% tax (30% tax + 30% penalty + surcharges).

5. **Sanction Required**: Prosecution requires government sanction (Chief Commissioner); protection against frivolous cases.

6. **Compounding Available**: For smaller evasions, settle by paying tax + penalty + interest to avoid trial.

7. **Global Cooperation**: Information exchange agreements (Panama Papers, FATCA, CRS) make it harder to hide foreign assets.

8. **GST Fraud Rampant**: Fake invoices, bogus ITC claims major problem; strict enforcement with imprisonment.

---

## Conclusion

Tax evasion is a serious economic offence that deprives the government of revenue essential for public services and creates an unfair playing field. India has comprehensive criminal provisions across income tax, GST, customs, and black money laws to deter and punish tax evasion.

While the law distinguishes between legitimate tax planning (avoidance) and illegal evasion, the line can be thin. The key differentiator is **intent** - deliberate concealment, falsification, or suppression constitutes evasion.

Recent global initiatives (Panama Papers, automatic information exchange, FATCA) have made it increasingly difficult to hide income and assets, whether domestic or foreign. Compliance is not just a legal obligation but increasingly enforced through technology (data analytics, AI for detecting anomalies).

For honest taxpayers, understanding obligations and maintaining proper records is crucial. For those tempted to evade, the consequences - criminal prosecution, imprisonment, heavy penalties, public disgrace - far outweigh any short-term savings.

---

**Document Version**: 1.0
**Effective Date**: Current as of December 6, 2025
**Last Updated**: December 6, 2025
**Prepared by**: Legal Protocol Documentation System
**Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)

---

**Disclaimer**: This protocol is for informational and educational purposes only and does not constitute tax or legal advice. For specific tax matters, consult a qualified Chartered Accountant or tax advocate.
