## IL-CRIM-THEFT.md
---

# India Criminal Law: Theft (Chori)

## Protocol ID
**IL-CRIM-THEFT**

## Domain
Criminal Law > Crimes Against Property

## Jurisdiction
India (All States and Union Territories)

## Statutory Framework

### Indian Penal Code (IPC), 1860
- **Section 378**: Definition of Theft
- **Section 379**: Punishment for Theft
- **Section 380**: Theft in Dwelling House, etc.
- **Section 381**: Theft by Clerk or Servant of Property in Possession of Master
- **Section 382**: Theft after Preparation Made for Causing Death, Hurt, or Restraint

### Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS), 2023 (Effective July 1, 2024)
- **Section 303**: Definition of Theft (formerly IPC 378)
- **Section 304**: Punishment for Theft (formerly IPC 379)
- **Section 305**: Theft in Dwelling House, etc. (formerly IPC 380)
- **Section 306**: Theft by Clerk or Servant (formerly IPC 381)
- **Section 307**: Theft after Preparation for Causing Death, etc. (formerly IPC 382)

---

## Overview

**Theft (Chori)** is the dishonest taking of movable property out of the possession of another person without that person's consent, with the intention of taking it permanently or for keeping it for a gain.

Theft is the foundational property offence in Indian criminal law. It is distinguished from other property offences by the element of **dishonest intention** and the act of **taking movable property** without consent.

### Key Characteristics
1. **Movable Property**: Only movable property can be subject of theft (not immovable property like land)
2. **Dishonest Intention**: The act must be done dishonestly (IPC Section 24: wrongful gain or wrongful loss)
3. **Without Consent**: Taking must be without the consent of the person in possession
4. **Out of Possession**: Property must be moved out of another's possession
5. **Permanently or for Gain**: Intention to keep permanently or for unlawful gain

---

## Definition of Theft (IPC Section 378 / BNS Section 303)

### Full Legal Definition

**IPC Section 378**: Whoever, intending to take dishonestly any movable property out of the possession of any person without that person's consent, moves that property in order to such taking, is said to commit **theft**.

### Seven Essential Ingredients of Theft

#### 1. **Dishonest Intention**
- The taking must be "dishonest" as defined in IPC Section 24
- **IPC Section 24**: A person is said to do a thing dishonestly if he does that thing with the intention of causing wrongful gain to one person or wrongful loss to another person
- **Wrongful Gain**: Gain by unlawful means of property to which the person gaining is not legally entitled
- **Wrongful Loss**: Loss by unlawful means of property to which the person losing is legally entitled

**Example**: A picks up B's watch from a table intending to keep it. A has dishonest intention. But if A picks up B's watch thinking it is his own, there is no dishonest intention, hence no theft.

#### 2. **Movable Property**
- Only movable property can be the subject of theft
- **IPC Section 22**: "Movable property" means corporeal property of every description, except land and things attached to the earth or permanently fastened to anything which is attached to the earth
- Includes: money, goods, animals, documents, electricity (by legal fiction)

**Examples of Movable Property**:
- Cash, jewelry, vehicles, livestock, crops after severance from land
- Electricity (Supreme Court held electricity is "movable property" for theft purposes)
- Computer data and software (debatable, but increasingly recognized)

**Not Movable Property**:
- Land, buildings, standing trees, fixtures permanently attached to buildings

#### 3. **Property of Another Person**
- The property must belong to someone other than the accused
- A person cannot commit theft of his own property
- Exception: A person can be guilty of theft of his own property if it is in lawful possession of another (e.g., pledged property)

**Example**: A pawns his watch to B. Later A takes the watch back without B's consent and without repaying the loan. A commits theft of his own watch because it was in lawful possession of B.

#### 4. **Without Consent**
- The taking must be without the consent of the person in possession
- **Consent Obtained by Fraud**: If consent is obtained by putting a person in fear of death or hurt, it is not valid consent
- **Consent of Authorized Person**: If property is in possession of a servant/agent, the consent of the master/principal is required

**Example**: A tells B (a shopkeeper) that he wants to examine a ring. B hands over the ring. A runs away with it. This is theft because B's consent was only for examination, not for taking possession.

#### 5. **Out of Possession**
- The property must be taken "out of the possession" of another
- **Possession** includes actual physical possession or constructive possession (legal control)
- The person from whom property is taken need not be the owner; possession is sufficient

**Example**: A borrows B's car for one day but keeps it for a week without permission. This is not theft at the time of borrowing (consent existed), but may constitute criminal breach of trust for wrongful retention.

#### 6. **Moving the Property**
- There must be some actual movement or severance of the property
- Even slight movement is sufficient if it is in the direction of taking
- The taking need not be completed; attempt to move is enough if intention exists

**Example**: A puts his hand in B's pocket and touches B's wallet but is caught before removing it. This may not be theft (no movement), but preparation for theft. However, if A lifts the wallet even slightly, it is theft.

#### 7. **Intention to Take Permanently or for Unlawful Gain**
- The accused must intend to:
  - Take the property permanently (deprive owner forever), OR
  - Take it temporarily for unlawful gain or wrongful loss to another

**Example 1 (Permanent Taking)**: A takes B's bicycle and sells it. Theft is complete.

**Example 2 (Temporary Taking for Gain)**: A takes B's car for a joyride without permission and returns it after 2 hours. This is NOT theft under IPC (no intention to keep permanently or for unlawful gain), but may be other offences.

**Exception**: If temporary taking causes wrongful loss to owner or wrongful gain to taker, it may constitute theft.

---

## Explanations to Section 378 (Important Clarifications)

### Explanation 1: Severance and Theft
A thing so long as it is attached to the earth, not being movable property, is not the subject of theft; but it becomes capable of being the subject of theft as soon as it is severed from the earth.

**Example**: A cuts down a tree in B's land with intent to steal the timber. The moment A severs the tree from the soil, theft is committed.

### Explanation 2: Causing a Thing to Move
A person who causes a thing to move is said to move it within the meaning of theft, even if the thing is made to move by the agency of another person or animal.

**Example**: A induces B's bullocks to move, thereby causing the cart attached to them to move out of B's possession. A commits theft.

### Explanation 3: Dishonest Removal of Obstacle
The consent mentioned in the definition may be express or implied, and may be given either by the person in possession, or by any person having for that purpose authority either express or implied.

**Example**: A finds a ring on the highway, not knowing to whom it belongs. If A keeps it without making reasonable efforts to find the owner, this may be theft (dishonest retention). But if A picks it up intending to find the owner, no theft.

### Explanation 4: Lawful Possession and Theft
A person cannot commit theft of property in his own possession unless the property is in the lawful possession of another person, and the taking is dishonest.

**Example**: A deposits goods with B as a pledge. If A takes them back dishonestly without B's consent and without redeeming the pledge, A commits theft, even though the goods belong to A.

### Explanation 5: Wild Animals
When wild animals are tamed or domesticated, they can be the subject of theft while they remain in a domesticated state or within the owner's control.

**Example**: A takes B's trained parrot from B's cage. Theft is committed. But if A catches a wild parrot in the forest, no theft (no owner).

---

## Punishment for Theft

### Section 379 IPC / Section 304 BNS: Simple Theft
**Punishment**:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to 3 years, OR
- **Fine**, OR
- **Both**

**Nature**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Any Magistrate

### Section 380 IPC / Section 305 BNS: Theft in Dwelling House, Vessel, or Vehicle
Whoever commits theft in any building, tent, or vessel used as a human dwelling, or used for the custody of property, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to 7 years, AND
- **Fine**

**Aggravating Circumstances**:
- Theft committed in a dwelling house (greater violation of trust and safety)
- Theft from a vessel (ship, boat)
- Theft from a vehicle used for custody of property

**Example**: A enters B's house through an open window at night and steals a laptop. This is theft in a dwelling house (Section 380), punishable with up to 7 years.

### Section 381 IPC / Section 306 BNS: Theft by Clerk or Servant
Whoever, being a clerk or servant, or employed in the capacity of a clerk or servant, commits theft in respect of any property in the possession of his master or employer, shall be punished with:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to 7 years, AND
- **Fine**

**Rationale**: Breach of trust and confidence reposed in the employee.

**Example**: A, a cashier in a bank, takes Rs. 10,000 from the cash counter for his personal use. This is theft by a servant (Section 381), even if A later returns the money.

**Distinction from Criminal Breach of Trust (Section 405)**:
- **Theft by Servant**: Taking property without being entrusted with dominion over it (mere possession for employer's benefit)
- **Criminal Breach of Trust**: Dishonestly misappropriating or converting property already entrusted with dominion over it

### Section 382 IPC / Section 307 BNS: Theft After Preparation for Causing Death, Hurt, or Restraint
Whoever commits theft, having made preparation for causing death, or hurt, or restraint, or fear of death, or hurt, or restraint, to any person, shall be punished with:
- **Rigorous Imprisonment**: Up to 10 years, AND
- **Fine**

**Aggravating Circumstances**:
- Preparation for causing death, hurt, or wrongful restraint in order to commit theft or to facilitate escape or to retain stolen property

**Example**: A and his associates, armed with weapons, enter B's house with the intention to commit theft and to cause hurt to anyone who resists. Even if no actual violence occurs, the preparation with weapons makes it theft under Section 382.

**Distinction from Robbery**:
- **Section 382 Theft**: Preparation for violence (may not actually use violence)
- **Robbery**: Actual use of violence or putting in fear of instant death/hurt during theft

---

## Distinction Between Theft and Related Offences

### 1. Theft vs. Robbery (IPC 390)

| **Element** | **Theft (Section 378)** | **Robbery (Section 390)** |
|-------------|-------------------------|----------------------------|
| **Violence** | No violence or putting in fear | Theft is or attempted to be committed with violence or fear of instant death/hurt |
| **Timing of Force** | No force used | Force used during commission of theft or to retain stolen property |
| **Punishment** | Up to 3 years (simple theft) | Minimum 1 year, up to 10 years (rigorous imprisonment) |
| **Nature** | Non-bailable (simple theft) | Non-bailable, more serious |

**Example**: A snatches B's chain from her neck while walking on the street. If A uses force or causes fear of instant harm, it is robbery. If A picks the chain from B's bag without B noticing, it is theft.

### 2. Theft vs. Extortion (IPC 383)

| **Element** | **Theft (Section 378)** | **Extortion (Section 383)** |
|-------------|-------------------------|------------------------------|
| **Mode of Taking** | Taking property dishonestly by moving it | Intentionally putting person in fear to deliver property |
| **Consent** | Without consent | With consent, but consent obtained by fear |
| **Fear Element** | No fear induced | Fear of injury to person or property, or fear of accusation |
| **Delivery** | Accused takes property | Victim delivers property |

**Example**: A threatens to publish defamatory content about B unless B pays Rs. 1 lakh. B pays. This is extortion (consent obtained by fear), not theft.

### 3. Theft vs. Criminal Breach of Trust (IPC 405)

| **Element** | **Theft (Section 378)** | **Criminal Breach of Trust (Section 405)** |
|-------------|-------------------------|---------------------------------------------|
| **Entrustment** | No entrustment; property taken without consent | Property already entrusted to accused in fiduciary capacity |
| **Possession** | Accused takes possession dishonestly | Accused already has lawful possession/dominion |
| **Act** | Taking movable property | Misappropriation or conversion or disposal in violation of trust |
| **Intention** | Dishonest intention at time of taking | Dishonest intention at time of misappropriation (may arise later) |

**Example**: A borrows B's book and later sells it. This is criminal breach of trust (book was entrusted to A), not theft (A had lawful possession).

### 4. Theft vs. Cheating (IPC 415)

| **Element** | **Theft (Section 378)** | **Cheating (Section 415)** |
|-------------|-------------------------|----------------------------|
| **Deception** | No deception; property taken | Deception induces victim to deliver property or consent |
| **Consent** | Without consent | With consent, but consent obtained by deception |
| **Delivery** | Accused takes property | Victim delivers property voluntarily (though deceived) |

**Example**: A impersonates a bank official and convinces B to transfer money to A's account. This is cheating (consent obtained by deception), not theft.

---

## Landmark Cases on Theft

### 1. **Pyare Lal Bhargava v. State of Rajasthan** (1963) - Dishonest Intention
**AIR 1963 SC 1094**

**Facts**: The accused took grain from a shop intending to pay for it later but did not have permission from the shopkeeper.

**Held**: The Supreme Court held that **dishonest intention is the essence of theft**. If there is no dishonest intention at the time of taking, there is no theft. If the accused genuinely believed he had the right to take the property or intended to pay, dishonest intention may be absent.

**Principle**: Dishonest intention must exist at the time of taking the property. Subsequent dishonest act (e.g., not paying later) does not convert innocent taking into theft.

### 2. **State of Maharashtra v. Mohammed Yakub** (1980) - Electricity as Movable Property
**AIR 1980 SC 1111**

**Facts**: The accused was charged with theft of electricity by installing unauthorized connections and bypassing the meter.

**Held**: The Supreme Court held that **electricity is "movable property"** within the meaning of Section 378 IPC, and unauthorized abstraction of electricity constitutes theft.

**Principle**: The definition of "movable property" in Section 22 IPC includes electricity. Dishonestly using or abstracting electricity without payment constitutes theft.

**Legislative Development**: The Electricity Act, 2003 now provides specific provisions for theft of electricity (Section 135), but the IPC provision remains applicable.

### 3. **K.N. Mehra v. State of Rajasthan** (1957) - Temporary Taking
**AIR 1957 SC 369**

**Facts**: The accused took a vehicle for temporary use without the owner's consent and returned it after a short period.

**Held**: For theft, there must be an intention to take the property **permanently** or to cause **wrongful loss** to the owner. Mere temporary deprivation without any intention to cause wrongful loss or wrongful gain does not constitute theft.

**Principle**: Temporary taking without dishonest intention or wrongful gain does not amount to theft. However, if temporary taking causes wrongful loss or gain, it may constitute theft.

### 4. **Ramjas Foundation v. Union of India** (1993) - Removal from Possession
**AIR 1993 SC 852**

**Facts**: The case involved the interpretation of "taking out of possession" in the context of trust property.

**Held**: The Supreme Court clarified that **possession** in Section 378 includes not only physical possession but also **constructive possession** (legal control over property). Taking property out of legal control without consent constitutes theft.

**Principle**: The concept of "possession" for theft is broader than physical custody. It includes legal possession, custody, and control.

### 5. **Shri Narayan v. State** (1962) - Consent Obtained by Deception
**AIR 1962 All 88**

**Facts**: The accused borrowed money from the complainant with no intention of returning it, having given false information about his financial status.

**Held**: If property is obtained with consent but the consent is induced by deception, the offence is **cheating** (Section 415), not theft. Theft requires taking **without consent**, whereas cheating involves obtaining property **with consent procured by deception**.

**Principle**: Distinction between theft (no consent) and cheating (consent obtained by deception) is critical for proper charge framing.

### 6. **State of Haryana v. Rajinder Singh** (2004) - Dishonest Retention
**2004 Cri LJ 4265**

**Facts**: The accused found a lost article and kept it without making efforts to find the owner.

**Held**: Finding lost property and keeping it with dishonest intention (no effort to find owner) can constitute theft if the finder has reasonable means of discovering the owner and intentionally omits to do so.

**Principle**: Dishonest retention of found property with knowledge or means of discovering the owner can be theft. Finder has a duty to make reasonable efforts to return lost property.

### 7. **Shiv Kumar v. State** (1999) - Servant's Temporary Taking
**1999 Cri LJ 3890**

**Facts**: A servant took his employer's money temporarily, intending to return it, but used it for gambling.

**Held**: Even if a servant takes property temporarily, if there is **dishonest intention** at the time of taking (intention to use for personal benefit causing wrongful loss to employer), it constitutes theft by servant under Section 381.

**Principle**: Temporary taking by a servant with dishonest intention is theft if it causes wrongful loss to employer or wrongful gain to servant.

---

## IPC to BNS Transition Mapping

| **IPC Section** | **BNS Section** | **Offence** | **Changes** |
|-----------------|-----------------|-------------|-------------|
| **378** | **303** | Definition of Theft | No substantive change; definition remains identical |
| **379** | **304** | Punishment for Theft | No change in punishment (up to 3 years or fine or both) |
| **380** | **305** | Theft in Dwelling House, etc. | No change in punishment (up to 7 years and fine) |
| **381** | **306** | Theft by Clerk or Servant | No change in punishment (up to 7 years and fine) |
| **382** | **307** | Theft After Preparation for Death/Hurt | No change in punishment (up to 10 years RI and fine) |

**Note**: The BNS 2023 has retained the theft provisions from IPC 1860 with minimal changes. The definition, essential ingredients, and punishments remain substantively the same.

---

## Practical Examples

### Example 1: Simple Theft from Public Place
**Scenario**: Ravi is sitting in a restaurant. He notices that the person sitting next to him, Amit, has left his mobile phone on the table and gone to the washroom. Ravi picks up the phone and puts it in his pocket, intending to sell it later.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Dishonest intention: Ravi intends to wrongfully gain by selling Amit's phone
2. ✅ Movable property: Mobile phone is movable property
3. ✅ Property of another: Phone belongs to Amit
4. ✅ Without consent: Amit did not consent to Ravi taking the phone
5. ✅ Out of possession: Ravi moved the phone from Amit's possession (constructive) to his own
6. ✅ Moving the property: Ravi picked up and pocketed the phone
7. ✅ Intention to take permanently: Ravi intends to sell the phone (permanent deprivation)

**Offence**: Theft under Section 379 IPC / Section 304 BNS (simple theft)
**Punishment**: Up to 3 years imprisonment or fine or both

### Example 2: Theft in Dwelling House
**Scenario**: Priya works as a maid in Mr. Sharma's house. One day, while Mr. Sharma is at work, Priya enters his bedroom and takes a gold necklace from the cupboard, intending to keep it.

**Analysis**:
- All seven ingredients of theft are satisfied (dishonest intention, movable property, without consent, etc.)
- **Aggravating factor**: Theft committed in a dwelling house
- **Additional consideration**: Priya is a servant, but if she was not entrusted with access to the bedroom/cupboard (she took it by entering without permission), it may be theft in dwelling house rather than theft by servant

**Offence**: Theft in Dwelling House under Section 380 IPC / Section 305 BNS
**Punishment**: Up to 7 years imprisonment AND fine

**Alternative**: If Priya had lawful access to the bedroom for cleaning purposes (entrusted access), it could also be theft by servant (Section 381).

### Example 3: Theft by Servant
**Scenario**: Rajesh is a cashier at a retail store. One evening, he takes Rs. 5,000 from the cash register and puts it in his pocket, intending to return it the next day after using it for an urgent personal expense. However, he does not inform his employer or take permission.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ All ingredients of theft are present
2. ✅ Rajesh is a servant (employee) of the store
3. ✅ Property (money) is in the possession of his employer
4. ✅ Even if he intends to return it, the taking is dishonest (wrongful temporary gain, wrongful loss to employer who loses use of money)

**Offence**: Theft by Clerk or Servant under Section 381 IPC / Section 306 BNS
**Punishment**: Up to 7 years imprisonment AND fine

**Note**: If Rajesh had been entrusted with dominion over the money (e.g., authorized to use it for business purposes), his dishonest use would be criminal breach of trust (Section 405) rather than theft.

### Example 4: No Theft (Lack of Dishonest Intention)
**Scenario**: Sanjay sees an umbrella at a restaurant which looks identical to his own. He picks it up thinking it is his and takes it home. Later he realizes it belongs to someone else and returns it immediately.

**Analysis**:
1. ❌ No dishonest intention: Sanjay genuinely believed the umbrella was his own at the time of taking
2. ✅ All other ingredients may be satisfied, but without dishonest intention, no theft

**Offence**: No offence committed (honest mistake)

**Principle**: Dishonest intention at the time of taking is essential for theft. Bona fide mistake negates dishonesty.

### Example 5: Theft After Preparation for Causing Death/Hurt
**Scenario**: A gang of four persons, armed with knives and rods, breaks into a jewelry shop at night. They tie up the security guard and threaten him with death if he raises an alarm. They then take jewelry worth Rs. 10 lakh and flee.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ All ingredients of theft are present
2. ✅ **Aggravating factor**: The gang made preparation for causing death or hurt (armed with weapons, threatened security guard)
3. ✅ The preparation and threat were to facilitate the commission of theft and escape

**Offence**: Theft After Preparation for Causing Death, Hurt, or Restraint under Section 382 IPC / Section 307 BNS

**Punishment**: Up to 10 years rigorous imprisonment AND fine

**Note**: If actual violence was used or the guard was put in fear of **instant** death/hurt during the taking, it would be **robbery** (Section 390), which has higher punishment.

### Example 6: Electricity Theft
**Scenario**: Mohan operates a small factory. To reduce his electricity bills, he installs a device to bypass the electric meter so that his actual consumption is not recorded. He dishonestly abstracts electricity worth Rs. 50,000 over six months.

**Analysis**:
1. ✅ Dishonest intention: Mohan intends to wrongfully gain by not paying for electricity
2. ✅ Movable property: Electricity is treated as movable property (Supreme Court in Mohammed Yakub case)
3. ✅ Property of another: Electricity belongs to the electricity supply company
4. ✅ Without consent: The company did not consent to unmetered supply
5. ✅ Taking out of possession: Mohan abstracted/diverted electricity from the company's supply

**Offence**: Theft of Electricity under Section 379 IPC / Section 304 BNS
**Alternative Charge**: Theft of electricity under Section 135 of the Electricity Act, 2003 (more specific)

**Punishment**:
- Under IPC: Up to 3 years imprisonment or fine or both
- Under Electricity Act, 2003: Up to 3 years imprisonment AND fine (minimum Rs. 50,000, may extend to twice the value of electricity stolen)

---

## Defenses to Theft

### 1. **Absence of Dishonest Intention**
- If the accused can show that there was no dishonest intention at the time of taking, theft cannot be proved
- **Example**: Bona fide belief that the property belonged to the accused, or that the accused had a right to take it

**Case Law**: *Pyare Lal Bhargava v. State of Rajasthan* (1963) - Genuine belief of right to property is a defense

### 2. **Consent of the Owner**
- If the owner or person in possession consented to the taking, there is no theft
- The consent must be free, not obtained by fraud or fear (otherwise it may be cheating or extortion)

**Example**: If B lends his car to A, A's taking of the car is not theft (consent exists)

### 3. **Claim of Right (Bona Fide Claim)**
- If the accused had a bona fide belief that he had a legal right to the property, even if the belief is mistaken, it negates dishonest intention
- The claim must be honest, not a mere pretext

**Example**: A takes property from B believing in good faith that it was pledged to him, though the pledge was invalid. If the belief is honest, no theft.

### 4. **Property Belonged to Accused**
- A person cannot commit theft of his own property unless it is in the lawful possession of another
- If accused is the owner and the property is not in lawful possession of another, taking it is not theft

**Example**: A removes his own goods from his own warehouse. No theft, even if creditors have a claim on the goods (unless goods were under legal custody/attachment).

### 5. **No Movement of Property**
- If the property was not actually moved, theft is not complete
- However, even slight movement with dishonest intention may constitute theft

**Example**: A puts his hand in B's pocket but is caught before touching the wallet. No theft (no movement yet).

### 6. **Mistake of Fact**
- If the accused took the property under a genuine mistake of fact (e.g., believed it was his own property), dishonest intention is absent

**Example**: A takes an umbrella from a stand, genuinely believing it to be his own (identical appearance). Later realizes mistake and returns it. No theft.

---

## Procedural Aspects

### Cognizance and Complaint
- **Simple Theft (Section 379)**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Any Magistrate
- **Aggravated Theft (Sections 380-382)**: Cognizable, Non-bailable, Triable by Magistrate of First Class

### Investigation
- Police can arrest without warrant (cognizable offence)
- FIR can be registered immediately upon complaint
- Investigation includes: recording statements, recovering stolen property, identifying accused

### Trial
- Charge sheet to be filed after investigation
- Trial before Magistrate (not requiring jury)
- Burden of proof on prosecution to prove all seven ingredients of theft beyond reasonable doubt

### Compounding
- **Simple Theft (Section 379)**: Compoundable with permission of court (Section 320 CrPC / Section 360 BNS)
- **Aggravated Theft (Sections 380-382)**: Non-compoundable

**Compounding**: The complainant and accused can reach a compromise and end the criminal case with court's permission (for simple theft only).

---

## Compliance Checklist

### For Potential Victims (Preventive Measures)
- [ ] Secure movable property in dwelling houses (locks, safes, alarms)
- [ ] Maintain inventory and records of valuable movable property
- [ ] Install CCTV cameras in vulnerable areas (shops, warehouses, parking)
- [ ] Conduct background verification of domestic servants and employees with access to property
- [ ] Implement access control systems for employees (limit access to cash, valuables)
- [ ] Report lost or stolen property to police immediately (timely FIR)
- [ ] Maintain insurance for valuable property against theft

### For Accused/Suspect (Legal Safeguards)
- [ ] Cooperate with investigation if innocent; provide evidence of lawful possession or consent
- [ ] Claim defenses: absence of dishonest intention, claim of right, mistake of fact, consent
- [ ] Seek bail (theft is bailable only for simple theft under Section 379; aggravated forms are non-bailable)
- [ ] Engage competent defense counsel
- [ ] Return stolen property if possible (may mitigate sentence, though not a complete defense)
- [ ] Explore compounding (compromise with victim) if offence is simple theft under Section 379

### For Investigating Officer
- [ ] Register FIR promptly upon complaint
- [ ] Identify and interview witnesses; record statements under Section 161 CrPC
- [ ] Recover stolen property and prepare recovery memo (seizure list)
- [ ] Establish chain of custody for recovered property
- [ ] Prove all seven ingredients of theft: dishonest intention, movable property, without consent, out of possession, movement, intention to take
- [ ] Collect evidence: CCTV footage, eyewitness testimony, confession (if voluntary), recovery of stolen goods
- [ ] Differentiate theft from related offences (robbery, extortion, breach of trust, cheating)
- [ ] File charge sheet within statutory time limits

### For Prosecutors
- [ ] Frame appropriate charge: Section 379, 380, 381, or 382 based on facts
- [ ] Prove dishonest intention (most critical element)
- [ ] Prove ownership or possession of property by complainant
- [ ] Prove lack of consent
- [ ] Prove movement of property by accused
- [ ] Anticipate and counter defenses (claim of right, consent, mistake)
- [ ] Examine witnesses thoroughly; corroborate evidence

### For Defense Counsel
- [ ] Challenge dishonest intention: establish bona fide claim of right or honest belief
- [ ] Challenge identification of accused (if based on circumstantial evidence)
- [ ] Argue consent of owner (if applicable)
- [ ] Argue lack of movement or possession (if applicable)
- [ ] Challenge ownership/possession of complainant
- [ ] Argue mistake of fact or innocent taking
- [ ] Seek acquittal if any of the seven ingredients is not proved beyond reasonable doubt
- [ ] If conviction likely, argue for leniency: first-time offender, return of property, family circumstances

---

## Cross-References

### Related Criminal Offences
- **Robbery (IPC 390)**: Theft with violence or fear of instant harm
- **Dacoity (IPC 391)**: Robbery by five or more persons
- **Extortion (IPC 383-389)**: Obtaining property by putting person in fear
- **Criminal Breach of Trust (IPC 405-409)**: Dishonest misappropriation of entrusted property
- **Cheating (IPC 415-420)**: Fraudulently inducing delivery of property
- **Mischief (IPC 425-440)**: Causing damage to property
- **Criminal Trespass (IPC 441-462)**: Entering property with intent to commit offence

### Constitutional Provisions
- **Article 21**: Right to Property (though no longer a fundamental right under Article 19(1)(f), protection of property from theft is part of rule of law)
- **Article 300A**: No person shall be deprived of property save by authority of law

### Procedural Laws
- **Code of Criminal Procedure (CrPC), 1973 / Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita (BNSS), 2023**
  - Section 154: Registration of FIR
  - Section 157: Investigation
  - Section 161: Examination of witnesses by police
  - Section 320: Compounding of offences (simple theft is compoundable with court permission)
  - Section 436: Bail (simple theft is bailable; aggravated theft is non-bailable)

### Evidence Law
- **Indian Evidence Act, 1872 / Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam (BSA), 2023**
  - Section 27: Admissibility of information leading to discovery of stolen property
  - Section 114: Presumption of knowledge (if stolen property found in possession soon after theft)
  - Illustration (a) to Section 114: If a person is found in possession of stolen property soon after the theft, the court may presume he is the thief or received it knowing it to be stolen

### Special Legislations
- **Electricity Act, 2003**: Section 135 (specific provision for theft of electricity)
- **Information Technology Act, 2000**: Cyber theft, unauthorized access to computer systems

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Seven Essential Ingredients**: Dishonest intention, movable property, property of another, without consent, out of possession, moving, intention to take permanently or for gain

2. **Dishonest Intention is Paramount**: Without dishonest intention at the time of taking, there is no theft, even if other elements are present

3. **Movable Property Only**: Land and immovable property cannot be subject of theft; only movable property (including electricity by legal fiction)

4. **Consent Negates Theft**: If property is taken with consent, it is not theft (but may be cheating if consent is obtained by deception, or extortion if obtained by fear)

5. **Distinction from Other Offences**:
   - **Theft**: Without consent, no violence, no deception
   - **Robbery**: Theft + violence or fear of instant harm
   - **Extortion**: Consent obtained by fear
   - **Cheating**: Consent obtained by deception
   - **Criminal Breach of Trust**: Property already entrusted, dishonest misappropriation

6. **Aggravated Forms Attract Higher Punishment**:
   - Simple theft: up to 3 years
   - Theft in dwelling house: up to 7 years
   - Theft by servant: up to 7 years
   - Theft after preparation for death/hurt: up to 10 years (rigorous imprisonment)

7. **Defenses**: Claim of right, absence of dishonest intention, consent, mistake of fact, ownership

8. **Compounding**: Simple theft (Section 379) is compoundable with court permission, allowing compromise between victim and accused

---

## Conclusion

Theft is the foundational property offence in Indian criminal law, distinguished by the element of **dishonest taking** of **movable property** without consent. Understanding the seven essential ingredients and the fine distinctions between theft and related offences (robbery, extortion, breach of trust, cheating) is critical for proper charge framing, investigation, and adjudication.

The law balances the need to protect property rights with the requirement of proving dishonest intention beyond reasonable doubt, ensuring that innocent mistakes or bona fide claims of right do not result in wrongful conviction.

With the transition to BNS 2023, the substantive law on theft remains largely unchanged, ensuring continuity and predictability in legal practice.

---

**Document Version**: 1.0
**Effective Date**: IPC provisions apply until June 30, 2024; BNS provisions effective from July 1, 2024
**Last Updated**: December 6, 2025
**Prepared by**: Legal Protocol Documentation System
**Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)

---

**Disclaimer**: This protocol is for informational and educational purposes only and does not constitute legal advice. For specific legal matters, consult a qualified criminal law advocate or legal expert.


---
## IL-CRIM-TRIAL.md
---

# Criminal Trial - India Legal Protocol

## Metadata
- **Protocol ID**: IL-CRIM-TRIAL
- **Version**: 1.0.0
- **Effective Date**: January 2025
- **Last Updated**: January 2025
- **Jurisdiction**: India (All States and Union Territories)
- **Domain**: Criminal Law
- **Subdomain**: Criminal Procedure
- **IPC Provisions**: N/A
- **BNS 2023 Provisions**: N/A
- **CrPC Provisions**: Sections 190-265, 309-327
- **BNSS 2023 Provisions**: Sections 210-285, 363-381
- **Related Protocols**: IL-CRIM-INVESTIGATION, IL-CRIM-EVIDENCE, IL-CRIM-APPEALS

---

## Overview and Purpose

### What is a Criminal Trial?

A **criminal trial** is the **formal judicial proceeding** where the prosecution presents evidence to prove an accused person guilty beyond reasonable doubt, and the accused has the opportunity to defend against the charges.

### Purpose of Criminal Trial

1. **Determine Guilt or Innocence**: Establish whether accused committed the offence
2. **Ensure Justice**: Fair opportunity to both prosecution and defense
3. **Protect Rights**: Safeguard constitutional and legal rights of accused
4. **Public Confidence**: Transparent process builds trust in justice system
5. **Sentence**: If convicted, determine appropriate punishment
6. **Acquit Innocent**: Protect innocent from wrongful conviction

### Fundamental Principles

1. **Presumption of Innocence**: Accused innocent until proven guilty (Article 21 + Section 101 Evidence Act)
2. **Burden of Proof on Prosecution**: Prosecution must prove guilt beyond reasonable doubt
3. **Right to Fair Trial**: Article 21 - procedure must be fair, just, reasonable
4. **Right to Defense**: Article 22(1) - right to be defended by lawyer
5. **Public Trial**: Open court proceedings (except specific cases)
6. **Speedy Trial**: Article 21 - undue delay violates fundamental rights

---

## Legal Framework and Statutory Basis

### Constitutional Foundation

#### Article 20: Protection in Respect of Conviction for Offences
- **(1)** No ex post facto laws (no retrospective criminal laws)
- **(2)** No double jeopardy (cannot be tried twice for same offence)
- **(3)** No self-incrimination (cannot be compelled to be witness against self)

#### Article 21: Right to Life and Personal Liberty
- Includes **right to fair trial**
- Includes **right to speedy trial**
- Procedure must be just, fair, and reasonable

#### Article 22: Protection Against Arrest and Detention
- **(1)** Right to consult and be defended by lawyer of choice

### Primary Legislation

#### Code of Criminal Procedure (CrPC) 1973
- **Chapter XIV**: Conditions Requisite for Initiation of Proceedings (190-199)
- **Chapter XV**: Complaints to Magistrates (200-210)
- **Chapter XVI**: Commencement of Proceedings Before Magistrates (204-210)
- **Chapter XVII**: Inquiry and Trial by Magistrates (211-259)
- **Chapter XVIII**: Trial Before Court of Session (225-250)
- **Chapter XX**: Trial of Warrant Cases by Magistrates (238-250)
- **Chapter XXI**: Summary Trials (260-265)
- **Chapter XXIII**: General Provisions as to Inquiries and Trials (309-327)

#### Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita (BNSS) 2023
- **Chapters corresponding to above** (Sections 210-381)
- Effective from **July 1, 2024**
- Substantively similar with enhanced provisions for victim rights

### Supporting Legislation

1. **Indian Evidence Act 1872 / Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam (BSA) 2023**: Rules of evidence
2. **IPC 1860 / BNS 2023**: Substantive criminal law
3. **Juvenile Justice Act 2015**: Trials of juveniles
4. **POCSO Act 2012**: Child sexual offence trials
5. **NIA Act 2008**: Terrorism trials

---

## Stages of Criminal Trial

### Pre-Trial Stage

#### 1. Taking Cognizance (Section 190 CrPC / 210 BNSS)

**What is Cognizance?**
Application of judicial mind to the facts of a case to determine if judicial proceedings should be initiated

**Who Takes Cognizance?**
**Magistrate** only (not police, not Sessions Judge initially)

**How Cognizance Taken?** (Section 190)

Three modes:
1. **Upon police report** (charge sheet under Section 173)
2. **Upon complaint** (private complaint by complainant)
3. **Upon information** from any person (other than police report) OR upon own knowledge

**Landmark Case**: **R.R. Chari v. State of U.P. (1951)**
- Cognizance is application of judicial mind
- Magistrate must be satisfied that prima facie case exists
- Mechanical taking of cognizance not permissible

#### 2. Inquiry/Summoning of Accused (Sections 200-204 CrPC / 223-229 BNSS)

**If Cognizance on Complaint**:

**Section 200**: Magistrate to examine complainant on oath

**Section 202**: Magistrate may:
- Postpone issue of process
- Direct investigation by police (if cognizable)
- Inquire himself or order inquiry

**Section 204**: If satisfied offence has been committed:
- **Issue summons** (for appearance of accused)
- OR **Issue warrant** (if case requires)

**If Cognizance on Police Report**:
- Directly proceed to frame charges (if sessions triable)
- OR summon accused (if Magistrate triable)

#### 3. Discharge (Section 239, 245 CrPC / 262, 268 BNSS)

**What is Discharge?**
Release of accused from case at pre-trial stage if no sufficient grounds to proceed

**When Granted?**
- No prima facie case
- Evidence insufficient
- Case based on conjecture

**Difference from Acquittal**:
- **Discharge**: Before trial begins (at framing of charges stage)
- **Acquittal**: After trial, on merits

**Landmark Case**: **State of Bihar v. Ramesh Singh (1977)**
- At discharge stage, court does not require proof beyond reasonable doubt
- Only **prima facie case** to be seen
- If two views possible, one leading to conviction and other to discharge, benefit to accused

#### 4. Framing of Charges (Sections 228, 240, 246 CrPC / 251, 263, 271 BNSS)

**Purpose**: Inform accused of precise accusation

**Procedure**:
1. Prosecution evidence heard (in Sessions cases - Section 228)
2. Accused heard on proposed charges (Section 240)
3. Charges framed in writing (Section 211)
4. **Charges read and explained** to accused (Section 228)
5. Accused asked to plead guilty or claim trial

**Contents of Charge** (Section 211 / 233 BNSS):
- Offence with which accused is charged
- Law and section under which offence punishable
- Particulars of offence (time, place, manner)

**Landmark Case**: **State of Maharashtra v. Som Nath Thapa (1996)**
- Charge is foundation of trial
- Defect in charge may vitiate trial
- But if accused not prejudiced, conviction can be upheld

**Effect of Plea**:
- **Pleads Guilty**: Court may convict on plea (Section 229)
- **Claims Trial**: Trial proceeds

---

## Types of Trials

### Based on Court

| Trial Type | Court | Offences | Procedure |
|-----------|-------|----------|-----------|
| **Warrant Cases by Magistrate** | Judicial Magistrate | Punishable with >2 years | Sections 238-250 CrPC / 261-274 BNSS |
| **Summons Cases by Magistrate** | Judicial Magistrate | Punishable with ≤2 years | Sections 251-259 CrPC / 275-285 BNSS |
| **Summary Trial** | Magistrate | Petty offences | Sections 260-265 CrPC / 321-326 BNSS |
| **Sessions Trial** | Sessions Court | Serious offences (death, life, >7 years) | Sections 225-237 CrPC / 248-260 BNSS |

### 1. Trial Before Court of Session (Sections 225-237 CrPC / 248-260 BNSS)

**Jurisdiction**: Offences **exclusively triable by Sessions Court**:
- Punishable with death
- Punishable with life imprisonment
- Punishable with imprisonment for term exceeding 7 years (if so specified in law)

**Procedure**:

#### Step 1: Committal (Section 209 CrPC / 230 BNSS)
- **Magistrate commits** case to Sessions Court (after taking cognizance)
- Committal is transferring case from Magistrate to Sessions Court

#### Step 2: Sessions Judge Takes Cognizance
- Charge sheet and case diary forwarded
- Sessions Judge examines record

#### Step 3: Framing of Charge (Section 228 CrPC / 251 BNSS)
- Prosecution evidence examined
- Accused heard
- If prima facie case exists: Charges framed
- If not: Accused discharged (Section 227 / 250 BNSS)

#### Step 4: Plea (Section 229 CrPC / 252 BNSS)
- Charge read and explained to accused
- Accused asked to plead

**If Pleads Guilty**:
- Judge may convict (after satisfying himself plea is voluntary and accused understands consequences)

**If Claims Trial**: Trial proceeds

#### Step 5: Prosecution Evidence (Section 231 CrPC / 254 BNSS)
- **Prosecutor's opening address** (optional)
- **Examination of prosecution witnesses**:
  - **Examination-in-Chief** (by prosecution)
  - **Cross-Examination** (by defense)
  - **Re-Examination** (by prosecution, if needed)
- **Documentary evidence** produced

#### Step 6: Statement of Accused (Section 313 CrPC / 367 BNSS)

**When**: After prosecution evidence concludes

**Purpose**:
- Give accused opportunity to **explain incriminating circumstances**
- Accused confronted with evidence against them
- Accused can offer explanations

**Procedure**:
- Court questions accused on incriminating circumstances
- Answers recorded
- **Not on oath**
- Can be used to corroborate defense but generally not incriminating (if accused gives new version, can be used against)

**Landmark Case**: **Rahim Beg v. State of U.P. (1973)**
- Section 313 examination is **mandatory**
- Failure to examine under Section 313 is **fatal irregularity**
- Trial may be vitiated

#### Step 7: Defense Evidence (Section 233 CrPC / 257 BNSS)

**After Section 313**:
- **Accused may produce defense evidence**:
  - **Defense witnesses** (examined, cross-examined, re-examined)
  - **Documentary evidence**
- Accused may also testify (optional)

**Right to Silence**: Accused cannot be compelled to testify (Article 20(3))

#### Step 8: Arguments (Section 234 CrPC / 258 BNSS)

**Prosecution's Final Arguments**: Summarize evidence, argue guilt

**Defense's Final Arguments**: Counter prosecution, argue innocence, highlight reasonable doubt

#### Step 9: Judgment (Section 235, 354 CrPC / 259, 408 BNSS)

**Court considers**:
- All evidence
- Prosecution arguments
- Defense arguments
- Applies legal principles

**Verdict**:
- **Conviction**: If guilt proved beyond reasonable doubt
- **Acquittal**: If reasonable doubt exists OR prosecution fails to prove guilt

**Judgment Requirements** (Section 354 / 408 BNSS):
- **In writing**
- **Points for determination**
- **Decision on each point** with reasons
- **Language understood** by accused

**Signature**: Signed and dated by Judge

#### Step 10: Sentencing (Section 235(2) CrPC / 259(2) BNSS)

**If Convicted**:
- **Separate hearing on sentence**
- Accused given opportunity to be heard on sentence
- Mitigating and aggravating circumstances considered
- Sentence pronounced

### 2. Warrant Cases by Magistrate (Sections 238-250 CrPC / 261-274 BNSS)

**Jurisdiction**: Offences punishable with **imprisonment >2 years** (but not exclusively triable by Sessions Court)

**Two Procedures**:

#### A. Where Police Report Filed (Section 238 CrPC / 261 BNSS)

**Procedure**:
1. Magistrate takes cognizance on police report
2. If triable by Magistrate, proceeds to hear case
3. **Prosecution evidence** presented
4. **Section 313 statement** of accused
5. **Defense evidence**
6. **Arguments**
7. **Judgment**

#### B. Where Complaint Filed (Section 244 CrPC / 267 BNSS)

**Procedure**:
1. Magistrate takes cognizance on complaint
2. Process issued to accused (summons/warrant)
3. **Accused appears**
4. **Particulars of offence** stated to accused
5. **Accused may be discharged** if no sufficient ground (Section 245 / 268 BNSS)
6. If not discharged, **prosecution evidence** heard
7. **Section 313 statement**
8. **Defense evidence**
9. **Arguments**
10. **Judgment**

### 3. Summons Cases by Magistrate (Sections 251-259 CrPC / 275-285 BNSS)

**Jurisdiction**: Offences punishable with **imprisonment ≤2 years OR fine only OR both**

**Simplified Procedure**:
1. **Substance of accusation** stated to accused (not formal charge)
2. Accused asked to plead
3. **If Pleads Guilty**: Convicted and sentenced
4. **If Claims Trial**:
   - Prosecution evidence
   - Section 313 statement
   - Defense evidence
   - Arguments
   - Judgment

**Key Difference**: No formal charge framing; simplified procedure

### 4. Summary Trial (Sections 260-265 CrPC / 321-326 BNSS)

**Jurisdiction**: **Petty offences** specified in Section 260

**Examples**:
- Theft where value <Rs. 2,000
- Receiving stolen property where value <Rs. 2,000
- Assisting in concealment of stolen property
- Offences under Cattle Trespass Act, etc.

**Procedure**: **Highly Simplified**
- Brief facts recorded
- Plea taken
- Evidence (brief)
- Judgment (reasons may be brief)

**Limitation on Sentence**:
- Imprisonment: **Maximum 3 months**
- Fine: No limit

**Appeal**: To Sessions Court (within 30 days)

---

## Trial Procedure: Step-by-Step

### 1. Recording of Evidence (Sections 272-283 CrPC / 338-352 BNSS)

**All Evidence Recorded**:
- Witness testimony
- Documentary evidence
- Physical evidence (exhibits)

**Recording Method**:
- **In writing** (or typed/recorded electronically - BNSS enhancement)
- In **open court**
- In **language of court** (or translated)

#### Examination of Witnesses

**Three Stages**:

##### A. Examination-in-Chief
- **By party calling witness** (prosecution calls prosecution witnesses; defense calls defense witnesses)
- **Questions**: Open-ended, non-leading
- **Purpose**: Elicit favorable evidence

**Leading Questions Prohibited** (Section 141 Evidence Act / BSA): Questions suggesting answer

##### B. Cross-Examination
- **By opposite party**
- **Questions**: Can be leading
- **Purpose**: Challenge credibility, elicit favorable facts, contradict

**Right to Cross-Examine**: Fundamental right (Article 21)

**Landmark Case**: **State of U.P. v. Ram Babu Misra (1980)**
- Right to cross-examination is essential component of fair trial
- Denial of cross-examination may vitiate trial

##### C. Re-Examination
- **By party who called witness**
- **Purpose**: Clarify matters arising in cross-examination
- **Cannot introduce new facts** not in examination-in-chief

### 2. Statement Under Section 313 CrPC / 367 BNSS

(Already covered above in Sessions Trial procedure)

**Key Points**:
- **Mandatory** - failure is fatal irregularity
- **After prosecution evidence** concludes
- **Not on oath**
- **Opportunity to explain** incriminating circumstances
- Answers can be used in evidence (but limited use)

### 3. Defense Evidence

**Accused's Rights**:
1. **Remain silent**: No compulsion to testify (Article 20(3))
2. **Testify**: Can give evidence (but then subject to cross-examination)
3. **Call defense witnesses**: Can examine witnesses to support defense
4. **Produce documents**: Can produce documentary evidence

**Burden**: No burden on accused to prove innocence (burden on prosecution to prove guilt)

### 4. Arguments

**Prosecution's Final Arguments**:
- Summarize evidence
- Connect evidence to charges
- Argue guilt beyond reasonable doubt
- Cite law and precedents
- Request conviction and sentence

**Defense's Final Arguments**:
- Challenge prosecution evidence
- Highlight contradictions, lacunae
- Argue reasonable doubt
- Argue innocence or lesser offence
- Request acquittal or lenient sentence

### 5. Judgment

**Requirements** (Section 354 CrPC / 408 BNSS):
1. **Points for determination** clearly stated
2. **Decision on each point** with reasons
3. **Findings** on facts and law
4. **Conviction or acquittal** clearly pronounced
5. **Language** understood by accused

**Standard of Proof**: **Beyond Reasonable Doubt**

**Landmark Case**: **Woolmington v. DPP (1935)** (English case adopted in India)
- **Golden thread of criminal jurisprudence**: Burden on prosecution to prove guilt beyond reasonable doubt
- Duty never shifts to accused to prove innocence

**Reasonable Doubt**: Doubt for which reason can be assigned; not fanciful or imaginary

**If Reasonable Doubt Exists**: Acquittal (benefit of doubt to accused)

### 6. Sentencing (Section 235(2) CrPC / 259(2) BNSS)

**If Conviction**:
- **Separate hearing** on sentence (bifurcated trial)
- **Accused heard on sentence**: Mitigating factors
  - First-time offender
  - Young age
  - Family circumstances
  - Socio-economic background
  - Provocation
  - No premeditation
  - Remorse
- **Prosecution heard**: Aggravating factors
  - Gravity of offence
  - Manner of commission (brutal, heinous)
  - Impact on victim/society
  - No remorse
  - Danger to society
- **Victim Impact Statement**: Can be considered (BNSS enhancement)

**Sentencing Principles**:
- **Proportionality**: Punishment should fit crime
- **Reformative**: Focus on rehabilitation (especially for young offenders)
- **Deterrence**: Deter accused and others from committing crimes
- **Retributive**: Society's retribution for crime

**Death Penalty Cases**: **"Rarest of Rare"** doctrine

**Landmark Case**: **Bachan Singh v. State of Punjab (1980)**
- Death penalty only in **rarest of rare cases**
- When alternative sentence is foreclosed
- Must consider mitigating and aggravating circumstances

---

## Rights of Accused During Trial

### Constitutional Rights

#### 1. Presumption of Innocence (Article 21 + Section 101 Evidence Act)
- Accused presumed innocent until proven guilty
- Burden on prosecution to prove guilt

#### 2. Right to Fair Trial (Article 21)
- Procedure must be fair, just, reasonable
- No bias, prejudice
- Adequate opportunity to defend

#### 3. Right to Speedy Trial (Article 21)
- Undue delay violates fundamental rights
- Remedy: Quashing of proceedings OR compensation

**Landmark Case**: **Hussainara Khatoon v. State of Bihar (1979)**
- Right to speedy trial is fundamental right
- Prolonged pre-trial detention violates Article 21

#### 4. Right Against Self-Incrimination (Article 20(3))
- Cannot be compelled to be witness against self
- Right to remain silent

#### 5. Right to Legal Representation (Article 22(1))
- Right to be defended by lawyer of choice
- Right to legal aid if indigent (Article 39A + Section 304 CrPC / 358 BNSS)

**Landmark Case**: **Hussainara Khatoon (1979)** + **Khatri v. State of Bihar (1981)**
- Free legal aid is fundamental right of poor, indigent accused
- Denial of legal aid may vitiate trial

#### 6. Right Against Double Jeopardy (Article 20(2))
- Person cannot be prosecuted and punished **twice for same offence**
- After acquittal or conviction, same person cannot be tried again for same offence

**Exceptions**:
- If first trial was invalid (lack of jurisdiction, etc.)
- If error apparent on face of record

**Section 300 CrPC / 354 BNSS**: Codifies double jeopardy protection

### Statutory Rights

#### 1. Right to Be Present During Trial (Section 273 CrPC / 339 BNSS)
- Accused has right to be present during trial (except in certain circumstances)
- Evidence must be taken in presence of accused

**Exceptions**:
- Accused absconding: Trial can proceed ex parte (Section 299 CrPC / 353 BNSS)
- Accused misbehaving: Can be removed temporarily

#### 2. Right to Cross-Examine Prosecution Witnesses
- Fundamental right
- Essential for fair trial

#### 3. Right to Call Defense Witnesses (Section 233 CrPC / 257 BNSS)
- Accused can summon witnesses
- Court to issue summons/warrants

#### 4. Right to Free Copy of Judgment (Section 363 CrPC / 417 BNSS)
- Convicted accused entitled to free copy of judgment (for appeal)

#### 5. Right to Interpreter (Section 281 CrPC / 350 BNSS)
- If accused doesn't understand language of court
- Evidence to be interpreted

#### 6. Right to Medical Examination
- If accused alleges torture or ill-treatment

---

## Victim's Rights in Trial

### Statutory Rights

#### 1. Right to Compensation (Section 357 CrPC / 411 BNSS)
- Court can order compensation to victim from fine imposed on accused
- State can also provide compensation (Section 357A)

#### 2. Right to Be Heard (Section 301 CrPC / 355 BNSS)
- Victim can oppose acquittal/discharge
- Can file appeal against acquittal (with leave)

#### 3. Right to Victim Impact Statement
- **BNSS Enhancement**: Victim can give statement on impact of crime
- Considered during sentencing

#### 4. Right to Free Legal Aid (Section 304 CrPC / 358 BNSS)
- Victim can get free legal aid
- Especially in sexual offence cases

#### 5. Special Protection in Sexual Offence Cases

**Section 327 CrPC / 391 BNSS**: **In-camera trial**
- Trial of rape and certain sexual offences held **in camera** (closed to public)
- Protect victim's privacy and dignity

**Section 53A CrPC / 52A BNSS**: Medical examination of rape victim
- Within 24 hours of incident
- By female doctor or in presence of female

**Section 164A CrPC / 183A BNSS**: Recording of statement of victim
- By Magistrate
- Under Section 164 (as soon as possible)

**Landmark Case**: **State of Punjab v. Gurmit Singh (1996)**
- Rape victim's testimony must be given utmost consideration
- Conviction can be based on sole testimony of victim if found credible

---

## Special Categories of Trials

### 1. Juvenile Trials

**Juvenile Justice (Care and Protection of Children) Act 2015**:
- **Child in Conflict with Law** (below 18 years): Not tried in regular criminal court
- **Juvenile Justice Board** (JJB) conducts inquiry
- **Not "trial"** - termed "inquiry"
- **Observation Home**, not jail
- **Rehabilitation focus**, not punishment

**Exception**:
- **Heinous offences** by juveniles 16-18 years: JJB can transfer to Children's Court (regular trial)

### 2. POCSO Trials

**Protection of Children from Sexual Offences (POCSO) Act 2012**:
- Special Courts designated
- **In-camera trial** mandatory (Section 327 CrPC)
- **Speedy trial**: Within **1 year** (as far as possible)
- **Child-friendly** procedures:
  - Support person for child
  - No direct confrontation with accused
  - Frequent breaks
  - Screen/one-way mirror
  - Video recording of testimony

### 3. Rape and Sexual Offence Trials

**In-Camera Trial** (Section 327 CrPC / 391 BNSS):
- Closed to public
- Only parties and lawyers present

**Victim's Identity Protection**:
- Name not disclosed in judgment
- Media cannot publish victim's identity

**Cross-Examination**: Defense can cross-examine, but court ensures no harassment

### 4. Terrorism Trials (NIA Act)

**National Investigation Agency (NIA) Act 2008**:
- Special NIA Courts
- Strict timelines
- Enhanced security
- Witness protection

---

## Evidence and Proof

### Burden of Proof

**General Rule** (Section 101 Evidence Act / BSA):
- **Burden on prosecution** to prove guilt
- **Never shifts to accused** (except specific statutory exceptions)

**Standard**: **Beyond Reasonable Doubt**

**Exceptions** (Burden shifts to accused):
- **Section 304B IPC / BNS** (Dowry death): Presumption of guilt if soon after marriage and cruelty proved
- **Section 113A Evidence Act / BSA** (Abetment to suicide): Presumption if soon before suicide and cruelty
- **Section 79 IT Act 2000**: Digital signature presumption
- **Section 4 PMLA 2002**: Burden to prove property not proceeds of crime (recently held unconstitutional in part)

### Types of Evidence

**See Protocol**: IL-CRIM-EVIDENCE.md

**Key Types**:
1. **Direct Evidence**: Eyewitness testimony
2. **Circumstantial Evidence**: Indirect evidence (circumstances forming chain)
3. **Documentary Evidence**: Documents, records
4. **Expert Evidence**: Forensic reports, medical opinions
5. **Dying Declaration**: Statement of person about cause of death (highly reliable)

**Circumstantial Evidence**: Conviction possible on circumstantial evidence alone if **chain is complete** and points **only to guilt** (no other hypothesis)

**Landmark Case**: **Sharad Birdhichand Sarda v. State of Maharashtra (1984)** (Panchsheel Principles):
1. Circumstances must be **fully established**
2. Circumstances must be **consistent only with guilt**
3. Circumstances must be **conclusive**
4. Must **exclude every hypothesis except guilt**
5. Chain of circumstances must be **complete** (no missing links)

---

## Trial Defects and Irregularities

### Curable Irregularities (Section 465 CrPC / 517 BNSS)

**General Rule**: Finding, sentence, or order not reversed **merely** because of error/irregularity in:
- Complaint
- Summons/warrant
- Charge
- Proclamation
- Order
- Proceedings

**Condition**: If **no failure of justice** resulted

**Principle**: **Substantial justice** over **technical niceties**

**Landmark Case**: **State of West Bengal v. Laisal Haque (1989)**
- Minor procedural irregularities do not vitiate trial
- Court looks at whether accused was prejudiced
- If substantial justice done, conviction upheld

### Fatal Defects (Not Curable)

**Examples**:
1. **Lack of jurisdiction**: Court had no authority to try case
2. **Violation of Section 313**: Failure to examine accused (Rahim Beg case)
3. **Violation of right to legal aid**: Indigent accused denied lawyer
4. **Violation of in-camera trial**: Rape trial held in open court
5. **Denial of cross-examination**: Prosecution witnesses not cross-examined (if defense wanted)

**Effect**: Trial vitiated; conviction set aside

---

## Judgment and Sentencing

### Judgment (Section 354 CrPC / 408 BNSS)

**Contents**:
1. **Serial number** of case
2. **Name of parties**
3. **Offence** charged
4. **Points for determination**
5. **Decision** on each point with reasons
6. **Findings** on facts and law
7. **Conviction or acquittal**
8. **If conviction**: Offence and section
9. **Sentence** (if convicted)
10. **Language** understood by accused

**Signature**: Judge signs and dates

**Pronouncement**: In open court

**Delivery**: Copy to accused (free if convicted)

### Sentencing Principles

**Competing Theories**:
1. **Deterrence**: Prevent crime by fear of punishment
2. **Retribution**: Society's vengeance/punishment for wrong
3. **Reformation**: Rehabilitate offender
4. **Incapacitation**: Remove dangerous offenders from society

**Indian Approach**: **Balancing** all theories

**Landmark Cases**:

#### Bachan Singh v. State of Punjab (1980)
- Death penalty only in **rarest of rare cases**
- Mitigating and aggravating circumstances to be balanced
- Life imprisonment is rule, death is exception

#### Machhi Singh v. State of Punjab (1983)
- Rarest of rare: Manner of commission, motive, anti-social character, magnitude
- When crime shocks collective conscience

#### Mithu v. State of Punjab (1983)
- Mandatory death sentence unconstitutional (Section 303 IPC struck down)
- Judicial discretion necessary

#### Dhananjoy Chatterjee v. State of West Bengal (1994)
- Rape and murder of minor girl
- Held: Rarest of rare case; death penalty confirmed
- Actually executed (2004) - rare instance

---

## Speedy Trial

### Constitutional Right (Article 21)

**Hussainara Khatoon v. State of Bihar (1979)**:
- Speedy trial is fundamental right
- Prolonged detention without trial violates Article 21
- Especially if accused already served period exceeding possible sentence

### Statutory Provisions

**Section 309 CrPC / 363 BNSS**: Trial to be held day-to-day
- Court to hear case **day-to-day** until witnesses examined
- Adjournments only for **special reasons** (to be recorded)
- **Maximum adjournment**: Not exceeding 15 days

### Remedies for Delay

1. **Writ Petition**: For quashing proceedings due to inordinate delay
2. **Compensation**: Article 32/226 - compensation for violation of speedy trial right
3. **Discharge/Acquittal**: If delay prejudices accused

**Landmark Case**: **Abdul Rehman Antulay v. R.S. Nayak (1992)**
- Right to speedy trial is fundamental
- Inordinate delay can be ground for quashing
- Balancing test: Prejudice to accused vs societal interest

---

## CrPC to BNSS Transition (Trial Provisions)

### Trial Procedure: CrPC 1973 vs BNSS 2023

| Aspect | CrPC 1973 | BNSS 2023 | Change |
|--------|-----------|-----------|---------|
| **Cognizance** | Section 190 | Section 210 | Section number changed; **procedure same** |
| **Framing of charges** | Sections 211-224, 228, 240, 246 | Sections 233-247, 251, 263, 271 | Section numbers changed; **procedure same** |
| **Sessions trial** | Sections 225-237 | Sections 248-260 | Section numbers changed; **procedure same** |
| **Warrant cases** | Sections 238-250 | Sections 261-274 | Section numbers changed |
| **Summons cases** | Sections 251-259 | Sections 275-285 | Section numbers changed |
| **Summary trial** | Sections 260-265 | Sections 321-326 | Section numbers changed |
| **Section 313 statement** | Section 313 | Section 367 | **Section number changed; procedure same** |
| **Evidence recording** | Sections 272-283 | Sections 338-352 | Electronic recording **enhanced** |
| **In-camera trial** | Section 327 | Section 391 | Same |
| **Judgment** | Section 354 | Section 408 | **Victim impact statement added** |
| **Sentencing** | Section 235(2) | Section 259(2) | **Bifurcated hearing continues** |
| **Speedy trial** | Section 309 | Section 363 | **Day-to-day trial continues** |

### Key BNSS Enhancements

#### 1. Electronic Evidence Recording
- Audio-video recording of proceedings permitted
- Electronic signatures
- Digital case files

#### 2. Victim Impact Statement
- Victim can give statement on impact of crime
- Considered during sentencing

#### 3. Enhanced Witness Protection
- Provisions for witness protection schemes

#### 4. Use of Technology
- Video conferencing for witnesses
- Electronic summons
- Digital case management

---

## Practical Compliance Checklist

### For Prosecution

#### Pre-Trial
- [ ] File charge sheet within **60/90 days** (if accused in custody)
- [ ] Ensure evidence properly collected and preserved
- [ ] List all witnesses (material and formal)
- [ ] Prepare case diary and investigation papers

#### At Cognizance/Framing of Charges
- [ ] Assist court in taking cognizance
- [ ] Oppose discharge application with evidence
- [ ] Ensure charges properly framed (complete, specific)

#### During Trial
- [ ] Present prosecution witnesses systematically
- [ ] Examine witnesses **in chief** effectively
- [ ] **Re-examine** to clarify matters from cross-examination
- [ ] Produce all **documentary evidence** with proper proof
- [ ] Object to inadmissible defense evidence
- [ ] Present **final arguments** comprehensively:
  - Summarize evidence
  - Connect evidence to charges
  - Establish guilt beyond reasonable doubt
- [ ] If conviction, argue for appropriate sentence (aggravating factors)

#### Post-Trial
- [ ] If acquittal, evaluate grounds for appeal
- [ ] If conviction, file appeal if sentence inadequate

### For Defense

#### Pre-Trial
- [ ] Apply for **bail** (anticipatory or regular)
- [ ] Collect **exculpatory evidence**
- [ ] Identify defense witnesses
- [ ] File for **discharge** if no prima facie case

#### At Framing of Charges
- [ ] Challenge **defective charges**
- [ ] Apply for discharge (if no sufficient grounds)
- [ ] Ensure charges specific and not vague

#### During Trial
- [ ] **Cross-examine** prosecution witnesses thoroughly:
  - Challenge credibility
  - Highlight contradictions
  - Elicit favorable facts
- [ ] Ensure accused's **Section 313 statement** properly recorded
  - Explain all incriminating circumstances
- [ ] Present **defense evidence**:
  - Defense witnesses
  - Alibi evidence
  - Documentary evidence
- [ ] Decide whether accused should testify (pros and cons)
- [ ] Present **final arguments** effectively:
  - Challenge prosecution evidence
  - Highlight reasonable doubt
  - Argue innocence or lesser offence

#### Sentencing (If Convicted)
- [ ] Present **mitigating circumstances**:
  - First-time offender
  - Young age
  - Provocation
  - Socio-economic background
  - Family responsibilities
  - Remorse
- [ ] Request lenient sentence

#### Post-Trial
- [ ] File **appeal** if convicted (within limitation period)
- [ ] Apply for **suspension of sentence** pending appeal

### For Courts

#### Cognizance and Framing of Charges
- [ ] Apply judicial mind to material (R.R. Chari principle)
- [ ] Issue process only if **prima facie case** exists
- [ ] Frame charges **precisely** (Section 211 / 233 BNSS)
- [ ] Discharge if no sufficient grounds (State of Bihar v. Ramesh Singh test)

#### During Trial
- [ ] Ensure **fair trial**:
  - Unbiased, impartial
  - Equal opportunity to both sides
- [ ] Record evidence **in open court** (except in-camera cases)
- [ ] Ensure **day-to-day trial** (Section 309 / 363 BNSS)
- [ ] Avoid unnecessary adjournments
- [ ] Conduct **Section 313 examination** properly (Rahim Beg mandate)
- [ ] Give accused opportunity to present defense
- [ ] If indigent accused, provide **free legal aid** (Hussainara Khatoon)

#### Judgment
- [ ] Apply **beyond reasonable doubt** standard (Woolmington principle)
- [ ] Write **reasoned judgment** (Section 354 / 408 BNSS):
  - Points for determination
  - Decision on each point with reasons
  - Findings on facts and law
- [ ] If **reasonable doubt**, acquit (benefit of doubt to accused)
- [ ] If **conviction**, conduct separate **sentencing hearing** (Section 235(2) / 259(2))
- [ ] Consider **mitigating and aggravating** circumstances
- [ ] Impose **proportionate sentence**
- [ ] Provide **free copy of judgment** to convicted accused

---

## Related Protocols and Cross-References

### Directly Related
- **IL-CRIM-INVESTIGATION**: Investigation leading to trial
- **IL-CRIM-ARREST-CUSTODY**: Arrest and custody during trial period
- **IL-CRIM-BAIL**: Bail during trial
- **IL-CRIM-APPEALS**: Appellate remedies post-trial
- **IL-CRIM-EVIDENCE**: Evidence law applicable in trial

### Offence-Specific
- **IL-CRIM-[SPECIFIC-OFFENCES]**: Trial procedures vary by offence
- **IL-CONSUMER-CYBER-CRIME**: IT Act trials
- **IL-CORP-FRAUD**: Corporate crime trials

### Rights Related
- **IL-CONSTITUTIONAL-RIGHTS**: Article 20, 21, 22 (trial rights)

---

## Conclusion

Criminal trial is the **crucible of justice** where:
- **Prosecution must prove guilt** beyond reasonable doubt
- **Accused has right to fair trial** under Article 21
- **Presumption of innocence** is sacrosanct
- **Speedy trial** is fundamental right
- **Burden never shifts** to accused (except statutory exceptions)

**Key Principles**:
1. **Fair trial is fundamental right** (Article 21)
2. **Presumption of innocence** (Section 101 Evidence Act)
3. **Burden on prosecution** (Woolmington principle)
4. **Standard: Beyond reasonable doubt**
5. **Benefit of doubt to accused**
6. **No double jeopardy** (Article 20(2))
7. **Right to legal aid** (Hussainara Khatoon)
8. **Speedy trial** (Article 21)
9. **Section 313 examination mandatory** (Rahim Beg)
10. **Victim's rights** enhanced (BNSS)

**Sentencing**:
- **Death penalty**: Rarest of rare (Bachan Singh)
- **Balance**: Deterrence, retribution, reformation
- **Victim impact statement**: Now considered (BNSS)

The transition from CrPC to BNSS **retains trial procedures** with **enhancements**:
- Electronic evidence recording
- Victim impact statements
- Enhanced witness protection
- Use of technology (video conferencing, digital case management)

**Ultimate Goal**: **Justice** - conviction of guilty, acquittal of innocent, fair procedure for all.

---

## Document Control

**Version History**:
- v1.0.0 (January 2025): Initial protocol creation

**Review Schedule**: Annual review; immediate update for landmark judgments or statutory amendments

**Feedback**: Submit to protocol administrator

**Disclaimer**: Informational purposes only. Consult qualified legal professionals for specific cases.

---

**End of Protocol: IL-CRIM-TRIAL**
