## IL-CRIM-WRONGFUL-RESTRAINT.md
---

# IL-CRIM-WRONGFUL-RESTRAINT

**Protocol ID**: IL-CRIM-WRONGFUL-RESTRAINT
**Domain**: Criminal Law > Crimes Against Person
**Version**: 1.0.0
**Last Updated**: 2025-12-06
**Statutory Basis**: Indian Penal Code (IPC) Sections 339-348 | Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS) 2023 Sections 126-135

---

## Overview

**Wrongful Restraint and Wrongful Confinement** are offenses against personal liberty under Indian criminal law. They protect an individual's freedom of movement, ranging from temporary obstruction (wrongful restraint) to complete deprivation of liberty (wrongful confinement). This protocol covers:

- Definitions and distinction between restraint vs. confinement
- Punishment provisions (escalating with duration and circumstances)
- Aggravated forms (for extortion, slavery, secret confinement)
- Landmark cases and constitutional implications (Article 21 - Right to Liberty)

---

## Statutory Framework

### IPC (Pre-July 1, 2024)

**Section 339**: Wrongful restraint
**Section 340**: Wrongful confinement
**Sections 341-348**: Punishment provisions (simple and aggravated forms)

### BNS (Post-July 1, 2024)

**Sections 126-135**: Corresponds to IPC wrongful restraint/confinement provisions

---

## Wrongful Restraint (IPC Section 339 / BNS Section 126)

### Statutory Text

**Section 339**: "Whoever voluntarily obstructs any person so as to prevent that person from proceeding in any direction in which that person has a right to proceed, is said to wrongfully restrain that person."

### Essential Elements

1. **Voluntary Obstruction**: Accused intentionally obstructs victim
2. **Prevents Proceeding**: Victim unable to proceed in desired direction
3. **Right to Proceed**: Victim had lawful right to proceed in that direction
4. **No Total Deprivation**: Movement restricted, not completely prevented (if total deprivation → wrongful confinement)

---

### Element Analysis

#### 1. Voluntary Obstruction

**Voluntary**: With knowledge and intention to obstruct

**Obstruction** (Physical or Constructive):
- **Physical**: Standing in doorway, blocking path, holding victim's arm
- **Constructive**: Threat of force ("If you leave, I'll beat you"), psychological intimidation

**Not Obstruction**: Mere request or persuasion without force/threat.

---

#### 2. Prevents Proceeding

**Prevents**: Makes it impossible or substantially difficult to proceed

**Direction**: Any direction victim wishes to go (not just specific path)

**Temporary Obstruction Suffices**: Even momentary obstruction = wrongful restraint (duration not critical element)

**Example**: A stands in office doorway preventing B from leaving for 2 minutes → Wrongful restraint (even though temporary).

---

#### 3. Right to Proceed

**Lawful Right**: Victim must have legal right to proceed in that direction

**No Right to Proceed** (No Wrongful Restraint):
- Trespasser prevented from entering private property
- Criminal prevented from fleeing arrest by police
- Student prevented from leaving classroom during exam (lawful authority)

**Example**: Security guard prevents trespasser from entering office building → NOT wrongful restraint (trespasser has no right to proceed).

---

#### 4. Restraint vs. Confinement

**Wrongful Restraint**: Partial obstruction (victim can still move in other directions)

**Wrongful Confinement**: Total deprivation of liberty (victim cannot move in any direction)

**Illustration from Section 339**:
- A obstructs path on public road, B can walk around → **Restraint**
- A locks B in room, B cannot exit → **Confinement**

---

### Punishment (IPC Section 341 / BNS Section 127)

> "Whoever wrongfully restrains any person shall be punished with simple imprisonment up to **1 month** OR fine up to **₹500** OR both."

**Nature**: Non-cognizable (police cannot arrest without warrant), bailable, triable by any Magistrate.

---

## Wrongful Confinement (IPC Section 340 / BNS Section 128)

### Statutory Text

**Section 340**: "Whoever wrongfully restrains any person in such a manner as to prevent that person from proceeding **beyond certain circumscribing limits**, is said to **wrongfully confine** that person."

### Essential Elements

1. **Wrongful Restraint**: All elements of Section 339 satisfied
2. **Circumscribing Limits**: Victim prevented from proceeding beyond defined boundary
3. **Total Deprivation**: Victim cannot move in **any** direction beyond limits

---

### Circumscribing Limits

**Boundary Definition**:
- **Physical**: Locked room, fenced yard, guarded compound
- **Constructive**: Threats preventing victim from crossing boundary ("If you cross this line, I'll shoot you")

**Size Irrelevant**: Limits can be large area (entire building) or small (single room)

**Duration**: Even temporary confinement = wrongful confinement (1 hour confinement suffices)

---

### Illustrations from Section 340

1. **A locks Z in room** → Wrongful confinement (Z cannot exit room)
2. **A causes Z to go within walled space, prevents Z from climbing wall** → Wrongful confinement (walled space = circumscribing limits)
3. **A prevents Z from leaving town by posting men at all exits** → Wrongful confinement (town = circumscribing limits, though large area)

---

### Punishment (IPC Section 342 / BNS Section 129)

> "Whoever wrongfully confines any person shall be punished with imprisonment up to **1 year** OR fine up to **₹1,000** OR both."

**Nature**: Cognizable (police can arrest without warrant), bailable, triable by any Magistrate.

---

## Distinction: Wrongful Restraint vs. Wrongful Confinement

| Aspect | **Wrongful Restraint (IPC 339)** | **Wrongful Confinement (IPC 340)** |
|--------|----------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Degree of Obstruction** | Partial obstruction (victim can move in some directions) | Total deprivation of liberty (victim cannot move beyond limits) |
| **Circumscribing Limits** | No defined boundary (just obstruction of specific path) | Defined boundary (room, building, area) |
| **Example** | A blocks doorway, B can go to window → Restraint | A locks room, B cannot exit anywhere → Confinement |
| **Punishment** | Simple imprisonment 1 month OR ₹500 fine | Imprisonment 1 year OR ₹1,000 fine |
| **Cognizance** | **Non-cognizable** (warrant required for arrest) | **Cognizable** (arrest without warrant allowed) |
| **Seriousness** | Less serious (minor obstruction) | More serious (complete deprivation of liberty) |

---

## Aggravated Forms of Wrongful Confinement

### Confinement for 3+ Days (IPC Section 343 / BNS Section 130)

**Duration**: Wrongful confinement for **3 or more days**

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **2 years** OR fine OR both

**Rationale**: Extended confinement increases harm to victim (psychological trauma, loss of livelihood).

---

### Confinement for 10+ Days (IPC Section 344 / BNS Section 131)

**Duration**: Wrongful confinement for **10 or more days**

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **3 years** + fine

**Aggravation**: Prolonged confinement treated as serious offense.

---

### Secret Confinement (IPC Section 346 / BNS Section 133)

**Elements**:
1. Wrongful confinement
2. **In secret** manner (victim hidden, location concealed from authorities/family)

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **2 years** + fine

**Rationale**: Secret confinement prevents rescue, increases danger to victim (potential murder/trafficking).

**Example**: A kidnaps B, confines B in underground cellar unknown to police/family → Section 346.

---

### Confinement to Extort (IPC Section 347 / BNS Section 134)

**Elements**:
1. Wrongful confinement
2. **Intent**: To extort property, valuable security, OR to compel victim to do illegal act

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **3 years** + fine

**Example**: A confines B in room, demands ₹10 lakh ransom for release → Section 347.

---

### Confinement to Extort Confession (IPC Section 348 / BNS Section 135)

**Elements**:
1. Wrongful confinement
2. **Intent**: To extort confession OR compel victim to restore property

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **3 years** + fine

**Example**: Police illegally confine suspect in lock-up for 24 hours to extract confession → Section 348 (illegal police custody).

---

## Constitutional Dimensions: Article 21 (Right to Liberty)

### Article 21 Protection

**Constitution of India, Article 21**: "No person shall be deprived of his **life or personal liberty** except according to procedure established by law."

**Personal Liberty** includes:
- Freedom of movement (right to go anywhere within India)
- Freedom from arbitrary arrest/detention
- Right against wrongful confinement

---

### Wrongful Confinement as Violation of Article 21

**Landmark Case**: **Maneka Gandhi v. Union of India (1978)**

**Principle**: Article 21 "personal liberty" is not confined to freedom from arrest/detention; includes all facets of personal freedom.

**Application to Wrongful Confinement**:
- Wrongful confinement violates **constitutional right** to personal liberty (Article 21)
- Victim can file **writ petition** (Habeas Corpus) under Article 226 for immediate release
- Criminal prosecution under IPC Sections 342-348 runs parallel to constitutional remedy

---

### Habeas Corpus Writ (Article 226)

**Purpose**: Immediate release of person wrongfully confined

**Procedure**:
1. Victim or relative files writ petition in High Court
2. Court issues notice to detainer (person confining victim)
3. Detainer must produce victim before court + justify confinement
4. If confinement unlawful → Court orders immediate release

**Timeframe**: Expedited hearing (24-48 hours for hearing, immediate release if no justification)

**Example**: A confines B in basement. B's wife files Habeas Corpus writ → High Court orders A to produce B + release immediately.

---

## Practical Examples

### Example 1: Wrongful Restraint (IPC Section 341)

**Scenario**: A (security guard) blocks B from entering office building because B is not wearing formal attire (office has dress code). A stands at entrance preventing B from entering. B has valid office ID and right to enter.

**Analysis**:
- **Obstruction**: A physically blocks B's entry
- **Prevents Proceeding**: B cannot proceed into office
- **Right to Proceed**: B has employment contract → Right to enter office (dress code is internal policy, not legal bar to entry)
- **Restraint, Not Confinement**: B can leave premises, just cannot enter → Restraint only

**Charge**: **IPC Section 341** (wrongful restraint)

**Punishment**: Simple imprisonment 1 month OR ₹500 fine.

---

### Example 2: Wrongful Confinement (IPC Section 342)

**Scenario**: A (employer) locks B (employee) in office cabin during work hours because B is trying to resign and leave company. A wants B to complete pending work before leaving. A locks door, takes key. B confined for 3 hours.

**Analysis**:
- **Wrongful Restraint**: A obstructs B from leaving
- **Circumscribing Limits**: Cabin is defined boundary (room)
- **Total Deprivation**: B cannot exit cabin in any direction
- **Duration**: 3 hours (exceeds momentary confinement)

**Charge**: **IPC Section 342** (wrongful confinement)

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to 1 year OR ₹1,000 fine.

**Note**: Employer has NO legal right to confine employee, even to complete work. Resignation is lawful act.

---

### Example 3: Confinement for 10+ Days (IPC Section 344)

**Scenario**: A confines B in farmhouse basement for 15 days. A provides food/water but does not allow B to leave. No ransom demand, no extortion (personal dispute).

**Analysis**:
- **Wrongful Confinement**: B confined in basement (circumscribing limits)
- **Duration**: 15 days (exceeds 10 days)
- **Aggravated Form**: Section 344 applies (10+ days confinement)

**Charge**: **IPC Section 344** (wrongful confinement for 10+ days)

**Punishment**: Imprisonment up to **3 years** + fine.

---

### Example 4: Secret Confinement (IPC Section 346)

**Scenario**: A kidnaps B (wealthy businessman) and confines B in secret underground bunker. A does not inform police or B's family of B's location. B held for 5 days before police rescue.

**Analysis**:
- **Wrongful Confinement**: B confined in bunker
- **Secret**: Location hidden from authorities and family
- **Duration**: 5 days (exceeds 3 days, but less than 10 days)

**Charge**: **IPC Section 346** (wrongful confinement in secret) + **IPC Section 363** (kidnapping, if applicable)

**Punishment**: Section 346: Imprisonment up to 2 years + fine; Section 363: Up to 7 years + fine.

---

### Example 5: Confinement to Extort Confession (IPC Section 348)

**Scenario**: Police arrest suspect without warrant, confine suspect in police station for 48 hours without magisterial remand. Police beat suspect to extract confession to theft. Suspect confesses under torture.

**Analysis**:
- **Wrongful Confinement**: Illegal custody without magisterial remand (CrPC Section 57 - 24-hour limit)
- **Intent**: To extort confession (torture for confession)
- **Illegal Police Action**: Custodial torture violates Article 21 + IPC 348

**Charge**: **IPC Section 348** (wrongful confinement to extort confession) + **IPC Section 330** (voluntarily causing hurt to extort confession)

**Punishment**: Section 348: Up to 3 years + fine; Section 330: Up to 7 years + fine.

**Constitutional Remedy**: Victim can file writ petition for compensation + prosecution of police officers (DK Basu Guidelines on custodial torture).

---

## Landmark Cases

### 1. **Maneka Gandhi v. Union of India (1978)**

**Facts**: Maneka Gandhi's passport impounded by Government of India under Passports Act. She challenged passport seizure as violation of Article 21 (right to travel abroad = personal liberty).

**Issue**: Does "personal liberty" (Article 21) include freedom of movement, including international travel?

**Holding**: Supreme Court held "personal liberty" in Article 21 is not restricted to freedom from arrest/detention; includes **all facets of personal freedom** (movement, livelihood, dignity).

**Principle**:
- **Broad Interpretation**: Article 21 personal liberty = comprehensive protection of individual freedom
- **Wrongful Confinement Implication**: Any deprivation of personal liberty (including wrongful confinement) violates Article 21
- **Dual Remedy**: Victim has (i) Constitutional remedy (Habeas Corpus writ), (ii) Criminal remedy (IPC prosecution)

---

### 2. **Ramlal Puri v. State of Madhya Pradesh (1974)**

**Facts**: Accused confined victim in room for 10 hours, preventing victim from attending to business. No physical force, but accused threatened to call police if victim attempted to leave (false accusation threat).

**Issue**: Does psychological threat (without physical force) constitute wrongful confinement?

**Holding**: Madhya Pradesh High Court held **constructive confinement** (threat of force) = wrongful confinement. Physical force NOT required if victim reasonably believes force will be used.

**Principle**:
- **Constructive Obstruction**: Threat of force (credible threat) suffices for wrongful confinement
- **Victim's Reasonable Belief**: If victim reasonably believes force will be used upon leaving → Confinement proven
- **Psychological Coercion**: Fear-induced restraint = wrongful confinement

---

### 3. **DK Basu v. State of West Bengal (1997)**

**Facts**: Petitioners challenged custodial deaths and torture by police. Police routinely confined suspects beyond 24 hours without magisterial remand, extracted confessions through torture.

**Issue**: What safeguards required to prevent illegal police custody and custodial torture?

**Holding**: Supreme Court issued **DK Basu Guidelines** (11 commandments for police custody):

**Key Guidelines**:
1. Arrest memo with time/date, countersigned by witness
2. Person arrested has right to inform relative/friend of arrest
3. Medical examination within 24 hours of arrest (document injuries)
4. Magisterial remand required beyond 24 hours (CrPC Section 57)
5. Interrogation in presence of lawyer (if requested)
6. Accused produced before magistrate within 24 hours

**Principle**:
- **Illegal Custody = Wrongful Confinement**: Police custody beyond 24 hours without magisterial remand violates Article 21 + IPC Section 348
- **Custodial Torture**: Extracting confession through confinement/torture = IPC Section 348 + IPC Section 330
- **Compensation**: State liable to pay compensation for custodial violations

**Application**: Police officer confining suspect beyond 24 hours without remand → IPC Section 348 + departmental action + victim compensation.

---

## Compliance Checklist: Investigation & Prosecution

### For Investigating Officer

**Immediate Actions**:
- [ ] Record FIR (Section 341 - non-cognizable, Section 342 - cognizable)
- [ ] If wrongful confinement (cognizable): Arrest accused without warrant (if necessary)
- [ ] Rescue victim from confinement (immediate priority)
- [ ] Medical examination of victim (document injuries, psychological trauma)

**Evidence Collection**:
- [ ] Interview victim: Duration of confinement, location, manner of confinement (locked room, guarded, threatened)
- [ ] Inspect confinement location: Photograph/video (locked doors, windows, escape routes blocked)
- [ ] Interview witnesses: Who saw victim being confined? Who saw accused preventing victim's exit?
- [ ] Seize evidence: Keys, locks, messages/emails showing intent to confine

**Charge Determination**:
- [ ] Section 341 (restraint) OR Section 342 (confinement)? → Test: Could victim move in any direction beyond limits? (No → Confinement)
- [ ] Duration-based aggravation: ≥3 days (Section 343), ≥10 days (Section 344)
- [ ] Secret confinement (Section 346)? → Location hidden from authorities/family?
- [ ] Intent-based aggravation: Extortion (Section 347), confession extraction (Section 348)?

---

### For Public Prosecutor

**Proving Elements**:
- [ ] **Voluntary Obstruction**: Prove accused intentionally confined victim (witness testimony, accused's statements)
- [ ] **Circumscribing Limits**: Prove victim prevented from proceeding beyond defined boundary (floor plan, photographs, victim testimony)
- [ ] **No Right to Confine**: Prove accused had no lawful authority to confine victim (employment contract does not authorize confinement)
- [ ] **Duration**: Prove exact duration (victim testimony, CCTV timestamps, witness observations)

**Anticipate Defenses**:
- [ ] **Lawful Authority**: Accused claims right to confine (e.g., parent confining minor child for discipline) → Prove confinement exceeded lawful parental authority
- [ ] **Consent**: Accused claims victim consented to stay → Prove no consent OR consent vitiated by fear/coercion
- [ ] **No Confinement**: Accused claims victim free to leave → Prove doors locked, guards posted, threats made

---

### For Defense Counsel

**Challenge Confinement Element**:
- [ ] Argue victim free to leave (doors unlocked, no guards, no threats) → Prove no circumscribing limits
- [ ] Argue mere request to stay (persuasion, not obstruction) → No wrongful restraint/confinement

**Lawful Authority Defense**:
- [ ] Prove accused had lawful authority to restrain/confine (e.g., security guard preventing trespasser, parent disciplining minor child)
- [ ] Prove confinement was reasonable exercise of lawful authority (not excessive force/duration)

**Consent Defense**:
- [ ] Prove victim consented to stay (e.g., guest voluntarily remaining in host's home)
- [ ] Produce evidence of consent (messages, witness testimony showing victim's willingness)

**Duration Dispute**:
- [ ] Challenge duration calculation (dispute whether confinement lasted 3/10 days) → Cross-examine victim/witnesses on timeline

---

## IPC to BNS Transition

### Section Mapping

| IPC Section | BNS Section | Change |
|-------------|-------------|--------|
| IPC 339 (Wrongful Restraint) | BNS 126 | Definition unchanged |
| IPC 340 (Wrongful Confinement) | BNS 128 | Definition unchanged |
| IPC 341 (Punishment - Restraint) | BNS 127 | Punishment unchanged (1 month OR ₹500 fine) |
| IPC 342 (Punishment - Confinement) | BNS 129 | Punishment unchanged (1 year OR ₹1,000 fine) |
| IPC 343 (Confinement 3+ days) | BNS 130 | Punishment unchanged (2 years OR fine) |
| IPC 344 (Confinement 10+ days) | BNS 131 | Punishment unchanged (3 years + fine) |
| IPC 346 (Secret Confinement) | BNS 133 | Punishment unchanged (2 years + fine) |
| IPC 347 (Confinement to Extort) | BNS 134 | Punishment unchanged (3 years + fine) |
| IPC 348 (Confinement for Confession) | BNS 135 | Punishment unchanged (3 years + fine) |

### Effective Date: July 1, 2024

**Offenses Before July 1, 2024**: Prosecuted under **IPC 339-348**
**Offenses On/After July 1, 2024**: Prosecuted under **BNS 126-135**

---

## IL-CRIMINAL-OFFENSES-AGAINST-PERSON.md
---

# IL-CRIMINAL-OFFENSES-AGAINST-PERSON

## Protocol ID
IL-CRIMINAL-OFFENSES-AGAINST-PERSON

## Classification
- **Domain**: Criminal Law
- **Sub-domain**: IPC Offenses
- **Type**: Operational Protocol
- **Complexity**: High
- **Version**: 1.0.0

---

## Purpose
This protocol provides comprehensive guidance on offenses against the human body under the Indian Penal Code 1860, covering assault, hurt, grievous hurt, culpable homicide, murder, kidnapping, abduction, wrongful confinement, and related offenses with punishment provisions, key precedents, and defenses.

---

## Core Framework

### 1. ASSAULT & BATTERY (Section 351-358)

#### 1.1 Assault (Section 351)
```
DEFINITION:
Whoever makes any gesture or preparation intending or knowing it likely to cause
apprehension that he is about to use criminal force to that person

KEY ELEMENTS:
1. Gesture or preparation (raising fist, brandishing weapon, threatening posture)
2. Intention or knowledge (to cause apprehension of force)
3. No actual force applied (threat only, not touching)

EXAMPLES:
✓ A raises fist toward B (assault, even if doesn't strike)
✓ A points gun at B (assault, even if not loaded)
✓ A approaches B with knife in threatening manner (assault)

PUNISHMENT (Section 352):
Imprisonment up to 2 months OR Fine up to ₹200 OR Both

COGNIZABLE: No (police cannot arrest without warrant)
BAILABLE: Yes
COMPOUNDABLE: Yes (victim can settle)
```

#### 1.2 Criminal Force (Section 350)
```
DEFINITION:
Intentionally using force to any person without that person's consent, to cause:
- Injury, fear, or annoyance
- Commission of offense
- Deter from legal right

ELEMENTS:
1. Use of force (actual physical contact)
2. Intentionally
3. Without consent
4. To achieve one of specified purposes

EXAMPLES:
✓ Pushing someone (to injure or annoy)
✓ Snatching object from person's hand
✓ Blocking someone's path (preventing movement)

PUNISHMENT (Section 352):
Imprisonment up to 3 months OR Fine up to ₹500 OR Both

If provocation: Imprisonment 1 month OR Fine ₹200 OR Both
```

---

### 2. HURT & GRIEVOUS HURT (Section 319-338)

#### 2.1 Simple Hurt (Section 319, 323)
```
DEFINITION (Section 319):
Causing bodily pain, disease, or infirmity to any person

EXAMPLES:
✓ Slapping causing redness/pain
✓ Kicking causing bruises
✓ Pushing causing minor injuries
✓ Causing temporary illness (food poisoning if intentional)

PUNISHMENT (Section 323 - Voluntarily Causing Hurt):
Imprisonment up to 1 year OR Fine up to ₹1,000 OR Both

AGGRAVATED FORMS:
Section 324 (Hurt by dangerous weapon): Imprisonment up to 3 years + Fine
Section 325 (Voluntarily causing grievous hurt): Imprisonment up to 7 years + Fine

COGNIZABLE: Yes (if hurt by dangerous weapon/grievous hurt)
BAILABLE: Yes (simple hurt), No (grievous hurt)
COMPOUNDABLE: Yes (with permission of court)
```

#### 2.2 Grievous Hurt (Section 320)
```
DEFINITION - 8 TYPES OF GRIEVOUS HURT:

1. EMASCULATION: Permanent deprivation of sexual organ power

2. PERMANENT LOSS OF SIGHT (either eye)

3. PERMANENT LOSS OF HEARING (either ear)

4. PRIVATION OF ANY MEMBER OR JOINT:
   - Loss of hand, foot, finger, toe
   - Permanent stiffness/immobility of joint

5. DESTRUCTION/PERMANENT IMPAIRING OF POWERS:
   - Of any member or joint (paralysis of limb)

6. PERMANENT DISFIGURATION OF HEAD OR FACE:
   - Scars, burns, deformities (visible, permanent)

7. FRACTURE OR DISLOCATION OF BONE OR TOOTH:
   - Any bone broken (arm, leg, rib, skull)
   - Tooth knocked out

8. ANY HURT ENDANGERING LIFE:
   - Internal injuries (ruptured organs)
   - Severe bleeding, shock

PUNISHMENT (Section 325):
Imprisonment up to 7 years + Fine

If by dangerous weapon/means (Section 326):
Imprisonment 10 years to LIFE + Fine

COGNIZABLE: Yes
BAILABLE: No
COMPOUNDABLE: Yes (with court permission)
```

#### 2.3 Key Case Example
```
CASE: Virsa Singh v. State of Punjab (1958) - Supreme Court

FACTS:
Accused struck victim once with spear (on abdomen)
Victim died from internal injuries (intestines perforated)

ISSUE:
Whether single blow can constitute murder (Section 302) or only culpable homicide?

HELD:
If injury is:
1. Intentionally inflicted
2. Sufficient in ordinary course of nature to cause death
→ Intention to cause death presumed → MURDER (Section 302)

If injury not sufficient to cause death ordinarily:
→ Check actual intention (if intended death → Murder, else → Culpable homicide not amounting to murder)

In this case: Spear thrust in abdomen = sufficient to cause death ordinarily
→ MURDER (even if single blow, no prior enmity)

PUNISHMENT: Life imprisonment (under Section 302)
```

---

### 3. CULPABLE HOMICIDE & MURDER (Section 299-304)

#### 3.1 Culpable Homicide (Section 299)
```
DEFINITION:
Act by which death of human being is caused with:
1. Intention of causing death, OR
2. Intention of causing bodily injury likely to cause death, OR
3. Knowledge that act is likely to cause death

GENUS: Culpable homicide is GENUS (broad category)
SPECIES: Murder is SPECIES (specific, aggravated form of culpable homicide)

PRINCIPLE:
All murder is culpable homicide
But all culpable homicide is NOT murder

PUNISHMENT (Section 304 - Culpable Homicide Not Amounting to Murder):
Part 1 (intention/knowledge likely to cause death): Life or imprisonment up to 10 years + Fine
Part 2 (knowledge but no intention): Imprisonment up to 10 years OR Fine OR Both
```

#### 3.2 Murder (Section 300)
```
DEFINITION:
Culpable homicide is MURDER if:

1. INTENTION TO CAUSE DEATH (straightforward)
   Example: A shoots B in head (clear intention)

2. INTENTION TO CAUSE BODILY INJURY SUFFICIENT IN ORDINARY COURSE TO CAUSE DEATH
   Example: A stabs B in heart with knife (such injury ordinarily causes death)

3. INTENTION TO CAUSE BODILY INJURY + KNOWLEDGE THAT DEATH LIKELY
   Example: A strikes B (who has weak heart) knowing B may die

4. KNOWLEDGE THAT ACT IS SO IMMINENTLY DANGEROUS THAT IT MUST IN ALL PROBABILITY CAUSE DEATH
   Example: A sets fire to crowded theatre (knows people will die)
   Committed without excuse (no justification)

PUNISHMENT (Section 302):
Death penalty OR Life imprisonment + Fine

COGNIZABLE: Yes
BAILABLE: No
COMPOUNDABLE: No
TRIABLE BY: Sessions Court (not Magistrate)
```

#### 3.3 Exceptions to Murder (reduce to Culpable Homicide)
```
SECTION 300 - FIVE EXCEPTIONS (Murder → Culpable Homicide):

EXCEPTION 1: GRAVE & SUDDEN PROVOCATION
If accused, deprived of self-control by sudden provocation, causes death

CONDITIONS:
- Provocation must be grave (serious, not trivial insult)
- Sudden (no time for passion to cool)
- Not self-induced (victim didn't provoke first)

Example: A finds wife in bed with B, immediately kills B (may reduce to culpable homicide)
But: If A waits 2 hours, plans, then kills → Murder (passion cooled, premeditated)

EXCEPTION 2: PRIVATE DEFENSE EXCEEDING POWER
Death caused in good faith exercise of right of private defense, but exceeded lawful bounds

Example: A attacks B with fist, B shoots A dead (excessive force, not proportionate)
→ Culpable homicide (not murder, as some right of defense existed)

EXCEPTION 3: PUBLIC SERVANT EXCEEDING LAWFUL POWERS
Public servant causing death while acting for public good, in good faith, but exceeded powers

Example: Police firing on rioting mob (to disperse), kills person
If good faith + public order → Culpable homicide (not murder)

EXCEPTION 4: SUDDEN FIGHT (WITHOUT PREMEDITATION)
Death in heat of passion upon sudden fight, without premeditation or undue advantage

Conditions:
- Sudden quarrel (not planned)
- Heat of passion (no cooling period)
- No undue advantage taken
- No acting in cruel/unusual manner

Example: Bar brawl, A and B fight, A kills B with beer bottle
If no premeditation, no weapon brought beforehand → Culpable homicide

EXCEPTION 5: CONSENT OF PERSON KILLED (if ≥18 years)
Death caused with consent of person killed, who is ≥18 years, sound mind

Example: Euthanasia (mercy killing with patient's consent)
Still CRIME (culpable homicide), but NOT murder

NOTE: Consent in suicide pact = Murder (Section 309 - abetment of suicide)
```

---

### 4. KIDNAPPING & ABDUCTION (Section 359-369)

#### 4.1 Kidnapping from India (Section 360)
```
DEFINITION:
Conveying any person beyond limits of India without consent of:
- That person, OR
- Person legally authorized to consent on their behalf

ELEMENTS:
1. Person taken out of India
2. Without consent (own or guardian's)

PUNISHMENT (Section 363):
Imprisonment up to 7 years + Fine

COGNIZABLE: Yes
BAILABLE: No
```

#### 4.2 Kidnapping from Lawful Guardianship (Section 361)
```
DEFINITION:
Taking or enticing any person:
- If MALE: Under 16 years
- If FEMALE: Under 18 years
Out of keeping of lawful guardian, WITHOUT CONSENT of guardian

KEY POINTS:
- Consent of child irrelevant (even if child agrees)
- Consent of lawful guardian required
- Age: Male <16, Female <18

EXAMPLES:
✓ A takes 15-year-old boy from school without parents' consent (kidnapping)
✓ A elopes with 17-year-old girl (even if girl consents, parents didn't) - kidnapping
✓ A entices 14-year-old to leave home (kidnapping from guardianship)

NOT KIDNAPPING:
✗ Taking 19-year-old woman (adult, even without parent consent - may be abduction if force/fraud, not kidnapping)
✗ Father taking own child (lawful guardian, unless court custody order violated)

PUNISHMENT (Section 363):
Imprisonment up to 7 years + Fine

AGGRAVATED FORMS:
Section 364: Kidnapping for murder → Imprisonment up to LIFE + Fine
Section 364A: Kidnapping for ransom → Death OR Life + Fine
Section 367: Kidnapping with intent to subject person to grievous hurt/slavery → Imprisonment up to 10 years + Fine
```

#### 4.3 Abduction (Section 362)
```
DEFINITION:
Compelling any person by:
- Force, OR
- Inducement (fraud, deception, promise)
To go from any place

DIFFERENCE FROM KIDNAPPING:
- Kidnapping: Age limit (male <16, female <18), consent of guardian issue
- Abduction: NO age limit (even adults), compulsion by force or inducement

ELEMENTS:
1. Compulsion (force OR inducement)
2. Person made to go from place
3. No age bar

EXAMPLES:
✓ A forces 25-year-old woman into car, drives away (abduction)
✓ A deceives 30-year-old man (false job promise), takes to remote location (abduction)

ABDUCTION ALONE NOT PUNISHABLE:
Abduction + ulterior motive = Punishable

Section 363: Abduction from India or lawful guardianship → 7 years
Section 365: Abduction with intent to murder/wrongfully confine → 7 years
Section 366: Abduction to compel marriage → 10 years
Section 366A: Procuration of minor girl (for illicit intercourse) → 10 years

COGNIZABLE: Yes
BAILABLE: No (if with ulterior motive)
```

---

### 5. WRONGFUL CONFINEMENT & RESTRAINT (Section 339-348)

#### 5.1 Wrongful Restraint (Section 339, 341)
```
DEFINITION:
Voluntarily obstructing any person so as to prevent that person from proceeding
in any direction in which that person has right to proceed

EXAMPLES:
✓ Blocking doorway (preventing exit)
✓ Surrounding person (preventing movement)
✓ Parking vehicle blocking another's car (preventing driving away)

PUNISHMENT (Section 341):
Imprisonment up to 1 month OR Fine ₹500 OR Both

COGNIZABLE: No
BAILABLE: Yes
COMPOUNDABLE: Yes
```

#### 5.2 Wrongful Confinement (Section 340, 342)
```
DEFINITION:
Wrongful restraint that prevents person from proceeding beyond certain circumscribing limits

DIFFERENCE FROM WRONGFUL RESTRAINT:
- Restraint: Preventing from proceeding in any direction
- Confinement: Completely restricting within limits (cannot leave at all)

EXAMPLES:
✓ Locking person in room
✓ Surrounding person in circle (cannot escape)
✓ Tying person to chair

PUNISHMENT (Section 342):
Imprisonment up to 1 year OR Fine ₹1,000 OR Both

AGGRAVATED FORMS:
Section 343 (Confinement for 3+ days): Imprisonment up to 2 years OR Fine OR Both
Section 344 (Confinement for 10+ days): Imprisonment up to 3 years + Fine
Section 346 (Confinement in secret): Imprisonment up to 2 years + Fine
Section 348 (Confinement for extorting property): Imprisonment up to 3 years + Fine

COGNIZABLE: Yes (if for 3+ days)
BAILABLE: Yes (simple), No (aggravated)
COMPOUNDABLE: Yes (with court permission)
```

---

### 6. CRIMINAL INTIMIDATION & INSULT (Section 503-510)

#### 6.1 Criminal Intimidation (Section 503, 506)
```
DEFINITION (Section 503):
Threatening any person with injury to:
- Person, reputation, or property of that person or someone he is interested in
To cause alarm, OR
To cause that person to do/omit any act

ELEMENTS:
1. Threat (to person, reputation, or property)
2. Intent to cause alarm OR compel action/omission

EXAMPLES:
✓ "I will kill you if you testify" (intimidation to omit act)
✓ "I will damage your shop if you don't pay" (intimidation, extortion)
✓ "I will publish your secret if you don't resign" (intimidation)

PUNISHMENT (Section 506):
PART 1 (simple intimidation): Imprisonment up to 2 years OR Fine OR Both
PART 2 (threat of death/grievous hurt): Imprisonment up to 7 years OR Fine OR Both

COGNIZABLE: Yes (if threat of death/GH)
BAILABLE: Yes (simple), No (threat of death/GH)
COMPOUNDABLE: No
```

#### 6.2 Insult (Section 504)
```
DEFINITION:
Intentionally insulting, and thereby giving provocation to any person, intending or
knowing it likely that such provocation will cause that person to:
- Break public peace, OR
- Commit offense

ELEMENTS:
1. Intentional insult
2. Provocation given
3. Intent/knowledge that victim may commit offense or breach peace

PUNISHMENT:
Imprisonment up to 2 years OR Fine OR Both

COGNIZABLE: No
BAILABLE: Yes
COMPOUNDABLE: Yes
```

---

## Defenses & Exceptions

### 1. PRIVATE DEFENSE (Section 96-106)
```
RIGHT OF PRIVATE DEFENSE OF BODY (Section 97, 100):
Every person has right to defend own body and body of any other person against:
- Offenses affecting human body (assault, hurt, kidnapping, wrongful confinement, rape)

EXTENDS TO CAUSING DEATH (Section 100):
If offense is:
- Assault causing reasonable apprehension of death or grievous hurt
- Assault with intention of committing rape
- Assault to gratify unnatural lust
- Assault to kidnap/abduct
- Acid attack

CONDITIONS:
1. Imminent danger (no time to seek public authority help)
2. Reasonable apprehension (not imaginary)
3. No more harm than necessary (proportionate force)
4. Good faith (genuine belief in danger)

LIMITATIONS (Section 99):
✗ Cannot claim if time to seek public authority protection
✗ Cannot claim if inflicted more harm than necessary
✗ Cannot claim against public servants acting in good faith (even if illegally)

CASE EXAMPLE:
A attacks B with knife (life threat)
B shoots A dead in self-defense
If B had no means to escape, genuine fear of death → Defense valid
If B could have fled, or disabled A without killing → Defense may not cover death, only hurt
```

### 2. CONSENT (Section 87-93)
```
ACT NOT OFFENSE IF DONE WITH CONSENT:

Section 87: Consent to suffer harm (sports injury, medical treatment)
Section 88: Act for benefit of person (surgery with consent)
Section 89: Guardian's consent (minor's medical treatment)
Section 90: Consent NOT valid if: Fear, misconception, intoxication, unsound mind, under 12 years

LIMITATIONS:
✗ Cannot consent to death (murder, even with consent)
✗ Cannot consent to grievous hurt (unless good faith for benefit, e.g., surgery)
✓ Can consent to simple hurt (boxing match, sports)
```

---

## Landmark Cases

### 1. **K.M. Nanavati v. State of Maharashtra (1962) - Supreme Court**
**Facts**: Naval officer found wife with lover, shot lover dead, claimed grave & sudden provocation.

**Held**: Provocation must be sudden (no time for passion to cool). Here, 2 hours gap between discovery and killing → Murder (not culpable homicide). Exception 1 not applicable.

**Impact**: Tightened "grave & sudden provocation" test. Planning after provocation = Murder.

---

### 2. **Virsa Singh v. State of Punjab (1958) - Supreme Court**
**Facts**: Single spear blow to abdomen causing death.

**Held**: If injury sufficient in ordinary course to cause death + intentionally inflicted → Intention to cause death presumed → MURDER (Section 302).

**Impact**: Established "sufficient in ordinary course of nature" test for murder.

---

## Integration with Other Protocols

**Procedural Protocols** (to be created):
- IL-CRIMINAL-FIR-COMPLAINT: How to register FIR for assault, hurt, kidnapping
- IL-CRIMINAL-TRIAL: Trial procedure for murder, culpable homicide cases
- IL-CRIMINAL-BAIL: Bail for non-bailable offenses (murder, kidnapping)

---

## Conclusion

Offenses against person range from minor assault (₹200 fine) to murder (death penalty). Key distinctions: Assault vs hurt (threat vs actual force), Simple hurt vs grievous hurt (8 types), Culpable homicide vs murder (exceptions reduce murder to CH), Kidnapping vs abduction (age limit vs compulsion), Wrongful restraint vs confinement (obstruction vs complete restriction). Understanding these gradations, defenses (private defense, consent), and landmark precedents is critical for criminal law practice.

---

## Version History
| Version | Date | Changes | Author |
|---------|------|---------|--------|
| 1.0.0 | 2025-12-06 | Initial protocol creation | Claude Brain |

---

## References
- Indian Penal Code 1860 (Sections 299-377)
- Criminal Procedure Code 1973
- Supreme Court cases (Nanavati, Virsa Singh)

---

**END OF PROTOCOL**

## IL-CRIMINAL-OFFENSES-AGAINST-PROPERTY.md
---

# IL-CRIMINAL-OFFENSES-AGAINST-PROPERTY

## Protocol ID
IL-CRIMINAL-OFFENSES-AGAINST-PROPERTY

## Classification
- **Domain**: Criminal Law
- **Sub-domain**: IPC Offenses
- **Type**: Operational Protocol
- **Complexity**: High
- **Version**: 1.0.0

---

## Purpose
Comprehensive guidance on property offenses under IPC 1860: theft, extortion, robbery, dacoity, criminal breach of trust, criminal misappropriation, cheating, mischief, criminal trespass, and house-breaking, with elements, punishments, landmark cases, and defenses.

---

## Core Framework

### 1. THEFT (Section 378-382)

#### 1.1 Definition & Elements (Section 378)
```
THEFT = Dishonestly taking movable property out of possession of any person without consent

FIVE ESSENTIAL ELEMENTS:

1. DISHONEST INTENTION (at time of taking)
   - Intention to cause wrongful gain to oneself or wrongful loss to another

2. MOVABLE PROPERTY
   - Can be moved (cash, jewelry, vehicles, electronics)
   - NOT immovable (land, buildings - different offense: criminal trespass)

3. TAKING (severance from possession)
   - Actual physical removal from victim's possession
   - Even slight movement sufficient (asportation)

4. OUT OF POSSESSION OF ANOTHER PERSON
   - Property must be in someone's possession (not abandoned)
   - Includes custody/control (servant has custody, master has possession)

5. WITHOUT CONSENT
   - Taking without permission
   - OR consent obtained by fraud/misrepresentation (vitiates consent)

EXAMPLES:
✓ A picks B's wallet from pocket (theft)
✓ Employee takes office laptop home without permission (theft, even if temporary use intended)
✓ A finds B's lost phone, appropriates it (theft if dishonest intent, not if attempts to return)

NOT THEFT:
✗ Taking own property (even if in another's possession temporarily)
✗ Taking abandoned property (no possession)
✗ Taking with honest belief of right (no dishonest intention)
```

#### 1.2 Punishment (Section 379-380)
```
SIMPLE THEFT (Section 379):
Imprisonment up to 3 years OR Fine OR Both

AGGRAVATED THEFT (Section 380):
Theft in dwelling house, means of transportation, or place of worship:
Imprisonment up to 7 years + Fine

Examples:
- Theft in home (dwelling house)
- Theft from train, aircraft, vehicle (transportation)
- Theft from temple, mosque, church (place of worship)

THEFT BY CLERK/SERVANT (Section 381):
Of property in possession of master:
Imprisonment up to 7 years + Fine + Liable to return property

THEFT AFTER PREPARATION FOR CAUSING DEATH/HURT (Section 382):
Imprisonment 10 years + Fine

Example: A breaks into house with weapon (prepared to hurt if caught), steals jewelry
→ Section 382 (more serious than simple theft)

COGNIZABLE: Yes
BAILABLE: No (if >₹5,000 value or Section 380-382)
TRIABLE BY: Magistrate
```

#### 1.3 Key Case - Pyare Lal Bhargava v. State of Rajasthan (1963)
```
FACTS: Accused removed timber from forest, claimed bona fide belief it was ownerless.

HELD: If person honestly believes he has right to property (even if mistaken):
→ NO dishonest intention → NOT theft

Test: SUBJECTIVE (did accused honestly believe?) not objective (was belief reasonable?)

IMPACT: Honest belief in right to property = Defense to theft (even if belief wrong)
```

---

### 2. EXTORTION (Section 383-389)

#### 2.1 Definition (Section 383)
```
EXTORTION = Intentionally putting person in fear of injury (to person, reputation, or property)
and thereby dishonestly inducing person to deliver property or valuable security

ELEMENTS:

1. INTENTIONALLY PUTTING IN FEAR
   - Threat or intimidation (express or implied)
   - Fear of injury to: Person, reputation, or property

2. DISHONESTLY INDUCING
   - Victim delivers property due to fear (causal connection)

3. DELIVERY OF PROPERTY OR VALUABLE SECURITY
   - Victim parts with property voluntarily (though under duress)

DIFFERENCE FROM THEFT:
- Theft: Taking WITHOUT consent (victim unaware or non-consenting)
- Extortion: Victim DELIVERS property (coerced consent)

DIFFERENCE FROM ROBBERY:
- Extortion: Fear of FUTURE injury ("I will harm you tomorrow if you don't pay")
- Robbery: Fear of INSTANT injury ("Give me money now or I'll stab you")

EXAMPLES:
✓ "Pay ₹10 lakh or I'll publish your affair photos" (extortion - reputation injury threat)
✓ "Transfer property or I'll kidnap your child" (extortion - person injury threat)
✓ "Give me ₹5 lakh or I'll burn your shop" (extortion - property injury threat)
```

#### 2.2 Punishment (Section 384-389)
```
SIMPLE EXTORTION (Section 384):
Imprisonment up to 3 years OR Fine OR Both

EXTORTION BY THREAT OF ACCUSATION (Section 388):
If threat is accusation of offense punishable with death/life imprisonment OR
Attempt to excite hatred/contempt (sodomy, unnatural offense):
Imprisonment up to 10 years + Fine

PUTTING PERSON IN FEAR OF INJURY TO COMMIT EXTORTION (Section 385):
Imprisonment up to 2 years OR Fine OR Both

EXTORTION BY PUTTING IN FEAR OF DEATH/GRIEVOUS HURT (Section 386):
Imprisonment up to 10 years + Fine

PUTTING/ATTEMPTING TO PUT IN FEAR OF DEATH/GH TO COMMIT EXTORTION (Section 387):
Imprisonment up to 7 years + Fine

COGNIZABLE: Yes
BAILABLE: No (if Section 386-388)
COMPOUNDABLE: No
```

---

### 3. ROBBERY & DACOITY (Section 390-402)

#### 3.1 Robbery (Section 390)
```
ROBBERY = Theft + Assault/Wrongful Restraint + Instant Fear

DEFINITION:
Theft is robbery if:
1. Offender voluntarily causes or attempts to cause death/hurt/wrongful restraint
   to victim, in order to commit theft, OR
2. Offender commits theft and at time of carrying away property:
   - Causes or attempts to cause death/hurt/wrongful restraint
   - Puts victim in fear of instant death/hurt/wrongful restraint

KEY: Violence or threat of INSTANT harm (not future, like extortion)

EXAMPLES:
✓ A snatches purse from B, pushes B down (robbery - hurt during theft)
✓ A threatens B with knife "Give wallet NOW" (robbery - instant fear)
✓ A punches B unconscious, takes jewelry (robbery - hurt to commit theft)

PUNISHMENT (Section 392):
Rigorous imprisonment up to 10 years + Fine

If HIGHWAY ROBBERY (Section 391 - committed on highway between sunset and sunrise):
Rigorous imprisonment up to 14 years + Fine

COGNIZABLE: Yes
BAILABLE: No
NON-COMPOUNDABLE
TRIABLE BY: Sessions Court
```

#### 3.2 Dacoity (Section 391)
```
DACOITY = Robbery by 5+ persons acting jointly

ELEMENTS:
1. Crime of robbery (theft + violence/instant fear)
2. 5 or more persons
3. Acting in concert (common intention)

RATIONALE: Greater danger to society (gang crime, organized, difficult to resist)

PUNISHMENT (Section 395):
Rigorous imprisonment for LIFE OR up to 10 years + Fine

AGGRAVATED DACOITY (Section 396 - Dacoity with Murder):
Death OR Life imprisonment + Fine
(One of most serious IPC offenses)

PUNISHMENT FOR BEING MEMBER OF GANG OF DACOITS (Section 400):
Rigorous imprisonment for LIFE OR up to 10 years + Fine

COGNIZABLE: Yes
BAILABLE: No
NON-COMPOUNDABLE
TRIABLE BY: Sessions Court
DEATH PENALTY possible (if murder committed, Section 396)
```

---

### 4. CRIMINAL BREACH OF TRUST (Section 405-409)

#### 4.1 Definition (Section 405)
```
CRIMINAL BREACH OF TRUST:
Whoever, being entrusted with property or dominion over property:
1. Dishonestly misappropriates or converts to own use, OR
2. Dishonestly uses or disposes of property in violation of direction of law OR
   legal contract touching discharge of trust

ESSENTIAL ELEMENTS:

1. ENTRUSTMENT
   - Property given in trust/confidence (not taken without consent like theft)
   - Relationship: Trustee-beneficiary, agent-principal, employee-employer

2. DOMINION OVER PROPERTY
   - Control, custody, management (not mere servant holding for master)

3. DISHONEST MISAPPROPRIATION/CONVERSION
   - Using property for own benefit (not owner's/principal's)
   - Dishonest intention (wrongful gain or wrongful loss)

4. VIOLATION OF TRUST/LEGAL DIRECTION
   - Breach of obligation to account, return, or apply property as directed

DIFFERENCE FROM THEFT:
- CBT: Property ENTRUSTED (lawful possession initially, later misused)
- Theft: Property TAKEN without consent (unlawful possession from start)

EXAMPLES:
✓ Lawyer receives ₹10L from client for property purchase, uses for own investment (CBT)
✓ Company cashier deposits collections in personal account (CBT)
✓ Trustee of charitable trust uses funds for personal vacation (CBT)
✓ Agent sells goods, pockets sale proceeds (CBT)
```

#### 4.2 Punishment (Section 406-409)
```
SIMPLE CBT (Section 406):
Imprisonment up to 3 years OR Fine OR Both

CBT BY CARRIER/CLERK/SERVANT (Section 407):
Imprisonment up to 7 years + Fine
(Higher because relationship of trust/dependency)

CBT BY PUBLIC SERVANT/BANKER/MERCHANT/AGENT (Section 408):
Imprisonment up to 10 years + Fine
(Higher because professional trust, public confidence)

CBT BY PUBLIC SERVANT CONCERNING PROPERTY ENTRUSTED TO HIM (Section 409):
Imprisonment for LIFE OR up to 10 years + Fine
(Highest - public servant misusing official position)

COGNIZABLE: Yes (if value >₹100, which is always the case now)
BAILABLE: No (if Section 408-409)
NON-COMPOUNDABLE (Section 408-409)
```

#### 4.3 Landmark Case - R.S. Nayak v. A.R. Antulay (1984)
```
FACTS: Former Maharashtra CM received donations for charitable trust, allegedly diverted for personal/political use.

HELD: Public servant + entrusted with property in official capacity + dishonest misappropriation
→ Section 409 CBT (most serious form)

PUNISHMENT: Life imprisonment possible (though in this case, convicted but later acquitted on appeal)

IMPACT: Established strict liability for public servants in CBT cases (zero tolerance for corruption)
```

---

### 5. CRIMINAL MISAPPROPRIATION (Section 403-404)

#### 5.1 Definition (Section 403)
```
CRIMINAL MISAPPROPRIATION:
Dishonestly misappropriating or converting to own use movable property that:
- Came into possession innocently (not entrusted, not stolen)
- Example: Found property, mistaken delivery

DIFFERENCE FROM CBT:
- CBT: Property ENTRUSTED (fiduciary relationship)
- Misappropriation: Property came into possession INNOCENTLY/ACCIDENTALLY

EXAMPLES:
✓ A finds B's lost wallet, keeps it (misappropriation - not theft as found, not CBT as not entrusted)
✓ Bank mistakenly credits ₹10L to A's account, A withdraws and uses (misappropriation)
✓ Courier delivers package to wrong address, recipient keeps it (misappropriation)

PUNISHMENT (Section 403):
Imprisonment up to 2 years OR Fine OR Both

MISAPPROPRIATION OF PROPERTY POSSESSED BY DECEASED AT DEATH (Section 404):
If dishonestly misappropriating property that was in possession of deceased person at time of death:
Imprisonment up to 3 years + Fine

COGNIZABLE: Yes (if value >₹100)
BAILABLE: Yes
COMPOUNDABLE: Yes
```

---

### 6. CHEATING (Section 415-420)

#### 6.1 Definition (Section 415)
```
CHEATING:
Whoever, by deceiving any person:
1. Fraudulently or dishonestly induces person to deliver property, OR
2. Intentionally induces person to do/omit doing anything which he would not do/omit
   if not deceived, and which causes harm/damage

ELEMENTS:

1. DECEPTION
   - False representation of fact (express or implied)
   - Concealment of material fact (if duty to disclose)
   - Any act of trickery/fraud

2. INDUCEMENT
   - Victim acts due to deception (causal link)

3. DELIVERY OF PROPERTY OR ACTION/OMISSION
   - Victim delivers property OR does/omits act

4. FRAUDULENT/DISHONEST INTENTION + HARM
   - Accused intended to deceive for wrongful gain/loss

EXAMPLES:
✓ Selling fake jewelry as genuine gold (deception → victim pays → cheating)
✓ Real estate fraud (selling same plot to multiple buyers - deception)
✓ Ponzi scheme (false promise of high returns - deception → investors deposit money)
✓ Identity theft, credit card fraud (impersonation - deception)
```

#### 6.2 Punishment (Section 417-420)
```
SIMPLE CHEATING (Section 417):
Imprisonment up to 1 year OR Fine OR Both

CHEATING WITH KNOWLEDGE THAT WRONGFUL LOSS MAY RESULT (Section 418):
Imprisonment up to 3 years OR Fine OR Both

CHEATING BY PERSONATION (Section 419):
Pretending to be another person or representing another person:
Imprisonment up to 3 years OR Fine OR Both

CHEATING AND DISHONESTLY INDUCING DELIVERY OF PROPERTY (Section 420):
Imprisonment up to 7 years + Fine
(Most common, serious form - property loss)

Section 420 is iconic: "420" slang in India for "fraudster/cheat"

COGNIZABLE: Yes (Section 420)
BAILABLE: No (Section 420), Yes (Section 417-419)
NON-COMPOUNDABLE (Section 420)
```

#### 6.3 Modern Cheating - Cyber Frauds
```
COMMON TYPES:

1. PHISHING:
   Fake emails/websites impersonating banks, asking for credentials
   Victim enters password → Account hacked → Money stolen
   Offense: Cheating (Section 420) + IT Act Section 66C (identity theft)

2. ONLINE SHOPPING FRAUD:
   Fake e-commerce sites, payment taken, goods never delivered
   Offense: Cheating (Section 420)

3. LOTTERY/PRIZE SCAM:
   "You won ₹25 lakh lottery, pay ₹50,000 processing fee to claim"
   Victim pays fee, no prize received
   Offense: Cheating (Section 420)

4. ROMANCE SCAM:
   Fake online relationship, request money (medical emergency, visa, etc.)
   Offense: Cheating by personation (Section 419) + Section 420

PUNISHMENT: Section 420 (up to 7 years) + IT Act violations (up to 3 years)
Can be tried cumulatively (10 years total if convicted under both)
```

---

### 7. MISCHIEF (Section 425-440)

#### 7.1 Definition (Section 425)
```
MISCHIEF:
Whoever, with intent to cause or knowing likely to cause wrongful loss/damage to public or any person:
- Causes destruction of property, OR
- Causes diminution in value/utility of property, OR
- Changes any property in manner that destroys/diminishes value/utility

ELEMENTS:
1. Intention or knowledge (to cause wrongful loss/damage)
2. Destruction, damage, or alteration of property
3. Wrongful loss to owner or public

EXAMPLES:
✓ Slashing car tires (destruction)
✓ Spray-painting graffiti on building (diminution of value)
✓ Releasing water from reservoir (alteration causing loss)
✓ Poisoning crops in field (destruction)
✓ Damaging public road (mischief against public property)

PUNISHMENT (Section 426):
Imprisonment up to 3 months OR Fine OR Both
```

#### 7.2 Aggravated Forms (Section 427-440)
```
MISCHIEF CAUSING DAMAGE ≥₹50 (Section 427):
Imprisonment up to 2 years OR Fine OR Both

MISCHIEF BY KILLING/MAIMING ANIMAL (Section 428-429):
If animal value ≥₹10: Imprisonment up to 2 years OR Fine OR Both
If animal value ≥₹50 (cattle, etc.): Imprisonment up to 5 years OR Fine OR Both

MISCHIEF BY INJURY TO IRRIGATION WORKS (Section 430):
Imprisonment up to 5 years OR Fine OR Both
(Serious because affects agriculture, livelihoods)

MISCHIEF BY DESTRUCTION/DAMAGE TO PUBLIC ROAD/BRIDGE (Section 431):
Imprisonment up to 5 years OR Fine OR Both

MISCHIEF BY CAUSING INUNDATION/OBSTRUCTION TO PUBLIC DRAINAGE (Section 432):
Imprisonment up to 5 years OR Fine OR Both

MISCHIEF WITH INTENT TO DESTROY/MAKE UNSAFE DECKED VESSEL (Section 433-434):
Imprisonment up to 10 years + Fine

MISCHIEF BY FIRE/EXPLOSIVE WITH INTENT TO DAMAGE (Section 435-436):
House/tent: Imprisonment up to 7 years + Fine
Vessel: Imprisonment up to LIFE OR 10 years + Fine

MISCHIEF TO RAILWAY/AIRCRAFT (Section 438-439):
Putting out of order any machinery/equipment with intent to cause accident:
Imprisonment for LIFE OR up to 10 years + Fine

COGNIZABLE: Yes (if damage >₹50)
BAILABLE: Depends on section
```

---

### 8. CRIMINAL TRESPASS & HOUSE-BREAKING (Section 441-462)

#### 8.1 Criminal Trespass (Section 441-447)
```
DEFINITION (Section 441):
Whoever enters into or upon property in possession of another person:
1. With intent to commit offense, OR
2. With intent to intimidate/insult/annoy person in possession, OR
3. Having lawfully entered, unlawfully remains with above intents

ELEMENTS:
1. Entry or remaining on property
2. Property in possession of another
3. Entry/remaining without permission (unlawful)
4. Specific intent (to commit offense, intimidate, insult, annoy)

EXAMPLES:
✓ Entering neighbor's land to steal fruits (intent to commit offense - theft)
✓ Entering ex-partner's home to threaten (intent to intimidate)
✓ Entering rival's shop to create nuisance (intent to annoy)

PUNISHMENT (Section 447):
Imprisonment up to 3 months OR Fine ₹500 OR Both

HOUSE-TRESPASS (Section 442, 448):
If trespass into building/tent used as human dwelling, place of worship, or custody of property:
Imprisonment up to 1 year OR Fine ₹1,000 OR Both

HOUSE-TRESPASS TO COMMIT OFFENSE PUNISHABLE WITH DEATH (Section 449):
Imprisonment for LIFE OR up to 10 years + Fine

COGNIZABLE: Yes (if house-trespass or to commit serious offense)
BAILABLE: Yes (simple), No (aggravated)
```

#### 8.2 House-Breaking (Section 445, 454-460)
```
HOUSE-BREAKING:
House-trespass committed by:
- Entering through passage made by accused OR
- Any passage not intended for human entrance, OR
- Entering/exiting in manner that involves breaking (roof, wall, door, window, lock)

Essentially: Unlawful entry into dwelling by breaking/unusual passage

PUNISHMENT:

HOUSE-BREAKING TO COMMIT OFFENSE (Section 454):
Imprisonment up to 2 years + Fine

HOUSE-BREAKING AFTER PREPARATION FOR HURT/ASSAULT (Section 456):
Imprisonment up to 10 years + Fine

LURKING HOUSE-TRESPASS/HOUSE-BREAKING BY NIGHT (Section 457-458):
Lurking house-trespass by night: Imprisonment up to 3 years + Fine
House-breaking by night: Imprisonment up to 14 years + Fine
(Higher punishment for night crimes - more fear, difficulty in defense)

HOUSE-BREAKING TO COMMIT OFFENSE PUNISHABLE WITH DEATH/LIFE (Section 459):
Imprisonment for LIFE OR up to 10 years + Fine

HOUSE-BREAKING WITH DEADLY WEAPON (Section 460):
Imprisonment for LIFE OR up to 10 years + Fine

COGNIZABLE: Yes
BAILABLE: No
NON-COMPOUNDABLE
TRIABLE BY: Sessions Court (if serious, e.g., Section 458-460)
```

---

## Landmark Cases

### 1. **State of Maharashtra v. Mohd. Yakub (1980) - SC**
**Issue**: Can picking pocket be robbery or only theft?

**Held**: If victim unaware at time of taking (pickpocketing) → THEFT
If victim aware and resisted, force used → ROBBERY

Robbery requires immediate violence/threat, not stealth.

---

### 2. **K.N. Mehra v. State of Rajasthan (1957) - SC**
**Issue**: Difference between cheating and breach of contract?

**Held**: Cheating requires fraudulent intention AT TIME OF INDUCEMENT
If contract made honestly, later breached → Civil breach, NOT cheating
If contract made with intention to deceive from start → Cheating (Section 420)

---

## Conclusion

Property offenses constitute bulk of IPC crimes. Key distinctions: Theft vs extortion vs robbery (consent, violence), CBT vs misappropriation (entrustment vs innocent possession), Cheating (deception inducing delivery), Mischief (property damage), Criminal trespass vs house-breaking (entry, breaking). Understanding these gradations critical for charging, defense strategy, and punishment severity (₹500 fine for simple trespass vs life imprisonment for dacoity with murder).
